3. **The enemy mortar's hunt for counter-battery range (v22) is now genuinely high priority**, not just a one-shot decision made while idle: `_update_enemy_mortar_positioning` re-aims every tick the friendly mortar is visible and out of range (not only when the enemy mortar has no move_target yet), so it continuously corrects toward the friendly mortar's actual current position instead of plodding on toward a possibly-stale point, and now stops the INSTANT it comes into range — even mid-transit — rather than finishing a walk to a farther point computed earlier.
4. **More tree cover.** Every TREES zone scaled up 1.4x per side (~2x area) around its existing center, roughly doubling total forest coverage from ~4% to ~7.7% of the map — more cover to actually use now that squads can deploy well outside the village (v20) and spread out instead of bunching (this round).

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed tree coverage now exceeds 6% of the map, `nearest_cover_point` reliably avoids an occupied zone (and still finds cover if literally every zone is marked occupied), three squads alerted together spread across at least two different cover points rather than converging on one, a tightly bunched ally can catch spillover fire while one well outside the radius never does, and the enemy mortar both stops immediately upon entering range mid-transit and continuously redirects toward a friendly mortar that relocates while still out of range. Follow-up full-battle stress runs (6 trials) came back with no crashes and a ~17% timeout rate, consistent with the established baseline — no regression from any of these changes.

### 2026-09-05 — v24: fixed a retreat that could walk a unit straight at a known enemy

Bug report: a friendly squad's retreat once headed forward, directly toward an enemy squad. Root cause: cover-seeking's existing `retreat_dir` filter (v15) only checks that a candidate zone isn't further in the wrong X-DIRECTION than tolerance — it says nothing about the candidate's actual distance to a specific known enemy position. Worse, the real gap was upstream of that: the player's own explicit General Retreat order (`order_general_retreat`) has always threaded `known_enemy_positions` through, but a unit's AUTOMATIC, threshold-triggered retreat — the far more common case, triggered every time a hit crosses that unit's own casualty threshold — went through `Unit.take_hit() -> _check_retreat() -> order_retreat()` with NO enemy-position data at all, because `Unit` itself has no view of the wider battle and nothing was threading it in from `CombatResolver.resolve_fire`, which calls `take_hit()` directly. A retreat picked with zero idea where the enemy was could pick the "nearest" qualifying cover zone even if an enemy squad happened to be standing right in or next to it.

Fixed on two levels:
1. **A real hard safety filter.** `GameConfig.nearest_cover_point` and `safest_cover_point` both gained a `known_enemy_positions` param (via a new shared `_exclude_dangerous` helper) that HARD-excludes any candidate zone within `DANGER_RADIUS` (250m) of a known enemy position — falling back to the unrestricted set only if that would leave literally nowhere to go, same graceful-fallback pattern used everywhere else in this system. Previously only the SPOTTER's `safest_cover_point` had any enemy-awareness at all (and even that only *prefers* the safest of the nearest few zones — no hard floor); ordinary squads and the mortar had none.
2. **Actually wiring known-enemy data through to where it was missing.** `Unit.take_hit()` gained a `known_enemy_positions` parameter, threaded to `_check_retreat()` and `_apply_mortar_casualties()` — the two paths that call `order_retreat()` on their own, with no player command involved. `CombatResolver.resolve_fire()` gained the same parameter, passed straight through to `take_hit()`. `BattleManager` now computes and supplies it at every place a hit gets resolved: `_resolve_fire_and_check_bunching` (ordinary squad/mortar fire, plus the bunching-spillover hit), and `_resolve_pending_counter_battery`'s direct hit. Every automatic retreat is now exactly as enemy-aware as the player's own general-retreat command already was.

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed both cover-point functions now refuse to land inside, or within `DANGER_RADIUS` of, a zone with a known enemy in it (while still falling back to *some* cover if every zone is technically "dangerous"), and — reproducing the exact reported scenario — a squad forced across its own retreat threshold with an enemy squad sitting right on its nearest cover zone retreats somewhere else entirely, never toward or next to that enemy. Confirmed the same for a mortar's fleeing crew after both an ordinary hit and a counter-battery hit specifically (the two paths that previously had zero enemy awareness). Six follow-up full-battle stress runs completed with no crashes, all resolving naturally within budget.

### 2026-09-05 — v25: mortar relocation is now an actual walk, not an abstract cooldown, and the dashboard gives mortar status equal billing

Follow-up questions from the user exposed that `relocate_cooldown` (a player-set doctrine slider, 1-15s) never represented anything real — it was a flat time penalty bolted onto `fire_timer`, disconnected from where the mortar actually went, how far, or how fast. Removed entirely. What replaces it:

1. **Relocation is now a real, simulated walk.** `BattleManager._relocate_mortar()` picks an actual destination — the nearest point with no line of sight from any known enemy (true concealment, reusing `nearest_hidden_point` from v22), or, with no known threat to hide from, simply the nearest cover (shoot-and-scoot displaces as standing procedure regardless of visibility — see below) — and walks the mortar there via the normal move_target system at a real speed (`GameConfig.MORTAR_RELOCATE_SPEED`, a hustling pace). There's no separate cooldown on top: the walk itself is what keeps the mortar from firing again (`_tick_fire`'s existing `has_move_target` gate), for exactly as long as the real distance and speed dictate — never an arbitrary flat number.
2. **Reload time is now realistic and tactical-clock-driven.** `Unit.reload_time` changed from 3 REAL/engine seconds (an arbitrary gameplay number) to 30 TACTICAL seconds (`Unit.reload_time`, driven by `scenario_delta` now, not `delta`) — a believable lay-load-fire cycle for a deliberate, spotter-corrected shot, tied to the same real-world clock as everything else introduced in v18, not a separate abstracted rate. Squad `fire_interval` was untouched — this ask was specifically about the mortar's own cycle.
3. **Distance and speed now respond to real circumstances.** A mortar that's actually taken counter-battery fire recently (`Unit.evading_counter_battery`, set in `_resolve_pending_counter_battery` whenever a strike lands within blast radius, hit or miss, and consumed on its next relocation) moves at `MORTAR_RELOCATE_SPEED_URGENT` and searches the wider `CONCEALMENT_SEARCH_RINGS_URGENT_M` rings instead of the normal ones — a crew that knows it's been found goes farther AND faster, not just one or the other. Relocating into TREES applies `MORTAR_RELOCATE_TREES_MULTIPLIER` (0.8x) — hauling a tube and base plate through undergrowth is real work.
4. **The two doctrines now mean something mechanically distinct**, which they didn't quite before (hold-position had accidentally picked up an unconditional post-fire relocation in v22, blurring the line): shoot-and-scoot ALWAYS relocates after firing, procedurally, whether or not it's currently spotted — that's the definition of the doctrine. Hold-position only relocates reactively, if actually spotted (`_update_friendly_mortar_concealment`'s existing per-tick check, unchanged) — it stays put and reloads faster unless something actually threatens it, the real tradeoff the doctrine choice is supposed to represent.
5. **`CasualtyDashboard` gives mortar status equal visual weight**, not a smaller, dimmer afterthought: each mortar now gets its own row at the same font size as the casualty line above it, plus a colored status bar (green/orange/gray/red for active/fleeing/withdrawn/destroyed) the same size as the casualty percentage bar — sized and styled to match, not to recede.

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed reload timing is driven by the tactical clock (not real/engine time), `_relocate_mortar` walks to a genuinely LOS-blocked destination at the correct speed, an urgent (recently-shelled) relocation goes measurably farther via the wider search rings and at the faster speed while consuming the flag, relocating into an actual TREES zone applies the terrain penalty, a counter-battery strike landing nearby sets the evading flag, shoot-and-scoot always relocates after firing even with no known threats while hold-position does not unless spotted, and the dashboard renders one equally-sized, correctly color-coded row per mortar (hiding unused rows on the player's single-mortar side). Six full-battle stress runs afterward completed with no crashes, no mortar ever inside a building, and — notably — zero timeouts, resolving faster overall than before now that mortar reload is tied to a realistic cadence instead of an arbitrary one.

### 2026-09-05 — v26: the enemy commander's general retreat no longer drags fully-active mortars along with it

Bug report: the enemy retreated with only 4 casualties reported and both mortars still active — seemingly contradicting the 40% `ENEMY_COMMANDER_RETREAT_THRESHOLD` (v19). Simulated the exact scenario directly (4/26 pips lost, both mortars untouched): `_enemy_situation_hopeless()` correctly returns `false` — the threshold math itself was never the bug. But "both mortars were in action" right up to the moment of a collective retreat pointed at a real design gap in `_check_enemy_commander_retreat()` itself: when it DOES fire (at a genuinely higher casualty count than the user had been tracking — easy to lose count of destroyed/withdrawn squads over a long battle), it swept EVERY active enemy unit into the retreat, mortars included. That's not right tactically: a mortar sitting well to the rear isn't at risk of being overrun the way forward infantry is, and a real commander keeps supporting fires going — harassing, covering the withdrawal, still hunting for counter-battery range — rather than pulling a perfectly good gun out of the fight for no reason.

Fixed two things:
1. **`_check_enemy_commander_retreat()` now only orders SQUADS to retreat** — mortars are explicitly excluded from the sweep and carry on with their normal behavior (firing, chasing counter-battery range per v22, evading if personally threatened) even once the infantry assault has been called off. A mortar still retreats on its own exactly as before if it's personally hit (`Unit._apply_mortar_casualties` — unconditional on any survived hit, unrelated to this change) — this only stops the infantry's collective defeat from automatically dragging an untouched mortar along too.
2. **The retreat banner now states the actual casualty percentage** that triggered it ("...the attack has failed (43% casualties) — mortars continue the fire mission") instead of a bare unexplained line — this exact kind of "why did that just happen" confusion shouldn't require reading source code to answer.

The player's own General Retreat button was deliberately left unchanged — it's an explicit command, not an AI judgment call, so it still pulls back everything including the player's own mortar if that's what's pressed.

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed a hopeless-triggered general retreat leaves fully-active mortars in `ACTIVE` state while still pulling back every still-active squad, the log banner includes both the real casualty percentage and a note that mortars continue, and a mortar that IS personally hit still retreats on its own regardless of the commander's squad-only order. Eleven full-battle stress runs afterward (including one that naturally triggered the general retreat) all completed cleanly with no crashes.

### 2026-09-05 — v27: the enemy mortar's counter-battery chase no longer requires an actual visual spot

Bug report: enemy mortars weren't trying to get into counter-battery range at all. Root cause: `_update_enemy_mortar_positioning` (v22, sharpened in v23) required the friendly mortar to be CURRENTLY visible (`is_visible`) before the enemy would react — but v22/v25 also gave the friendly mortar an aggressive concealment-seeking habit (hide immediately after every shot, or the instant it's spotted between shots). Combined, the friendly mortar had become so good at staying hidden that the enemy rarely got a large enough visibility window to ever notice it, let alone chase it — the mechanic existed but had almost nothing to trigger on in practice.

The fix models something the game already had half of: real counter-battery detection doesn't require ongoing visual contact with the crew — it comes from tracking the outgoing round's muzzle blast or trajectory the instant it fires (`_resolve_mortar_counter_battery`'s whole premise already relies on this). `_launch_mortar_shot` now records `_last_detected_mortar_fire[mortar] = {position, time}` on EVERY shot, any mortar, regardless of visibility. A new `_known_friendly_mortar_position()` gives the enemy's chase logic a position to work with from two sources — live visibility if currently available (most accurate), falling back to the last detected firing position if that's within `MORTAR_FIRE_DETECTION_EXPIRY` (180 tactical seconds, roughly the counter-battery response window) — old enough and the mortar's almost certainly moved on, not worth chasing a stale fix. A mortar that hides perfectly between shots no longer becomes un-huntable — it's still given away every time it actually fires, which is the whole point of having fired in the first place.

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed no fix exists before the mortar has ever been seen or heard firing, live visibility gives an exact fix, firing gives a fix even when the mortar was never visually spotted at all, a stale detection expires correctly, live visibility is preferred over an older detected position when both are available, and — directly reproducing the reported bug — an enemy mortar successfully chases and reaches counter-battery range using ONLY a detected firing position, having never once visually spotted the target. A dedicated full-battle run with the friendly mortar deployed deep in the rear (4662m/4700m from the two enemy mortars, both initially out of range) confirmed both enemy mortars closed distance and reached exactly-in-range positions (3495m/3496m) purely from firing detection. Further full-battle stress runs completed cleanly with no crashes.

### 2026-09-05 — v28: a real structural deadlock (100% of battles!) and the friendly mortar's over-eager fleeing, both fixed

Bug report: an implausibly clean "SUCCESSFUL DEFENSE" (0% player casualties, only 15% enemy casualties, "Enemy withdrew: Enemy Squad, Enemy Squad") — the user correctly judged this shouldn't have happened. Investigation found something much more severe than a bad verdict: **10 out of 10 test battles were ending via the stagnation timeout** (`_check_battle_end`'s "nobody's fired or moved in 15 real seconds" safety valve), not through actual decisive combat. Dumping full battlefield state at the moment of stagnation showed why: multiple squad pairs had completely clear LOS to each other but every one sat 500-2200m apart — well beyond `SQUAD_ENGAGEMENT_RANGE` (400m) — with nothing on either side able to close that distance. Root cause: an enemy squad that breaks for cover under fire has never had any way to resume its advance afterward — it just holds that position for the rest of the battle. That was always true, but harmless while cover was concentrated near the village (v1-era map); now that tree cover is doubled and spread across the whole 5km map (v23), a squad breaking off early in its march can land somewhere permanently out of range of everything, with no path back into the fight. The stagnation timeout was correctly detecting a real dead end — the actual bug was upstream, in the AI never trying to un-stall itself.

Fixed with `BattleManager._update_enemy_squad_advance()`: an enemy squad that has already broken for cover (`sought_cover`), is currently idle, and has nothing worth shooting at (`_pick_target` comes up empty) now resumes closing on `GameConfig.VILLAGE_CENTER` in bounded rushes (`ENEMY_ADVANCE_RUSH_DISTANCE`, 400m per leg) rather than sprinting blindly — it still pauses to reassess (and fire, if something's now in range) between rushes, real fire-and-movement rather than one long blind dash. Re-measured: full stagnation-ending rate dropped from 10/10 to roughly 30-40%, and inspecting those remaining cases individually showed they're now genuine dead ends — a single isolated player squad, physically cut off from the enemy-occupied village by a hill or building with no possible LOS, nothing left to resolve. (One earlier reading of "6/10 still stagnating" during this investigation turned out to be inflated by a bug in the TEST script itself, reusing one `CombatLog` across trials so a stale "stalemated" message from an earlier trial falsely tainted a later one that had actually ended in a real defeat — worth noting since it looked like a much smaller improvement than it actually was until caught.)

Second report, same conversation: the friendly mortar didn't need to run from counter-battery range — it could stand and fight, or simply stay concealed — and wasn't being careful enough to remain out of LOS in the first place. Three fixes:
1. **`_update_friendly_mortar_concealment` now checks for a live target first** — being spotted no longer means automatic flight; if the mortar currently has something worth shooting at (including an enemy mortar that's wandered into range), it stands and fights. Concealment is the fallback for being exposed with nothing to show for it, not a reflex to being seen.
2. **`GameConfig.nearest_hidden_point` now tries the reverse slope of an actual nearby hill first** (`_reverse_slope_candidate` — a point 75% of the way down a hill's radius, on the far side from the threats' average direction), before falling back to the previous random-ring sampling. Real elevation masking like this tends to stay valid even if a threat shifts position somewhat; a point that merely tests clear against today's exact threat positions doesn't have that property.
3. **The friendly mortar is now never visually spotted at all**, categorically (`_refresh_visibility` skips it outright, even at point-blank range with dead-clear LOS) — a well-sited, camouflaged crew isn't something rifle squads happen to notice by scanning the horizon in this game's model. The only way the enemy ever gets a fix on it is by detecting it actually firing (the muzzle-blast/trajectory detection from v27's counter-battery-range chase) — which also means the "spotted, flee" reactive trigger now essentially never fires for a hold-position mortar at all; it just holds its ground unless it draws counter-battery fire, exactly matching "should never allow the enemy to observe it directly."

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed an idle enemy squad with nothing to shoot at resumes its advance while one that still has a target does not, that the resumed advance moves in bounded legs rather than one sprint, that the friendly mortar stays invisible under a barrage of forced spotting rolls at point-blank range yet still yields a detection fix the instant it fires, that a spotted mortar with a live target stands and fights instead of fleeing, and that the reverse-slope search finds a genuinely hidden spot next to an actual hill directly rather than by chance. Six full-battle stress runs (both doctrines) and a further 15-trial stagnation-rate check afterward confirmed no crashes and a stable, now-legitimate stagnation rate.

### 2026-09-05 — v29: mortars now lead a retreating target, and a unit under fire while retreating zigzags

Bug report: "When units are retreating, the mortar shooting at them should try to lead them. I don't think it does. But if a retreating unit is taking artillery fire, it should zig zag a bit to reduce the chance of getting hit."

The user was right about the first half. `BattleManager._mortar_aim_point()` only extrapolated an aim point ahead of a target when `Unit.movement_predictable` was true — but `order_retreat()` always sets `movement_predictable = false` (it also drives a separate, deliberately-unrelated accuracy penalty in `CombatResolver.resolve_fire` for genuinely erratic movement), so a retreating unit was never led at all, only ever shot at its exact current position. Fixed by decoupling the two: `_mortar_aim_point()` now extrapolates whenever the target's heading is actually knowable right now — `has_move_target` (a retreat's first leg to cover, or any other directed move) for the direction and speed, or, new, a direct `Unit.State.RETREATING` branch using the fixed team-based retreat direction and `retreat_speed` for the unescorted final dash to safety. `movement_predictable` keeps its old, narrower job untouched: only the hit-chance multiplier, not whether a shot leads at all.

Second half: a retreating unit that has actually had a mortar round resolve against it (hit, or a near-miss still within `MORTAR_EVASION_RADIUS`) now starts juking — `Unit.zigzagging`, set in `_resolve_pending_mortar_shots` and never cleared for the rest of the withdrawal, since once you know you're under a barrage you keep broken-field running rather than relaxing after one lucky dodge. Only affects the final retreat leg (`_step_retreat` — exactly the leg the lead-aiming fix above can otherwise lead cleanly); the first leg to cover already uses ordinary point-to-point movement. Implemented as discrete random lateral "jinks" rather than a smooth sine wave — a periodic motion has a period a mortar's own extrapolation could eventually ride out, so direction and speed re-roll at a randomized interval (`ZIGZAG_JINK_MIN_INTERVAL`/`MAX_INTERVAL`, 6-12 tactical seconds) at a fraction of the unit's own retreat speed (`ZIGZAG_LATERAL_SPEED_FRACTION`, 0.6) — real sideways progress, not a wobble in place. No new hit-chance math: this works entirely through the existing drift-vs-`MORTAR_EVASION_RADIUS` check, exactly like any other reason a target might not be where a shell lands.

(Counter-battery fire (`_resolve_pending_counter_battery`) only ever resolves against a mortar still in `State.ACTIVE` — a retreating unit is unreachable by that path today, so it was left out of the zigzag trigger; nothing currently exercises it, and adding it in would be speculative.)

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed a retreating target's aim point now leads by `retreat_speed * MORTAR_FLIGHT_TIME`, that a cover-bound squad is led along its actual heading regardless of `movement_predictable`, that `zigzagging` gets set on both a resolved hit and an evaded near-miss against a retreating target (but not otherwise), that a zigzagging unit's lateral position visibly wanders over time while a non-zigzagging one holds a perfectly straight line, and an 8-trial full-battle stress run with real retreats occurring in every run confirmed no crashes or hangs with both mechanics active together.

### 2026-09-05 — v30: losing a mortar (or its crew) now counts in the AAR exactly like losing a squad

Bug report: "In evaluating the battle results, losing the mortar should count against the player, and killing enemy mortars should count in favor. Mortar crew casualties should count the same as any other casualties." — clarified this was specifically about the final battle report, not the retreat-threshold trigger logic (which was already unaffected).

Root cause: a mortar's `max_pips` was hardcoded to `1`, while its actual crew strength (`crew_size`, 4) was tracked entirely separately via `crew_killed`. `take_hit()` unconditionally dropped a unit's `pips` by 1 before branching on kind, so a mortar's single pip was always fully spent on its *first* hit — whether that hit killed one crew member (leaving three survivors who abandon the gun and retreat) or wiped the whole crew outright. Since `BattleManager._compute_side_stats()` (the exact numbers the AAR's casualty percentages and exchange ratio are built from) sums `max_pips`/`pips` across every unit on a side, a mortar's fate — grazed or annihilated — always cost the side a flat, trivial 1 pip out of the total, dwarfed by any squad's 4. Destroying an enemy mortar barely moved the exchange ratio; losing your own barely dented your casualty percentage.

Fixed by making a mortar's pips real crew accounting instead of a binary flag: `max_pips` is now set to `crew_size` in `setup()`, and `_apply_mortar_casualties()` sets `pips = crew_size - crew_killed` directly (rather than `take_hit()`'s generic `pips -= 1`, which no longer runs for mortars at all — moved below the mortar branch's `return`). A mortar that loses one crew member out of four now shows 3/4 pips, same granularity as a squad; one that loses its whole crew shows 0/4 — costing the side exactly as many pips as a fully destroyed squad, not a token 1. No other mortar behavior changes: still one random casualty roll per hit (`randi_range(1, remaining)`), still abandons the gun and retreats the instant any crew survive, still only truly `DESTROYED` when the last crew member falls.

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed a fresh mortar starts at `max_pips == crew_size` pips, a partial hit sets `pips == crew_size - crew_killed` and sends survivors into `RETREATING` rather than ending the unit, a full-crew loss zeroes pips and sets `DESTROYED`, and `_compute_side_stats` on a one-squad-one-mortar side now costs exactly the same pips (4) for destroying either one — where before the mortar cost only 1. A 6-trial full-battle stress run confirmed the new `pips_total` (17 = 3 squads × 4 + mortar's 4 + spotter's 1) held up end-to-end with no crashes.

### 2026-09-05 — v31: the AAR report text no longer overflows its background box

Bug report (screenshot): the after-action report's text was spilling out past the right edge of its dark background panel, onto the battlefield map behind it — e.g. "Enemy withdrew: ... Enemy Mortar (2/4 crew killed, g" running straight off the edge instead of wrapping.

Root cause: `main.gd`'s report `Label` was added directly as a child of the `ColorRect` background — a plain `Control`, not a `Container`. A bare `Control` never actually caps a child's width below that child's own *natural* (unwrapped) content size: `custom_minimum_size` can only raise a control's effective minimum, never lower it below what the control would otherwise need. So even with `autowrap_mode` set and an explicit `size = Vector2(540, 480)` assigned, Godot silently re-expanded the label back out to whatever width its longest unwrapped line actually needed (over 1000px for a long casualty list) the moment that exceeded 540 — autowrap never got a chance to do anything, because the label was never actually narrow enough to need to wrap. Confirmed directly: an isolated reproduction of the exact same property assignments, in the exact same order, showed the label's final size stuck at `(1070, 480)` regardless of whether `autowrap_mode` was set before or after `size` — proving this wasn't a property-ordering bug but a structural one.

Fixed by giving the label a real `Container` ancestor, matching the pattern `combat_log.gd` already uses successfully for its own scrollable text: a `ScrollContainer` (540×480, horizontal scrolling disabled so it can't just let the line run off sideways instead of wrapping) between the background and the label, with the label's `size_flags_horizontal` set to `SIZE_EXPAND_FILL`. A `Container` *does* forcibly assign its child's width from the outside, which is the one thing a bare `Control` parent won't do — so the label now actually wraps at 540px, and the `ScrollContainer` clips (and, if a report ever runs long enough, scrolls) anything that still doesn't fit vertically, rather than letting it spill out over the map.

**Verified:** reproduced the exact bug headlessly first (bare-Control parent → label stuck at its unwrapped natural width, confirmed numerically), then confirmed the `ScrollContainer` fix constrains the label to the intended 540px width and keeps its right edge inside the background panel's own right edge, using the same long multi-squad casualty-list text from the bug report.

### 2026-09-05 — v32: significantly more forest, as irregular natural blobs instead of rectangles

Request: "Let's significantly increase the amount of trees on the map. And they should not be in rectangles. The forested areas should have natural-seeming shapes."

Forested terrain had been a flat list of 16 axis-aligned `Rect2` zones sharing `TERRAIN_ZONES` with the BUILDING zones — visually boxy, and every terrain query (`get_terrain_type_at`, `nearest_cover_point`, `safest_cover_point`, `_exclude_dangerous`, the drawing code) worked directly in terms of `rect.has_point`/`rect.position + rect.size`. The map already had exactly the right technique for "irregular, natural-looking footprint" sitting right next to this code: HILLS defines each hill's effective radius at any angle as a base radius warped by a small sum of cosine harmonics (`_hill_radius_warp`), giving hills a non-circular, elongated silhouette instead of a perfect Gaussian bump. Forested areas now use the identical technique — refactored into a shared `_radius_warp(harmonics, theta)` used by both — via a new `FOREST_PATCHES` array: each patch is a `center_m` + `radius_m` + its own `warp_harmonics`, with `_forest_radius_at`/`_point_in_forest_patch` doing the containment test and `_draw_forest_patch` filling the exact same warped-radius outline as a polygon (mirroring `_draw_hills`' contour-ring approach) before scattering tree symbols across it, keeping only the ones that actually land inside the blob.

`TERRAIN_ZONES` now holds only the 3 BUILDING zones (the village + 2 farmhouses) — trees are a wholly separate array, since they never needed to share BUILDING's LOS-blocking or rect-based path-crossing logic in the first place (trees only ever affected cover/concealment, never hard LOS — see `has_direct_los`'s comment, unchanged). The cover-point search functions (`nearest_cover_point`, `safest_cover_point`, `_exclude_dangerous`) now iterate a unified `_all_cover_zones()` list — buildings and forest patches wrapped in one shape-agnostic "zone" dict (`center`, plus either `rect` or `patch`) — with `_cover_zone_contains`/`_random_point_in_cover_zone` dispatching on which kind of zone it is, so the exact same weighted-nearest-zone selection and DANGER_RADIUS/avoid-positions logic now works over both shapes identically.

Patch count went from 16 to 30 (roughly doubled), with typical radii also somewhat larger than the old rects' equivalent footprint — a real, substantial increase in total tree coverage, not just more small copses. Two of the new patches ("village hill NW/NE," reusing the original rects' rough positions) initially had their warp-inflated max reach come within a few meters of actually poking into the village's BUILDING rect — caught by a headless overlap scan comparing every patch's worst-case reach against every building's nearest point, not by inspection — and fixed by trimming those two patches' radii back to a comfortable margin (>30m clear in both directions).

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed the patch count increased as intended (and `TERRAIN_ZONES` now holds only buildings), that a patch's actual radius varies meaningfully by angle and that points just past its base radius can fall genuinely outside it (i.e., this isn't secretly still a circle or square), that BUILDING still takes precedence over TREES at a shared point, that `nearest_cover_point`/`safest_cover_point` always land inside real cover terrain (including with `avoid_buildings=true`, which continues to correctly exclude every building), and that trees still don't affect `has_direct_los` (unchanged). A dedicated overlap scan confirmed no forest patch's worst-case footprint reaches any building after the two radius fixes. An 8-trial full-battle stress run with the new terrain active completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-05 — v33: a second fieldable setup — a drone team replacing the artillery spotter

Request: at launch, let the player choose between "Level 0" (the existing artillery spotter) and "Level 1," a drone team — a 3-person crew flying a rotation of 4 real Mavic-class scout drones (12km round-trip range, ~21-minute flight time each), always keeping exactly one airborne (scouting, mortars the top priority) and a second standing by fully charged in case the first is shot down, with the other two recovering/recharging. The drone loiters at 300m, "hard to see and hard to hit," and gives a genuinely better view than a ground spotter — including catching a shoot-and-scoot mortar's new position and seeing through concealment that would fool a ground observer. The user also specified, mid-implementation, the real 21-minute Mavic flight-time spec, and that a routine swap should have no coverage gap — the standby launches as the old one is coming in, not after it's actually landed.

This is the largest single change of the session — a new game mode, a new unit lifecycle, and new spotting rules — so it leaned hard on reusing existing, already-proven machinery rather than inventing parallel systems:

1. **Mode selection**: a new `LevelSelectScreen` (`GameConfig.ReconMode.SPOTTER`/`DRONE_TEAM`) is now the very first thing `main.gd` shows, before deployment. The choice persists across "try again" restarts within a session — level-select only appears once, at launch.

2. **The drone team** (`Unit.Kind.DRONE_TEAM`) is a normal deployed unit, reusing the spotter's exact deployment zone/position and — mechanically — the mortar's crew-casualty model verbatim (`_apply_crew_casualties`, renamed from `_apply_mortar_casualties` now that two kinds share it): `max_pips == crew_size` (3), one decisive hit either kills some crew and sends survivors fleeing (no more drone operations once that happens) or wipes the whole crew outright. It never fires and never itself observes anything — it's purely the fleet's home base.

3. **The airborne drone** (`Unit.Kind.DRONE`) is the one genuinely new lifecycle in the game: unlike every other unit, it's created and freed per-sortie rather than existing for the whole battle. Only the CURRENTLY airborne drone is a real `Unit`; the rest of the 4-airframe fleet is plain bookkeeping on `BattleManager` (`_drones_ready_for_launch`, `_drone_recovering` timers, `_drones_destroyed`) — a grounded drone isn't part of the battle in any way. `_update_drone_operations` runs the whole rotation every tick: ticks recovery timers, updates the airborne sortie's flight-time/range budget (`GameConfig.DRONE_MAX_FLIGHT_TIME`/`DRONE_ROUND_TRIP_RANGE`) and re-picks its search target, and — in ONE single check — launches a fresh drone the instant the sky is empty and a standby is ready. Routing both "just got shot down" (via the `state_changed` signal clearing `active_drone`) and "hit its flight budget" (`_update_active_drone` clearing it directly) through that same single launch check means a swap is never more than one tick late either way, which is what actually delivers the "no gap on a routine swap" behavior — the standby launches the same tick the old one turns for home, not after any simulated flight back.

4. **Search AI** (`_drone_search_target`, re-evaluated every tick, not set once): orbit a currently-visible enemy mortar if there is one (by far the highest priority, per the request) → failing that, fly to wherever an enemy mortar was last DETECTED FIRING (a new `_known_enemy_mortar_fire_position`, the exact mirror of the existing `_known_friendly_mortar_position` used for the enemy's own counter-battery chase — same underlying `_last_detected_mortar_fire` dict, just read from the other side) → failing that, any other visible enemy unit → with no lead at all, sweep toward the road corridor to establish contact. "Sees where a shoot-and-scoot mortar moved to" falls out of this almost for free: the drone's detection range is big enough that simply staying near a mortar's last-known area re-acquires it after a nearby relocation, no separate tracking logic needed.

5. **A genuinely better sensor**, not the spotter's numbers with a bonus bolted on: `effective_detection_range`/`roll_spot`/`has_live_observer` (`CombatResolver`) now branch on `observer.kind == DRONE` — a much larger flat detection range (no elevation-bonus double-dipping, since it's already always higher than anything on the map), `has_aerial_los` instead of `has_direct_los` (identical BUILDING blocking, but NO ground-elevation masking at all — 300m clears every hill on this 35m-max-height map), and its own concealment table where TREES is a much smaller penalty than at ground level (porous canopy from above) but BUILDING is a bigger one (a roof fully hides what's under it regardless of viewing angle) — the reverse of the ground-level ordering. This is the actual mechanism behind "may see enemies who think they're concealed."

6. **Hard to see, hard to hit**: as a TARGET, the drone skips terrain-based concealment/cover entirely (it isn't standing on any of it) in favor of two flat, severe multipliers — `DRONE_SPOT_CHANCE_MULTIPLIER` (0.15) and `DRONE_HIT_CHANCE_MULTIPLIER` (0.1) — applied regardless of who's looking or shooting. One hit still destroys it outright (`max_pips = 1`, unmanned, no crew to partially lose) but getting that hit is deliberately rare.

7. **Casualty accounting**: a drone is equipment, not personnel — the 3-person crew is safe at the ground station regardless of what happens to whatever's flying. `_compute_side_stats` now explicitly skips `Kind.DRONE` when summing `pips_total`/`pips_lost` (though a destroyed one still appears in the AAR's loss list, just not in the casualty percentage), and `_anyone_moving`/`_all_done_fighting`/`_has_active_units` all skip it too — it's ALWAYS "moving with intent" while airborne (constantly re-picking a search target), which would otherwise both defeat the stalemate timeout and let a lone flying camera falsely keep a wiped-out side "still fighting" or "holding the village."

8. **Dashboard**: a drone-fleet status row (airborne/ready/recharging-with-ETA/lost counts, or the ground crew's own status if IT'S out of action) with the same visual weight as the existing mortar row, shown only in DRONE_TEAM mode.

**Verified:** headless tests covering the full fleet-rotation lifecycle — correct initial rotation (1 airborne + 1 standby + 2 recharging), a shoot-down triggering the standby's launch on the very next tick, a routine RTB (both time-budget and range-budget triggers) doing the same with zero gap when a standby is ready, aerial LOS genuinely ignoring a hill that blocks ground-level LOS between the same two points, the search AI's priority order (visible mortar > last-detected-fire position), drone hit/spot rates landing near the configured severe multipliers over 2000 trials, drone losses correctly excluded from the human-casualty tally while drone-team crew casualties correctly feed into it exactly like a mortar's, and the drone-alone exclusion from the stalemate/holding checks. Both `LevelSelectScreen` and `DeploymentScreen` (in each mode) instantiate and route correctly in isolation. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (6 per mode) completed with no crashes, and the drone-mode trials confirmed real RTB/shoot-down activity actually occurred rather than just being reachable code.

### 2026-09-05 — v34: drones actually fly home instead of vanishing, plus a real recharge-time number

Bug report: "Why do the drones suddenly disappear? They should still fly in to landing. However, the next one can be launched while this is happening." Also asked directly whether `DRONE_RECHARGE_DURATION` (45 minutes in v33) was researched or just guessed.

The honest answer on the second question: guessed, not researched — unlike the 21-minute flight time and 12km round-trip range, which came straight from the user's own numbers. Fixed with an actual spec: a real Mavic-class flight battery (~5000mAh) takes about 96 minutes to charge from empty on a standard charger; `DRONE_RECHARGE_DURATION` is now 100 minutes (96 plus a few minutes for the physical swap/inspection), with a comment explaining where that comes from instead of just asserting it.

The disappearing-drone bug was exactly what it looked like: `_update_active_drone`'s RTB branch did `player_units.erase(d); d.queue_free()` the INSTANT the flight-time/range budget ran out — the old drone was deleted in the same tick the replacement launched, with no actual return flight simulated at all. Fixed by giving BattleManager a second tracked slot, `returning_drone`, alongside `active_drone`: when RTB triggers, the old drone hands off search duty (`active_drone = null`, immediately triggering the standby's launch as before) but keeps existing as a real Unit with `has_move_target` pointed at `drone_team`'s position — the same generic movement system that already drives every other unit (`_tick_movement`, unchanged) carries it home for real, at its normal cruise speed, over real time. A new `_update_returning_drone()` just watches for `_step_toward_target` to naturally clear `has_move_target` on arrival, and only THEN frees it and starts its recharge clock. It's still a legal target while inbound (still `state == ACTIVE`, still in `player_units`) — a returning drone can still be shot down, handled by extending `_on_drone_state_changed` to check both slots, not just `active_drone`.

Chasing down a stress-test failure this surfaced a second, unrelated, pre-existing bug (present since v33, not introduced by this fix): if the drone team itself was destroyed or forced to retreat while a drone was airborne or inbound, `_update_drone_operations`'s "nobody left to fly them" branch froze and freed that drone WITHOUT counting it anywhere — not destroyed, not recovering, not ready, just gone from every count. Caught by a headless stress test asserting an invariant (airborne + inbound + ready + recovering + destroyed must always equal the 4-airframe fleet size) that a plain "does it crash" test would never have noticed. Fixed by counting an abandoned drone as a loss in that branch too, so the fleet total is always fully accounted for no matter how a sortie ends.

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed a routine RTB now hands off to a real, still-existing `returning_drone` (not freed) while a replacement launches the same tick; that it's genuinely stepped toward `drone_team`'s position tick over tick by the ordinary movement system rather than teleporting; that it actually lands (freed, recharge timer started) only once arrival is real; that a shoot-down mid-return-flight is handled correctly; and that the fleet-accounting invariant now holds through an 8-trial full-battle stress run that previously caught a real violation of it (missing exactly one airframe) within the first several trials.

### 2026-09-05 — v35: the drone stops fixating on infantry, and gets a real, longer-lasting fix on mortar fire

Bug report: "drone team is prioritizing enemy infantry too much. Even if it has found infantry, its main job is to look for enemy mortars, not hover over the infantry." Follow-up: "If enemy mortar fires, drone team should have a pretty good idea where to look for it."

Root cause of the first part: `_drone_search_target()`'s priority order had a third tier — "any other currently-visible enemy unit" — below the two mortar-specific checks. Since a visible SQUAD's position rarely changes fast and there was nothing above it in priority once no mortar was visible or recently heard from, the drone would just fly to that squad and re-select the same point every tick indefinitely — parking over infantry instead of continuing to search, exactly the reported behavior. Fixed by removing that tier entirely: a merely-visible squad is no longer a search target at all. In its place, with no mortar lead, the drone now runs `_drone_sweep_target()` — a real patrol across the enemy's whole road corridor, advancing to the next waypoint once within `DRONE_SWEEP_WAYPOINT_RADIUS` (500m) rather than flying to and sitting at one single fixed midpoint (the old blind-sweep fallback had exactly the same fixation problem, just on empty ground instead of on infantry). The patrol deliberately does NOT aim at the enemy's actual (fixed) mortar emplacement coordinates — that would be the drone secretly knowing something it hasn't earned by observation, breaking the live-visibility/fog-of-war model every other part of this game respects.

Second part: the drone already had a fallback tier for "wherever an enemy mortar was last DETECTED FIRING" (`_known_enemy_mortar_fire_position`, added in v33 alongside the search AI), but it was gated by the SAME expiry the enemy's own ground-based counter-battery chase uses (`MORTAR_FIRE_DETECTION_EXPIRY`, 180 tactical seconds / 3 minutes) — reasonable for a mortar reacting from nearby, but often not long enough for a drone that could be anywhere on the map when the shot fires and has to physically fly there first. Fixed with a separate `DRONE_MORTAR_FIRE_LEAD_EXPIRY` (600 seconds / 10 minutes, covering a realistic flight across most of the map's diagonal at `DRONE_CRUISE_SPEED`) — `_known_enemy_mortar_fire_position` now takes an `expiry` parameter (defaulting to the original constant for the counter-battery-chase caller) so the same underlying detection data serves both a fast ground reaction and a slower aerial one without duplicating the tracking dict.

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed a visible-but-otherwise-unremarkable enemy squad no longer becomes the search target (falls through to a sweep waypoint instead), that the sweep patrol actually advances through successive road waypoints as the drone arrives near each one rather than sitting at a fixed point, that a mortar-fire fix well past the enemy's own short reaction window is still usable by the drone (and only actually expires after the much longer drone-specific window), and that a currently-visible mortar still wins over everything else as top priority. An 8-trial full-battle stress run with the updated search AI held the same fleet-accounting invariant from v34 throughout, with no crashes.

### 2026-09-05 — v36: real, sourced Mavic 3 numbers replace the guessed/combat-derated flight envelope

Question: "A drone should be able to fly for a total of 21 minutes. Were they staying airborne for anywhere near that long?" — then, after confirming they weren't, "use the realistic figures, not my figures" and "look up the actual performance characteristics of the mavic and use them," plus a request to look up realistic recharge times too.

Measured first, before touching anything: instrumented several full battles to record each sortie's actual `drone_flight_time` at the moment it ended. With the v33/v34 numbers (21min / 12km round trip / 10 m/s cruise), average sortie length was **13.2 minutes — 63% of the 21-minute target**, confirming the suspicion directly. Root cause: those three numbers weren't mutually consistent. Flying continuously (which the drone always is) at 10 m/s for the full 21 minutes covers 12.6km — already past the 12km round-trip budget — so the RANGE limit, not the TIME limit, was almost always what actually cut a sortie short, and the "reserve enough to physically get home" logic (checked every tick against current distance from base) made real sorties end well before even that raw 12km/10m/s ≈ 20-minute mark whenever the search had carried the drone any real distance from its ground station.

Per the explicit instruction, replaced the combat-derated guess with DJI's own published Mavic 3 specs instead of re-deriving another guess: **46-minute rated max flight time**, and a **30km max flight distance** rated at a sustained 50.4 kph — both real, cited numbers, not adjusted for combat conditions this time. `DRONE_CRUISE_SPEED` is now exactly that 50.4 kph (the speed DJI's own distance figure was actually measured at), so the range and speed numbers are at least drawn from the same real test rather than mismatched the way the old ones were. `DRONE_RECHARGE_DURATION` (100 tactical minutes: ~96 to fully charge a Mavic 3 battery from empty on the standard 65W charger, plus a few minutes for the physical swap) was already sourced correctly in v34 and needed no change — independently re-confirmed via search this round (DJI's spec sheet + charging guide both cite the same 1h36m figure).

Re-measured after the swap: genuine RTB completions now average **16.0 minutes** against a 46-minute target (about 35%), with wide variance (as little as a few minutes when the search ranges far, upward of 30+ minutes when it doesn't) — confirmed via a forced-local-orbit test that a drone kept near its own ground station gets much closer to the rated time, while normal free-ranging search pulls it noticeably short. This isn't a further bug to chase: it's the same real physics a return-to-base reserve always has — the farther a sortie ranges from its base, the more of its total endurance has to be held back for the trip home, exactly matching the user's own observation mid-investigation ("it would however depend on the rate of battery usage — a lot of flying around could mean less time"). Where the player deploys the drone team's ground station (closer to the likely search area vs. well to the rear) now has a real, visible effect on how much of the rated 46 minutes a typical sortie actually achieves.

**Verified:** a 14-trial full-battle stress run (10 drone-mode + 4 spotter-mode) confirmed no crashes and the fleet-accounting invariant from v34 held throughout with the new numbers; SPOTTER mode is completely unaffected by any of this. All measurement scripts were throwaway and removed afterward per the usual workflow.

### 2026-09-05 — v37: contour lines were crossing near the top-left of the map

Bug report: "In the top left of the map, there are crossing contour lines. This is mathematically impossible." Follow-up, before any fix was proposed: "You may need to rework how the geometry works in that case. Every point on the map should have an elevation, and gameplay should use it, including line of sight. It's not just a matter of drawing hills."

Checked the gameplay/geometry model FIRST, before touching anything, since that's the more serious possibility the follow-up raised: `elevation_m()` already computes a single continuous elevation at any point on the map as the sum of every hill's (warped) Gaussian contribution — there's no separate or partial version of it — and `has_direct_los()` already samples that exact same combined function at 20 points along every sightline. That part was already correct and needed no rework; it's what the original per-hill drawing bug never actually touched.

The real bug was isolated entirely to `_draw_hills()`: each hill drew its OWN contour rings from ONLY its own Gaussian formula (`_hill_radius_warp` scaled by that one hill's own height/radius), completely ignoring every other hill's contribution to the actual combined field `elevation_m()` computes. For any hill far enough from its neighbors this is indistinguishable from correct (the other hills' contributions there really are negligible) — but the hill at (350m, 550m), "ridge west of the village, rear," sits close enough to the village hill at (1300m, 1550m) — center-to-center distance ~1380m against a combined halo reach of ~1440m — that their independently-drawn rings visibly overlapped in exactly the top-left area reported, and wherever two hills' rings cross like that, it looks exactly like "impossible" crossing contours, because they don't actually represent the one true elevation field at that spot.

Fixed by tracing REAL contour lines of the combined field instead: a standard marching-squares algorithm over a grid sampled from the actual `elevation_m()` (40m spacing), computed once and cached (`_contour_segments_cache`) since the terrain is static and never needs retracing after the first draw. `_draw_hills` now just replays those cached line segments every frame, colored by level exactly as before, rather than drawing each hill's isolated ring.

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed the cache produces a substantial number of real traced segments; that a sample of segment midpoints actually have elevation (via the real `elevation_m()`) matching their claimed contour level, both generally and specifically within the village-hill/west-ridge overlap zone that produced the original bug report; and — the actual claim under test — that no two segments from DIFFERENT contour levels intersect anywhere on the map (checked pairwise within a spatial-bucket neighborhood, not a full unrestricted crossing scan, for tractability across ~1000 segments), confirming real contour lines never cross now that they're traced from the one true field instead of approximated per-hill.

### 2026-09-05 — v38: crash — a pending mortar shot could hold a reference to a drone that had already landed

Bug report: a Godot editor screenshot showing "Trying to assign invalid previously freed instance" at `_resolve_pending_mortar_shots`, `var target: Unit = shot.target`.

Root cause: mortar fire isn't resolved instantly — a shot is recorded in `_pending_mortar_shots` at launch (holding a direct reference to its target) and only resolved `MORTAR_FLIGHT_TIME` (40 tactical seconds) later. Every unit kind except one persists in `player_units`/`enemy_units` for the whole battle even once destroyed or withdrawn (a permanent husk, which is exactly why `_resolve_pending_mortar_shots` already checked `target.state == DESTROYED/WITHDRAWN` as a normal, expected case). `Kind.DRONE` is the one exception introduced in v33/v34: a drone that completes a routine return-to-base is actually `queue_free()`'d once it lands (see `_update_returning_drone`), not left behind as a husk. If an enemy mortar targets a drone and that drone lands normally before the 40-second flight time is up — an entirely routine, expected sequence, not a rare edge case — the pending shot's `target` reference goes stale, and the existing `DESTROYED`/`WITHDRAWN` state check never even runs: reading `.state` off an actually-freed Object, or in this case just assigning it to a typed `Unit` variable at all, is itself what throws "Trying to assign invalid previously freed instance."

Fixed with an `is_instance_valid()` check — but the first attempt still crashed on the identical line, because the check ran on the ALREADY-typed `var target: Unit = shot.target` assignment, and it's that exact assignment (not anything done with `target` afterward) that raises the error for a freed instance. Moving the validity check to run on the raw `shot.target` Variant, before it's ever assigned to a typed `Unit` variable, actually fixed it.

**Verified:** wrote a direct reproduction first — a pending shot targeting a drone, the drone force-landed (freed) exactly like a routine RTB does, then the shot resolved after its flight time — which reproduced the exact reported error message and stack location before the fix, and resolved with no error after moving the check before the typed assignment. A 15-trial full-battle stress run in drone mode (long enough to include real enemy-mortar-vs-drone engagements and routine landings happening concurrently) completed with no crashes or errors.

### 2026-09-05 — v39: the drone stops parking on mortars that are no longer worth watching

Request: "If an enemy is retreating and has either gotten out of mortar range or is a low priority target, the drone team should not bother tracking them. Look for more important targets instead."

`_drone_search_target()`'s top tier tracked any visible enemy mortar with `state != DESTROYED` — which included `RETREATING`, even though a retreating mortar has already had its crew abandon the gun for good (`Unit._apply_crew_casualties` — it can never fire again regardless of how well it's watched, for the rest of the battle). The same tier also had no concept of range at all: a live, active mortar sitting well outside `GameConfig.MORTAR_MAX_RANGE` of the friendly mortar got exactly the same priority as one sitting right next to it, even though the friendly mortar mechanically cannot act on the far one no matter how well the drone tracks it (`_pick_target`'s own range gate would reject it regardless of visibility). Both cases wasted the drone's search effort holding position over a target that could never actually translate into a shot — exactly what the request describes as "not worth bothering with." The fallback tier (last-known mortar-fire position) had the identical two gaps.

Fixed by tightening both tiers to `state == ACTIVE` (excludes RETREATING and DESTROYED alike) and adding a new `_in_friendly_mortar_range()` check against the live friendly mortar's current position. The fallback tier now evaluates every enemy mortar's own last-fire record individually rather than just taking whichever fired most recently — so an older but reachable fix correctly wins over a fresher one sitting outside range. With no ACTIVE friendly mortar at all (e.g. it's been destroyed), range stops being a meaningful concept to judge against, so `_in_friendly_mortar_range` deliberately doesn't exclude anything in that case — better to keep tracking mortars for general awareness than to give up on the mission entirely just because the gun is temporarily down. `_known_enemy_mortar_fire_position` (the old, range-blind fallback helper, added in v33) is now dead code with these two tiers folded directly into `_drone_search_target` and was removed.

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed a retreating (already-abandoned) mortar is no longer tracked, an out-of-range active mortar is no longer tracked, a live in-range active mortar is still top priority as before, an older-but-in-range fire position correctly beats a fresher out-of-range one from a different mortar, tracking isn't blocked by range when there's no active friendly mortar to judge it against, and a destroyed mortar is (still) never tracked. A 10-trial full-battle stress run in drone mode completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-06 — v40: casualties now count real people — a squad is 9

Request: "In the after action report, casualties should count people lost, for both sides. The mortar and drone crews are people too. For both sides, a squad is 9 people."

The mortar (`MORTAR_CREW_SIZE`, 4) and drone team (`DRONE_TEAM_CREW_SIZE`, 3) already tracked real headcounts and already fed into the side-wide casualty tally exactly like anyone else (v30, v33) — that part of the request was already true and needed no change, just confirmation. The gap was the SQUAD: `max_pips` was a flat 4 for both sides, an abstract "strength meter" number rather than a real headcount. Mechanically a squad already took casualties one real person at a time per hit (`take_hit`'s generic `pips = max(pips - 1, 0)` — this was never an abstract multi-person "burst" the way the mortar/drone crew model is) — the only thing wrong was the total it counted down from. Fixed with a new `Unit.SQUAD_SIZE = 9`, replacing the old `max_pips = 4` in the squad branch of `setup()` — applying identically to both teams since that branch is team-agnostic, so both sides' squads are now real 9-person squads with no other change needed.

This is a real, deliberate combat-pacing change, not just a cosmetic one: a squad now takes up to 9 hits to destroy outright instead of 4, and retreat thresholds (still fractions, so unaffected by the rescale) now trigger at proportionally more absolute casualties. Also updated the AAR report and the live dashboard to say "personnel" instead of "pips" in the user-facing text, now that the underlying numbers are literally real headcounts rather than an abstract game unit — internal field/variable names (`pips`, `max_pips`) were left alone as pure internal implementation detail, unrelated to what's actually asked for here.

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed both a player and an enemy squad are created with 9 max pips, that a 9-person squad survives exactly 8 hits and is destroyed by the 9th (not 3 or 4), that the mortar/drone team crews are unaffected and still tied to their own real crew-size constants, and that the AAR report text and the live dashboard both now say "personnel" with no remaining "pips" wording. A 6-trial full-battle stress run in each recon mode confirmed correct total headcounts (3 squads × 9 + mortar 4 + spotter 1 = 32, or + drone team 3 = 34) and that battles still resolve normally, in a comparable number of steps to before, with no crashes.

### 2026-09-06 — v41: the drone team carries spare batteries — swapping is fast, recharging is slow

Request: "The Mavic team should carry four spare Mavic batteries. Batteries are easily swappable. Used batteries should be placed on recharge. But this should greatly alleviate the problem of running out of charge."

Until now, "airframe" and "battery" were the same tracked resource: a landed drone sat in `_drone_recovering` for the full `DRONE_RECHARGE_DURATION` (~100 minutes) before it could fly again, exactly as if each of the 4 airframes had exactly one battery and no way to swap it. That's not how a real team like this actually operates — they carry spares specifically so a landed airframe doesn't have to sit out a full recharge cycle.

Split the model into two separately-tracked resources: `DRONE_FLEET_SIZE` (4) airframes and `DRONE_FLEET_SIZE + DRONE_SPARE_BATTERIES` (4+4=8) batteries. An airframe that lands now gets a fast swap (`DRONE_BATTERY_SWAP_DURATION`, 4 minutes — popping in a charged spare and a quick power-on/GPS check) if a charged spare is available, and its own spent battery goes onto the charger independently, in the background, for the full 100 minutes. Only when EVERY spare is already in use does a landed airframe have to sit and wait for a battery — a much rarer event with real spares in reserve than with one battery per airframe and no swapping. New states track this: `_drones_ready_for_launch` (unchanged meaning), `_drones_swapping` (mid battery-swap, a few minutes), `_drones_awaiting_battery` (grounded, genuinely out of batteries), `_charged_batteries_spare` (charged, not installed anywhere), `_batteries_recharging` (spent, on the charger) — a battery finishing its charge goes straight to a waiting airframe first, only overflowing into the spare pool once nothing's actually grounded for lack of one. A shot-down drone loses its currently-installed battery along with the airframe — neither rejoins any pool.

At H-hour, a well-prepared team starts with everything topped off: all 4 airframes AND all 4 spares fully charged (nothing's been flown yet, so nothing needs a recharge) — a real, meaningfully deeper initial reserve than the old model's "1 standby + 2 already recharging from the start."

**Verified:** headless tests covering the full lifecycle — correct initial state (all airframes ready, all spares charged), a routine landing consuming a spare and queuing the spent battery for recharge, the swap actually taking real time before the airframe becomes ready, an airframe correctly left waiting when no spare is available, a finished recharge correctly prioritizing a waiting airframe over the spare pool (and going to the pool when nobody's waiting), and a shot-down drone's battery correctly vanishing with it rather than being recovered. A 10-trial full-battle stress run held a strict combined airframe+battery accounting invariant throughout (every one of the 4 airframes and 8 batteries always exactly accounted for, adjusted only by the running shoot-down count) with no crashes. Directly measured the actual claim: across 20 full battles, the team never once ran an airframe out of batteries — the spare pool never dropped below 3 of its starting 4 — confirming this really does "greatly alleviate the problem of running out of charge," not just in theory.

### 2026-09-06 — v42: battery STATE, not just a recharge countdown

Follow-up to v41: "The game should still be tracking battery state, both while the drone is on the ground and while it is in the air. If no fully charged battery is available, the team might launch a drone with a partly charged battery. Also, if the drone lands with partial charge still in the battery, that should impact recharge time. But the game wouldn't track recharge time directly. It would track charge level, with recharge time being a consequence." Confirmed mid-implementation: "This also includes batteries that are not currently attached to a drone."

v41 modeled batteries as a binary: fully charged (in `_charged_batteries_spare`) or on a fixed `DRONE_RECHARGE_DURATION` countdown (`_batteries_recharging`). That's not what was asked for — every battery, everywhere (airborne, grounded and ready, mid-swap, or sitting uninstalled), now has a real `charge` level from 0.0 to 1.0, and flight endurance / recharge time are both *derived* from that level rather than being separately tracked countdowns:

- `Unit.drone_flight_time`/`drone_distance_flown` (two derived-from-elapsed-time fields) are replaced by a single `Unit.drone_battery_charge`, depleting at a fixed rate calibrated so a full charge lasts `GameConfig.DRONE_FULL_CHARGE_FLIGHT_TIME` (the range-derived ~35.7 minutes established in v36) of continuous flight. The RTB trigger is now "is there enough charge left to cover the trip home plus a safety margin," expressed entirely in charge terms.
- On the ground, `_battery_pool: Array[float]` replaces the old spare-count/recharging-array split — every uninstalled battery in this one array just charges continuously (`charge = min(1.0, charge + delta/DRONE_RECHARGE_DURATION)`), whatever its current level. A battery that lands with a third of its charge still in it reaches full in a third of the normal recharge time, automatically, with no special-casing needed — recharge time falls straight out of the charge deficit rather than being counted down on its own.
- `_drones_ready: Array[float]` and `_drones_swapping: Array[Dictionary]` (`{time_left, charge}`) replace the old plain counts — a grounded, ready-to-launch airframe's installed battery has its OWN real charge level, not an assumed 100%.
- Every time an airframe needs a battery — the very first H-hour launch, a routine post-landing swap, or a relaunch after a shoot-down — it's handed the single highest-charge battery actually available (`_pop_best_battery`), which is "might launch a drone with a partly charged battery" exactly: the team doesn't wait around for a full charge, it takes the best it's got.
- The old `_drones_awaiting_battery` state is gone — it turns out to be mathematically impossible with an 8-battery/4-airframe ratio (at most 4 batteries can ever be installed in existing airframes at once, so at least `DRONE_SPARE_BATTERIES` always remain in the pool) — but "always something available" doesn't mean "always full," which is the actual nuance this request was about.

The dashboard's drone row now shows live charge percentages (the airborne drone's current charge, the best charge sitting ready, the best charge in the spare pool) instead of plain counts, so a depleted fleet is visible at a glance rather than looking identical to a fully-topped-off one.

**Verified:** headless tests confirmed charge depletes at the expected rate while flying, a launch or swap always grabs the single best-charged battery available (not just "any" or "always full"), a just-landed battery's remaining charge is correctly folded into the pool rather than discarded, a battery starting partway charged reaches full proportionally faster than an empty one would (directly exercising "recharge time is a consequence"), a shot-down drone's battery vanishes with it rather than rejoining any pool, and that the pool is provably never empty by construction. A combined airframe+battery accounting invariant — including every tracked charge value staying within [0,1] — held across 8 full battles with no crashes. The live dashboard was confirmed to actually reflect real-time charge changes, not just static counts.

### 2026-09-06 — v43: enemy mortars become the drone team's top priority; a backup drone covers the handoff, or the primary sacrifices itself

Request: "While it is possible to fire at them, enemy mortars should be top priority for the drone team. If a mortar fires, this reveals its approximate position. If possible, a drone should go straight there, even if it is tracking a lower-priority target such as an enemy squad. Also, if a drone is over a functioning enemy mortar, a replacement drone should be sent to ensure continuous coverage. If this is not possible, the drone might sacrifice itself by remaining in place until the battery expires. This would only be done if said sacrifice enables friendly forces to fire at the enemy mortar (corrected mid-request from "the friendly mortar," specifically to stay accurate once other ways to engage a mortar exist later). If it is not possible to fire at the enemy mortar, this prioritization should change. It is important that this be based on whether or not it is possible to fire at the enemy mortar, not directly on the presence or absence of the friendly mortar. This is because we might later add other ways to shoot at the enemy mortar."

The "go straight there, revealed by firing, top priority over a squad" half of this was already in place from v39 (`_drone_search_target`'s two tiers: a currently-visible active mortar, then its last-detected-fire position) and needed no change. New work was the other half: continuous coverage via a backup drone, and self-sacrifice when a backup isn't possible.

Per the explicit forward-compatibility requirement, the "can we act on this" question was split into two: `_can_engage_position(pos)` (strict — true only if a real ACTIVE friendly mortar is actually in range of `pos`; false with no way to engage at all) is now the one thing the backup/sacrifice decision keys off, while the existing, more lenient `_in_friendly_mortar_range` (used only for search-target selection, where "no reference point" should mean "keep tracking anyway") now delegates to it. When a second way to hit a mortar shows up later, only `_can_engage_position` needs to grow a new clause — the sacrifice logic itself never has to change.

`backup_drone` and a generalized `returning_drones: Array[Unit]` (was a single nullable var, now genuinely can hold both the outgoing primary and an old backup at once) round out the drone-slot model alongside the existing `active_drone`. `_drone_time_until_rtb(d)` is a new time-domain reframing of the existing RTB charge check (charge-to-spare converted back into seconds via the full-charge flight time) — needed because the backup has to be timed, not just triggered by a boolean.

Mid-implementation correction: the first pass launched a backup the instant the active drone started tracking a functioning, engageable mortar, regardless of how much charge the active drone had left — potentially leaving a backup sitting idle for most of its own limited endurance. The user caught this before it was reported as finished: "Sorry, I misdescribed above. If a drone is over a functioning enemy mortar and is about to retreat due to low charge, a backup would be sent, timed to arrive just as the first drone needs to retreat." Fixed by comparing `_drone_time_until_rtb(active_drone)` against `backup_travel_time` (straight-line distance from the ground station to the watched mortar, at cruise speed) — a backup only launches once the primary's remaining time in the air has shrunk to roughly what a fresh launch needs to cover the same ground, so the relief actually arrives close to when the primary must leave rather than idling early.

Self-sacrifice (`_update_active_drone`'s RTB branch, when `backup_drone == null`): if the primary is due home over a watched, engageable mortar, it holds station instead of turning for base. If its charge is still positive, it just keeps orbiting on the dwindling remainder; only once genuinely at zero charge does it actually go down (`_crash_drone`, a distinct combat-log line from a shoot-down) — spending every last second of a battery that would otherwise be wasted flying home unproductively. If the mortar stops being visible or engageable partway through (it relocates, the friendly mortar is lost, etc.), the sacrifice is abandoned and the drone just RTBs normally on whatever charge remains, exactly as the "based on whether it's possible to fire, not on whether the mortar itself exists" requirement demands.

**Verified:** a 14-test headless suite covering the full matrix — backup launches once (and only once) the timed threshold is crossed, not merely because a mortar is being watched; no backup without a ready spare airframe; a backup correctly follows the watched mortar's current position; a shot-down primary is instantly replaced by a standing backup; a backup itself is sent home normally on low charge and never sacrifices in the primary's place; sacrifice triggers only when genuinely engageable and is abandoned mid-hold if that stops being true; and the existing airframe/battery accounting invariant from v41/v42 holds throughout a 10-trial full-battle run with backups active.

A full-battle stress run (60 real trials, no synthetic charge overrides) surfaced a genuine, worth-recording finding rather than a bug: real backup launches never actually occurred in any of the 60 trials. Root cause, isolated by direct instrumentation of `_visible_engageable_mortar()` (the exact predicate the backup/sacrifice trigger uses) across 30 further trials: it returned null on every single tick throughout every trial, even though the enemy mortar's raw `is_visible` flag DOES go true in most battles (8 of 10 in a separate check) — the gap is that those sightings mostly land on a mortar that's already `RETREATING` (crew abandoning after taking a hit) rather than still `ACTIVE`, or land while the active drone's own first battery is being spent during the pre-contact fast-forward search phase, well before the enemy mortar has fired even once. Combined with real contact typically not lasting anywhere near a full ~36-minute battery once it does start, the specific coincidence this mechanic depends on — a still-functioning, currently-spotted mortar, watched by a drone that's genuinely running low on charge — essentially never arises in this scenario's current pacing. The mechanic itself is confirmed correct by the unit suite; whether to also tune battle length, mortar exposure, or battery life so it actually surfaces in play is a balance question left open rather than decided unilaterally here.

### 2026-09-06 — v44: a mortar crew 300m below a drone would usually never notice it

Request: "A mortar crew would usually not notice a small drone 300 meters above them. Investigate real-world info about this and give them a chance-per-minute to notice. But I'm guessing the chance should be very low, such that even if the drone is there for several minutes, they would still be highly unlikely to notice."

Until now, a ground unit "spotting" an airborne drone ran through the exact same generic `roll_spot` formula as spotting anyone on the ground — `SPOT_CHANCE_PER_SECOND` times `DRONE_SPOT_CHANCE_MULTIPLIER` (0.15 × 0.15 ≈ 2.25%/second before distance falloff), further scaled by `MOVING_SPOT_MULTIPLIER` whenever the drone's `activity` happened to read `MOVING` (which is most of the time, since `_update_active_drone` reissues a move order essentially every tick even while effectively loitering in place) and gated by the same 900m ground detection-range formula used for spotting a person. None of that actually models the real question — a distracted mortar crew occasionally glancing at the sky, not a continuous, terrain-and-movement-aware search.

Researched real numbers before picking any: a dedicated visual-detection study (Ahn et al., *Distance and Visual Angle of Line-of-Sight of a Small Drone*, Applied Sciences 2020) found only a 50% chance of visually acquiring a Mavic-class airframe at ~307m — and that's for an observer ACTIVELY SCANNING for it under good conditions. The same study found unaided HEARING did markedly worse: most participants couldn't hear the drone even once they could already see it. Dedicated acoustic-sensor systems (microphone arrays, not bare ears) report usable ranges from roughly 35m (high-confidence) up to 300-600m in ideal, low-wind conditions — real hardware built specifically for this job, not a distracted person's ears mid-firefight.

A mortar crew isn't scanning the sky at all — they're loading, laying, and watching the ground for threats, so what actually needs modeling is an occasional, distracted glance happening to catch something, not a deliberate search. Added `CombatResolver._roll_ground_notices_drone`, a self-contained replacement for the ground-observer/drone-target case that bypasses the generic formula entirely (no concealment table, no `MOVING_SPOT_MULTIPLIER`, no elevation bonus — none of it describes "is anyone glancing at the right patch of sky right now"): only a building overhead still blocks it outright (`has_aerial_los` — hills don't, since nothing on this map is tall enough to occlude a drone flying above every hill), real slant range is computed from actual horizontal distance plus `DRONE_ALTITUDE_M` (both in real meters, not the map's scaled position units), and beyond a hard `DRONE_GROUND_NOTICE_MAX_RANGE_M` (400m, just past the visual study's own effective range) it's simply impossible regardless of time spent. The actual roll is a genuine chance PER MINUTE (`DRONE_GROUND_NOTICE_CHANCE_PER_MINUTE = 0.01`, converted to a per-tick probability via proper compounding — `1-(1-p)^(delta/60)`, not a naive linear scale, so it stays correct at any tick size including the accelerated fast-forward tactical clock) — roughly two orders of magnitude below the study's best-case per-look figure, reflecting that a working crew's attention is elsewhere almost all of the time. At 1%/minute, a crew watched for 5 minutes still has ~95% odds of never noticing, and even 10 minutes only reaches ~90% odds of remaining unnoticed — "highly unlikely even after several minutes," exactly as asked, while a very long loiter (a full ~36-minute battery) does start to carry real risk (~30% cumulative), which feels right rather than making the mechanic totally inert.

The drone's OWN view of the ground (the reverse direction — spotting an enemy mortar or squad) was untouched: that's a genuinely different sensor situation (a camera looking down at what's under it) and keeps using `DRONE_DETECTION_RANGE`/`DRONE_CONCEALMENT_MULTIPLIER` exactly as before.

**Verified:** a statistical smoke test confirmed the empirical notice rate over 20,000 independent one-minute rolls landed within 0.0001 of the target 1%, that accumulating the same one-minute exposure in 600 separate 0.1s ticks (as a real battle actually does) produces the same rate rather than compounding incorrectly, that a mortar placed thousands of meters away never notices no matter how long it's given, that one placed comfortably inside `DRONE_GROUND_NOTICE_MAX_RANGE_M` eventually does given enough tries, and that the drone-observes-ground direction is completely unaffected.
