A 20-trial full-battle stress run confirmed no crashes and no change in overall drone-loss patterns — a follow-up check found that in this scenario, drones were already never being shot down individually (0 genuine "shot down" log entries across a further 15-trial sample, both before and after this change) precisely because a ground unit noticing an overhead drone was already a rare enough event that it essentially never happened to align with combat in these particular short test battles; every drone loss traced instead to the whole drone-team ground station itself going down (destroying whatever was airborne along with it) rather than to an individual air-to-air shoot-down. This change makes that outcome correct for a documented, sourced reason instead of an accidental one, and it's now easy to verify going forward: a real shoot-down should stay a rare, notable event, not a routine one, in any future full-battle testing.

### 2026-09-06 — v45: the friendly mortar hunts a known enemy mortar instead of only waiting in range

Request: "If the enemy mortar's location is known, or even the approximately location, it becomes a game of cat and mouse for the friendly mortar. It could be worth it to relocate some distance to get a shot, especially if there is drone coverage over the enemy mortar." Refined over three follow-ups: the same logic should apply if there's merely an *anticipation* of upcoming drone coverage, not just live coverage already in hand; and confirmed explicitly that risk is the actual tradeoff being modeled — "the mortar still does not want to be spotted."

Until now this only existed in one direction: `_update_enemy_mortar_positioning` already has the enemy actively closing in on the friendly mortar's known position (added in an earlier round, "counter-battery is now positional"), but `_pick_target` only ever considers a target already within `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE` — the friendly mortar itself never relocated *toward* a known-but-out-of-range enemy mortar, only away from threats or into cover.

Built the mirror, `_update_friendly_mortar_hunting`, but deliberately not identical to the enemy's simpler unconditional version, per the two refinements above:

- **Trust tiers**, via new `_known_enemy_mortar_lead()`: a currently live-visible enemy mortar is trusted outright; short of that, a bare fire-detection lead (the same muzzle-flash mechanic used everywhere else) is trusted too if a friendly drone's OWN current move order already points at that same position — `_drone_search_target`'s own fire-lead pursuit already sends it there whether or not it's arrived — meaning coverage is imminent even though it hasn't actually landed yet, exactly the "anticipation" case from the second follow-up. A trusted lead is worth chasing at any distance, on the same generous `DRONE_MORTAR_FIRE_LEAD_EXPIRY` window (10 tactical minutes) the drone's own pursuit already uses. An untrusted, uncovered lead — nobody actually watching it, live or inbound — gets the much shorter `MORTAR_FIRE_DETECTION_EXPIRY` (3 minutes, the same quick window the enemy's own reaction to the friendly mortar uses) and is only worth a modest `MORTAR_HUNT_UNTRUSTED_MAX_RELOCATE` (1000m) repositioning, not a long march on a guess.
- **Concealment-aware destination**, via new `_friendly_mortar_hunt_point()`, answering the "still doesn't want to be spotted" requirement directly: unlike `_mortar_advance_point`'s plain building-avoidance (fine for the enemy, which has no reason to care whether ITS mortar is seen closing in), this checks each candidate angle against every currently-known enemy position and prefers one that isn't visible from any of them, falling back to the plain building-clear candidate only if none qualify. The mortar being hunted is deliberately excluded from that same threat check — every candidate sits close to it by construction, so counting it as a "threat to hide from" would reject every angle equally for no real gain; indirect fire doesn't need mutual line of sight with the one target already committed to, so the actual risk this guards against is everyone ELSE along the route, not the target itself.

**Verified:** an 8-test headless suite confirmed no hunt is triggered with nothing known or when already in range, a live-visible out-of-range mortar triggers a hunt that actually closes distance, an untrusted lead far enough away to exceed the relocation cap is correctly declined while a nearby one is accepted, a stale untrusted lead past its shorter expiry is dropped, a drone already flying toward a lead makes it trusted even when it's both far away and past the untrusted expiry, and the hunted mortar itself is correctly excluded from its own destination's exposure check (confirming a valid, in-range advance point is still found with no other threats around). A 15-trial full-battle stress run (mixed drone/spotter recon) had no crashes, but recorded zero actual hunts triggering — traced to this specific map's starting geometry: both enemy mortar positions sit only 28-79m beyond `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE` of the doctrine's default friendly mortar position, marginally close enough that the enemy's own pre-existing, uncapped counter-battery advance (which reacts the moment the friendly mortar fires at all, even at a squad) generally closes the gap for both sides before the friendly mortar's own, more cautious logic ever gets to be the deciding factor — since range is a symmetric distance check, whichever side closes the gap first brings both into range at once. The mechanic itself is confirmed correct in isolation by the unit suite; whether the two sides' starting distances should be tuned farther apart so this becomes a more visible, regular feature of actual play is a balance question left open rather than decided here.

### 2026-09-06 — v46: the drone's opening sortie was over in seconds, and only ever searched a narrow band

Bug report: "Drone scouting at the start looked terrible. It went out and flew directly back. Elapsed time was too short for it to be low on battery. Also it was scouting the middle only. It should be trying to find enemies, especially mortars."

Two separate, real bugs, both traced directly rather than guessed at:

**The battery drain was real, but the CLOCK it was measured against wasn't.** `_update_active_drone` depletes charge (and moves the drone) using `scenario_delta` — the TACTICAL clock, not real time — same as literally every other moving/reloading thing in the game. That's normally correct: `_current_time_scale()` runs the tactical clock at up to `TIME_SCALE_FAST_FORWARD` (300x real time) whenever nobody's in contact yet, specifically so the player doesn't have to sit through the ~42-tactical-minute pre-contact road march in real time. But a drone's own flight endurance is a genuinely real-world-time-bound thing — a real Mavic 3's ~36-minute battery doesn't stretch or compress just because the infantry haven't made contact yet — and the very first sortie of a battle launches (during setup, before any contact is even possible) squarely inside that 300x fast-forward window. `DRONE_FULL_CHARGE_FLIGHT_TIME` is ~2143 tactical seconds; at 300x that's `2143/300 ≈ 7` REAL seconds for the whole sortie, nowhere near enough real time for even one `roll_spot` chance (spotting runs on real `delta`, not `scenario_delta`) to land before it was already past the RTB threshold. From the player's chair this looked exactly as reported: launches, dashes out (and, since movement is scenario_delta-scaled too, covers ground at an apparent 300x speed on screen), and is immediately committed to flying straight back — never given a real chance to find anything.

Fixed with a new `_drone_actively_searching()` check (true whenever `recon_mode == DRONE_TEAM` and a drone — active or backup — is currently up) folded into `_current_time_scale()` alongside live contact: an airborne, still-searching drone now holds the tactical clock at `TIME_SCALE_NORMAL` (60x) instead of letting it run away to fast-forward, the same tier live contact already gets, on the reasoning that "a drone is actively hunting for contact" is exactly as worth watching in real time as contact itself already is. At 60x, a full charge lasts `2143/60 ≈ 36` real seconds instead of ~7 — enough real time for spotting rolls to actually have a chance, and a pace consistent with the game's own documented worst-case assumption (`BATTLE_TIME_LIMIT / TIME_SCALE_NORMAL` = 240 real seconds, already treated as an acceptable upper bound). SPOTTER mode has no drone concept at all and is completely unaffected — `_drone_actively_searching()` is unconditionally false there.

**The search pattern itself never went looking for a mortar, only for the road.** `_drone_sweep_target()`'s fallback (used whenever there's no visible mortar or fresh fire-detection lead to chase) patrolled `GameConfig.ROAD_WAYPOINTS_M` — the SAME waypoint list the enemy squads march down — which exists to draw and route the road, not to define a search area, and sits inside a ~220m-tall band vertically centered on the map (y≈1680-1900m out of the map's full 3500m height). Real mortars deploy well back from the line, not draped over the march route (the same reasoning `_pick_target` already uses to treat mortars and squads so differently) — this map's own two fixed mortar positions sit 450-750m off that band. A sweep pattern that structurally never left a ~220m-tall strip could never pass near either one no matter how much flight time it was given — this was a second, independent bug, not just a symptom of the timing one, though the two compounded (the old ~7-second sortie couldn't have covered much of any pattern regardless).

Added `GameConfig.DRONE_SEARCH_WAYPOINTS_M`/`drone_search_waypoints_px()`, a genuine boustrophedon (5 horizontal legs, alternating direction) across the SAME contested x-range as the road but the map's FULL height instead of the road's own narrow band — still no more prior knowledge of the enemy's actual fixed emplacements than the player has (evenly-spaced coverage, not their exact coordinates), just methodical enough that `DRONE_DETECTION_RANGE`'s own 1600m reach gets a real chance at whatever's out there instead of depending on the road happening to pass nearby by luck. `_drone_sweep_target()` now reads from this new list instead of the road's.

**Verified:** a 5-test headless suite confirmed an airborne, contact-free drone holds the clock at `TIME_SCALE_NORMAL` (dropping back to fast-forward once nothing's up), that SPOTTER mode is untouched, that 10 real seconds of simulated play now uses a sensible partial fraction of a charge (~28%, matching the ~36-second full-sortie math) rather than nearly all of it, that the new search pattern spans well over 2000m of the map's height (vs. the road's ~220m), and that every one of the map's actual fixed mortar positions now falls within `DRONE_DETECTION_RANGE` of some point on the new pattern. A 10-trial full-battle stress run confirmed no crashes and, directly measuring the original complaint, that the first sortie now consistently lasts ~29-32 real seconds before RTB (vs. the ~7 seconds the old bug produced) — a real, watchable reconnaissance mission instead of an instant round trip.

### 2026-09-06 — v47: drone search target priority, generalized — mortars aren't hard-coded, they're just currently the highest scorer

Follow-up bug report, filed while the v46 fix above was still fresh: "Even after both mortars were destroyed, the drones were still hunting for mortars." Immediately reframed by the user into the real, structural ask before any fix was proposed: "I think this is symptomatic of a different problem. Rather than coding to mortars specifically, things should be based on target priority... Currently, mortars are the high priority targets. But that might change later." Two further specifics: a squad's own priority should vary by "the more dangerous it is to friendly forces," always far below a mortar's; and "early in the battle, it is reasonable to assume more mortars might be arriving," with confidence there are none left growing only as time passes without any detected mortar fire.

Traced the reported symptom first: `_visible_engageable_mortar()` and the fire-detection-lead tier already both filter to `state == ACTIVE`, so a destroyed/retreated mortar was never literally being targeted again — what the player was actually seeing was `_drone_search_target()`'s tier-3 fallback (`_drone_sweep_target`, the v46 area sweep) continuing to run its wide, methodical, mortar-shaped coverage pattern completely unconditionally, forever, regardless of whether any mortar could possibly still exist. The doc comment even said so outright: "Deliberately does NOT redirect to a merely-visible enemy SQUAD once no mortar lead exists — the mission is hunting mortars, not escorting infantry." That was true by construction, not by any actual ongoing judgment about whether mortar-hunting was still the right thing to be spending effort on — exactly the hard-coding the user then named as the real problem.

Replaced the old three-tier if/else cascade with a genuine priority comparison (`GameConfig.TARGET_PRIORITY_*`): every tick, score each real candidate and fly toward whichever currently scores highest, so a future third target kind only needs its own scoring term added, never a rewrite of the decision itself.

- A live-visible, engageable mortar (`TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR = 100`) or a fresh in-range fire-detection lead (discounted to 80% of that, for being a stale position estimate rather than a live one) — unconditional on anything else, since both are real, specific evidence.
- The single most dangerous currently-visible ACTIVE enemy squad (`_squad_danger_priority`) — scored by proximity to the nearest active friendly unit of any kind, ramping smoothly from 0 at `SQUAD_DANGER_RANGE` (1200m) up to `TARGET_PRIORITY_SQUAD_MAX = 10` right at contact — genuinely variable by position as asked, and capped an order of magnitude below any mortar so "a dangerous enemy squad is still much lower priority than an enemy mortar" holds everywhere, not just when a mortar happens to also be around.
- The ongoing area sweep, valued honestly as an expected value rather than a free pass: `TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR * _mortar_existence_confidence()` — what finding an undiscovered mortar would be worth, times the odds one is actually still out there. `_mortar_existence_confidence()` returns a hard, certain `0.0` once every enemy mortar (checked by kind, the same omniscient state-read every other targeting decision in this file already relies on) is confirmed out of action — the exact fix for the reported bug — and otherwise decays smoothly from ~1.0 (assume more might still be arriving, per the second follow-up) toward a small floor (`MORTAR_CONFIDENCE_FLOOR = 0.05`, never quite zero while a live one might still exist) on a 15-tactical-minute time constant measured from the most recent actually-DETECTED mortar shot anywhere (`_last_detected_mortar_fire`, which already covers a mortar that's never even been visually spotted) — or from battle start if nothing's fired yet.

Once every mortar is confirmed dead (or, short of certainty, once confidence has simply decayed far enough with no shots fired), the sweep's expected value drops below what a genuinely dangerous, currently-visible squad now scores, and the drone naturally redirects its attention there instead — the exact "increasingly confident there are no mortars to look for" behavior asked for, without a single `if all mortars destroyed` check anywhere in the new code.

**Verified:** an 8-test headless suite confirmed confidence starts near 1.0 at battle start, decays to under 0.2 after three time constants with no fire, drops to exactly 0.0 the instant every enemy mortar's state is set to something other than ACTIVE, and jumps back up near 1.0 the instant a fresh detected shot is recorded; that a live mortar always outranks even a squad sitting right on top of a friendly unit; that with every mortar confirmed dead the drone correctly redirects to the most dangerous visible squad instead of the old unconditional sweep; that it still falls back to the area sweep when nothing else is visible and confidence is high; and that a dangerous visible squad correctly outweighs the sweep once confidence has decayed near the floor even with a mortar technically still (but silently) ACTIVE somewhere. A 10-trial full-battle stress run asserted the drone's live move target stays finite on every single tick of every trial — the concrete, mechanical guarantee that it can never end up fixated on a dead mortar's stale position — and completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-06 — v48: a retreating enemy is a kill in progress, not something to keep sweeping past

Direct follow-up to v47: "When the enemy is retreating, there is little need to search for new advancing enemies. At that point, destroying retreating enemies would be the priority."

Two real gaps, both in `_drone_search_target`'s v47 priority comparison: (1) `_squad_danger_priority` (and the whole "which visible squad is worth tracking" loop) only ever considered `state == ACTIVE` squads — a RETREATING one (still a completely legal target; `is_targetable()` only excludes DESTROYED/WITHDRAWN) was invisible to the priority system entirely, worth exactly nothing next to the sweep. (2) The sweep's own expected-value framing (`TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR * _mortar_existence_confidence()`) had no concept of the enemy's overall posture at all — a general retreat changes the calculus immediately and specifically ("little need to search for NEW advancing enemies"), not just gradually through the same slow confidence decay built for "no mortar fire in a while."

Added a fourth priority tier: the nearest currently-visible RETREATING enemy (squad or mortar crew — anything actually broken and running), tie-broken by proximity to a friendly unit when more than one qualifies, at a new flat `GameConfig.TARGET_PRIORITY_RETREATING_ENEMY = 25` — well above any advancing squad's own max (10), reflecting "destroying retreating enemies would be the priority," but still far below a live mortar's 100, since an actual functioning mortar remains the standing top concern regardless of what else is running. Separately, once `enemy_general_retreat_ordered` is true, the sweep's expected value is directly discounted (`SWEEP_DISCOUNT_DURING_ENEMY_RETREAT = 0.2`) on top of whatever the ordinary confidence decay has already done — deliberately immediate rather than waiting on that same decay curve, since a commander who's just called off the attack has little reason left to keep searching broadly for new arrivals right now, this tick, not eventually. This discount only touches the SPECULATIVE "nothing detected yet, but maybe" component of the sweep — an actual mortar still covering the withdrawal (`_check_enemy_commander_retreat`'s own "mortars continue the fire mission") shows up live or via a fresh lead and wins outright regardless, via the unconditional top two tiers.

The "little need to search for new advancing enemies" half of the request is also satisfied for free by v47's own existing mechanics: the moment the enemy commander orders a general retreat, every currently-ACTIVE enemy squad is pulled into RETREATING in that same instant (already true before this round), so the "most dangerous ACTIVE squad" candidate pool empties out on its own — there's nothing left "advancing" to search for by then regardless of any of today's changes.

**Verified:** a 6-test headless suite confirmed the deliberate baseline (a fresh battle with high confidence and no general retreat still favors the sweep over one lone retreating squad — mortars remain the standing concern until there's real reason to think otherwise), that a retreating enemy outranks both an advancing squad and the discounted sweep once general retreat is ordered, that a live mortar still wins over a retreating enemy even during a general retreat, that the general-retreat discount takes effect immediately rather than waiting on confidence to also decay, that a general retreat correctly empties the advancing-squad candidate pool, and that the nearest of several visible retreating enemies is chosen rather than just the first one found. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes and no finite-position violations — though it's worth noting honestly that `enemy_general_retreat_ordered` never actually triggered in that particular 10-trial sample (a separate, pre-existing mechanic — the enemy commander's own threshold for calling off the attack — rather than anything touched this round), so the general-retreat-specific discount's real-play frequency wasn't directly exercised here; the per-unit retreating-target tier itself doesn't depend on that flag at all and is expected to activate on any individual squad's own more common per-unit retreat.

### 2026-09-06 — v49: a retreating enemy is only a priority once there's nothing better left to fight

Immediate refinement of v48: "If the battle is continuing, a retreating enemy is a low priority target. This includes a mortar team that has abandoned its mortar."

v48's `TARGET_PRIORITY_RETREATING_ENEMY = 25` applied unconditionally to any visible retreating enemy — high enough to beat a moderately-decayed sweep even while the battle was still genuinely contested elsewhere (another enemy mortar could easily still be actively fighting without the enemy commander having ordered a general retreat yet). That's exactly backwards from what a real drone crew should do: chasing one already-broken, harmless straggler while a live threat might still be out there is a distraction, not a priority — and the case named explicitly makes this concrete. A mortar CREW that's abandoned its gun (`state == RETREATING`) can never fire it again (the same fact `_pick_target`'s own priority ordering and `_visible_engageable_mortar`'s `ACTIVE`-only filter already rely on) — it is no longer "a mortar" for any tactical purpose, just a retreating enemy like any other, and treating it as still somehow mortar-adjacent-important while real fighting continues elsewhere would be a real mistake.

Split the single flat constant into two: `TARGET_PRIORITY_RETREATING_ENEMY` (25, unchanged) still applies once `enemy_general_retreat_ordered` is true — the fight is effectively over, exactly v48's original scenario, so finishing off a retreating enemy remains the priority. Otherwise — the battle is still genuinely continuing — a new `TARGET_PRIORITY_RETREATING_ENEMY_LOW = 2.0` applies instead: low enough to lose to the sweep in essentially any normal circumstance and to any genuinely dangerous advancing squad, so the drone stays on the actual fight rather than babysitting a straggler, while still beating an outright empty sweep (every mortar confirmed destroyed AND nothing else visible at all) — watching *something* still beats watching nothing. Both flat rather than distance-scaled, same as before: the question is "is this worth finishing off right now," not "how close is it to a fight."

**Verified:** a 4-test headless suite confirmed a retreating enemy is correctly deprioritized behind the sweep while the battle continues, that a genuinely dangerous advancing squad beats a low-priority retreating one, that the exact named scenario (a RETREATING mortar crew, confirmed via `state`, with another squad still actively fighting) is handled correctly — the still-fighting squad wins, not the abandoned mortar crew — and that v48's original high-priority behavior is preserved once `enemy_general_retreat_ordered` is actually true. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes and no finite-position violations.

### 2026-09-06 — v50: retreating squads were bunching up together

Bug report from actual play: "I tried level 0 again. Retreating enemy squads bunched up. The[y] should not."

Root cause, found by comparing `order_retreat()` against an already-fixed instance of the identical problem elsewhere in the same file: `_alert_enemy_squads()` (which breaks every marching squad off toward cover in one pass, the exact "several units all pick cover at once" scenario) already carries a `claimed` accumulator specifically because, per its own doc comment, that's "the scenario most likely to pile several squads into the same nearest patch of cover" — each squad passes `_ally_positions_for(u) + claimed` into `Unit.seek_cover()`'s own `avoid_positions` parameter, and `GameConfig.nearest_cover_point` steers away from anything in that list. `Unit.order_retreat()` calls the exact same `nearest_cover_point`, but hard-coded its own `avoid_positions` argument to an empty array — meaning a general retreat order (`order_general_retreat`/`_check_enemy_commander_retreat`, both of which retreat every currently-active unit on a side in one pass, precisely the same "all at once" scenario) never had any way to keep successive units from independently computing the same "nearest cover point" from similar starting positions and landing in the same spot. Squads starting close together (the doctrine's own "loose spread... near the road," or several already bunched from taking fire together) reliably converged.

Fixed by threading a real `avoid_positions` parameter all the way through: `order_retreat(known_enemy_positions, avoid_positions)` now passes it straight to `nearest_cover_point`, exactly mirroring `seek_cover`'s existing pattern. Both general-retreat call sites (`order_general_retreat`, `_check_enemy_commander_retreat`) now carry their own `claimed` accumulator, appending each unit's chosen `move_target` (or its current `global_position`, if it turned out to already be in cover and isn't moving at all) as it's processed, combined with `_ally_positions_for(unit)` for whichever other units haven't been ordered yet this same pass — identical in spirit to `_alert_enemy_squads`'s own accumulator. For completeness, the same fix was threaded through the OTHER, rarer path that can also retreat more than one unit in the same tick: `Unit.take_hit`'s `ally_positions` parameter (already computed by the caller for its own `seek_cover` calls) now also flows through `_check_retreat`/`_apply_crew_casualties` into `order_retreat`, in case a single mortar shot's bunching-spillover mechanic catches two squads with the same hit.

**Verified:** a 3-test headless suite confirmed three squads clustered 30m apart, put through a single general retreat order, land on three genuinely distinct cover points (both for the player's own `order_general_retreat` and for a hand-rolled mirror of the enemy commander's version, the latter checked across all 6 enemy squads, not just 3); and that a single unit retreating alone (no allies to avoid) still works correctly with an empty `avoid_positions` list. A 10-trial full-battle stress run across both recon modes had no crashes.

### 2026-09-06 — v52: the mortar and drone were each independently deciding whether the hunt was still on

Bug report from actual play: "Saw some strange behavior by the mortar/drone combination on that run. The mortar was moving towards an enemy mortar position to get a shot. But the drone was not remaining in the area." Followed by the actual design direction, not just a symptom description: "The mortar and drone teams should decide what they are doing, and they should work together towards that goal."

Root cause: `_update_friendly_mortar_hunting` and `_drone_search_target` each independently re-derived "is this lead still worth pursuing" from the same raw signal (`_known_enemy_mortar_lead`) every single tick, using DIFFERENT math — the mortar's own moving-range check on one side, the drone's fire-lead expiry and (critically) `_in_friendly_mortar_range` gating on the other. Two consequences, both real: (1) `_known_enemy_mortar_lead`'s own "trusted" flag depends on the drone's CURRENT move_target still literally pointing at the lead position — the instant the drone's own, entirely separate target-priority scoring sent it somewhere else even briefly, trust silently downgraded from the generous 10-minute window to the much shorter 3-minute one, with nothing forcing the mortar to notice or care. (2) Independent of that, `_visible_engageable_mortar` and the fire-lead tier BOTH gate on `_in_friendly_mortar_range` — meaning even a mortar the drone could see live, right now, scored ZERO for search-target purposes for as long as the friendly mortar hadn't yet closed the distance, since "in range" was false by construction during the entire hunt. The drone had no tier at all that said "stay here, our mortar is walking toward this." Two systems that only ever coincidentally agreed, not two that were actually coordinating.

Fixed by introducing a genuine shared decision, consulted by both instead of independently re-derived by each: `_joint_mortar_hunt_target` (a `Unit` reference, tracked by identity so it survives exactly the kind of transient drift that broke things before) plus `_update_joint_mortar_hunt`, which runs once per tick before either system. It forms the instant `_known_enemy_mortar_lead` reports a genuinely TRUSTED fix (live, or a drone already closing on it) that no friendly mortar can engage yet, and — this is the actual fix — HOLDS regardless of how that same trust signal re-evaluates on later ticks, only releasing when the objective itself resolves: the target's no longer ACTIVE, some friendly mortar can now actually engage it (handed off cleanly to the normal, already-existing engagement tiers), or a generous `JOINT_MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_DURATION` (30 tactical minutes) safety valve runs out. An untrusted, bare fire-detection lead still never forms a commitment — that stays the mortar's own solo, distance-capped gamble exactly as before, since there's genuinely no drone coverage to coordinate with in that case.

`_drone_search_target` gained a new tier reading straight from this shared commitment — full mortar priority, and deliberately NOT gated by `_in_friendly_mortar_range` the way the existing live/lead tiers are, since the entire point is holding station over a target that isn't in range yet. `_update_friendly_mortar_hunting` now walks toward the SAME shared commitment unconditionally (no distance cap) rather than re-querying the lead and its trust flag on its own; the untrusted-gamble path is untouched. A new `log_joint_mortar_hunt` entry narrates the moment the commitment actually forms ("Mortar and drone team commit to a coordinated hunt for...").

**Verified:** a 7-test headless suite confirmed a commitment forms on a live, out-of-range mortar and never forms for a merely untrusted lead; that it genuinely survives the exact reported failure mode — the drone's move_target deliberately reassigned elsewhere mid-hunt, then confirmed the drone's own `_drone_search_target()` still returns to the committed position and the commitment itself is untouched by the next tick's update; that it correctly clears once the target comes into range or stops being ACTIVE; that the friendly mortar pursues a committed target across a distance that would fail the untrusted gamble's own cap; and that the untrusted solo-gamble path still works unchanged with no commitment in play. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes and no finite-position violations, and — a real, worthwhile signal after several earlier features turned out rare in practice — the joint hunt actually formed in 5 of the 10 real trials, a healthy real-play frequency rather than a theoretical-only mechanic.

### 2026-09-06 — v53: the drone's own local searching, not just its transit, favored the enemy's axis of attack

Bug report: "Early drone sweeps seem to prefer going along the axis of attack. They should not." Clarified before any fix was proposed, distinguishing transit from actual searching: "I mean the drone might move along that axis to a search area. But once it reaches the area, the above applies."

Root cause: v46/v51's sweep pattern was 5 rows, each spanning the FULL 3400m contested width at a fixed y before stepping to the next row. A single row's leg was, by itself, one long uninterrupted dash along the enemy's own east-west axis of attack — meaning the drone's actual in-area searching (not merely the transit needed to first reach a given part of the grid, which is real and unavoidable) was axis-dominated for the entire time it was conducting that leg, exactly the distinction the follow-up drew.

Replaced the 5-row, full-width pattern with a genuine 5x5 GRID: `GameConfig.DRONE_SEARCH_GRID_COLUMNS_M` (5 columns, ~850m apart) × `DRONE_SEARCH_GRID_ROWS_M` (5 rows, 750m apart, unchanged from before), 25 waypoints total, still visited in boustrophedon order (each row alternating direction) via the same `_drone_sweep_target` progression mechanic — no change to that mechanism, just a finer-grained set of points to feed it. With comparably-sized ~750-850m legs in BOTH directions instead of one axis being 4x the length of the other, a drone actively working any local part of the grid now alternates between horizontal and vertical motion constantly rather than favoring the attack axis — it still has to cross real distance to first reach a given area (unavoidable and correctly unaddressed by this change), but the actual searching, once there, no longer prefers one axis over the other. `DRONE_SWEEP_WAYPOINT_RADIUS` (how close counts as "arrived," triggering advance to the next waypoint) was scaled down from 500m to 200m to match — at the old value, the drone would have "arrived" at a new, much-shorter ~750-850m leg's endpoint at over half its actual length, cutting every leg badly short.

`DRONE_INITIAL_SWEEP_MIDDLE_INDICES` (v51's weighted-random starting point, favoring the road's own y-band) was updated from the old 10-waypoint layout's `[4, 5]` to the new 25-waypoint grid's actual center row, `[10, 11, 12, 13, 14]` — the underlying weighting logic (55% center, uniform elsewhere) was untouched, only the indices identifying which waypoints count as "center" needed to move with the new grid shape.

**Verified:** a 4-test headless suite confirmed the grid is genuinely 25 points spanning the expected width and height, that no single leg between consecutive waypoints exceeds ~1200m in either direction (measured: 850m max horizontal, 750m max vertical — comparable, not axis-dominated), that the boustrophedon correctly alternates direction every row, and that the updated middle-start indices actually land on the grid's real center row. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes and no finite-position violations.

### 2026-09-06 — v51: the drone's opening search was identical, and predictable, every single game

Bug report: "The drone always seems to start out by searching the top of the map. The pattern should not be predictable. It might be reasonable to start out with the middle more often than not. But not every time."

Root cause was a single hard-coded line: `start_battle` always set `_drone_sweep_index = 0`, and index 0 of v46's `DRONE_SEARCH_WAYPOINTS_M` boustrophedon is `(4950, 300)` — the map's top edge. Since the sweep is a fixed, deterministic cycle once it starts, an always-identical starting index meant an always-identical opening search pattern, every single battle.

Added `_random_initial_drone_sweep_index()`, called once at `start_battle` instead of the fixed `0`: a weighted pick, not uniform — `GameConfig.DRONE_INITIAL_SWEEP_MIDDLE_CHANCE` (55%) chance of starting on the CENTER leg (indices 4/5, the same y-band as the road — the single most operationally relevant strip, since that's where the enemy squads actually march), and a genuinely uniform pick among all 8 other indices the rest of the time. 55% deliberately reads as "more often than not, but not every time," exactly as asked, rather than picking an arbitrary round number.

**Verified:** a 4-test headless suite confirmed the function doesn't always return the same index (specifically, doesn't always return the old fixed 0), that the empirical middle-start fraction across 2000 calls lands almost exactly on the configured 55% (measured 0.550) while remaining genuinely non-exclusive, that every index returned is a valid waypoint index, and that 30 repeated `start_battle` calls land on 8 different starting indices rather than one fixed value. A 10-trial full-battle stress run confirmed no crashes, with the actual starting indices observed varying freely across trials (4, 8, 6, 5, 3, 6, 7, 1, 4, 5) rather than being stuck at 0 or any other single value.

### 2026-09-06 — v54: hunting is good, but the mortar shouldn't range indefinitely far from home

Bug report: "Hunting is good. But there are limits. The mortar should not venture too far from what it considers safe territory. On the last trial, it went well forward on the bottom portion of the map. That seems too bold, even if no enemy squads are known to be there."

v45/v52 deliberately made a TRUSTED hunt (live-visible, or a drone already closing on the lead) unconditional on distance — "worth going after regardless of distance, same as the enemy's own unconditional chase." That framing turned out to be missing a real, separate constraint: distance from known danger and distance from home are two different questions. `_friendly_mortar_hunt_point` already handles the first (routing around known threats along the way); nothing at all handled the second, so a genuinely trusted lead could pull the mortar arbitrarily far from wherever it was actually deployed, with no ceiling — exactly what let it end up "well forward" even with no known enemy squads anywhere nearby to have triggered caution.

Added `_friendly_mortar_home_position` (the player's own doctrine deployment choice, captured once in `_spawn_player_units` and never touched again) and `GameConfig.MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_RANGE_FROM_HOME` (half of `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE` — real room to reposition for a shot, not half the map). This caps every hunt DESTINATION, trusted or not, checked in two places: `_update_joint_mortar_hunt` won't even let a commitment FORM if the resulting destination would fall outside that radius (no sense having the drone commit its own top priority to escorting a hunt the mortar will never actually be allowed to complete), and `_update_friendly_mortar_hunting` re-checks the same cap on every relocation step as a second, independent guard — deliberately separate from, and unconditional on, whether any enemy squads are currently known to be nearby, since "too far from safe territory" was the actual complaint, not "too close to a known threat" (that's already handled elsewhere).

**Verified:** a 4-test headless suite confirmed the home position is correctly captured at deployment, that a hunt whose destination stays within the safe-territory radius still proceeds normally (both the joint commitment forming and the mortar actually moving), that a joint commitment never forms at all when the only reachable destination would fall outside that radius, and that the untrusted-gamble path independently respects the same cap even with no commitment in play. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes; the mortar didn't relocate at all in this particular sample (consistent with a few earlier features that turned out to trigger rarely with this specific map/doctrine combination), so the cap's real-play frequency wasn't directly exercised here, though it's proven correct in isolation by the unit tests.

### 2026-09-06 — v55: a squad bolting for cover could run straight toward a threat it already knew was there

Follow-up bug report from the same session: "Similarly, a friendly squad should not advance into dangerous territory. I just saw one move into the middle of a bunch of enemy squads."

Root cause, found by the same kind of comparison that found v50's bunching bug: `Unit.order_retreat` already threads `known_enemy_positions` through to `GameConfig.nearest_cover_point`'s danger-avoidance logic (excluding any candidate cover zone within `DANGER_RADIUS` of a known threat), but `Unit.seek_cover` — used for the OTHER reactive movement path, a squad breaking for cover under fire or bolting after a mortar hit (`RELOCATE_ON_MORTAR_HIT_CHANCE`) — never had a `known_enemy_positions` parameter at all. Every caller (`take_hit`'s two branches, `_alert_enemy_squads`) already had the right data on hand — `take_hit` receives it as its own parameter, `_alert_enemy_squads` can compute it the same way `order_retreat`'s own callers do — it just was never being passed through the one function that actually needed it to make the danger-avoidance logic in `nearest_cover_point` do anything at all.

Added the missing `known_enemy_positions` parameter to `seek_cover`, threaded straight through to `nearest_cover_point` exactly like `order_retreat` already does, and updated both real call sites (`take_hit`'s ENEMY-breaks-from-march branch and its either-side bolt-under-mortar-fire branch, plus `_alert_enemy_squads`) to actually pass it. A squad diving for cover now gets the same "don't run toward a threat you already know is there" protection a retreating one already had.

**Verified:** a 2-test headless suite confirmed `seek_cover` given a synthetic "known enemy" at what would otherwise be the nearest cover zone picks something else instead, and that it still works normally with no known enemies at all (the existing, unaffected default case). A 10-trial full-battle stress run (run alongside v54's) had no crashes.

### 2026-09-06 — v56: a squad ordered to retreat from a hopeless position might surrender instead

Request: "If the enemy orders a general retreat, a squad that is in a poor position for retreating might decide to surrender. It might also not decide to surrender. This should be random, with the chance of surrender getting higher the worse their position is. Chance of surrender also goes up if other enemy squads are not nearby. If anybody does surrender, this should be shown in the after-battle summary."

Added a new `Unit.State.SURRENDERED` — alive and unharmed like `WITHDRAWN`, but a distinct outcome worth reporting separately rather than folding into "withdrew safely" (a squad that surrendered didn't escape, it laid down its arms in place). Threaded through everywhere a state is checked: `is_targetable()` excludes it (can't be fired on any more, same as `WITHDRAWN`/`DESTROYED`), `_refresh_visibility` and the pending-mortar-shot resolver both stop tracking/allowing hits on it, and `Unit._draw()` fades it out (a pale white tint, distinct from a plain withdrawal's grey) with no pip bar or cover ring, same visual treatment as leaving the fight any other way.

The actual decision lives in `_check_enemy_commander_retreat` — the ONE place enemy squads get ordered into a general retreat (scoped deliberately to the enemy's own automatic commander decision, not the player's own `order_general_retreat` button; a human player choosing to retreat their own squads isn't the same situation this models). Right before each squad would normally get `order_retreat`, a new `_squad_surrender_chance` roll decides whether it does instead — two independent, real judgments a squad leader would actually weigh, matching the two factors named: `SURRENDER_POSITION_RANGE` (proximity to the nearest active player unit — a squad already right on top of the enemy has little real hope of disengaging cleanly) and `SURRENDER_ISOLATION_RANGE` (proximity to the nearest OTHER active enemy squad specifically, not a supporting mortar well to the rear, since mutual infantry support during a withdrawal is what actually matters). Each maps to a 0-1 "badness" score, weighted (0.5 position, 0.3 isolation) and summed, then capped at `SURRENDER_MAX_CHANCE` (0.6) — deliberately well short of certainty even in the worst position, matching "it might also not decide to surrender."

The AAR's existing `_compute_side_stats` (already tracking destroyed/withdrawn/still-retreating lists per side) gained a matching `surrendered` list, and `_end_battle`'s report gained matching "Player surrendered" / "Enemy surrendered" lines alongside the existing ones — the exact "shown in the after-battle summary" requirement.

**Verified:** a 5-test headless suite confirmed a squad far from danger with an ally right on top of it gets exactly zero surrender chance, that the worst case (right on a player unit, nearest ally 5000m away) hits the exact `SURRENDER_MAX_CHANCE` cap without exceeding it, that isolation alone (moving a nearby ally far away, position held constant) measurably raises the chance, that a `SURRENDERED` unit is correctly no longer targetable, and that running `_check_enemy_commander_retreat` for real (forced casualties to trip `_enemy_situation_hopeless`) actually produces a `SURRENDERED` squad across repeated trials at the capped chance, with `_compute_side_stats` correctly reporting it. A 15-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes — though `enemy_general_retreat_ordered` never actually triggered in that sample at all, the same pre-existing rarity already noted for v48 and v54 (the commander's own 40%-casualty threshold, untouched by this change), so the mechanic's real-play frequency wasn't directly exercised here; it's proven correct in isolation by the unit tests, and will fire the moment that pre-existing threshold does.

### 2026-09-06 — v57: friendly forces surrender far less readily than the enemy

Request: "Friendly forces should be more reluctant to surrender than enemy forces. This is Ukraine, and Ukrainians expect terrible treatment in Russian hands."

v56 had only ever wired the surrender mechanic into `_check_enemy_commander_retreat` — the enemy's own automatic commander decision — deliberately leaving the player's side out entirely, on the reasoning that a human player's own deliberate retreat order wasn't the same situation. Re-reading this request against that scope: "more reluctant... than enemy forces" is a comparison, not "should never happen," so the right fix is a genuine asymmetry between the two sides rather than leaving the player side as a hard, undifferentiated zero.

Generalized `_squad_surrender_chance` to work for either team's squads — "opposing" and "own side" in its position/isolation math now just flip based on `squad.team` instead of being hard-coded to the enemy's perspective — and wired the identical roll into `order_general_retreat` (mirroring `_check_enemy_commander_retreat`'s own pattern exactly, squads only, mortar/spotter excluded same as before). Then added a per-side multiplier, applied to the raw position+isolation badness score before the shared `SURRENDER_MAX_CHANCE` cap: `SURRENDER_WILLINGNESS_MULTIPLIER_ENEMY = 1.0` (unchanged baseline) against `SURRENDER_WILLINGNESS_MULTIPLIER_PLAYER = 0.15`. This isn't modeled as ordinary, symmetric battlefield reluctance — it's a direct reflection of the setting: credible, extensively documented reporting (UN and Human Rights Watch monitoring among it) describes systematic mistreatment of Ukrainian POWs in Russian custody, giving a Ukrainian squad in a hopeless position real, well-founded reasons the enemy simply doesn't share to keep fighting or trying to get out rather than lay down arms.

**Verified:** a 3-test headless suite confirmed that two squads placed in the objectively identical worst-case position (badness score 0.8, both axes maxed) produce very different outcomes — the enemy squad hits the shared 0.6 cap exactly, while the player squad's chance works out to exactly the raw 0.8 score scaled by the 0.15 multiplier (0.12), a 5x difference from the same starting position; that `order_general_retreat` can still actually surrender a player squad in that worst case, just rarely (confirmed via repeated trials at the low resulting probability); and that mortar/spotter units are correctly excluded from surrendering on the player's side too, matching the enemy's own existing restriction. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes.

### 2026-09-06 — v58: surrender was triggering too easily at real, safe retreat distances

Bug report from actual play: "That was too much surrendering. I can see surrender by enemies that are 100 meters away from friendlies, or who are surrounded. But someone surrendered while 600 meters away with a clear retreat path. That should not happen."

Root cause was the ORIGINAL v56 formula's own shape, on two counts. First, `SURRENDER_POSITION_RANGE` was 1200m — the same reference `SQUAD_DANGER_RANGE` uses for a genuinely different question (how much of a threat an ADVANCING squad poses to someone else), not "is my own retreat actually in jeopardy." At 600m, that gave `position_badness = 1 - 600/1200 = 0.5` — already half-badness at a distance that should read as perfectly safe. Second, and worse, the two factors were ADDITIVE (`0.5*position + 0.3*isolation`), which meant isolation contributed a real chance ALL ON ITS OWN even with position_badness at or near zero — a squad with no enemy anywhere close but no ally nearby either could still roll a meaningful surrender chance, which is backwards: nobody surrenders just because their friends are elsewhere, if nothing is actually threatening them.

Fixed both. `SURRENDER_POSITION_RANGE` dropped to 300m — tight, close-quarters territory where ~100m still reads as genuinely dangerous (badness ≈0.67) but 300m+ is already zero. More importantly, the formula is now MULTIPLICATIVE rather than additive: `chance = position_badness * (SURRENDER_ISOLATION_BASE_FACTOR + SURRENDER_ISOLATION_AMPLIFIER * isolation_badness)`, replacing the old separate position/isolation weights. Position danger is now the actual GATE — with zero proximate threat, the chance is exactly zero, full stop, no matter how isolated the squad is. Isolation instead AMPLIFIES real danger once it exists (a multiplier ranging from 0.3 with an ally right there up to 1.0 fully alone) — "surrounded" is what actually pushes a squad over the edge, not merely "alone with nothing nearby," matching the two cases named as legitimate ("100m away," "surrounded") against the one named as illegitimate ("600m with a clear path").

**Verified:** a 4-test headless suite confirmed a squad 600m out (verified against the actual nearest player unit's real distance, not an assumed one) never surrenders even with an ally right next to it, that the same holds even when fully isolated (position alone gates it to exactly zero regardless of isolation), that 100m out with an ally nearby carries a real, meaningful chance (0.317), and that the identical 100m position becomes measurably worse when isolated instead (0.600, the shared cap) — directly reproducing all three calibration points from the report. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes.

### 2026-09-06 — v59: a spotted squad is rarely alone — the drone should look around it, not just at it

Request: "If the drone sees enemies somewhere, it may want to search the general vicinity for more enemies. I say 'may' because there can be higher priorities, such as going to a region that is known, or even suspected, to have an enemy mortar."

`_drone_search_target`'s squad-tracking tier already scored the single most dangerous visible squad correctly (`_squad_danger_priority`) — the gap was purely in what position that tier actually sent the drone to: the squad's own exact coordinates, meaning the drone just parked directly overhead one confirmed contact instead of using the opportunity to look for whatever else might be nearby (more squads, or a mortar sitting just off to the side, real infantry deployments this map's own mortar and squad positions already model as distinct from each other). The "may" in the request — this shouldn't win when there's a better reason to be elsewhere — was already satisfied by the tier's own existing, deliberately low `TARGET_PRIORITY_SQUAD_MAX` (10): any live mortar, fire lead, or the team's joint hunting commitment (all 80-100) already outranks it unconditionally, and the general sweep's own confidence-scaled value ("even suspected to have an enemy mortar," the sweep's whole reason for existing) frequently outranks it too. Nothing about the priority ordering needed to change — only what position the squad tier resolves to once it does win.

Added `_drone_vicinity_search_point`, mirroring `_drone_sweep_target`'s own arrival-then-advance shape (GameConfig.DRONE_VICINITY_SEARCH_ARRIVAL_RADIUS) but circling the currently-highest-priority visible squad at DRONE_VICINITY_SEARCH_RADIUS (600m — comfortably inside DRONE_DETECTION_RANGE, so the original contact never actually drops out of view while the drone works the ground around it) instead of patrolling the whole map's fixed grid. Re-centers on that squad's position fresh every tick, so the circle follows if it moves, or re-anchors cleanly if a different, now more dangerous squad takes over the tier.

**Verified:** a 3-test headless suite (with the sweep's own competing expected-value term deliberately zeroed out via confirmed-destroyed mortars, so the squad tier's behavior could be isolated and observed directly) confirmed the drone searches a point 600m from the spotted squad rather than sitting exactly on it, that arriving at the current point on the circle advances to a genuinely new one still on the same circle, and that a live, engageable mortar still unconditionally outranks vicinity-searching around a spotted squad regardless. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes.

### 2026-09-06 — v60: the mortar should never end up ahead of its own infantry

Bug report: "I saw the mortar again go way in front of (and also below, in terms of the screen) the [friendly] squads. This should never happen." (Corrected from an initial "enemy squads" — the actual complaint is about the FRIENDLY mortar advancing past its OWN friendly squads, not the enemy's.)

v54 already capped how far a hunt could pull the mortar from its own deployment position (`MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_RANGE_FROM_HOME`), but that cap is a simple radius — nothing stopped a destination within that radius from still landing well past the friendly infantry actually holding the line, since `_friendly_mortar_hunt_point`'s candidate angles are chosen purely to get in range of the target and avoid exposure to KNOWN enemies, with no awareness of where the mortar's OWN squads are at all. A mortar is fire support — a real crew stays behind the friendlies screening it, never advances past the frontmost one or drifts outside the vertical band those squads actually occupy, regardless of how good the intel on a target is.

Added `_friendly_mortar_hunt_destination_is_reckless`: rejects any candidate destination whose x exceeds the frontmost (highest-x) currently-ACTIVE friendly squad's own x ("ahead of the line"), or whose y falls outside the band spanned by those same squads' y positions ("above or below the line" — covering the "below" half of the report directly). With no active friendly squad left at all to judge a line against, there's nothing to violate, and the existing home-radius cap is left to do what judging is still possible. Wired into both `_update_joint_mortar_hunt` (so a reckless hunt never even forms a commitment the drone would waste its own priority escorting) and `_update_friendly_mortar_hunting`'s own relocation step, exactly mirroring how the v54 home-radius check is applied in both places.

**Verified:** a 4-test headless suite confirmed a destination ahead of the frontmost friendly squad is correctly flagged reckless, that one outside the friendly squads' own y-band is too (the "below" case named directly), that a destination genuinely behind the line is accepted, and that a joint commitment never forms at all when the natural hunt point for a given lead would land somewhere reckless. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes — though a further, more targeted 5-trial diagnostic found the mortar could still end up numerically "ahead" of the frontmost ACTIVE squad in most trials (up to ~594m); checked directly, in all 5 of those cases the mortar was NOT hunting or moving at all at that moment — the cause was friendly squads themselves moving (retreating, or relocating for cover) past an already-stationary mortar, not the mortar advancing into a reckless position. That's a different, legitimate phenomenon outside today's fix (which is specifically, and per the unit tests correctly, about the mortar's OWN hunting decisions never choosing to advance past the line) — whether the mortar should also actively reposition to stay behind a shifting front line as squads fall back is a separate question left open rather than folded into this fix.

### 2026-09-06 — v61: the drone found a live mortar and immediately abandoned it

Bug report: "On that trial, the drone found a mortar and immediately left the area. I'm not sure why. It might have been OK if the mortar was out of range and not expected to go in range soon. But the behavior is highly suspect."

Root cause: `_drone_search_target`'s top tier used `_visible_engageable_mortar`, which requires the mortar to be currently in range of the friendly mortar (`_in_friendly_mortar_range`) — a condition that genuinely belongs to a DIFFERENT question. That same function is also what the backup-drone launch trigger and the self-sacrifice decision key off, and for THOSE, requiring actual engageability is correct — there's no point sacrificing a drone or spending a backup's flight time over a mortar nothing can currently shoot at. But reusing it for the much more basic "is this worth the drone's attention at all" question meant that the instant a live-visible mortar turned out to be out of range AND not a valid hunt target either (too far for `MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_RANGE_FROM_HOME`, or — per v60, added the same session — its only reachable firing position would be reckless), NONE of tiers 1/2/3 had anything to say about it any more, and the drone dropped a confirmed, live sighting entirely in favor of whatever else was competing (a squad, or the sweep).

Split the question in two. `_visible_engageable_mortar` keeps its existing, strict, range-gated meaning and every one of its existing callers (the backup-launch trigger, the active drone's self-sacrifice check, the backup drone's own hold/return decision) untouched. A new `_visible_active_enemy_mortar` — any currently visible, still-ACTIVE enemy mortar, full stop, no range gate at all — is what `_drone_search_target`'s top tier uses instead: a live enemy mortar is always worth watching, because the situation can change (either side's mortar can reposition) and simply maintaining contact has real value on its own, independent of whether firing on it happens to be feasible this exact instant.

**Verified:** a 3-test headless suite confirmed the drone keeps watching a live mortar placed far beyond both engagement range and the hunt-feasibility caps (explicitly checked that no joint commitment forms for it first, so the test is actually exercising the "nothing else claims it either" case the bug report described), that the stricter engageable check still correctly returns null in that exact same scenario (proving the backup/sacrifice decisions remain unaffected), and that a genuinely in-range mortar is still tracked at its own exact position as before. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes.

### 2026-09-06 — v62: drone scouting should focus on where the enemy is actually likely to be

Request: "drone scouting should focus on areas where the enemy is likely to be."

v53's fallback sweep visited a 5x5 grid in a fixed, uniform boustrophedon — every part of the map got exactly equal attention over time, whether it was the road's own busy y-band or the map's far, empty edges. v51's own "weighted random start" only biased the FIRST waypoint of a battle; once underway, the sequential cycle treated every point identically regardless of location, so any early bias washed out over a long sweep.

Replaced the fixed cyclic advance with `_weighted_random_sweep_index`, re-rolled every time the drone arrives at its current point (not just once at battle start): each of the grid's 5 rows gets a weight (`GameConfig.DRONE_SWEEP_ROW_WEIGHTS = [0.05, 0.15, 0.6, 0.15, 0.05]`, far-north to far-south), so the row sharing the road's own y-band is picked 60% of the time — the same reasoning v51 already used for the initial pick, now applied continuously — while the two outermost rows still get a real, if much smaller, share (5% each) rather than being ruled out. Column (x) stays uniform within whichever row wins, since the enemy could plausibly be anywhere along the road's own length. This isn't the same thing as knowing the enemy's actual, exact positions — the road itself is drawn on the map, public knowledge like the doctrine deployment screen already treats it, not secret intelligence — it's just weighting search effort toward where real activity is known to concentrate instead of spreading it evenly across empty ground. v51's own now-redundant `DRONE_INITIAL_SWEEP_MIDDLE_INDICES`/`_CHANCE` constants and `_random_initial_drone_sweep_index` function were removed outright — `start_battle` now just calls the same shared weighted picker directly.

**Verified:** a 2-test headless suite confirmed the empirical row-selection frequency across 4000 calls lands close to each configured weight (measured counts 210/631/2365/604/190 against expected ~200/600/2400/600/200, with every row, including both edges, genuinely reachable), and that repeated arrivals keep re-rolling to different points rather than getting stuck on one. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes.

### 2026-09-06 — v63: mortar casualties, and mortar targeting, now weigh unit strength

Request: "Casualties from artillery fire should be proportional to the size of the unit hit. A 9-person squad may therefore be a juicier target than a squad that has taken casualties. I say 'may be' because this is just one of the considerations that go into targeting. For example, if the damaged squad is dangerous to friendly units and the undamaged one is not, it may still be better to attack the damaged squad." Two real, connected mechanics, both previously flat/uniform.

**Casualties.** `Unit.take_hit`'s squad branch applied a flat `pips -= 1` regardless of `from_mortar` — a full 9-person squad and a 2-person remnant lost exactly the same one person to a mortar round, even though a shell's fragmentation genuinely covers an area and a denser formation has more people actually in it. Added `GameConfig.mortar_casualty_count(current_pips)`: `round(current_pips * MORTAR_CASUALTY_FRACTION)` (20%), floored at 1 and capped at whatever's actually left — a full squad now loses 2 per mortar hit, tapering back to the old flat 1 once a squad is down to 6 or fewer. Direct (non-mortar) fire is untouched — a rifle round is aimed at one person, not an area, so it stays a flat single casualty regardless of unit size.

**Targeting.** `_pick_target`'s choice among non-mortar candidates (mortars themselves still always win outright, unconditionally, unchanged) used to be uniformly random — no consideration of a target's own remaining strength or how dangerous it currently is. Added `_mortar_target_value(unit, target)`: `casualty_value` (the target's own current pips — a fuller unit is a juicier target, mirroring the new casualty math directly) plus `danger_value` (`_squad_danger_priority`, now generalized to take a `threatened_team` parameter instead of being hard-coded to the player's own side, so a MORTAR judges danger to ITS OWN side specifically). Equal weights, since both terms already land on comparable 0-9/0-10 scales by construction. `_pick_target` now does a genuine weighted-random choice (`_weighted_mortar_target_pick`) among candidates using this value — not a deterministic "always pick the single best one" (real targeting isn't perfectly rational, and a guaranteed-optimal AI would be trivially predictable), but a real bias toward higher-value targets. This directly implements the "may be" framing from the request: a damaged-but-dangerous squad can and does outweigh an undamaged-but-harmless one when the danger term is large enough, exactly as described.

**Verified:** a 6-test headless suite confirmed the casualty-count formula's edge cases (9→2, 6→1, 1→1), that direct fire still costs exactly one casualty regardless of strength, that mortar fire actually costs 2 against a full squad and only 1 against an already-worn-down one in a real `take_hit` call, that an undamaged squad is valued higher than a nearly-wiped-out one at equal (zero) danger, and — the exact scenario named in the request — that a damaged-but-dangerous squad (right on top of a friendly unit) outweighs an undamaged-but-harmless one (far from everything) by a real margin (11.0 vs 9.0). A further statistical test confirmed the weighted pick actually favors the higher-value target across many rolls (1778 vs 222 over 2000) while still leaving the lower-value one reachable. A 15-trial full-battle stress run across both recon modes had no crashes, with every unit's pip count independently verified to stay within `[0, max_pips]` on every single tick — the concrete guarantee that the new proportional math never produces an invalid casualty count.

### 2026-09-06 — v64: a retreating squad shouldn't read as "dangerous" just for being nearby

Bug report: "During the enemy retreat, I saw the mortar firing at a squad with very few people left when nearly-full squads were spotted and nearby. This should not happen."

Root cause: v63's `_mortar_target_value` added `danger_value` for any SQUAD target via `_squad_danger_priority`, with no check on the target's own `state`. A `RETREATING` squad is fleeing, not fighting — it isn't actually threatening anyone, even if it happens to still be near a friendly unit — but the formula scored it exactly like an `ACTIVE` squad standing its ground. That let a nearly-empty, retreating squad's proximity alone inflate its danger term enough to outrank nearby, nearly-full squads that were still actually fighting, which is precisely the v63 "may be" tradeoff misfiring in a case it was never meant to cover. The drone's own squad-danger search (`_drone_search_target`) already gates its equivalent scoring on `state == Unit.State.ACTIVE`; the mortar's version had no such gate.

Fixed `_mortar_target_value` to only compute `danger_value` for a target that is both a SQUAD and currently `ACTIVE` — a `RETREATING` (or any non-ACTIVE) squad now contributes zero danger regardless of proximity, matching the same gate the drone already uses. Casualty potential (a target's own current pips) is unaffected either way — a retreating squad can still be a perfectly valid, even juicy, target on casualty grounds alone; it just can no longer additionally out-rank a fighting squad by pretending to be a threat it isn't.

**Verified:** a 3-test headless suite confirmed a `RETREATING` squad sitting directly on top of an active friendly unit scores zero danger (value equals its casualty potential alone, not inflated by proximity), that a nearly-full `ACTIVE` squad correctly outvalues that nearly-empty retreating one, and — as a guard against overcorrecting — that the exact same weak squad still `ACTIVE` (still fighting) at that same contact-range spot CAN outweigh a distant full squad, preserving v63's intended tradeoff for the case it was actually meant to cover.

### 2026-09-06 — v65: enemy squads now bend their advance toward cover, and can flank

Request: "Enemy squads should attempt to avoid approaching the friendlies in open terrain. I'm not saying it can never happen, but usually it won't. Instead they should try to utilize cover." Follow-up: "They might also try to outflank."

`_next_advance_point` (the bounded "advance by rushes" leg an enemy squad takes once it's broken off the road march and has nothing left to shoot at right now) was a pure straight line toward `GameConfig.VILLAGE_CENTER` — no terrain awareness at all, despite the game already having a rich cover/concealment model (`GameConfig.is_in_cover`/`get_terrain_type_at`, `has_direct_los`) that every REACTIVE cover behavior (bolting under fire, retreating) already used. Nothing proactively used it while a squad was still healthy and advancing.

Rather than building real pathfinding, `_next_advance_point` now generates several candidate rush endpoints bent off the direct line at fixed angles (`GameConfig.ENEMY_ADVANCE_ANGLES_DEG = [-50, -25, 0, 25, 50]`), each still making real net progress toward the village, and picks among them with a weighted-random roll (`_weighted_advance_point_pick`/`_score_advance_candidate`) rather than a deterministic best-of. Scoring rewards a candidate that actually lands in cover terrain (`ENEMY_ADVANCE_COVER_BONUS`, a standing preference independent of known contacts) and, separately, one that every currently-known player position is unable to directly see (`ENEMY_ADVANCE_CONCEALMENT_BONUS`, via `has_direct_los` against `_known_enemy_positions`) — with an angle penalty so a bigger bend has to actually earn its keep, and a base weight floor so the literal straight-line-through-the-open candidate stays reachable, just not favored. This single mechanism serves both requests at once: the cover bonus is "usually avoid the open," and a wide-angle candidate that happens to break a spotted defender's sightline is a real, if mild, flanking maneuver — the enemy bends around whichever side actually offers concealment from what it currently knows about, rather than either always charging straight in or executing a scripted flank.

Scoped to the post-road-march advance-by-rushes phase only (`_update_enemy_squad_advance`'s caller) — the enemy's initial scripted road march (`set_path`/`ROAD_WAYPOINTS_M`, from v17) is a separate, pre-authored mechanic left untouched, since it isn't really "approaching the friendlies" so much as moving into the theater.

**Verified:** a 6-test headless suite confirmed the scoring function's arithmetic directly (open/straight = exactly base weight; open/wide-angle = penalized but floored at 0.1, never zero; landing in the isolated farmhouse's BUILDING zone scores strictly higher than open ground at the same angle; a candidate the village's own building blocks a known threat from seeing scores higher than one right next to that same threat), and that the weighted pick statistically favors a much-higher-weighted cover candidate (420-421/500 across two runs) while still leaving open, off-angle candidates reachable (multiple distinct points, including the literal straight line, still got picked across 300 rolls with no terrain to differentiate them). A 15-trial full-battle stress run (three squads a side, alternating recon modes) had no crashes, with every unit's pips staying in range and every position staying finite on every tick throughout.

### 2026-09-06 — v66: the friendly mortar's hunt route ignored known exposure once no candidate was fully hidden

Bug report: "While hunting an enemy mortar, the friendly mortar exposed itself to both vision and fire from enemy squads. That should not happen."

`_friendly_mortar_hunt_point` (the friendly mortar's own concealment-aware routing while closing on a known enemy mortar fix, from several rounds back) checks 7 candidate angles around the target and returns the first one with no known threat able to see it — genuinely safe, since a squad's own direct fire already requires `has_direct_los` too (see `_pick_target`), so "hidden" already means "cannot actually be engaged by any known squad," not just a heuristic. The bug was the fallback: when a known enemy squad screening the target could see EVERY one of the 7 angles (entirely plausible — squads often sit right around the mortar they're protecting), the old code fell back to whichever building-clear candidate happened to be checked FIRST in a fixed order, with no regard for how exposed it actually was. That could walk the friendly mortar into full view and full direct-fire range of a known enemy squad, purely because that angle was tried first — exactly the reported symptom.

Fixed: when no candidate is fully hidden, `_friendly_mortar_hunt_point` now picks whichever building-clear candidate keeps the most distance from its single nearest known threat, instead of the first one tried. This isn't full concealment, but real standoff — often enough to push the candidate outside `SQUAD_ENGAGEMENT_RANGE` of that threat even without breaking line of sight to it. The fully-hidden case (the common, ideal outcome) is unchanged.

**Scoping note:** this only helps against threats the player's side already KNOWS about (`_known_enemy_positions`) — a squad that hasn't been spotted yet is invisible to this check by construction, the same fog-of-war limitation every other cover/concealment routine in the game already has. Not treated as a bug; a crew can only avoid what it knows is there.

**Verified:** a 4-scenario headless suite independently re-derived, from the same primitives the function itself uses (`is_building_at`/`path_crosses_building`/`has_direct_los`), what the documented contract says the answer must be for each scenario (rather than hand-predicting which candidates a real, hill-warped map happens to hide, which turned out to be fragile against the actual terrain) — covering no known threats, a threat beside a hunted target with every candidate actually hidden, a threat sitting on the target with NOTHING hidden anywhere (confirming the farthest-standoff pick), and two scattered threats producing a genuine mix of hidden and exposed candidates (confirming a hidden one is always preferred over a closer-but-exposed one). All four matched exactly. A 15-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes, with pips and positions staying valid on every tick throughout.

### 2026-09-06 — v67: the general-withdrawal tactical-clock tier now runs at exactly double speed

Request: "We can run things at double speed during any retreat." Corrected immediately after an over-broad first attempt: "No, not any unit retreating. Only when the whole side is retreating. The action you are showing should be the same as previously. Just show it at 2x speed."

First pass added a new tier that fired for ANY single unit's own individual retreat and let it win over live contact elsewhere on the field — broader than intended, and it changed WHEN the speed-up applies, not just the number. Reverted that: the condition for the fast tier is exactly what it was before (`_general_withdrawal_in_progress()` — a whole-side order, not an individual unit's own threshold retreat — AND no live contact left anywhere; live contact still wins over it, unchanged), and the ONLY actual change is the tier's value: `GameConfig.TIME_SCALE_GENERAL_RETREAT` is now defined as `TIME_SCALE_NORMAL * 2.0` (120) instead of the previous flat 180 — the same withdrawal sequence, shown twice as fast, nothing else different.

**Verified:** a 5-test headless suite confirmed the constant is exactly double normal, that no-contact/no-retreat is still fast-forward, that an isolated INDIVIDUAL retreat with no contact stays plain fast-forward rather than picking up the general-retreat tier (the specific over-broadening this corrects), that a genuine general withdrawal with no contact left gets the (now doubled) tier, and that live contact still beats the general-retreat tier during a general withdrawal exactly as before.

**Process note:** while reverting, the first edit left a stale duplicate `const TIME_SCALE_GENERAL_RETREAT` declaration in `game_config.gd` (the original 180.0 line, never removed by the initial broadening edit since that edit's replacement text didn't include it) — caught immediately by the headless run failing to parse the class at all, fixed before any test could pass.

### 2026-09-06 — v69: casualties split into killed / heavily wounded / walking wounded, and retreats now have to decide what to do with them

Request: "Let's differentiate casualties into killed, heavily wounded, and walking wounded, with some of the last group still able to fight. When retreating, we want to take the wounded with us if possible. But if the wounded are not walking, this would slow the retreat. This differentiation of casualties happens for both sides. The enemy may choose to leave their wounded behind, especially if taking them along entails more risk. Such left-behind enemy wounded would surrender."

Every SQUAD casualty (a pip lost — see `Unit.take_hit`) used to just vanish from `pips` as an undifferentiated loss. Added `Unit._categorize_casualties`, rolling each newly-lost pip independently into `killed_count`, `heavily_wounded_count`, or `walking_wounded_count` via `GameConfig.CASUALTY_KILLED_FRACTION`/`CASUALTY_HEAVILY_WOUNDED_FRACTION` (30%/35%, walking wounded the 35% remainder) — a multi-casualty mortar hit naturally produces a believable mix rather than every person from one hit landing in the same bucket. `pips` itself keeps meaning exactly what it always did (current fighting strength — nothing here touches targeting, retreat-threshold math, or the mortar-casualty scaling from v63); the new counts are purely about what happens to the people who left it. KILLED needs no further tracking. WALKING_WOUNDED can move under their own power, so they cost nothing extra during a retreat — that's the concrete, if understated, form "some of the last group still able to fight" takes here: it's *why* they were never a retreat liability in the first place, rather than a separately mechanically-modeled fighting-while-wounded sub-state (this game abstracts a squad as pips, never individual soldiers, so a per-person "still able to fight" flag would be a much bigger departure than the request needs). HEAVILY_WOUNDED are alive but immobile — the one group `order_retreat` now has a real decision to make about.

That decision, `Unit._resolve_wounded_evacuation`, runs once, the instant a SQUAD with any heavily wounded actually starts retreating (whichever of the three existing `order_retreat` call sites triggered it — a squad's own threshold, either commander's general-retreat order). Both sides default to "take the wounded with us": carrying them costs real retreat speed (`GameConfig.HEAVILY_WOUNDED_SLOWDOWN_PER_PERSON` = 12% per person carried, floored at `HEAVILY_WOUNDED_MIN_RETREAT_SPEED_FRACTION` = 40% of normal so a badly mauled squad still makes some progress), applied by directly scaling `retreat_speed` and the cover-leg `move_speed` — safe to mutate in place since a unit only ever retreats once. The ENEMY alone additionally WEIGHS this instead of just accepting it: a genuine risk-weighted roll (not a hard cutoff, matching the same idiom as `_weighted_mortar_target_pick`/`_weighted_advance_point_pick`) against proximity to the nearest known player position (`GameConfig.WOUNDED_ABANDON_DANGER_RANGE` = 600m, scaled by how many are actually being carried via `WOUNDED_ABANDON_CHANCE_PER_PERSON`, capped at `WOUNDED_ABANDON_MAX_CHANCE` = 85%) — closer, and more people to carry, both raise the odds of leaving them. If abandoned, they move to `wounded_left_behind_count` (the request's "such left-behind enemy wounded would surrender" — reported in the AAR as captured, alongside the existing `surrendered` squads tally) and the unit retreats at full, unencumbered speed instead. The player side has no such abandon option today — matching the request's own asymmetric framing ("we want to take the wounded with us"; "the enemy MAY choose to leave theirs").
