Wired combat-log feedback (`log_wounded_carried`/`log_wounded_abandoned`) into all three `order_retreat` call sites via a shared `_log_wounded_evacuation_outcome` helper, following the same one-shot-flag-polled-by-BattleManager pattern already used for `sought_cover`/`bolted_for_cover`. `_compute_side_stats`/`_end_battle`'s AAR now break the casualty line into killed/wounded and add a "left behind, captured" line whenever it's nonzero.

**Verified:** a 6-test headless suite confirmed categorized casualties sum exactly to the count rolled and track the configured fractions within tolerance over 3000 rolls (30.2%/34.6%/35.3% against 30/35/35 targets), that the player carries wounded even at point-blank risk with the exact expected speed multiplier applied, that the enemy never abandons with no known threat at all or one well beyond the danger range (both deterministic), that the enemy's abandon rate under maximum point-blank risk tracks the configured 85% cap (measured 87.0% over 500 trials), and that an actual abandonment moves every heavily-wounded person to captured together, zeroes the count, and restores full retreat speed. A 15-trial full-battle stress run added a hard per-tick conservation check (`killed + heavily_wounded + walking_wounded + wounded_left_behind` must always exactly equal pips actually lost, for every squad, every tick) alongside the usual pips/position validity checks — held throughout, with 3 of the 15 trials producing a real in-play abandonment.

**Follow-up, same session:** "The end of battle report should differentiate among the different types of casualties." The AAR's casualty line originally combined heavily wounded and walking wounded into one lump "wounded" figure — split into "%d killed, %d heavily wounded, %d walking wounded" per side, so all three categories this feature actually tracks are visible in the final report, not just two of them (the existing separate "left behind, captured" line already covered the fourth). Verified directly against a real battle's final `_compute_side_stats` output for both sides.

**Bug report, same session:** "Enemy casualties: 31/62 personnel (50%) — 9 killed, 4 heavily wounded, 12 walking wounded. That math does not work. 9+4+12=25. It should add up to 31." Confirmed and fixed. Root cause: `killed_count`/`heavily_wounded_count`/`walking_wounded_count`/`wounded_left_behind_count` were only ever populated for SQUAD kind — a MORTAR or DRONE_TEAM crew casualty (`Unit._apply_crew_casualties`, an entirely separate decisive small-crew model, unaffected by this feature) still reduced `pips` and so still counted toward the side's `pips_lost` headline number, but was invisible to the new breakdown entirely; a SPOTTER's single-pip loss on destruction had the identical gap. Fixed at both sources: `_apply_crew_casualties` now adds every `crew_killed` increment to `killed_count` too (the crew model already has no separate "wounded" concept — a hit is decisive, someone's either fine or part of `crew_killed` — so this isn't a simplification, it's the same fact recorded in the shared field), and a SPOTTER's loss counts as killed_count outright (one person, one-hit-fragile, no graduated split needed). `_compute_side_stats` no longer restricts the summation to SQUAD kind — every personnel kind (SQUAD, MORTAR, SPOTTER, DRONE_TEAM; DRONE itself stays excluded as equipment, not personnel) now contributes, so `killed + heavily_wounded + walking_wounded + wounded_captured` is a genuine identity against `pips_lost`, not something that happens to usually hold. The follow-up request "captured are also casualties — that can be included in the report" is handled by folding `wounded_captured` directly into the same casualty line (rather than a separate line shown only when nonzero) specifically so the four numbers are always visibly right there summing to the total, not something the reader has to notice was omitted.

**Verified:** a 12-trial full-battle stress suite asserted the exact identity (`killed + heavily_wounded + walking_wounded + wounded_captured == pips_lost`) for BOTH sides on every single tick, not just at battle end — held throughout every trial, including several with real captured wounded (`c=2`/`c=2`/`c=3` in three of the twelve) and several with real mortar/drone-team crew losses folded correctly into the killed tally alongside squad losses.

**Follow-up, same session — three more AAR issues from a real report:** "If the enemy:friendly losses were 29:1, why does it show 0 player casualties? Also, one enemy squad turned white. Does that mean all dead? Or surrendered? If the latter, why does the report show 0 captured?" Plus a UI request: "let's have a 'copy' button for the report" (resolved as selectable text instead, per the follow-up: "the text could be selectable. Either resolution is OK.").

1. **Misleading exchange ratio at zero player losses.** `_end_battle`'s displayed ratio divided by `max(player_stats.pips_lost, 1)` — the same floor the VERDICT math needs to avoid a division by zero — but showing that floored value as "29.00 : 1" reads as a real, precise ratio when the actual fact is simpler and better news than any finite number: zero player losses, period. The verdict's own use of the floored ratio is unchanged (it's a reasonable, generous stand-in for "effectively infinite" when deciding SUCCESSFUL DEFENSE); only the display now special-cases `pips_lost == 0` with "no player casualties at all (N enemy)" instead of a number.
2. **White means surrendered, not destroyed — confirmed, not a bug.** `Unit._draw`: `SURRENDERED` renders `Color.WHITE` at 50% alpha; `DESTROYED` renders dark charcoal gray, not white. The two are visually distinct by design (a destroyed unit is gone, a surrendered one is alive and out of the fight) — this was a request for clarification, not a fix.
3. **A surrendered squad's own remaining personnel were invisible in the report.** "0 captured" was correct for its actual, narrower meaning (`wounded_left_behind_count` — individually abandoned HEAVILY_WOUNDED during a retreat's evacuation decision, this round's own v69 mechanic), but genuinely confusing next to "Enemy surrendered: Enemy Squad" with no headcount — the reader has no way to tell how many people that represents. Deliberately did NOT fold a surrendered squad's current `pips` into the same "captured" figure — those personnel were never subtracted from `pips_lost` in the first place (they're alive, not casualties), so mixing them in would have broken the just-established `killed + heavily_wounded + walking_wounded + wounded_captured == pips_lost` identity. Instead, the surrendered line itself now names the headcount: `"Enemy surrendered: Enemy Squad (5 personnel)"` — two clearly separate, honestly labeled categories instead of one that looks incomplete.
4. **Report text is now selectable/copyable.** `main.gd`'s AAR display swapped its plain `Label` for a `RichTextLabel` (`bbcode_enabled` stays off — the default — so the report's own "===", "%", ":" render as literal text, never parsed as markup) with `selection_enabled = true`; `scroll_active = false` + `fit_content = true` keeps the actual scrolling behavior identical to before (still handled by the wrapping `ScrollContainer`). Click-drag to select, Ctrl+C to copy — no custom button needed.

**Verified:** a 2-test headless suite drove `_end_battle()` directly against hand-set casualty/state scenarios — confirmed the zero-player-casualties case shows the spelled-out wording and never the misleading "29.00 : 1", and confirmed a surrendered squad's line includes its exact personnel count. The RichTextLabel swap was confirmed via a clean full-project headless compile (no dedicated test — this is a presentational property change with no game logic to verify, consistent with how this session has treated other pure-text/UI tweaks).

**Correction, same session:** user pushed back on keeping "captured" (abandoned wounded) and "surrendered" (a whole squad's remaining personnel) as separate, non-summing categories: "Captured is captured, regardless of the reason." / "If someone is wounded and captured, report them as 'captured'." Merged them: `_compute_side_stats` now folds a SURRENDERED unit's own remaining `pips` into the SAME `captured` total as `wounded_left_behind_count`, and — since those personnel were never previously subtracted anywhere — also adds them to `pips_lost`, bringing a fully-surrendered unit's contribution up to its full `max_pips` (whatever it had already lost to killed/wounded, plus everyone captured at the moment of surrender). No double-counting: a person already tallied as `killed_count`/`heavily_wounded_count`/`walking_wounded_count` before surrendering was already excluded from `pips`, so surrender-time `pips` and pre-surrender casualty categories are disjoint by construction — exactly satisfying "if wounded and captured, report as captured" without needing any special-case check. Renamed the internal `wounded_captured` field to plain `captured` throughout, matching the now-broader meaning. The per-unit "Enemy surrendered: Enemy Squad (N personnel)" line stays as narrative detail on top of the aggregate — not a second place that count lives.

**Verified:** a direct test on a squad carrying pre-surrender casualties (1 killed, 1 heavily wounded, 1 walking wounded, 1 already-abandoned-wounded) plus 5 remaining pips at the moment of surrender confirmed `captured` correctly totals 6 (5 newly surrendered + 1 previously abandoned) and `killed+heavily_wounded+walking_wounded+captured` exactly equals `pips_lost`, which itself now correctly equals the squad's full `max_pips` — the whole unit fully accounted for as lost to its side. A 15-trial full-battle stress run re-confirmed the reconciliation identity on every tick with no regressions (three trials produced real individually-abandoned-wounded captures; no trial happened to roll an actual squad surrender this run, but the direct test above exercises that path precisely).

### 2026-09-06 — v70: text throughout the UI is now selectable, not just the AAR report body

Request: "Let's make all text throughout the UI selectable. In particular in the AAR, it should be possible to copy the header at the same time as the rest of it."

Two things, really. First, the AAR's own selectability from last round's fix (a `RichTextLabel` with `selection_enabled`) still had a real gap: a plain click-drag selection can never extend past whatever's currently visible inside the wrapping `ScrollContainer` — once a report is tall enough to actually need scrolling, there was no way to drag-select the header together with everything below it, since extending the drag past the visible bottom edge doesn't auto-scroll. Fixed by also enabling `context_menu_enabled` — RichTextLabel's own right-click menu includes Select All, which works regardless of scroll position, then Copy. Second, the broader "all text throughout the UI" ask: added `GameConfig.make_selectable_label(text)`, a small factory returning a `RichTextLabel` pre-configured to behave like a drop-in Label replacement (no bbcode, no own scrollbar — `fit_content`/`scroll_active` sized to defer to a wrapping Container exactly like Label would) but with real selection + the same context-menu Select-All/Copy. Swapped in everywhere a `Label` previously showed real, meaningful text: `CombatLog` entries, `LevelSelectScreen`'s title and both mode descriptions, `DoctrinePanel`'s instructional/explanatory text, and `CasualtyDashboard`'s live per-side/per-mortar/drone-fleet readouts (including the two spots that used `add_theme_font_size_override("font_size", ...)` — RichTextLabel's equivalent theme property is `normal_font_size`, not `font_size`, so those two call sites were updated to the correct name rather than silently losing their font-size override).

**Deliberately left alone:** `main.gd`'s `_elevation_label`/`_clock_label` — tiny, single-value HUD overlays reassigned every single frame, drawn directly over the map. Nothing there is worth selecting or copying, and RichTextLabel's per-frame `.text` reassignment cost is real overhead with zero payoff for content nobody would select mid-motion.

**Verified:** a 5-test headless suite confirmed `make_selectable_label`'s exact configuration (RichTextLabel, `selection_enabled`, `context_menu_enabled`, no own scrollbar, sizes to content, bbcode off so literal "%"/"="/":" in real report text never gets parsed as markup), that CombatLog entries come back as selectable RichTextLabels, and that LevelSelectScreen/DoctrinePanel/CasualtyDashboard all still build and (for the dashboard) refresh with live text with no runtime errors after the conversion.

**Bug report, next round — two real deployment-screen regressions:** a screenshot showed the doctrine sidebar rendering only "Squad 1" before a large dead gap down to "Start Battle" (Squad 2/3 and the mortar section nowhere visible), and the drone team's on-map label reading "Drone Te" instead of "Drone Team."

1. **DoctrinePanel's squad/mortar sections were being pushed off-panel.** Root cause, found via a headless layout diagnostic (printing every child's actual computed `size`): `DoctrinePanel._build_squad_section`'s `threshold_label` sits in an `HBoxContainer` next to its slider — the ONE place among all of v70's conversions where a converted label is the horizontal/main-axis child of a container, rather than stretched to full width by a VBoxContainer's cross-axis behavior like every other one. Without a real width assigned before Godot computes its wrapped-text minimum size, the RichTextLabel reported a wildly inflated height — measured at ~760px for one short line, as if wrapped against a near-zero-width column — which alone exceeded the panel's entire fixed 500px height, pushing Squad 2, Squad 3, and the mortar section off the visible area with nothing rendering wrong, just missing. Fixed by explicitly setting `autowrap_mode = TextServer.AUTOWRAP_OFF` on that one label — it was never meant to wrap in the first place (a short inline caption beside a slider), so this exactly restores the single-line sizing the plain Label it replaced always had. Every other v70 conversion was re-checked against this same failure mode (a selectable label as a horizontal-container's main-axis child) and confirmed clear.
2. **"Drone Team" was truncated to "Drone Te" on deployment tokens.** Unrelated to v70 — `UnitToken._draw`'s `draw_string` call had used a fixed 60px width for the label since long before this session; "Squad 1–3" (7 chars) and "Mortar" (6 chars) fit, but "Drone Team" (10 chars) didn't, and `draw_string` clips rather than wraps when text exceeds its given width. Widened to 90px (with the draw origin re-centered to match).

**Verified:** a headless regression test asserts `DoctrinePanel`'s content height never exceeds its fixed panel height and that all three squad-section labels are present and sanely sized (under 100px, not the ~760px this bug produced) — passed after the fix (measured content height 500px against a 500px panel, previously 2621px). The token label width fix has no game-logic behavior to test (a pure draw-call constant), confirmed via a clean full-project headless compile.

**Follow-up, same session:** "We can remove the exchange ratio portion from the AAR. It is duplicative of other portions." Correct — with the casualty lines already spelling out each side's killed/wounded/captured breakdown, a separate "Exchange ratio (enemy : player personnel lost)" line added nothing a reader couldn't already work out, and (per the round before this one) was also prone to reading as misleadingly precise when player losses were zero. Removed the line entirely; `exchange_ratio` itself is untouched and still drives the verdict thresholds (SUCCESSFUL DEFENSE/PYRRHIC DEFENSE/TACTICAL WITHDRAWAL), it's just no longer printed.

**Verified:** a direct `_end_battle()` test confirmed the report text no longer contains "Exchange ratio" anywhere, while the verdict itself (computed from the same underlying ratio) still comes out correctly.

### 2026-09-06 — v71: mortar/drone-team crew casualties were unconditionally "killed" — should have been a real mix

Bug report: "Mortar casualties always seem to refer to 'killed', including on the sidebar at the right of the screen. That has to be wrong. The sidebar should probably refer to 'casualties'. The one that shows while the battle is going. The AAR can break them down."

Root cause: v69's wounded-evacuation work added `killed_count`/`heavily_wounded_count`/`walking_wounded_count` tracking, but `Unit._apply_crew_casualties` (the mortar/drone-team crew model — small crew, a hit is decisive) folded every newly-down crew member straight into `killed_count`, unconditionally, on the reasoning "the model has no separate wounded crew concept." That reasoning doesn't actually hold: a crew hit knocking someone OUT OF ACTION isn't the same claim as that person being DEAD, any more than it is for a squad — the decisive part of this model was always "how many go down and stop crewing the weapon in one hit," never "how many of them are corpses." SPOTTER's own single-casualty case had the identical bug, copied from the same (wrong) reasoning.

Fixed both to use the same `_categorize_casualties` roll a SQUAD's casualties already get, instead of a blanket `killed_count` increment — `_apply_crew_casualties` now calls it with however many crew went down in that hit, producing a genuine killed/heavily-wounded/walking-wounded mix. Renamed the underlying `crew_killed` field to `crew_casualties` throughout (Unit, BattleManager, CasualtyDashboard, CombatLog) — the old name was itself part of the problem, since it kept getting typed as "X/Y crew killed" wherever it appeared. Per the request's own framing: `CasualtyDashboard`'s live mortar-row and drone-team-row text (the actual "sidebar... while the battle is going") now says "N/N crew casualties," never "killed"; the AAR (`_crew_survivor_label`, `_compute_side_stats`'s destroyed-unit list) now breaks a crew's casualties down by category — "N/N crew casualties: N killed, N heavily wounded, N walking wounded" — matching the per-side breakdown style already established for squads. `CombatLog`'s own live "entire crew is down" line got the same "casualties" wording fix.

**Verified:** a 3-test headless suite confirmed 400 simulated crew hits produce a genuine mix (294 killed / 359 heavily wounded / 344 walking wounded — not remotely "always killed") with every casualty landing in exactly one bucket, that the sidebar's mortar status text contains "casualties" and never "killed," and that the AAR's crew survivor label correctly breaks a hand-set 1/1/1 killed/heavy/walking split down by name. A 15-trial full-battle stress run re-confirmed the killed+heavily_wounded+walking_wounded+captured==pips_lost reconciliation identity on every tick with no regressions, with real crew wounded (not just killed) appearing in all 15 trials.

### 2026-09-06 — v72: drone battery now charges for climb-out and letdown, not just cruise

Follow-up to a direct question: "I find myself wondering if drone charge is going down as fast as it should. Please compare in-game performance with the actual performance of the Mavic 3. Include flight speed and altitude gain in your analysis." Researched via DJI's own published specs: `DRONE_CRUISE_SPEED` (14 m/s / 50.4 km/h), `DRONE_MAX_FLIGHT_TIME` (46 min), and `DRONE_ROUND_TRIP_RANGE` (30 km) all already matched DJI's rated figures exactly, and the drain-rate math was already correctly calibrated against the range-derived flight time rather than the separately-rated hover-condition 46-minute figure. The one real gap: climbing to `DRONE_ALTITUDE_M` (300m) and descending back down at the end of a sortie were entirely unmodeled — the drone was treated as instantaneously at cruise altitude/speed from the moment it launched. At DJI's rated Normal-mode ascent/descent speed (6 m/s each), that's 50 seconds each way — about 4.5% of a full ~35.7-minute charge — that a real sortie spends neither cruising nor searching, which the pure cruise-speed model was quietly giving away for free. User confirmed: "Yes, I want the in-game battery to match real-world battery life."

Not modeled as an extra movement phase (a drone doesn't move horizontally while climbing or descending straight up/down over a fixed point, so there's nothing for the existing `move_speed`/`move_target` system to actually simulate) — instead a one-time charge deduction, converted through the same charge-per-second rate as cruise drain (`DRONE_FULL_CHARGE_FLIGHT_TIME`), applied at the two moments they actually happen: `GameConfig.DRONE_LAUNCH_CHARGE_COST` the instant a sortie launches (`_launch_drone`/`_launch_backup_drone`, applied to the popped battery before the drone ever starts moving), and `DRONE_LANDING_CHARGE_COST` the instant one actually completes its return flight and lands (`_update_returning_drones`, deducted from the recovered battery before it re-enters the pool). Neither applies to a shoot-down or a battery-dry sacrifice crash, since neither of those ever actually lands — exactly matching reality. `_drone_time_until_rtb` (which both the RTB decision and the eager-backup-launch timing key off) now also reserves enough charge to cover the eventual landing cost on top of the existing flight-time-home + safety-margin reserve, so a drone never turns for home with "just enough" and then comes up short paying for the letdown; every deduction is also clamped at 0 as a backstop.

**Verified:** a 6-test headless suite confirmed the new constants compute correctly (300m at 6 m/s = exactly 50s each way, cost = 50s worth of charge at the established rate), that both a fresh launch and a backup launch are charged the climb cost against the actual popped battery, that a drone with only the safety margin reserved (nothing extra for landing) now correctly reads as needing to RTB, and that a real landing deducts the letdown cost from the recovered battery — checked via the swap-queue entry, since `_update_returning_drones` immediately re-pops the just-recovered battery back out for the airframe now swapping onto it — with a clamp-at-zero case confirmed separately. A 10-trial full-battle stress run in DRONE_TEAM recon mode had no crashes, with an explicit check that no airborne, pooled, ready, or swapping battery value ever went negative across any trial.

**Follow-up, same session:** "Right if the drone battery drops to zero, the drone should just fall to Earth." Correct, and v72's own launch-charge-cost addition had opened a real gap: `_crash_drone` (destroyed, no landing, permanent loss) previously only fired from ONE specific path — a drone deliberately sacrificing itself past its safety margin to keep watching a mortar, draining until genuinely dry. Everywhere else (a normal RTB, or now, in principle, a fresh sortie launched on an already-critically-low battery that the new climb-out cost could push to exactly 0 before its own RTB check ever gets a chance to run) still just... kept flying, on paper, with zero charge.

Generalized the rule: `_update_active_drone`/`_update_backup_drone` now check `drone_battery_charge <= 0.0` FIRST, unconditionally, before anything else — a drone with no battery left doesn't glide, it falls, full stop, regardless of why it got there. The deliberate sacrifice case is unchanged in spirit (it still ignores the safety margin and keeps watching), it's just no longer a separate code path with its own duplicate zero-check — the same top-of-function rule ends it on whichever tick it actually runs dry. `_crash_drone` now takes an optional `watched` (defaulting to null): non-null gets the existing "sacrificed while holding station over X" log message, null gets a new generic "battery dies mid-flight — the airframe falls" one, so a launch-already-critical crash doesn't get mislabeled as a deliberate sacrifice it never actually was.

**Verified:** a 4-test headless suite confirmed an active OR backup drone launched with exactly 0.0 charge crashes immediately rather than being allowed to fly home (never added to `returning_drones`, since it never gets a landing), that a genuine sacrifice scenario still resolves to the same permanent-loss outcome, and that a healthy-charge drone is correctly left alone (no false-positive crashes). A 10-trial full-battle stress run in DRONE_TEAM recon mode had no crashes and no negative battery values, with 0-2 drones lost per trial across the usual mix of causes.

### 2026-09-06 — v73: the sidebar's casualty dashboard and combat log were overlapping

Bug report (a screenshot): "The right side of the screen could use a visual cleanup" — the AAR report, the enemy's second mortar row, and the combat log were all visibly rendering on top of each other in an unreadable mess.

Root cause, confirmed via a headless layout diagnostic (instantiating `CasualtyDashboard` for real, letting a few frames of layout settle, then reading its actual `.size`): the panel's declared `custom_minimum_size` (320×300, dating from before the mortar-row and drone-row additions in earlier rounds) was 86px shorter than its real rendered height with every row showing — 400 with the drone row, 2 player mortar row slots, and the enemy's 2 mortar rows all present (386px content + panel padding). `main.gd` positioned `CombatLog` right below it at a fixed y=370, which cleared the OLD (wrong) 300px estimate but not the real ~386-400px one, so the log's own content — including the full AAR report, added as one entry via `log_battle_end` — rendered directly on top of the dashboard's lower rows.

Fixing the offset alone wasn't enough: the whole window is a fixed 700px tall (`project.godot`), and 55 (dashboard's start) + 400 (its real height) + margin + `CombatLog`'s previous 340px would run 100+ px past the bottom of the window regardless of where it started. Resized `CombatLog` itself down to 230px (still fully scrollable, same content as ever — its own doc comment already frames it as "for scrolling back through what happened, not for reading at a glance," so a shorter visible window is a real but acceptable trade) and repositioned it to y=465, which now clears the dashboard's real height with a 10px gap and leaves 5px to spare at the bottom of the window instead of overflowing it.

**Verified:** a headless test built the actual `CasualtyDashboard` and `CombatLog` used by `main.gd`'s real layout constants, exercised the worst case (DRONE_TEAM recon mode, enemy's 2 mortars both active) and confirmed the dashboard's bottom edge no longer reaches the combat log's top, and that the combat log's own bottom edge stays within the 700px window instead of running off it — both checked directly against measured `.size` values, not assumptions.

### 2026-09-06 — v74: the drone's fallback sweep now avoids ground already confirmed clear

Request: "If the enemy is known not to be an a particular area, the drone does not need to look for them there."

`_weighted_random_sweep_index` (the fallback grid search once nothing more specific is worth the drone's attention — see v62) already weighted its 5 rows toward the road's own y-band, but that bias is static, baked in before the battle even starts — it never adapted to anything actually LEARNED during play. Once an enemy unit is confirmed no longer any kind of threat — DESTROYED, WITHDRAWN, or SURRENDERED, exactly the same "not still a threat" boundary `_known_enemy_positions` itself already draws (v68) — the ground it was last known to occupy is genuinely known-clear: real information gained through play, not a guess. Added `_area_confirmed_clear(point)`, true when such a unit's own position falls within `GameConfig.DRONE_SWEEP_CLEARED_RADIUS_M` (600m, roughly one search leg) of `point` — using the real, omniscient unit state rather than requiring this specific drone to have personally witnessed the kill, the same simplification `_mortar_existence_confidence` already relies on ("a real commander eventually being told the enemy mortars are destroyed").

`_weighted_random_sweep_index` was refactored from a two-stage row-then-uniform-column pick into a genuine weighted-random pick across all 25 grid cells at once, each cell's base weight its row's own share (`ROW_WEIGHTS[row] / columns_per_row` — mathematically identical to the old two-stage pick when nothing's cleared, so this is a generalization, not a behavior change, for the common case), multiplied by `GameConfig.DRONE_SWEEP_CLEARED_WEIGHT_MULTIPLIER` (0.15, not zero — a cleared area is much less worth revisiting but not certain to stay empty forever, matching this game's usual "real decisions aren't perfectly certain" idiom) for any cell `_area_confirmed_clear` flags.

**Verified:** a 3-test headless suite confirmed `_area_confirmed_clear` matches the exact ACTIVE/RETREATING-vs-DESTROYED/WITHDRAWN/SURRENDERED boundary directly, that the row distribution with nothing cleared is statistically unchanged from the original two-stage scheme (measured 197/573/2396/636/198 against expected 200/600/2400/600/200 over 4000 rolls), and that placing a destroyed enemy exactly on the single highest-weighted cell drops its pick rate from 364 to 59 per 3000 trials (~16%, closely matching the 0.15 multiplier) while staying reachable, not hard-excluded. A 10-trial full-battle stress run in DRONE_TEAM recon mode had no crashes.

### 2026-09-06 — v75: limited mortar ammunition, a real resupply pipeline, and a physical pickup point

Request: "Let's introduce limited mortar ammunition. Each mortar team on both sides enters battle with 20 rounds. Once the enemy is sighted, resupply can be requested. An additional 20 rounds will arrive in a rear area a random period of time after the request is made. The median time should be 60 minutes later, but with substantial variability. A further 20 rounds will arrive after approximately another hour, again with substantial variability. But each resupply mission will fail 10% of the time. The commander and mortar team should both be notified if that happens. This requires all mortar teams to be smart about what shots are and are not worth taking. In particular if an enemy mortar is out there, it is important to have ammunition to shoot at it. The commander and mortar team should both be notified when the resupply is roughly 15 minutes out. However, this guess might again prove to be incorrect." Refined twice more, mid-implementation: "The mortar team will need to go to the resupply point to pick up the resupply. The commander can place said resupply point anywhere along the friendly map edge." / "The resupply point should be placed during opening setup." Two remaining open questions (does the enemy get its own resupply point, and who decides when the mortar goes to fetch) were resolved by the user before building further: the enemy's point is picked algorithmically by its own "commander" (centered on its own mortars' position, on its own edge — no player input), and pickup is fully autonomous, the same way every other unit decision in this doctrine-driven game already is, not a manual player command.

**Ammunition.** `Unit.mortar_rounds_remaining` (new field, set to `GameConfig.MORTAR_STARTING_AMMO` = 20 in `setup()`) is spent one round per shot the instant it's launched (`_launch_mortar_shot`, hit or miss — you don't get the shell back) and gates firing outright: `_tick_fire` refuses to even attempt a shot at 0 rounds, exactly like the existing building-clearance check it sits beside.

**The request/arrival/pickup pipeline.** `request_mortar_resupply(mortar)` (called from a new "Request Mortar Resupply" button for the player, and automatically by `_update_enemy_mortar_resupply_ai` for the enemy the moment it's sighted the player and isn't already full) schedules BOTH waves up front — `GameConfig.MORTAR_RESUPPLY_WAVE_COUNT` = 2 — as independent log-normal delays (`GameConfig.sample_resupply_delay`: median-parameterized, not mean, and genuinely right-skewed — it can run late by far more than it can run early, which is what "substantial variability" should actually look like for a supply run), with wave 2's delay measured from wave 1's own already-random arrival ("a further 20 rounds... after approximately another hour" reads as one more hour on top of the first, not from the original request). `_update_mortar_resupply` ticks every request toward its ETA warning and eventual arrival/failure (`GameConfig.MORTAR_RESUPPLY_FAILURE_CHANCE` = 10%, independently per wave) every tick, logging each event (both request and outcome land in the shared combat log, which already serves as the single channel both "commander" and "mortar team" read from — there's no separate notification channel to split between them in this game's existing structure).

Rounds that arrive don't teleport onto the gun — they wait at a resupply point the mortar has to physically travel to and from (`_update_mortar_resupply_fetch`/`_start_mortar_resupply_trip`/`_advance_mortar_resupply_trip`, reusing the ordinary move_target/has_move_target system, so a mortar en route is just as unable to fire as any other moving unit already is). The player places their own point during deployment — a new draggable `UnitToken` (a distinct square marker, `is_resupply_point`, not styled via `Unit.Kind` since it isn't a combat unit at all) constrained to `GameConfig.PLAYER_RESUPPLY_DEPLOYMENT_ZONE`, a narrow strip along the player's own western map edge ("anywhere along the friendly map edge"). The enemy has no deployment screen to place one on, so `GameConfig.choose_enemy_resupply_point()` picks algorithmically instead: a point on the enemy's own eastern edge, centered on its own mortars' average position.

The fetch decision is autonomous and deliberately not eager: a mortar breaks off to collect waiting rounds immediately if it's completely dry (nothing else useful to do regardless), but otherwise only once it's already idle (no target worth engaging right now) — an active hunt or fire mission already claiming its movement isn't interrupted just to top off early, directly implementing "mortar teams need to be smart about what's worth it." Once there, it collects everything waiting (multiple resolved waves can accumulate before a trip actually happens) and returns to wherever it was standing when the trip started, not to the resupply point.

**Ammo conservation.** `_pick_target`'s mortar branch now holds fire on a squad target once down to `GameConfig.MORTAR_AMMO_RESERVE_FOR_COUNTER_BATTERY` (5) rounds or fewer — a firm floor, not a probabilistic tendency, matching the request's own "it is important" framing. An enemy mortar target is entirely unaffected by this (mortar-vs-mortar priority already wins outright and unconditionally, checked first, regardless of ammo), which is the literal mechanism behind "if an enemy mortar is out there, it is important to have ammunition to shoot at it."

**The imperfect ETA warning.** "The commander and mortar team should both be notified when the resupply is roughly 15 minutes out. However, this guess might again prove to be incorrect" is taken literally, not just as flavor text: the warning threshold for each wave is its OWN independently-sampled log-normal delay (median 15 minutes) subtracted from that wave's arrival time — not `arrival - exactly 15 minutes` computed with perfect hindsight. The stated "roughly 15 minutes" genuinely can and does turn out wrong by the time the run actually resolves, the same way a real logistics ETA would.

**Sidebar layout, again.** Adding a second button (retreat + resupply) above the casualty dashboard pushed the whole sidebar's vertical budget past what last round's v73 fix had just settled — re-measured real button heights (31px each) and re-derived every position/size down the column: retreat (20–51), resupply (54–85), dashboard (95–495), combat log (505–695, its own declared height trimmed from 230 to 190 to fit) — all within the fixed 700px window, verified the same way v73 was.

**Scoping note:** an AAR-level ammo/resupply summary and a visible on-map marker beyond the deployment-screen token weren't built — not asked for, and the live sidebar (now showing "in action (N rounds)" per mortar) and combat log already cover what a player needs to make resupply decisions during play.

**Verified:** a 7-test suite covering starting ammo, request eligibility (rejects before sighting, rejects a duplicate while one's pending, schedules wave 2 after wave 1's own arrival), sticky sighting flags, the delay distribution (2000 samples: measured median 3573s against a requested 3600s, real spread from 631s to nearly 22000s — genuinely right-skewed), wave resolution matching the configured failure rate, and the ETA warning always firing strictly before its own wave's arrival. A second 7-test suite covered ammo gating end to end: `_pick_target` declining a squad-only target list at/below the reserve, still engaging normally above it, and ignoring the reserve entirely for an enemy mortar target; `_tick_fire` refusing to fire at 0 rounds; `_launch_mortar_shot` costing exactly one round; and a full fetch-trip round trip (departs when dry, collects exactly the waiting rounds on arrival, returns to its original position, cleans up on completion) plus resupply-record cleanup once a cycle is fully resolved and collected. A third suite confirmed the enemy's resupply point sits on its own edge centered on its own mortars, that `DeploymentScreen.get_positions()` includes a resupply point inside its own deployment zone by default, and that the enemy AI correctly requests once sighted-and-not-full and correctly declines when already at full ammo. The sidebar layout fix was verified the same way as v73 (real measured control sizes, not assumptions). A 15-trial full-battle stress run across both recon modes had no crashes and no negative ammo anywhere, with 13 of 15 trials actually requesting resupply during play and 3 of 15 completing a full physical fetch-and-return trip.

### 2026-09-06 — v75 follow-up: no button — the request is automatic on first sighting, for both sides

Correction: "No one should have to click to request mortar resupply. The request should be made automatically as soon as the first approaching enemy is spotted." The "Request Mortar Resupply" button from the initial build (added when the request only specified the enemy's side as automatic) was the wrong call — removed entirely, along with its `can_request_player_mortar_resupply`/`request_player_mortar_resupply` wrappers, which no longer had a caller.

Generalized `_update_enemy_mortar_resupply_ai` into `_update_mortar_resupply_requests`, iterating both `player_units` and `enemy_units` identically: any mortar on either side requests its own resupply the instant its side's `_has_sighted_enemy` flag flips true, throttled only by "no request already in flight" — not by current ammo level. That last part is a real, deliberate change, not just wiring the same rule up to both sides: the original enemy-only version also skipped requesting once already at a full load, which meant a mortar sitting at its starting 20/20 (true of every mortar at the moment of first contact, before it's fired a shot) would never actually trigger a request until it later dropped below max — directly contradicting "as soon as the first approaching enemy is spotted." With a resupply run taking on the order of an hour or more to arrive at all, waiting for an actual shortage before asking would already be too late anyway; a real commander gets the supply chain moving the moment contact looks likely, not after the guns go quiet for want of rounds.

Removing the button also freed the sidebar's second button row back up — re-measured and re-derived the whole column again (retreat button 20–51, dashboard 65–465, combat log 475–695, its own declared height restored from 190 back to 220 now that there's room), the same way v73 and the earlier round of this same feature both did.

**Verified:** a 4-test suite confirmed no request fires before sighting, that a mortar still sitting at its full starting ammo requests resupply the instant it's sighted (the specific case the old gate broke), that a second tick doesn't re-roll or duplicate an already-pending request, and that the enemy side behaves identically. The sidebar layout was re-verified the same way as before (real measured control sizes). An 8-trial full-battle stress run confirmed no crashes, no negative ammo, and the player's own mortar requesting resupply very early in every single trial (step 81–232 out of runs lasting 1800–5700+ steps) — consistent with "on first sighting," not "once low."

### 2026-09-06 — v75 follow-up 2: relief already on the way is a reason to stop hoarding, not keep hoarding

Request: "If resupply is known to be coming soon, that is a reason to use up one's remaining ammo before collecting the resupply, assuming targets are available."

`_pick_target`'s ammo-reserve conservation (hold the last `GameConfig.MORTAR_AMMO_RESERVE_FOR_COUNTER_BATTERY` rounds back for a possible enemy mortar sighting rather than spend them on a squad) made sense as a firm policy when those rounds were genuinely all that was left. It stopped making sense the moment resupply is actually confirmed inbound — hoarding against a hypothetical future mortar sighting when fresh rounds are already on their way just wastes the reserve's own value while it sits unused against an available target right now.

Added `_mortar_resupply_imminent(mortar)`: true once this mortar's own pending request either has rounds already sitting at the resupply point (arrived, awaiting pickup) or has had a wave's "roughly 15 minutes out" ETA warning fire without that wave having resolved yet — either way, real rounds are genuinely expected soon, not just requested-and-who-knows-when. `_pick_target`'s reserve check now only actually withholds fire when this is false; once true, a squad target at or below the reserve threshold is engaged normally again, same as if ammo weren't scarce at all. This doesn't force firing — a mortar with nothing worth shooting still won't fire regardless — it just stops declining a target it otherwise would have, "assuming targets are available" exactly as the request frames it. The enemy-mortar-always-wins-priority rule above this check is completely unaffected either way.

**Verified:** a 6-test suite confirmed `_mortar_resupply_imminent` reads false with no pending request and false for a pending-but-not-yet-warned one, true once a wave's warning has fired but not resolved, and true once rounds are already waiting for pickup; and confirmed `_pick_target` still conserves the reserve exactly as before when nothing's imminent (regression check) but engages an available squad target once it is. A 10-trial full-battle stress run had no crashes and no negative ammo.

### 2026-09-06 — v75 follow-up 3: the 5-round reserve becomes a genuine sliding scale, plus a live resupply status readout

Two requests together: "Estimated resupply status should appear near the mortar remaining rounds." / "If resupply is NOT imminent, rounds would be kept unless an enemy mortar can be fired at." The second one turned out to need a clarifying question — restating the existing 5-round reserve rule, or something bigger? — and the answer redefined the whole mechanic: "It can be a sliding scale. The less ammo is left, the greater the inclination to hold some. The sooner resupply is expected, the more willing one should be to fire. Five shots remaining should never be a magical number. However, ammo is not a consideration when shooting at an enemy mortar. That shot should be taken. It's only a consideration when shooting at squads."

Replaced the previous hard `MORTAR_AMMO_RESERVE_FOR_COUNTER_BATTERY` (5 rounds) / `_mortar_resupply_imminent` boolean pair entirely with two independent, continuous 0.0–1.0 factors that combine into a genuine hold-fire PROBABILITY, not a threshold: `_mortar_ammo_scarcity(mortar)` — 0.0 at a full `MORTAR_STARTING_AMMO` load, ramping linearly to 1.0 as rounds approach zero — and `_mortar_resupply_urgency(mortar)` — 0.0 with nothing pending or the soonest still-unresolved wave still `GameConfig.MORTAR_RESUPPLY_URGENCY_HORIZON_MINUTES` (30) or more away, ramping linearly to 1.0 as that arrival approaches, with rounds already sitting at the resupply point (arrived, awaiting pickup) counting as maximally urgent. `_pick_target`'s conservation check is now `hold_fire_chance = scarcity * (1.0 - urgency)`, rolled once per tick rather than compared against a fixed number — a full load never hesitates regardless of urgency, empty-handed with nothing coming holds almost every time, and anywhere between genuinely slides on both axes at once. This only ever runs once the enemy-mortar-candidates check above it has already come up empty, so "ammo is not a consideration when shooting at an enemy mortar" needed no code at all — it was already true by construction, just reaffirmed by the user as the one non-negotiable exception to the whole conservation model.

Added `BattleManager.mortar_resupply_status(mortar)`, a public accessor returning the same state `_mortar_resupply_urgency` computes from in player-friendly form (not pending / "~Nm out" / "ready for pickup"), and wired it into `CasualtyDashboard._mortar_status_text` right next to the rounds count: `"in action (12 rounds, resupply ~45m out)"`, with no suffix at all when nothing's pending so a mortar that hasn't requested resupply doesn't clutter its own row.

**Verified:** an 8-test suite confirmed the scarcity curve at full/half/empty ammo (0.0/0.5/1.0 exactly), the urgency curve at no-request/beyond-horizon/half-horizon/rounds-waiting (0.0/0.0/0.5/1.0 exactly), that a full-ammo mortar never holds fire across 50 trials, that a near-empty mortar with nothing pending holds fire in the large majority of trials (measured 475/500 ≈ 95%, matching the predicted 0.95 × 1.0 hold chance at 1/20 ammo almost exactly), that the same near-empty mortar with rounds already waiting for pickup NEVER holds fire (0/500 — urgency=1.0 zeroes the chance regardless of scarcity), that an enemy mortar target is always taken regardless of ammo or urgency, that `mortar_resupply_status`'s three shapes match each state, and that the dashboard text correctly includes both the rounds count and the resupply suffix together. A 10-trial full-battle stress run confirmed both factors stay correctly bounded [0,1] on every tick for every mortar and that generating the dashboard text never throws, with no crashes or negative ammo.

### 2026-09-06 — v68: a surrendered unit is not a threat to anyone

Bug report: "A surrendered enemy squad should not be considered a threat by anyone. In this particular case, I saw a mortar flee from it. But that is not the only application of the principle."

Root cause, at the shared source rather than the one symptom: `_known_enemy_positions(team)` — consumed by cover-point selection, retreat routing, the friendly mortar's own hunt-route concealment (`_friendly_mortar_hunt_point`, fixed just last round), and the new enemy-advance concealment/danger scoring (v65's `_score_advance_candidate`) — only excluded `DESTROYED` units from counting as a "known enemy position." A `SURRENDERED` unit (laid down its arms in place, alive and unharmed — `is_targetable()` already correctly excludes it from ever being fired upon) still passed that filter, so every one of those consumers kept treating it exactly like a live, armed contact: worth hiding from, worth routing around, worth fleeing. `WITHDRAWN` (reached safety, off the field entirely) had the identical gap.

Fixed by tightening `_known_enemy_positions` to explicitly require `state == ACTIVE or state == RETREATING` — the only two states where a unit is still actually capable of shooting back — instead of merely excluding `DESTROYED`. One change at the shared source fixes every consumer at once, which is the direct answer to "not the only application of the principle": no call site needed its own special-case check.

**Verified:** confirmed directly that `_known_enemy_positions` includes ACTIVE and RETREATING enemies but excludes SURRENDERED, WITHDRAWN, and DESTROYED ones from an otherwise-identical set of five, and reproduced the exact reported scenario — a surrendered squad sitting right beside a mortar being hunted, positioned so it would have appeared to screen every candidate angle under the old logic — confirming the friendly mortar's hunt route now takes the plain direct-line candidate as if the surrendered unit weren't there at all. A 15-trial full-battle stress run (also covering v67 above) had no crashes, with an explicit per-tick check that no surrendered unit's position ever appeared in `_known_enemy_positions`; 2 of the 15 trials had an actual surrender occur during play, both clean.

### 2026-09-06 — v75 follow-up 4: enemy mortar logistics are real but never narrated

Request: "The player should not know the ammunition or resupply status of enemy mortars." The whole resupply pipeline added in v75 — ammo counts, request/arrival/failure timing, the physical fetch trip — was built identically for both sides from the start, which was correct for the mechanic's internal behavior but wrong for what the player gets told about it: an enemy mortar's rounds-remaining and resupply ETA are logistics detail no player-side observer would actually have visibility into, unlike a spotted position or a general retreat order (both already narrated, and both things an observer plausibly COULD see).

Added `_should_narrate_mortar_logistics(mortar)` (`battle_manager.gd`, right after `_resupply_point_for`) — true only for `Unit.Team.PLAYER` — and gated all six `combat_log.log_mortar_resupply_*` call sites behind it: the request itself, the ETA warning, arrival, failure, departing for the fetch trip, and collecting rounds on arrival. The enemy side still calls `request_mortar_resupply`, still schedules and resolves both waves, still runs the fetch trip and adds rounds to `mortar_rounds_remaining` exactly as before — none of that changed, only whether it gets written to the log the player reads.

`CasualtyDashboard._mortar_status_text` had the same leak on the sidebar side: an enemy mortar's ACTIVE row showed the same `"in action (%d rounds%s)"` as the player's, rounds count and resupply suffix included. Fixed with a single early return — `Unit.Team.PLAYER` keeps the full detail, anything else gets a plain `"in action"` with no numbers at all.

**Verified:** a direct test confirmed a player mortar's dashboard text still contains both a rounds count and a resupply suffix (given a `BattleManager` with a pending, ready-to-collect resupply record), while an otherwise-identical enemy mortar's text is exactly `"in action"` with no digits, "rounds," or "resupply" anywhere in it. A 2-trial full-battle stress run (one per recon mode, 6 tactical hours each) confirmed the enemy mortar's `mortar_rounds_remaining` still changes during play — the mechanic is still fully live — while scanning every combat log entry in both runs (103 and 147 entries) for any resupply-related text naming an enemy mortar found none.

### 2026-09-06 — v75 follow-up 5: fetch trips wait for safety and don't force a trip home

Request: "When the mortar is out of ammo, it may want to go to the resupply point if it is safe. Also, once it gets resupply, there is nothing wrong with firing from the resupply point or nearby. It can move if there is a reason. But it does not have to."

Two independent tightenings of the fetch-trip mechanic added in v75. First, `_update_mortar_resupply_fetch`'s "completely dry, go immediately regardless" branch didn't weigh exposure at all — a mortar with nothing left to shoot with would set out for a fixed, standing resupply point even while currently spotted by the enemy, walking into the open with no way to shoot back if it went wrong. Added `_safe_to_fetch_resupply(mortar)` (true when not currently `is_visible` to the opposing side) and gated the dry-mortar departure on it: `idle or (out_of_ammo and _safe_to_fetch_resupply(m))`. A dry mortar under an enemy's eye now simply holds its position (it already can't fire regardless) until contact is lost, then departs the very next tick — no extra state needed since `is_visible` is already recomputed live every tick. The pre-existing idle-and-not-dry path (nothing worth shooting at right now, but still has rounds) is untouched — that path was already discretionary and wasn't what the request called out.

Second, `_advance_mortar_resupply_trip` used to force a "returning" leg after collection, walking the mortar all the way back to wherever it happened to be standing when the trip started — implying there was something wrong with actually firing from the resupply point itself, which isn't true. Removed the forced return leg and the `origin`/`phase` bookkeeping it needed entirely: `_mortar_resupply_trip` is now a plain "trip in progress" marker (mortar → true) rather than a dictionary, and arrival now just collects the waiting rounds, logs it (still gated on `_should_narrate_mortar_logistics`), and erases the trip — leaving the mortar exactly where it is. "It can move if there is a reason, but it does not have to" needed no new logic: the mortar's ordinary post-shot shoot-and-scoot relocation (`_relocate_mortar`, already run after every shot for a shoot-and-scoot crew) and enemy-fix-driven repositioning already fire from wherever the mortar currently stands, resupply point included — a real reason to move still moves it; the absence of one no longer manufactures a trip it didn't need.

**Verified:** a 3-case direct test confirmed a dry mortar with a valid, in-range, visible target available (isolating this from the separate idle path) holds its position while spotted, departs for the resupply point the very next tick once no longer spotted, and — once arrival is simulated — collects the waiting rounds and remains exactly at the resupply point with no move order issued and no trip left pending. A 2-trial full-battle stress run (one per recon mode, 6 tactical hours each) had no crashes and no negative ammo on any mortar, with one full fetch trip observed starting and completing cleanly during the run that had one.

### 2026-09-07 — v76: squad tactics — the enemy flanks for the mortar, friendly squads screen and don't get surrounded

Request: "I want squad tactics for both sides. The enemy should attempt to flank the friendly squads. In particular in order to reach the mortar. But if the mortar is out of action, they still might try to flank in order to get an advantageous battle for the village. Friendly squads are trying to protect the mortar and to prevent themselves from getting into a hopeless position. Nobody wants to end up surrounded by enemies that are under cover. Friendly squads may move to block flanking movements, or to keep themselves from becoming surrounded."

Preceded by an investigation prompted by "it seemed like the enemy squads were firing either very little or not at all": a 20-trial instrumented stress run found no such suppression (enemy squads routinely out-fired the player's own in the synthetic default scenario), and nothing in the session's recent mortar-scoped changes touched squad code at all — the most likely explanation settled on was a display/attention one (the combat log only narrates a squad's first reveal-by-fire, never every subsequent shot; a squad's own direct-fire tracer is brief and short next to a mortar's dramatic arc), not a bug, and the user asked to leave rendering alone for now. That thread is unrelated to this feature but is why this round started with a `before-flanking` git tag rather than assuming a clean baseline.

**The enemy's objective becomes the mortar, not just the village.** `_update_enemy_squad_advance`'s advance-by-bounds (already existed, post-first-contact only) always rushed toward a fixed `GameConfig.VILLAGE_CENTER`. Added `_enemy_advance_objective()`: the friendly mortar's own current position, if it's genuinely `ACTIVE` and the enemy actually has a fix on it (reusing `_known_friendly_mortar_position` — live sight or a still-fresh muzzle-flash detection, exactly what already drives the enemy's own counter-battery chase), otherwise the village exactly as before — "if the mortar is out of action, they still might try to flank... for the village" needed no separate code path, just the existing fallback. `_next_advance_point`/`_score_advance_candidate` needed no other change to work toward the new objective — they already operated on "the direct line to wherever we're headed," just fed a different target now.

**A real flanking bias, not just a new destination.** The existing concealment bonus (reward a candidate no known player position can see) already produced some route-around behavior, but nothing penalized walking straight up to a known defender's face — only "no bonus" for being seen, same score as being seen from a mile off. Added `GameConfig.ENEMY_ADVANCE_EXPOSURE_PENALTY` (2.5, roughly one cover bonus's worth): a candidate within `SQUAD_ENGAGEMENT_RANGE` AND direct LOS of a known player position now takes a real score penalty on top of forfeiting the concealment bonus, making a wide berth around a known defended position pay off much more clearly than before, especially now that there's a specific point (the mortar) worth actually routing around something to reach rather than a diffuse village center.

**Friendly squads screen the mortar.** New `_update_friendly_squad_positioning()` (called each tick, mirroring `_update_enemy_squad_advance`'s own idle-and-nothing-to-shoot gate — a squad already fighting never gets reassigned mid-engagement): for the single nearest known enemy within `MORTAR_FLANK_THREAT_RADIUS` (1500m) of the mortar's own actual position that has no `ACTIVE` friendly squad within `MORTAR_FLANK_CORRIDOR_WIDTH` (300m) of the straight line between the two (`_lane_is_screened`/`_nearest_unscreened_mortar_threat`), the nearest still-idle squad moves out to `MORTAR_PROTECTIVE_RADIUS` (350m) from the mortar, on the bearing toward that threat — directly interposing itself, not marching all the way out to meet the threat at its own position. Only the single closest open lane is answered per tick, one squad at a time. The player's own side always uses the mortar's real position here, never an aged/uncertain fix — unlike the enemy, the commander always knows exactly where their own gun is.

**Friendly squads don't stand to be surrounded.** New `_reposition_for_encirclement(u, known_enemies)`: known enemies within `FRIENDLY_ENCIRCLEMENT_DETECT_RADIUS` (600m) of a squad are checked for their angular spread around it (sorted bearings, 360° minus the largest gap between consecutive ones) — below `FRIENDLY_ENCIRCLEMENT_ANGLE_THRESHOLD_DEG` (140°) they're all roughly "over there," not surrounding. Spread alone isn't enough to flee, though: "nobody wants to end up surrounded by enemies that are under cover" is taken literally — at least `FRIENDLY_ENCIRCLEMENT_MIN_COVERED_FRACTION` (half) of the nearby contacts also have to actually be sitting in real TREES/BUILDING cover, the genuinely hard-to-dislodge case, before a squad treats its position as hopeless. When it does, it pulls back a bounded `FRIENDLY_REPOSITION_RUSH_DISTANCE` (300m) toward the center of mass of the rest of its own side (every other `ACTIVE` unit, mortar and spotter/drone team included, not just squads) rather than standing to be overrun — reassessed next tick like every other bounded-rush movement in this doctrine, not a single committed sprint. A squad that pulls back for its own survival this tick is excluded from being picked to go screen a flanking lane elsewhere in the same tick — it's already got its own problem.

**What's deliberately not narrated.** Neither of these decisions logs *why* — no "the enemy has found the mortar" entry when it starts flanking toward it. That would hand the player intel about the enemy's own knowledge state (whether the mortar's position has actually been discovered) that they wouldn't otherwise have, the identical fog-of-war line already drawn around the enemy's own mortar ammo/resupply status (see the v75 follow-up above). The friendly side's own two new actions ARE narrated (`log_squad_blocking_flank`/`log_squad_consolidating`) — those are the player's own units, exactly the kind of "moment that changes the picture" the combat log already exists for.

**Verified:** a 10-case direct test suite confirmed the objective is the mortar when active-and-known and falls back to the village once destroyed/withdrawn/retreating or once the fix goes stale; that an otherwise-identical advance candidate scores lower when it sits inside a known defender's engagement range with LOS than one far away; that two known contacts spread wide but standing in the open don't trigger encirclement, that two contacts bunched in the same general direction don't regardless of cover, and that two contacts spread wide AND actually sitting in real forest-patch cover do trigger a pull-back toward an ally; that a squad sitting on the direct enemy-to-mortar line screens it and one off to the side doesn't; and an end-to-end case confirming an idle squad moves to the correct point on the protective ring toward an unscreened threat. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes, with the mortar-blocking behavior triggering in every single trial (43 events total) — genuinely live in ordinary play, not just reachable in a contrived test. Encirclement pull-backs never triggered in these particular short synthetic battles (default squad/mortar placement and a short road-march-driven fight don't happen to produce a genuine multi-side, dug-in encirclement in the time available) — covered instead by the direct unit test above, which exercises the actual trigger condition directly. A separate 10-trial run confirmed squad-vs-squad engagement is still healthy on both sides after the advance-objective and scoring changes (a wide range of shot counts on both sides, no sign of the new logic suppressing ordinary engagement).

### 2026-09-07 — v76 follow-up: three goals in real tension, not just three separate scoring terms

Bug report: "Enemy squads got bunched up in the village in that run. They should [be] trying to attain the following goals, which will often contradict each other: 1) be in cover, 2) surround the friendly squads, 3) hunt the mortar. Bunching up in the village had them succeed in goal 1 but fail utterly in 2 and 3. More balance is needed. Each goal at times needs to be ignored in order to attain the other goals."

Root cause, once traced past v76's own scoring above: the actual moment several enemy squads visibly clump together was never the ongoing advance-by-bounds v76 added (`_next_advance_point`) — it was `_alert_enemy_squads`, the reflex that breaks EVERY still-marching squad off its road-march column at once, the instant any one of them takes fire. That reflex called the plain, purely-proximity `Unit.seek_cover` (`GameConfig.nearest_cover_point`, weighted among only the 2-3 nearest cover zones), with zero awareness of the mortar objective or of spreading out around anything — several squads that were all still close together on the same road (a real road-march column, often only ~30m apart) independently reaching for "nearest cover from MY position" reliably resolves to the same one or two nearby patches. Goal 1 alone, solved locally per-squad, with goals 2 and 3 never entering the decision at all — exactly the reported failure mode.

Three changes, in order of how much they actually mattered:

**The initial break-for-cover is now routed through the same multi-goal scoring as the ongoing advance**, not a separate proximity-only reflex. `_alert_enemy_squads` now calls `_next_advance_point` for each squad instead of `Unit.seek_cover` — cover, concealment/exposure, and the new encirclement bonus (below) all get weighed on this very first move, not just on later ticks a squad that quickly finds a target may never actually reach.

**A genuine "spread out" pressure**, not just "avoid the exact same point." Added `GameConfig.ENEMY_ADVANCE_ENCIRCLE_BONUS` (3.0, the same order of magnitude as the cover bonus, deliberately — see the doc comment on why all these terms are kept comparable) and `_angle_diff`/`_known_position_centroid`/`_encirclement_pivot`: a candidate's bearing, relative to the known friendly cluster's centroid once there's an actual contact (or the objective itself before then — so several squads converging on an unsighted mortar/village still don't stack on the same bearing), is compared against every OTHER active enemy squad's own current bearing around that same pivot, and rewarded for being angularly far from the nearest one. This is the actual mechanism behind "surround," not a byproduct of cover-seeking.

**Room to actually act on that pressure.** The original `ENEMY_ADVANCE_ANGLES_DEG` capped at ±50° specifically so "every candidate still makes real net progress toward the objective" — a fine rule for a slow, tick-by-tick advance, but it left a squad with no way to meaningfully circle to a different side of a nearby objective from a tight starting cluster, however hard the new encirclement bonus wanted to push it there. Widened to ±120° — genuinely backward-leaning at the extremes, a real (if heavily angle-penalized) temporary step away from the objective in service of getting to a different side of it. `_next_advance_point` also gained a `max_step` override (`INF` for the one-time alert scatter, so it covers most/all of the remaining distance to `ENEMY_SURROUND_STANDOFF_RADIUS` in one decisive move instead of the ordinary bounded rush) and an `extra_bearings_deg` parameter, so squads processed later in the same alert event see the bearings their allies-in-this-same-tick just claimed a moment ago, not just everyone's stale pre-alert positions.

Also fixed a real overshoot bug found while building this: the standoff-radius "stop before walking onto the objective" check (v76) compared the CURRENT distance against the radius before moving, but capped the rush distance against the FULL remaining distance, not the remaining distance minus the radius — a squad already within one rush of the objective could jump clean through the standoff ring in a single bound. Fixed by capping `rush` at `distance_to_objective - ENEMY_SURROUND_STANDOFF_RADIUS`.

**Honest about the result:** this is weighted-random scoring over a discrete angle set, not a guaranteed even distribution — two squads can still coincidentally settle on very similar bearings, especially with a widened but still finite set of candidate directions. A direct reproduction (6 squads in a realistic ~30m-spaced road-march column, one shared `_alert_enemy_squads` event) showed a real, wide spread (max separation over 4km, 4 of 6 squads meaningfully apart from their nearest neighbor) rather than the original single-point collapse, but not a perfectly even ring every time — matching "usually, not always," the same idiom already used everywhere else weighted-random picks appear in this doctrine.

**Verified:** confirmed `_angle_diff`'s wraparound math directly, that the encirclement bonus scores an unclaimed bearing higher than one an ally already holds, and that the standoff-radius overshoot fix holds (a squad within the radius stops, one outside it still advances, capped correctly against the radius rather than the full distance). A direct reproduction of the reported scenario (6 enemy squads in a tight road-march column, one `_alert_enemy_squads` call) confirmed real spread (see above) instead of collapsing onto the same point or the same one or two cover zones. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes and healthy squad-vs-squad engagement on both sides throughout; per-trial minimum pairwise distance among surviving active enemy squads was noisy (a single coincidentally-close pair among five or six otherwise-spread squads easily dominates a bare minimum), which is why the direct reproduction test above — checking the full spread, not just the closest pair — is the one that actually confirms the fix.

### 2026-09-07 — v77: the drone watches for a flanking approach, not just known contacts

Request, after confirming the flanking rework above actually worked: "Friendly drone should now include searching for flanking enemies in its priorities. This is definitely more important than hovering over known enemy squads. I'm not sure how its importance compares to enemy mortars. Research and/or use your judgment." The drone's own search priority (`_drone_search_target`, a tiered scoring scale, highest to lowest: a live/leaded/joint-hunt-committed enemy mortar, then the best known squad, then a broken straggler, then a blind grid sweep) had nothing between "an enemy mortar" and "a squad already spotted" — no notion of proactively watching for a threat that hasn't been spotted yet at all, which is exactly what the new flanking AI (v76) can now produce.

Judgment call on where it ranks, since the request left that open: placed at `GameConfig.TARGET_PRIORITY_FLANK_WATCH = 30.0` — comfortably above `TARGET_PRIORITY_SQUAD_MAX` (10, a known squad's own ceiling) and `TARGET_PRIORITY_RETREATING_ENEMY` (25, finishing off an already-broken straggler), but well below `TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR` (100) and its lead/joint-hunt variants (80/100). The reasoning: an actual, already-located enemy mortar remains the single biggest, most repeatable threat there is — real rounds landing on real people every reload — and nothing preventative should outweigh acting on one right now. A squad already spotted is already a known, bounded quantity the friendly side is watching and can react to. An UNSPOTTED flanking approach is the genuinely dangerous unknown in between: exactly the kind of surprise that turns into "surrounded by enemies under cover" (see the v76 follow-up above) before anyone gets a chance to react, worth clearly outranking a squad the game already knows about, but not worth outranking a mortar that's costing lives on every shot.

New `_drone_flank_watch_target()`: checks a fixed ring of `GameConfig.DRONE_FLANK_WATCH_BEARINGS_DEG` (8 compass directions, every 45°) around the friendly mortar at `MORTAR_FLANK_THREAT_RADIUS` — deliberately the SAME radius that already defines "close enough to be a real threat" for the friendly squads' own screening behavior (v76's `_update_friendly_squad_positioning`), so the drone watches exactly the zone an unscreened approach would go on to trigger a squad response in. A bearing is skipped if it's already screened by an ACTIVE friendly squad (reuses `_lane_is_screened` from the same v76 machinery — no point watching a lane a squad's already covering) or if a known enemy position already sits near it (already covered by a higher-priority tier). Sticky like the existing blind-sweep waypoint (`_drone_sweep_target`'s own pattern): keeps heading to the same point once picked rather than re-rolling every tick, until the drone actually arrives (`DRONE_FLANK_WATCH_ARRIVE_RADIUS`) or that bearing stops qualifying (now screened, or something's since been spotted there) — otherwise a bearing that briefly loses and re-wins the weighted pick against a neighbor would have the drone flitting between them instead of committing to actually checking one. Wired into `_drone_search_target` as a new tier, right after the joint-mortar-hunt commitment and before the known-squad-hover tier.

**Verified:** an 8-case direct test suite confirmed: no active mortar means nothing to flank-watch; with nothing screened or known, a valid point on the ring is picked; a bearing screened by a friendly squad is never picked (checked across 30 fresh re-rolls); a bearing already sitting on a known enemy position is never picked (same 30-re-roll check); with every bearing covered, the function correctly returns "nothing worth watching" rather than some fallback; the point is sticky across repeated calls with nothing changed; a known, dangerous squad loses the priority roll to an open flank; and a live, engageable enemy mortar still wins outright over flank-watching regardless. A 10-trial full-battle stress run (DRONE_TEAM mode) had no crashes, with the drone spending a real, meaningful fraction of its search time on flank-watch in every trial (as low as 4/26 ticks, as high as 48/75) — genuinely competing for the drone's attention in ordinary play, not just reachable in a contrived scenario.

### 2026-09-07 — v77 follow-up: an out-of-ammo mortar seeks safety instead of just sitting there

Request: "A mortar that is out of ammo would try to go to a safer area." A dry mortar (`mortar_rounds_remaining <= 0`) was already handled for the "go fetch resupply" case (`_update_mortar_resupply_fetch`, gated on it being safe — see the earlier v75 follow-up) and for the "currently spotted while idle" case (`_update_friendly_mortar_concealment`, player-only), but a dry mortar that's neither currently being watched nor has rounds waiting to collect yet just sat exactly where it was — just as exposed as an armed one, with nothing to show for it if something found it.

New `_update_mortar_safety_relocation()`, run right before `_update_friendly_mortar_concealment` each tick: any `ACTIVE`, idle (no `has_move_target` — so an already-claimed fetch trip from earlier this same tick is left alone) mortar on EITHER side with zero rounds left calls the same `_relocate_mortar` a shoot-and-scoot crew already uses after firing (nearest concealment from known threats, or just the nearest cover with nothing known) — deliberately NOT gated on being currently spotted, unlike the concealment check below it: being defenseless is reason enough on its own to look for better cover, not just a reaction to being seen, the same "standing procedure, not a purely reactive move" framing `_relocate_mortar`'s own doc comment already uses for shoot-and-scoot. Symmetric across both sides, like every other piece of the ammo/resupply mechanic — but only the player's own move is narrated (`log_mortar_relocating_out_of_ammo`, gated on `_should_narrate_mortar_logistics`); the enemy's happens exactly the same way, silently, same as its resupply fetch trips already do — revealing that an enemy mortar just relocated FOR THIS SPECIFIC REASON would be exactly the kind of enemy-logistics leak the v75 follow-up fog-of-war fix already exists to prevent.

**Verified:** a 4-case direct test suite confirmed an out-of-ammo player mortar relocates and gets a narrated log entry, an out-of-ammo enemy mortar relocates identically but silently, a mortar with ammo remaining is entirely unaffected, and — running the real tick order (fetch decision, then safety relocation) — a mortar that already claimed a fetch trip this same tick keeps that destination rather than being redirected. A 10-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes and no negative ammo, with the new relocation actually triggering in the trials where a mortar ran dry with no resupply yet in hand (0 in shorter battles that never got a mortar to zero, up to 22 in a longer one).

### 2026-09-07 — v78: direct fire gets a real close-range lethality bonus

Request: "Fire against units in the open should be significantly deadlier than it currently seems to be, especially if the range is short." Two separate gaps, once traced through `CombatResolver.resolve_fire`: the OPEN exposure penalty for direct (squad) fire was only 1.3x — actually LESS dangerous than simply being caught moving (`MOVING_HIT_MULTIPLIER` = 1.6x), which undersold "exposed, no protection at all" for a stationary target relative to the doc comment's own claim of "brutal." And range to target didn't factor into direct-fire hit chance AT ALL — a shot at point-blank range and one at the full edge of `SQUAD_ENGAGEMENT_RANGE` (400m) were exactly as likely to land, which is nothing like how rifle marksmanship actually works.

Two changes in `CombatResolver`: `SQUAD_COVER_MULTIPLIER[OPEN]` raised from 1.3 to 1.6, bringing a stationary exposed target in line with a moving one instead of notably safer. And a new `GameConfig.SQUAD_CLOSE_RANGE_HIT_MULTIPLIER` (2.0), applied in `resolve_fire` as an independent factor — `lerp(2.0, 1.0, clamp(distance / SQUAD_ENGAGEMENT_RANGE, 0, 1))` — stacking on top of (not replacing) the existing cover/moving multipliers: point-blank is a real 2x over the baseline rate, tapering linearly to a neutral 1.0x by the time a target is all the way out at the edge of engagement range. Applies to direct fire only, gated on `attacker.kind != Unit.Kind.MORTAR` — a mortar's plunging, spotter-relayed fire doesn't get easier to aim just because the target happens to be closer to the tube, so mortar hit chance is completely unaffected by this change.

Combined effect: a stationary target caught in the open at point-blank range now takes a shot at `0.20 * 1.6 * 2.0 = 0.64` per attempt (up from a flat `0.20 * 1.3 = 0.26` regardless of range before this change) — genuinely brutal, as originally intended — while cover keeps doing real work at any range (`TREES`/`BUILDING`'s own tiny multipliers mean even the doubled close-range bonus still lands on a small number, e.g. `0.20 * 0.2 * 2.0 = 0.08` for TREES at point-blank).

**Verified:** a statistical test (4000 trials per condition) measured a point-blank exposed-in-the-open hit rate of 0.650 against an expected 0.640, and a full-engagement-range rate of 0.332 against an expected 0.320 — both within noise, and the close-range rate is comfortably (>1.5x) higher than the far one as intended. A same-setup check confirmed TREES cover still holds hit chance under 0.08 even at point-blank range (measured 0.077) — cover isn't undermined by the new bonus. A separate check confirmed mortar fire's measured hit rate is statistically identical at close and far range (0.257 vs 0.264) — the range bonus genuinely doesn't apply to indirect fire. A 10-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes, with casualty percentages spread across a reasonable 10%-56% range — battles are deadlier but not degenerating into instant, one-sided wipes.

### 2026-09-07 — v78 follow-up: grounding the close-range curve in real infantry-combat data

Request: "Probably a good idea to check your values against real life info, to the extent that it is available" / "Try to model what happens in real life." The v78 close-range bonus above was a reasonable first cut but an unjustified SHAPE choice — a straight-line interpolation from 2x at the muzzle down to 1x at `SQUAD_ENGAGEMENT_RANGE` (400m), picked without checking whether real hit probability actually falls off linearly with distance. It doesn't.

Researched actual combat data: the US Army's 1948 Operations Research Office study of roughly 3 million WWII/Korea casualty reports found rifle hit probability "satisfactory only up to 100 yards, declining rapidly beyond," with the vast majority of engagements (and hits) occurring within 300 yards even though the rifles themselves were accurate to nearly three times that distance — hit probability is dominated by real-world factors (suppression, fear, target exposure time, terrain) that erode fast with range, not a smooth linear taper. Separately, 1960s M16 test data (favorable range conditions, not full combat stress) put 50% hit probability on a stationary man-sized target at roughly 250m — consistent with "good out to law of diminishing returns well past 100m, but nothing like point-blank."
