Together this describes a front-loaded curve: most of the advantage concentrated at genuinely close range, a rapid initial falloff, then a long, nearly-flat tail — not a straight line.

Replaced the linear interpolation with exponential decay: `1.0 + (SQUAD_CLOSE_RANGE_HIT_MULTIPLIER - 1.0) * exp(-distance_m / SQUAD_CLOSE_RANGE_DECAY_M)`, with the new `SQUAD_CLOSE_RANGE_DECAY_M` (150m) chosen so the curve lands close to the cited data points: ~1.72x still at 50m, ~1.55x at 100 yards (still "satisfactory," matching the ORO framing), down to ~1.16x by 300 yards ("declining rapidly," matching where ORO says the vast majority of combat — and its lethality — has already played out) and ~1.03x by `SQUAD_ENGAGEMENT_RANGE` itself (effectively baseline, same endpoint the old linear version had). The peak multiplier itself (2.0x at point-blank) was left as-is — no source found gave a precise enough number to justify moving it, and the shape correction is what was actually wrong, not the peak.

**Verified:** re-ran the same style of statistical check (5000 trials per range point) at five real-world-meaningful distances (0m, 50m, 100 yards, 300 yards, and 400m) and confirmed measured hit rates matched the new formula at every point (e.g. 0.497 measured vs 0.494 expected at 100 yards, 0.358 vs 0.372 at 300 yards). A second direct check confirmed the curve is genuinely concave (front-loaded) rather than linear: the multiplier drop from 0m→50m (0.283) is larger than the drop from 50m→100m (0.203), matching how real hit-probability falloff actually behaves rather than losing value at a constant rate. A 10-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes, with casualty percentages spread across a reasonable 13%-62% range.

### 2026-09-07 — v78 follow-up 2: mortar cover checked against real data too

Follow-up request: "yes check mortar lethality too, including counter battery fire and the impact of cover." Same research-then-calibrate treatment as the direct-fire pass above, this time for `MORTAR_COVER_MULTIPLIER` and the counter-battery timing constants. (An initial research pass surfaced current Ukraine casualty-cause statistics — drones now causing the large majority of casualties there — but the user correctly flagged this as the wrong analog: that figure is dominated by FPV/loitering-munition STRIKE drones directly killing people, whereas this game's drone is pure reconnaissance for directing mortar fire, closer to a WWII/Korea-era artillery spotter aircraft or a modern ISR platform. "Right now, we are in an era where the sources of fire are infantry and artillery. Drones are for recon." That framing was dropped entirely — it never actually informed any constant, and the actual calibration below draws on timeless infantry/artillery doctrine instead.)

**Counter-battery timing checked out already.** Modern reporting on Ukraine's counter-battery cycle (radar detection in seconds, "1-2 minutes, 3 at the outside for rounds on target") lines up closely with the existing `COUNTER_BATTERY_DELAY_MIN`/`MAX` (60-180 tactical seconds) — no change made. Real shoot-and-scoot displacement (a Caesar self-propelled howitzer completing a full fire-and-move cycle in about a minute) likewise supports the existing asymmetry between `MORTAR_COUNTER_BATTERY_SCOOT_CHANCE` (0.06) and `_HOLD_CHANCE` (0.22) — a crew that displaces promptly is genuinely several times less likely to be caught than one that doesn't, which is exactly what those two constants already encode.

**Mortar cover was the real gap.** `MORTAR_COVER_MULTIPLIER`'s OPEN-to-BUILDING spread was only ~1.9x (1.3 vs 0.7) — barely more dangerous to be caught in the open than dug in under a hard roof. US Army FM 7-90 (Tactical Employment of Mortars) planning figures say otherwise: a STANDING, exposed platoon under sustained 60mm mortar fire takes roughly 20% casualties; a PRONE one takes under 10%; one dug in WITH OVERHEAD COVER takes under 10% and "mostly by direct hits" — a real, several-fold protective effect, not a ~2x one. Retuned `TREES` from 0.85 to 0.5 (undergrowth breaks up fragment paths and forces some rounds to detonate in the canopy rather than at ground level — real, but well short of a hard roof) and `BUILDING` from 0.7 to 0.3 (matching the "mostly direct hits only" finding for genuine overhead cover), landing on a ~4.3x OPEN-to-BUILDING spread. Left `SQUAD_COVER_MULTIPLIER` alone for comparison: mortar cover is still deliberately far weaker than direct fire's own table (TREES/BUILDING at 0.2/0.1) — a mortar's plunging fire still punishes cover built to stop a flat rifle trajectory far better than a rifle does, the whole reason these are two separate tables — just not AS weak as the old 1.3/0.85/0.7 spread claimed.

**Verified:** a statistical test (4000 trials per terrain type) measured mortar hit rates of 0.517/0.207/0.128 for OPEN/TREES/BUILDING against expected values of 0.520/0.200/0.120 — all within noise — and confirmed the OPEN-to-BUILDING spread is now 4.33x, comfortably in the "real, several-fold" range FM 7-90 describes rather than the old ~1.9x. A 10-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes, with casualty percentages spread across a reasonable 15%-68% range.

### 2026-09-07 — v78 follow-up 3: a drone directing mortar fire is genuinely more accurate than a spotter

Follow-up request: "Mortar fire would still be impacted by drone recon. The drone makes the mortar much more deadly as it provides better recon than a spotter ever can." Correctly identified a real gap: `recon_mode` (SPOTTER vs. DRONE_TEAM) already changed how the player finds and tracks the enemy, but never fed back into the mortar's own accuracy once it actually fired — a drone-directed shot and a spotter-directed shot at an identical target landed with identical odds.

Researched the actual real-world effect: multiple accounts from the Russo-Ukrainian war put drone-directed artillery/mortar accuracy improvements at roughly 200-250% over ground-observer-adjusted fire, with a dramatically shorter sensor-to-shooter cycle (one account: 3-5 minutes with UAV cueing versus roughly 30 minutes without) — a drone operator watches where the round actually lands and corrects the next one immediately, where a ground observer needs line of sight to both the target AND the impact point from one fixed spot, then relays corrections by voice. (An earlier research pass in this same round had leaned on current Ukraine casualty-cause statistics — drones now the majority cause of casualties there — before the user corrected that framing: those figures are dominated by FPV/loitering-munition STRIKE drones directly killing people, which has nothing to do with this game's drone, a pure reconnaissance asset. "Right now, we are in an era where the sources of fire are infantry and artillery. Drones are for recon." That distinction was dropped from the reasoning entirely — the actual accuracy-improvement figures used below are about recon quality feeding indirect fire, not strike drones.)

New `GameConfig.DRONE_DIRECTED_MORTAR_ACCURACY_MULTIPLIER` (1.75, set conservatively within the cited 200-250% range rather than at the high end, so even the single most favorable case — a fully exposed target in the open — isn't a mathematical certainty) and `BattleManager._drone_directing_mortar_fire(attacker)`: true only for the PLAYER's own mortar (the enemy has no doctrine-level recon choice to begin with) firing while `recon_mode == DRONE_TEAM` AND a friendly drone — active or backup, either one being airborne means the team's ISR feed is live — is actually up right now, checked fresh at shot RESOLUTION (impact, ~40 tactical seconds after firing), the same moment the target's own moving/terrain state is already read, not frozen at the instant the round left the tube. Threaded through as a new `drone_directed` parameter on `CombatResolver.resolve_fire`, applied as a flat multiplier stacked on top of everything else (cover, moving-target predictability) — cover still does its full job regardless; this is a targeting-accuracy bonus, not a way to see through a roof.

**Verified:** a 6-case direct test confirmed the gating logic (never active in SPOTTER mode; never active in DRONE_TEAM mode with nothing airborne; active with either an active or a backup drone alone; never for the enemy's own mortar; never for a non-mortar attacker), plus a statistical check (4000 trials each) measuring plain vs. drone-directed mortar fire in the open at 0.529 (expected 0.520) and 0.915 (expected 0.910) respectively — a clear, substantial (>1.5x) jump matching the formula exactly. A 10-trial full-battle stress run (mixed recon modes) had no crashes, with the player's own mortar hit-rate-per-shot noisy at this sample size (10-35 shots per trial) but directionally higher in DRONE_TEAM trials than SPOTTER ones, as expected.

### 2026-09-07 — v79: the AAR report stops duplicating itself into the sidebar

Bug report (with a screenshot): "The right side of the window needs some visual cleanup." The sidebar showed the CasualtyDashboard's rows running straight into a giant, unformatted dump of the exact same after-action report already shown properly in its own dedicated overlay (`main.gd._on_battle_ended`'s `report_background`/`report_scroll` — a wide, scrollable, nicely-margined box) — `BattleManager._end_battle()` was calling BOTH `combat_log.log_battle_end(report_text)` (cramming the whole multi-line report, "=== AFTER-ACTION REPORT ===" divider and all, into the narrow ~300px combat log as one giant entry with no space to breathe) AND `battle_ended.emit(report_text)` (which is what actually drives the proper overlay). The sidebar dump was pure redundancy — once the battle ends, `combat_log` stays on screen next to the far larger, better-formatted overlay showing the identical text, serving no purpose beyond cluttering the one part of the screen the player's eye would otherwise land on first.

Removed the `combat_log.log_battle_end(report_text)` call from `_end_battle()` entirely, and deleted `CombatLog.log_battle_end()` itself since that was its only caller. The overlay report (built from the same `battle_ended` signal payload, untouched) is unaffected — it was always the properly-formatted presentation; this only removes the redundant, badly-cramped second copy.

**Verified:** a direct test ran a full battle to completion and confirmed the `battle_ended` signal still carries the complete report text (unaffected), while scanning every remaining combat log entry confirmed none of them contain the AAR report anymore.

Follow-up in the same round: "We also don't want the sidebar log to visually overlap the casualties section." A real, separate layout bug from the AAR duplication above — same recurring class of bug as v73/v75 (CasualtyDashboard's declared height falling behind its actual content as rows grew across the session). A headless measurement with every row at its true worst case — both mortar rows showing the full rounds+resupply-suffix text, and critically the DRONE_TEAM fleet row with EVERY status component populated at once ("1 airborne ..., 1 backup ..., N inbound, N ready ..., N swapping battery, N spare batteries ..., N lost" — a real state normal play can reach, not a contrived one) — measured 478px of real height against the panel's declared 400px minimum: a 78px overlap into `CombatLog` sitting right below it at a fixed y. The single-line assumption baked into the original 400px sizing had quietly gone stale as the mortar and drone rows both grew richer status text across the session, without anyone re-measuring since.

Resized `CasualtyDashboard.custom_minimum_size` to `(320, 500)` (real margin over the measured 478px worst case, matching this codebase's established practice of sizing to real measured content rather than a guess) and shifted the whole column below it down to match: `main.gd`'s `combat_log.position` from `(1020, 475)` to `(1020, 575)`, and `CombatLog.SIZE` from `(300, 220)` to `(300, 120)` — the fixed 700px window (retreat button 20-51, dashboard 65-565, combat log 575-695) leaves the combat log itself shorter than before, the direct tradeoff of the dashboard now legitimately needing more room; it still scrolls, just shows fewer lines at once without scrolling.

**Verified:** rebuilt the exact three-element column (retreat button, dashboard, combat log) at their real `main.gd` positions with the same worst-case content, and confirmed via direct measurement that the dashboard's bottom edge (565) sits above the combat log's top edge (575, a real 10px gap) and the combat log's own bottom edge (695) stays inside the fixed 700px window with margin to spare — no overlap anywhere in the column, even at the worst case that had been missed before.

### 2026-09-07 — v80: a hit mortar crew doesn't automatically abandon the gun, and ammo can cook off

Request: "Research the actual behavior of a Ukrainian mortar team after it is hit. Right now I have them abandoning the gun and fleeing. But is that actually the response? Does it depend on the general situation in the area?" Followed mid-round by: "It might also depend on whether the mortar's own ammo cooks off. That can be included in the game and displayed to the user."

Researched real crew-served-weapon doctrine rather than assuming the existing behavior was right. US infantry battle drills treat "man the crew-served weapon first, evacuate the wounded second" as standard practice — a casualty prompts cross-leveling the remaining crew to keep the weapon firing, not automatic abandonment. FM 7-90 cites a Korean War-era high-survivability mortar position design explicitly built to let a squad "continue to fight, even during intense enemy countermortar fire." Modern Ukraine reporting on mortar/artillery crews centers on pre-emptive shoot-and-scoot survivability (displacing fast enough to avoid counter-battery in the first place) rather than any doctrine of fleeing after every hit — and separately confirms ammunition cook-off (a round already primed by heat/blast setting off adjacent stacked rounds) as a real, if situational, hazard once a position is actually hit. `Unit._apply_crew_casualties` (both mortar AND drone-team crews previously) had every survivable hit trigger an unconditional `order_retreat` — the literal opposite of the doctrine found.

**Redesigned as a genuine risk-weighted decision for MORTAR crews specifically** (a drone-team crew has no equivalent "someone else takes over" option — there's no weapon to cross-level, just a ground-control link — so it keeps the old unconditional-retreat behavior unchanged). New `Unit._mortar_crew_holds_position(known_enemy_positions)`: `hold_chance = remaining_crew_fraction * (1.0 - overrun_risk)`, where `overrun_risk` ramps from 0 (nothing known nearby) to 1.0 as a known enemy position closes to within the new `GameConfig.MORTAR_CREW_OVERRUN_DANGER_RANGE` (600m, mirroring the existing `WOUNDED_ABANDON_DANGER_RANGE` scale). This directly answers "does it depend on the situation": a full-strength crew hit by long-range counter-battery fire with nothing threatening it on the ground holds and keeps fighting almost every time; the same crew with an enemy squad closing to overrun range flees almost regardless of how many hands are still on the tube — the crew's own physical safety from being overrun outweighs the tactical value of one more round downrange, exactly the situational dependence asked about. A crew that chooses to hold is marked `evading_counter_battery` either way, so its next relocation (whatever triggers it — the next shot's shoot-and-scoot, the out-of-ammo safety check, or simply being spotted while idle) is the more urgent version, the same way an actual counter-battery near-miss already behaves.

**Ammo cook-off**, per the follow-up: new `Unit._roll_mortar_ammo_cookoff()`, rolled on every survivable mortar hit. No source gave a citable probability for a field mortar position specifically — `GameConfig.MORTAR_AMMO_COOKOFF_MAX_CHANCE` (0.35) is a documented judgment call, not a cited figure — but the qualitative logic is solid: chance scales linearly with how many rounds are actually stacked at the gun right now (`mortar_rounds_remaining / MORTAR_STARTING_AMMO`), so a mortar that's already fired off its load or hasn't been resupplied yet has nothing left to cook off, literally zero chance, while a freshly-resupplied full load is at its most dangerous to its own crew. A cook-off destroys whatever rounds were left (there's no stockpile left to protect), can knock down additional survivors in its own right (a violent secondary blast at the position is a real danger to whoever's still standing there), and always forces the crew out regardless of how the ordinary hold-or-flee roll would have gone — nobody stays next to their own exploding ammunition. Per "displayed to the user": narrated via a new `log_mortar_ammo_cookoff` entry for either side unconditionally (not gated behind the enemy-logistics fog-of-war rule from the v75 follow-up — a secondary explosion is an externally observable event, not private ammo-count/resupply-timing data), using the same one-shot poll-and-clear flag pattern (`ammo_cooked_off`) `bolted_for_cover` already established, checked in `BattleManager._log_hit_consequence` ahead of the destroyed/abandoned checks so a cook-off that also finishes off the crew narrates as cause then effect.

**Verified:** a 7-case direct test suite confirmed the hold-chance formula at three points (full crew/no threat ≈0.75 hold rate — note: this specific case starts from 3/4 crew remaining, not literally untouched — half crew/no threat ≈0.5, full crew/threat at distance zero ≈0.0), the cook-off chance scaling linearly with ammo (0/20/10 rounds → 0.0/0.35/0.175 measured against expected), that a cook-off reliably occurs at full ammo across enough trials and always forces the crew out (retreating or destroyed, never left holding position), that a drone-team crew still always retreats after any survivable hit, and that a cook-off is correctly narrated with its one-shot flag cleared afterward. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes, with both new mechanics genuinely engaging in ordinary play — cook-offs occurred in roughly half the trials, and "abandon the gun" log entries (now conditional rather than automatic) still occurred in most of them.

### 2026-09-07 — v81: the enemy's own status is only as good as what's actually been scouted

Request: "During the battle, we should not know the enemy status except to the extent it has been scouted. We can have estimates. And drone scouting may prove certain things." Followed mid-round by: "Even after the battle, this remains true. But if we hold the position, drones can greatly help with the assessment, and this can be included in the AAR. The game does not need to show that portion. Just include it in the AAR."

The live `CasualtyDashboard` and the end-of-battle AAR were both fully omniscient about the enemy side — `Enemy: 28/62 personnel lost (45%)` and the AAR's detailed killed/wounded/captured breakdown and named destroyed/withdrawn lists all read the enemy's TRUE internal state directly, the same data the enemy's own AI uses, regardless of what the player's own side had actually witnessed. The player's own side stays exactly this way (a commander always knows their own losses) — this is specifically about the enemy.

**New per-unit knowledge snapshot** (`Unit.player_has_been_sighted`/`player_known_pips`/`player_known_state`, populated by a new `BattleManager._update_player_intel()` run every tick): whenever an enemy unit is `is_visible`, its current pips and state are snapshotted; the snapshot then simply stays wherever it last was once contact is lost, exactly like a real commander's last report doesn't un-happen when the observer loses sight of something. A unit never sighted at all defaults to "assumed still active and undamaged" for aggregate-total purposes (it still counts toward the known order of battle) but contributes zero confirmed losses — crediting a kill nobody actually witnessed would be worse than just not knowing.

**`BattleManager._compute_side_stats` gained an `estimated` parameter**, doing the same math as before but reading `player_known_*` instead of the live truth, skipping the detailed killed/heavily-wounded/walking-wounded/captured breakdown entirely (battlefield observation alone can support "this unit was destroyed or pulled back," not the exact mix of who died vs. was carried off — that needs an actual look at the aftermath), and only listing a unit in the destroyed/withdrawn/retreating/surrendered name lists if it's actually been sighted in that state. `casualty_stats(team)` (the live dashboard's own read) now always returns the true figures for the player's own side and the estimate for the enemy's — the dashboard code itself didn't need to change at all. The dashboard's enemy aggregate line gets a "~" prefix when estimated, and enemy mortar rows use the same known-state snapshot: never sighted reads as a genuine "status unknown" (a dedicated gray status-bar color, distinct from every real state color) rather than assuming "in action"; sighted-and-active-but-damaged reads "in action (N/4, last seen)" — reachable now that a wounded mortar crew can hold position instead of automatically fleeing (v80) — and DESTROYED drops the crew-casualty count entirely since it's always the full crew by definition, not worth repeating in numbers.

**The AAR gets an accuracy upgrade under two conditions**, per the follow-up: holding the position (the existing `held` check already used for the verdict) lets a real commander walk the battlefield afterward for a genuine post-battle assessment — bodies, abandoned equipment, prisoners — regardless of recon mode; OR using DRONE_TEAM recon, which never needed to hold any ground in the first place to get one last, unobstructed overhead look before the fight ends. Only when NEITHER applies (pulled back, spotter-only) does the AAR fall back to the exact same fog-of-war estimate the live dashboard showed throughout — "the position wasn't held for a battlefield assessment," stated plainly in the report text rather than silently printing a number that looks authoritative but isn't. The VERDICT itself (SUCCESSFUL DEFENSE/PYRRHIC DEFENSE/TACTICAL WITHDRAWAL/DEFEAT) is always judged on the true enemy toll regardless of what gets displayed — win/loss scoring has to be fair even when the report shown to the player is deliberately incomplete, the same principle already applied to the enemy AI playing with full knowledge of its own mortar ammo the player never sees (v75).

**Verified:** a 7-case direct test suite confirmed an unsighted enemy unit is assumed undamaged but still counted in the known total, that a sighted-then-lost unit's estimate freezes at its last confirmed snapshot even as its true state keeps changing, that the AAR gets the true enemy toll when the position is held, that it ALSO gets the true toll under DRONE_TEAM recon even without holding, that it falls back to the honest estimate only when neither applies, and that the dashboard's enemy mortar row correctly reads "status unknown" before any sighting and "confirmed destroyed" after one. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes, with the live estimate consistently under-reading the true enemy casualty percentage as expected — and, unprompted, DRONE_TEAM trials tracked much closer to the true figure (e.g. 39% estimated vs. 39% true, 40 vs. 40, 19 vs. 19) than SPOTTER trials (e.g. 34 vs. 44, 6 vs. 13) — "drone scouting may prove certain things" falling directly out of the drone's existing, already-superior detection coverage rather than needing any bespoke certainty mechanic of its own.

### 2026-09-07 — v81 follow-up: holding the ground only recovers what was actually left there

Bug report: "The AAR was suspiciously specific about numbers of enemy casualties and severity of wounds. We would have some idea. Try to figure out how much we would know in real life." Followed by: "Especially if we hold the ground, we are likely to be able to learn what they have left behind on the battlefield" — and, when an initial pass leaned partly on assumption rather than checking further: "take your research above my guesses."

The v81 "confirmed" AAR tier (held the position, or drone recon) had been treating the WHOLE enemy side as fully knowable once either condition was met — exact killed/heavily-wounded/walking-wounded/captured breakdowns for every unit, including ones that had successfully pulled out. Researched actual battle-damage-assessment practice: even a body count taken on ground a force has physically seized is understood as an UNDERCOUNT of true enemy dead, specifically because a retreating force removes its own fallen whenever it gets the chance to — the casualties genuinely left behind to examine are the ones from a unit that had no chance to save anyone, i.e. one actually destroyed where it stood (or one already in custody, having surrendered). A unit that got away — WITHDRAWN, or still RETREATING when the fight ended — took its dead, its wounded, and everyone still standing with it; holding the ground it fought over doesn't put any of that back for inspection.

`_compute_side_stats` now makes this call PER UNIT instead of once for the whole side: a `WITHDRAWN`/`RETREATING` unit is `unrecoverable`, and gets the SAME last-known-snapshot treatment the live dashboard's fog-of-war estimate already uses (Unit.player_known_pips/_known_state), no matter how the overall call was made — even under a "confirmed" read. A `DESTROYED` or `SURRENDERED` unit is the opposite case: fully assessed from the TRUE figures, and — this is the part the research corrected rather than an assumption — REGARDLESS of whether that specific unit was ever actually watched being destroyed. Holding the ground (or a drone's last look) lets a commander walk over and find remains/prisoners they never personally witnessed falling; it just can't recover what a unit that got away took with it. The detailed killed/wounded breakdown is likewise only ever credited from unrecoverable-vs-not on a per-unit basis, so a "confirmed" AAR in a battle where most of the enemy got away now correctly reports much less detail than one where the enemy was decisively destroyed in place — no longer suspiciously precise regardless of how the fight actually went.

**Verified:** a 4-case direct test suite confirmed a destroyed-in-place unit is fully assessed even when never directly observed happening (the specific case the research corrected), that a withdrawn unit's assessment stays capped at its last actual observation even under an overall "confirmed" read (and appears under "still retreating," its last KNOWN state, not "withdrawn," its true one), that a withdrawn unit never observed at all contributes nothing and appears in no category, and that a still-retreating unit's detailed killed/wounded breakdown is correctly withheld even though its aggregate loss is still credited from the last snapshot. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes, position held in every trial) had no crashes and confirmed the safety invariant that the new, more realistic figure never exceeds the old, fully-omniscient one.

### 2026-09-07 — v82: one commander's standing order, not three separately negotiated ones

Request: "Research what decisions a Ukrainian commander might have reasonably made both before and during the battle. NOT what they were allowed to do... what they would typically decide themselves, as opposed to leaving to subordinates." Framed explicitly around mission command's actual division of labor — commander's intent set centrally, execution decentralized — not a formal chain-of-command permissions question.

Researched Ukraine's own documented adoption of mission command (centralized planning, decentralized execution, junior leaders acting on understood intent without asking permission) and, more specifically, US infantry doctrine on who actually decides a withdrawal: FM 3-21.8 states a platoon "never withdraws except upon the verified order of higher authority," and a related tactics analysis is blunt that withdrawal timing "belongs to senior commanders, not frontline troops" — a unit doesn't decide for itself, from its own casualties alone, that it's time to pull back. Checked every other player-facing decision in the game against this same lens (deployment positions, mortar shoot-and-scoot, resupply request timing, target selection, drone search priorities) and found them already well-placed: sector/position assignment and fire-support employment are exactly the kind of thing a defensive OPORD sets at the commander's level, while target picking, ammo conservation, resupply timing, and drone search priority are exactly the "how" that mission command leaves to the subordinate actually holding the weapon or flying the sensor. Only one control didn't match: `DoctrinePanel` had the player individually dial in a separate retreat-casualty threshold for EACH of the three squads, as if each one had privately negotiated its own breaking point with command — the opposite of one coherent, commonly-understood intent.

Replaced the three per-squad sliders with a single "standing order, whole force" threshold, applied identically to every squad when the doctrine dict is built (`main.gd._on_start_pressed`). This isn't merely simpler UI — it's a real correctness fix for what a company/platoon commander's defensive order actually looks like: ONE stated intent ("hold as long as you can" vs. "pull back early to preserve the force"), given up front so nobody has to get a radio call through mid-fight to do something already authorized, not three individually bargained percentages. The existing General Retreat button is untouched — it's the same authority's live override on top of the standing order, exactly matching a commander issuing a further, later order when the situation changes.

**Verified:** a direct test confirmed the panel still builds correctly, `get_retreat_threshold()` returns the slider's default (0.30), and building the doctrine dict the same way `main.gd` does applies that exact single value to every squad rather than three independent ones. No battle-simulation code changed (`Unit.retreat_threshold` is consumed identically regardless of where the value came from), so no stress run was needed beyond the existing regression coverage.

### 2026-09-07 — v81/v82 follow-up: drone alone doesn't recover a battlefield you gave up, and a firing mortar isn't "unknown"

Two bug reports. First: "The AAR was showing exact and specific numbers of different types of enemy casualties, even though we withdrew. I am skeptical that our info would be that good." Traced to v81's own AAR condition — `held or recon_mode == DRONE_TEAM` — which let DRONE_TEAM recon alone unlock the fully true enemy toll even after a withdrawal. That was an overreach past what the earlier "drone scouting may prove certain things" framing actually supported: a drone is a live sensor that makes the DURING-battle scouting picture better (already fully modeled — see the stress-test evidence in the original v81 entry that drone trials track much closer to true values purely from better live coverage), but it isn't a way to walk a battlefield the side has just abandoned. Once the ground is given up, a drone goes home with whatever it already scouted, exactly like a spotter does. Tightened the condition to `held` alone — only physically holding the ground (bodies, equipment, prisoners actually found) unlocks the true figures; any withdrawal, regardless of recon mode, now correctly falls back to the same live fog-of-war estimate the dashboard showed throughout.

Second: "if an enemy mortar is firing, its status is not 'unknown'." Correct — the dashboard's enemy mortar row only checked `player_has_been_sighted` (a visual confirmation), ignoring the muzzle-flash/trajectory fire-detection mechanism (`_last_detected_mortar_fire`) the enemy's OWN counter-battery chase already relies on to find the friendly mortar without ever seeing it. A mortar that's actively shooting at you is obviously "in action" whether or not anyone's laid eyes on it. Added `BattleManager.mortar_recently_detected_firing`/`mortar_minutes_since_detected_firing` (public accessors mirroring `mortar_resupply_status`'s own pattern) and wired them into `_enemy_mortar_status_text`: never-sighted-but-recently-firing now reads "in action (detected firing ~Nm ago)" with the ACTIVE status color, rather than "status unknown" in the neutral gray — still with no casualty count attached, since firing alone reveals nothing about remaining strength.

**Verified:** a 4-case direct test suite confirmed DRONE_TEAM recon alone no longer unlocks the true AAR toll without holding, that holding still does, that a never-sighted-but-recently-detected-firing mortar reads "in action" with a real time-ago figure, and that a mortar with neither a sighting nor a fire detection still correctly reads "status unknown." A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes, with the fire-detection case genuinely engaging in nearly every trial (a common, real intermediate state — an enemy mortar shooting before it's ever been visually confirmed).

### 2026-09-07 — v81/v82 follow-up 2: holding the ground doesn't recover an enemy's evacuated wounded either

"Holding the ground might or might not unlock everything. Enemy wounded might still be evacuated and we would not know their status." Then, mid-round: "We might not be able to distinguish enemy walking wounded who got away from unwounded enemies."

The previous follow-up already drew the "left behind vs. got away" line at the UNIT level (a DESTROYED/SURRENDERED unit is fully assessable; a WITHDRAWN/RETREATING one isn't) — but even for a unit that's genuinely wiped out where it stood, its casualties accumulated hit by hit over the course of the fight, not all at once. Its WALKING wounded — ambulatory almost by definition — are exactly the casualties a losing side can, and does, evacuate under their own power at any point during that fight, right up until the position is finally overrun; unlike the dead or the immobile heavily wounded, there's no guarantee they were still there when the position actually fell. So "holding the ground" doesn't mean everything about even a confirmed-destroyed unit is knowable — specifically, its walking wounded's fate isn't.

For a fully DESTROYED unit specifically, the math is exact rather than a further guess: `killed + heavily_wounded + walking_wounded` fully accounts for `max_pips` (nobody escaped unwounded, or the unit wouldn't be DESTROYED) — so "how many are unaccounted for once bodies and immobile wounded are tallied" is a real, computable figure, just one that can't be asserted as specifically "walking wounded found and confirmed" the way the killed and heavily-wounded counts can be.

`_compute_side_stats` gained an `is_enemy_side` parameter: for the enemy (never the player's own side, always fully known) with a recoverable unit, `walking_wounded_count` is no longer credited to the `walking_wounded` field at all — it's tallied separately as `evacuated_unknown`, still a real, counted personnel loss (folded into `pips_lost` and the aggregate total exactly as before), just not asserted as a confirmed find. The AAR's enemy casualty line drops "walking wounded" from its wording entirely and reports "N more unaccounted for (likely evacuated wounded...)" instead; the per-unit destroyed-mortar/drone-team name-list entries do the same ("...unaccounted for" instead of "...walking wounded"). The player's own casualty line is completely untouched — a commander knows exactly what happened to their own wounded.

**Verified:** a 3-case direct test suite confirmed an enemy unit's walking wounded move from the `walking_wounded` field into `evacuated_unknown` (with killed, heavily wounded, and the aggregate total all still fully confirmed) even under a "held" read, that the player's own side is completely unaffected by this enemy-only nuance, and that the actual AAR text uses the honest "unaccounted for" wording rather than asserting a walking-wounded figure for the enemy. A 10-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes and confirmed the breakdown never exceeds the aggregate total (it can legitimately fall short, once units that got away are mixed in, but never overclaim).

### 2026-09-07 — v81/v82 follow-up 3: a real bug — the "got away" rule was silently eating the PLAYER's own casualties too

Bug report: "It is saying we had 3 casualties in our mortar crew but 0/34 personnel lost. That isn't possible." Correct — a real regression, not a display nuance. `_compute_side_stats`'s `unrecoverable` check (`u.state == WITHDRAWN or RETREATING`, added in the previous follow-up) had no gate on which SIDE was being computed — it fired for a RETREATING/WITHDRAWN unit regardless of team. For a player unit in that state, this set `unit_estimated = true`, and since `Unit.player_has_been_sighted` is ONLY ever populated for `enemy_units` (see `_update_player_intel`), it reads `false` by default for every player unit too — tripping the `if unit_estimated and not player_has_been_sighted: continue` early-exit and silently dropping that unit from `pips_lost` entirely. A mortar crew that took 3 casualties and retreated got logged in the combat log (a real, separate code path) but vanished from the aggregate tally — exactly the reported symptom.

Fixed by gating `unrecoverable` on a new prerequisite: `is_enemy_side and (state == WITHDRAWN or RETREATING)`. The player's own `_compute_side_stats(player_units)` call never passes `is_enemy_side`, so it now unconditionally gets the true figures regardless of unit state, exactly as intended — a friendly unit that retreats never becomes a mystery to its own commander. Auditing every other call site turned up a SECOND, related bug this same gate fixed as a side effect: `_end_battle`'s `true_enemy_stats` (used for the win/loss verdict and exchange-ratio scoring) had been computed with `is_enemy_side = true` in the previous follow-up, meaning the fog-of-war/evacuation logic was silently leaking into the SCORING path too — a battle could score as a loss or a wash when the enemy was actually crushed, just because the crushed units happened to be ones the player never got a good look at. Fixed by keeping `true_enemy_stats` as a plain, unqualified `_compute_side_stats(enemy_units)` call (pure ground truth, matching how `_enemy_situation_hopeless`'s own internal AI check was already correctly written) and computing the DISPLAYED `enemy_stats` as a fresh, separate call (`_compute_side_stats(enemy_units, not held, true)`) instead of reusing `true_enemy_stats` — scoring and display are now fully decoupled, the way the design always intended but the code didn't actually enforce.

**Verified:** a 4-case direct test suite reproduced the exact reported bug (a retreating player mortar with 3 casualties, never touched by `_update_player_intel` since that only ever runs on `enemy_units`) and confirmed it's now counted correctly, confirmed a withdrawn player squad is likewise counted, confirmed the enemy's own internal `_enemy_situation_hopeless` AI check still reads true ground truth regardless of what the player has scouted, and confirmed the AAR's verdict is scored on the true exchange ratio (8 enemy lost to 1 player lost, scoring SUCCESSFUL DEFENSE) even while the DISPLAYED enemy figure correctly stays fog-of-war-limited (0/9 — a retreating, never-sighted unit) in the very same report. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes and confirmed `casualty_stats(PLAYER).pips_lost` exactly reconciles with the sum of every individual player unit's own losses in every single trial — the invariant the original bug violated.

### 2026-09-07 — v81/v82 follow-up 4: a held-position AAR can be a genuine mix of confirmed and estimated, not one blended number

"It still said 'enemy casualties 32/62'. That number has got to be an estimate. You can separate out estimated from confirmed info." Correct: holding the position unlocks the true figures for units actually left behind (destroyed/surrendered), but the previous follow-up's own per-unit "got away" rule means a unit that withdrew or was still retreating when the battle ended STILL falls back to its last-known snapshot even under an overall "held" call. The single boolean `enemy_stats.estimated` the AAR text branched on couldn't see that distinction — it only reflected whether the OVERALL call was the confirmed one, so a report built from a genuine mix (some units destroyed and fully assessed, others gone with everything they had) printed as one falsely-precise number with no hint that part of it was still just a scouting-based guess.

`_compute_side_stats` now tracks `confirmed_pips_lost` and `unconfirmed_pips_lost` as two running totals that always sum to `pips_lost` — each unit's own contribution lands in whichever bucket matches THAT unit's own `unit_estimated` verdict, not the call's overall flag. `_end_battle`'s report text now branches three ways instead of two: nothing confirmed at all (the existing pure-estimate wording, unchanged), everything confirmed (the existing "confirmed on the battlefield" wording, now labeled as such explicitly), and — new — a genuine mix, which gets three separate lines: what's confirmed on the battlefield (with its own killed/heavily-wounded/captured/unaccounted-for breakdown), a distinct "plus an estimated N more among units that got away" line, and an explicit "overall" total combining both, so the reader can see exactly how much of the final number is solid ground and how much is still a guess.

**Verified:** a 3-case direct test suite confirmed a fully-destroyed enemy force reads as fully confirmed with no estimate caveat, that not holding the position still gives the pure-estimate wording unchanged, and that a genuine mix (one unit destroyed in place, one that got away after being observed taking partial losses) produces the three-line breakdown with the confirmed and estimated figures reported separately and the overall total correctly combining both. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) had no crashes and confirmed `confirmed_pips_lost + unconfirmed_pips_lost` exactly equals `pips_lost` in every trial, with the genuine-mix case (both greater than zero) appearing in most of them — this is the common case in ordinary play, not a rare edge case.

Immediate follow-up: "The AAR line 'Enemy casualties overall' can say that it is estimated." The mixed case's third line combined a confirmed figure with an estimated one into a single total but never actually said so — reading, by itself, exactly like the fully-confirmed case's authoritative number. Changed to `"Enemy casualties overall (partly estimated): ~%d/%d personnel (~%.0f%%)"` — both the label and the "~" prefixes now make clear this combined figure isn't a hard count, matching the "~" convention the pure-estimate case already used. Verified with a direct test confirming the mixed-case output.

### 2026-09-07 — small correction: "1 rounds" is never correct

"Mortar ammo should never read '1 rounds'. In that particular case, it is '1 round'." A plain `"%d rounds" % n` format string doesn't pluralize, so a player mortar's dashboard line read "in action (1 rounds)" once it was down to its last round. Added `GameConfig.round_count_text(n)` — a one-line helper returning `"1 round"` or `"N rounds"` as appropriate — and used it everywhere a mortar's round count reaches the player, not just the one place the bug was noticed: `CasualtyDashboard._mortar_status_text`'s player ACTIVE-state line, and `CombatLog.log_mortar_resupply_arrived`/`log_mortar_resupply_collected` (both currently always show a multiple of `GameConfig.MORTAR_RESUPPLY_ROUNDS` = 20 so can't actually hit "1" under today's balance, but would silently reintroduce the same bug if that constant ever changed).

**Verified:** a direct test confirmed `round_count_text` returns the singular form only for exactly 1 and the plural form for 0, 2, and 20, and that `_mortar_status_text` for a mortar with 1 round on hand no longer contains "1 rounds" anywhere in its output.

### 2026-09-07 — v83: a west flank — new ground, a first-ever camera, enemy flanking, deeper retreat, and resupply that can be denied

"Let's add a region to the left of the current map. Nobody should be set up there. But units can enter that area. If it becomes relevant to the battle, the displayed map should scroll so we see where everything is." Follow-up, once asked what should actually send units there and how large: "Both. Also the mortar might pull back there if it seems sensible. Furthermore, consider whether resupply should go to the mortar itself, or to a drop-off point as it does currently. Act on your research. Yes it's a significant change." — "1500m wide." Then, mid-design, after a first pass modeled resupply as instant/invulnerable: "If the mortar resupply goes onto the map, they should be vulnerable to getting shot at. That in turn might cause the mortar to fall back to meet them. Again, research." — corrected twice more when the research leaned on modern (2024+) Ukrainian FPV-drone reporting: "No, these are scouting drones only. Not attacking drones" and "Remember we are in an early part of the war." This game's drones are recon-only and the setting predates FPV/strike-drone logistics interdiction — the vulnerability had to come from conventional recon-and-mortar/infantry fire, not a drone-strike mechanic.

This landed as five interlocking changes, planned together (a Plan-mode design pass, verified against the actual codebase before any code was written) and built in dependency order:

**1. World extension.** `GameConfig.WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_M = 1500.0` (300px at the map's fixed 0.2 px/m scale) of open, undeveloped ground west of x=0 — no new hills/forest/buildings, matching "nobody set up there." Checked every terrain/LOS function against negative x: `elevation_m`, `get_terrain_type_at`, `has_direct_los` all needed no changes (pure geometry, or a default-OPEN fallthrough already in place); `nearest_hidden_point`'s ring-sampling fallback has no bounds check at all, so an urgent, threatened mortar near today's west edge can *already* land a hidden-point candidate out there — no new mortar-relocation code needed for that piece, it's an emergent consequence of the terrain functions being correct. One real fix: `_build_contour_cache`'s grid assumed an origin of x=0, so contour lines wouldn't have been drawn in the new region at all (a hard-edged void reading as a rendering bug the first time anyone panned there) — shifted the grid's origin to `-WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_M` and threaded the offset through `_marching_squares_cell`. One genuinely new (not a bug) discovery from testing: completely flat, undeveloped ground offers *no* LOS-blocking concealment at all in this engine's model (only buildings/elevation break a sightline) — so `nearest_hidden_point` legitimately finds nothing out there most of the time, exactly as it would in any other open stretch of the existing map. That's accurate terrain modeling, not a defect.

**2. The game's first-ever camera.** The map has never needed one — the whole 5000m map already fits inside the fixed 1000px display column at 0.2 px/m, so nothing was ever off-screen. Adding 1500m of new width at the same scale is the first time that stops being true (300px of new content, over a 1000px window). The map (`BattleManager`/`DeploymentScreen`) and the sidebar UI are both direct children of the same root `Node2D` with no isolation between them — a Camera2D on the root would drag the sidebar along with it. Fixed by wrapping only the map content in a `SubViewport` (behind a `SubViewportContainer` occupying the exact same screen rect the map always has) with its own `Camera2D`, leaving every sidebar/HUD node completely untouched. `Camera2D.limit_left/right/top/bottom` set to exactly the world bounds constrain `position.x` to `[200, 500]` (200 = fully reveals the west edge, 500 = today's original view) and pin `position.y` at 350 — the camera never needs to move vertically. A new `GameConfig.compute_camera_target_x(relevant_positions)` — pure geometry, stays at the default 500 unless something relevant has actually drifted west of x=0 — combined with `BattleManager._camera_relevant_positions()`, which enforces the same fog-of-war rule as everywhere else in this game: the player's own units always count regardless of visibility, but an enemy unit only counts once actually spotted, so the camera can never pan toward an unspotted enemy and leak its position for free. `main.gd._process()` lerps the camera toward that target every frame. One real bug caught only by actually running the game (not just compiling it): `Camera2D.current = true` set *before* the camera was added to the tree threw a script error in this Godot build and silently aborted the rest of `_ready()` — fixed by calling `make_current()` *after* `add_child`, the more robust API either way.

**3. Mortar resupply — direct delivery, but exposed.** Research (FM 7-90 ch. 9) confirms the doctrinal default is a supply run moving *forward* to the unit's actual position; a fixed rear collection point the crew has to travel to and from — this game's original design — is the fallback, used only when terrain or enemy activity prevents that. Redesigned: a wave's arrival (same log-normal delay/failure-chance timing as before, unchanged) now spawns a real `Unit.Kind.RESUPPLY_RUN` — small, unarmed, one-hit-fragile (`max_pips=1` reuses the exact same generic take-hit path a DRONE already goes through, no special-case branch needed) — at the resupply point, with its `move_target` refreshed to the mortar's *current* position every tick rather than a destination set once. Because it's a real member of `player_units`/`enemy_units`, it is spotted, targeted, and fired on by the exact same generic code every other unit already goes through (`Unit.is_targetable`, `_refresh_visibility`, `_pick_target`) — none of that needed a single change. A hit destroys it outright and the mortar gets nothing (`log_resupply_run_destroyed`); reaching the mortar delivers the rounds directly (`log_mortar_resupply_delivered`) and the run despawns. Symmetric for the enemy side, silently — meaning the player's own squads/mortar can spot and interdict an *enemy* resupply run too, for free, from the same generic code. The user's "mortar might fall back to meet them" idea is real doctrine (a designated linkup) and came almost free of the run's own live-tracking design: since the run always heads toward the mortar's current position, an idle mortar (not firing, not evading) more than `MORTAR_RESUPPLY_LINKUP_TRIGGER_RANGE` (1000m) from its own resupply point now closes some of that distance itself, both sides converging faster with zero extra coordination code. The old fetch-trip machinery (`_start_mortar_resupply_trip`, `_advance_mortar_resupply_trip`, `_safe_to_fetch_resupply`, `_update_mortar_resupply_fetch`, the `rounds_waiting` field) is fully removed, not just superseded.

**4. Deeper player retreat under real pressure.** `PLAYER_SAFE_X` stays the *ordinary* line, but a unit that's still within a live threat's danger range (`_still_under_pressure`, reusing the exact same threshold `_mortar_crew_holds_position` already applies) the instant it reaches that line doesn't stop — it escalates once (`Unit.retreat_extended`, a one-way flag) to a deeper line `PLAYER_EXTENDED_SAFE_X`, 100m short of the true world edge, and only then is marked WITHDRAWN. A unit that reaches the ordinary line with nothing chasing it still stops exactly where it always did — this only ever adds depth, never removes the old behavior. Enemy retreat (toward its own home edge, unrelated to this new ground) is completely untouched by construction.

**5. Enemy flanking through the new ground.** ~35% of enemy squads (`ENEMY_FLANK_CHANCE`, rolled once at spawn) are designated to swing wide through a waypoint 1000m into the west flank before pivoting to the normal mortar/village objective — basic fire-and-maneuver, not every element advancing on the same axis. This reuses 100% of the *existing* per-tick candidate-generation and scoring machinery (`_next_advance_point`/`_score_advance_candidate`/`_weighted_advance_point_pick`, all already objective-agnostic) — the only change was making `_enemy_advance_objective` take the asking unit and return its flank waypoint until it arrives, then fall through permanently. `_encirclement_pivot`'s own no-arg call (a shared per-batch reference point, not any one squad's real destination) is deliberately left untouched.

**Verified:** targeted unit tests per change (terrain-function sanity for x<0 including the contour-grid extension; `compute_camera_target_x`'s clamping and the fog-of-war-respecting position filter; the full resupply-run lifecycle — spawn, live tracking, delivery, interdiction-by-hit, abort-if-the-mortar's-gone, the dashboard's three real status states, and the linkup trigger/non-trigger; the retreat-depth escalation plus its no-pressure and enemy-untouched regression cases; the flanking waypoint routing and its fall-through). A full-battle stress run (both recon modes, all five changes together) confirmed no crashes/hangs and sampled that the new emergent behaviors actually occur in practice, not just in isolated unit tests — see the stress run's own printed statistics for the exact trial counts. Manual playtest still needed for anything genuinely visual/interactive (camera pan feel, deployment drag-and-drop through the new SubViewport, contour rendering at the x=0 seam) — flagged explicitly since there's no GUI automation available for this native desktop app in this environment.

### 2026-09-08 — v83 follow-up: no more fixed resupply point — a run enters from whatever edge is actually on screen

Two corrections to v83's resupply redesign, given together: "If there is no more supply point, it should not show on the map during setup. Also, the resupply unit should enter from the leftmost edge of whatever is currently visible."

v83 kept the deployment-phase "Resupply Point" token as a place the player still chose (now just the run's spawn origin, with no remaining mechanical effect on delay — a tradeoff the original entry already flagged as worth revisiting). Read together, both parts of this correction resolve that exact tension the same way: since the point no longer does anything the player can meaningfully decide, it shouldn't be presented as a decision at all. Removed entirely — `DeploymentScreen` no longer creates or draws a resupply token or zone, `UnitToken.is_resupply_point`/`setup_resupply_point` are gone (the square-marker drawing was only ever used for this one token), and the doctrine dict passed into `BattleManager.start_battle` no longer carries a `resupply_point` key at all.

In its place: a resupply run now enters wherever the map's own camera currently shows as the edge on that side — the west edge for the player, the east edge for the enemy — at the mortar's own current y, tracking `BattleManager.current_camera_x` (pushed down from `main.gd` every frame, since the actual `Camera2D` node lives there, not in `BattleManager`). This is more than a UI simplification: if the camera has panned to reveal the west flank because a mortar relocated out there, the run now visibly enters from THAT edge — genuinely off-screen — instead of reappearing back at the original x=0 boundary and crossing ground that's already on screen, which would have looked like it teleported in mid-view. The resupply-linkup idle behavior (a mortar closing distance toward an inbound run) now targets the run's own live position directly rather than a fixed point it used to reference — simpler than before, and one less thing tied to a place that no longer exists. `GameConfig.choose_enemy_resupply_point()`, `PLAYER_RESUPPLY_DEPLOYMENT_ZONE`, and `PLAYER_RESUPPLY_DEFAULT_POSITION` are all fully removed as a result, not just superseded.

**Verified:** a direct test confirmed the deployment screen now creates exactly 5 tokens (3 squads, mortar, spotter — no resupply token) and that `get_positions()` no longer returns a `resupply_point` key; confirmed the entry point tracks `current_camera_x` correctly at both the default (un-panned) view and fully panned west, always relative to the CURRENT edge rather than the original map boundary; confirmed the enemy's own entry point mirrors this on the east edge. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) after the removal had no crashes or hangs.

### 2026-09-08 — v83 follow-up 2: the west flank looked blank because it was blank

"The region to the left of the main map should have terrain similar to what the main map has. It seems to be blank." Correct, and by design at the time — v83 deliberately left the new region free of `HILLS`/`FOREST_PATCHES`/`TERRAIN_ZONES` on the theory that "nobody set up there" meant undeveloped ground. That reasoning conflated two different things: no deployment zones or buildings (still true) doesn't mean no natural terrain at all. The rest of the map isn't wall-to-wall development either — it's mostly open ground WITH scattered hills and woodland, and that's the texture the new region was missing, not "cover" specifically.

Added 2 hills (`Vector2(-600, 1200)` and `Vector2(-1050, 2650)`, 17-22m, matching the height/radius range of the existing 7) and 6 forest patches spread across the region (matching the ~150-200m radius range and roughly the same density as the rest of the map's 20+ patches), continuing naturally from the existing "far west rear woods" patch that already sat close to x=0. No new `TERRAIN_ZONES` (buildings/village) — that part of the original reasoning still holds; a building is a deployment-adjacent development choice, hills and woodland are just terrain, present everywhere else on the map including plenty of spots nobody deploys into either.

A genuine bonus, not something separately asked for: v83's own testing had found that the west flank offered literally no LOS-blocking concealment at all (`nearest_hidden_point` came up empty every time), an accepted limitation at the time since only buildings/elevation break a sightline in this engine's model and neither existed out there. Adding real hills fixes that as a side effect — a mortar relocating into the west flank now has genuine reverse-slope cover to find, not just distance from the fight.

**Verified:** a direct test confirmed a point at the new hill's center reads a real, substantial elevation (~24m, matching its authored 22m plus ambient terrain), a point at a new forest patch's center reads as `TREES` not `OPEN`, the contour-line grid now traces real rings near the new hill, and — the concealment fix — a mortar placed near the new hill and evading urgently now finds genuine reverse-slope concealment where before it found none. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) confirmed no regressions.

### 2026-09-08 — v83 follow-up 3: the camera shouldn't snap back on its own, and retreats shouldn't dash blindly through known ground

"Once the map scrolls to a side, it can stay there unless there is a need to scroll it back. Retreating units should go through areas where there are not likely to be enemy. They should not just go left."

**Camera.** `compute_camera_target_x` previously always re-centered on the westmost currently-relevant position, snapping straight back to the default view the instant nothing relevant remained near the west edge — a real annoyance once the west flank sees any real traffic (a flanking squad passing through, say): the view would yank back to center the moment that squad died or moved on, even if everything else was still perfectly visible right where the camera already was. Redesigned as hysteresis instead of continuous re-centering: the function now also takes the camera's own current x, and only proposes moving it at all when something relevant has actually drifted OUTSIDE the current view — off the west edge (need to reveal more) or off the east edge (panning west can hide part of the map's own east side at this fixed zoom, so a relevant unit can end up hidden there too) — and even then moves only as far as needed to bring it back into comfortable view, not all the way back to the default center. Everything that's already visible, staying visible, is reason enough to leave the camera exactly where it is.

**Retreat pathing.** `_step_retreat`'s final leg (the long straight dash toward the safe line, once the initial dive-for-cover hop is done) was a pure x-only line, blind to anything the retreating unit might already know is out there — harmless when the only possible threat was somewhere back toward the road it came from, a real problem now that an enemy squad can be flanking through the west flank, potentially sitting right on what would otherwise be a straight retreat line. Added a reactive lateral steer: every tick, if a known enemy is within the same danger-range threshold `_still_under_pressure` already uses (mortar crew vs. everyone else), the unit bends away from it — scaled up as the threat gets closer, capped well under full retreat speed so it's still always making real x-progress toward safety, never stalling to purely dodge. Re-evaluated every tick against whatever's currently known, not a route committed to once — it reacts immediately if a threat is freshly spotted mid-retreat, and relaxes immediately once nothing nearby is close enough to matter. Applies symmetrically to both sides, same as every other threat-aware routine in this file.

**Verified:** direct tests confirmed the camera stays exactly put with nothing relevant, correctly pans west/east only when something is genuinely forced out of the current view (not just "nearby"), and crucially does NOT snap back to center once a west-side threat is gone as long as everything relevant is still inside the current view. For retreat: a unit with no known threat still dashes on a pure x-only line (regression check), a unit with a known threat nearby steers toward higher y away from one positioned at lower y, and still makes real x-progress toward the safe line the whole time. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) confirmed no regressions.

### 2026-09-08 — v83 follow-up 4: the drone shouldn't linger over ground it already looked at

"Sometimes the drone spends a lot of time concentrating on an area and not finding any enemy there. It should not be searching where it knows the enemy isn't." Clarified: "areas it has recently scouted I mean" — not a permanent blacklist, since the enemy situation genuinely changes over time; specifically the drone shouldn't keep re-rolling back to ground it was just over minutes ago.

The blind-search sweep (`_drone_sweep_target`/`_weighted_random_sweep_index`) already had one "don't bother going back" mechanism — `_area_confirmed_clear`, a permanent penalty once a specific enemy unit is confirmed dead/withdrawn/surrendered near a waypoint — but nothing tracked the much more common case: the drone itself flew over a grid cell, saw nothing, and its next weighted-random re-roll could easily land right back on that same cell (or hop straight to it) purely by chance, especially since the road-band row is weighted 60% of all search effort and only has 5 columns to spread across. That's exactly "concentrating on an area and not finding anything" — not a bug in the weighting itself, just nothing discouraging an immediate repeat visit.

Added a second, TEMPORARY penalty alongside the existing permanent one: `BattleManager._drone_sweep_last_visited` records the scenario time the drone actually arrived at each grid waypoint (25 cells total); `_recently_visited_weight_multiplier` reduces that cell's selection weight to `DRONE_SWEEP_RECENTLY_VISITED_MIN_WEIGHT_MULTIPLIER` (0.2 — a smaller cut than the permanent 0.15 penalty, since "I looked and saw nothing" is weaker, non-permanent evidence than "someone confirmed died here") the instant it's left, then linearly ramps back to full weight over `DRONE_SWEEP_RECENTLY_VISITED_COOLDOWN_S` (20 minutes tactical time — a judgment call, long enough to actually spread the search out, short enough that a quiet area doesn't stay under-searched all battle). The two penalties multiply together where both apply, same as every other multi-factor weighting already in this file.

**Verified:** direct tests confirmed the multiplier is 1.0 for a never-visited cell, exactly the minimum the instant a cell is left, back to 1.0 once the full cooldown has passed, and linearly interpolated at the halfway point; a 3000-trial statistical test confirmed a recently-visited cell's actual pick rate dropped from an 11.6% baseline to 2.5% — a real, meaningful reduction, not a token nudge; and a direct test confirmed `_drone_sweep_target` actually records the visit the instant the drone arrives. A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) confirmed no regressions.

### 2026-09-08 — v83 follow-up 5: the drone was crossing back and forth over the mortar's own blind-side checks, not the broad sweep

"Still seeing the drone going back and forth over an area that is devoid of enemies. Just keeps going back and forth, reversing course multiple times per game second." The previous follow-up fixed the broad grid sweep; this turned out to be a completely different mechanism — `_drone_flank_watch_target`, the drone's ongoing check of the friendly mortar's unscreened compass bearings, which doesn't require any enemy nearby at all (it's a preventive check, not a reaction to a spotted contact) and matches "an area devoid of enemies" exactly.

Built a headless diagnostic that drove `_drone_search_target` over thousands of simulated ticks with an active friendly mortar and zero enemy units, logging every time the returned target changed and whether the new direction reversed the previous one. It confirmed flank-watch was the actual source, and the root cause: picking the next bearing to check via `qualifying[randi() % qualifying.size()]` — a pure random choice among every currently-unscreened compass bearing around the mortar, with no regard for where the drone already was. A random pick could (and reproducibly did) send the drone clear across the mortar to a nearly opposite bearing right after finishing one, then back again shortly after — the "keeps going back and forth" the report described. The function was already explicitly built to be "sticky" (never re-picks mid-flight, only on arrival or disqualification) — that protection just didn't cover what happens at the moment of the NEXT pick.

First attempt (picking whichever qualifying bearing is nearest the drone's current position instead of a random one) fixed the wild long-distance crossings but introduced a worse, more mechanical failure: once only two bearings remain open — a completely ordinary situation as a battle progresses and squads increasingly screen more of the compass — "nearest, excluding the one just finished" has exactly one possible answer each time, so the drone locks into a perfect, perpetual two-point ping-pong forever. Diagnosed via the same tool before it ever reached a live battle.

The actual fix: track which of the 8 fixed compass bearings the drone is currently checking, and always advance to the NEXT one in a fixed rotational order that currently qualifies, wrapping around — mirroring `_drone_vicinity_search_point`'s own established step-and-wrap pattern for circling a spotted squad, rather than either a random or a purely-local choice. This is what an actual methodical compass sweep looks like: every open bearing gets checked once per lap, in order, never doubled back on, and never gets stuck cycling between the same two — while degrading gracefully (and correctly) to simple alternation in the genuine edge case where only two bearings are open at all, since going back and forth between exactly two things is the only possible correct behavior once there's nothing else left to check.

**Verified:** a direct test confirmed a normal multi-bearing scenario sweeps through at least 5 of the qualifying bearings with zero direction reversals; a forced degenerate scenario (only 2 of 8 bearings left open) confirmed it correctly keeps alternating between exactly those two rather than getting stuck on one; and a direct test confirmed calling the function twice in a row with no movement or time elapsed returns the identical point (no needless re-picking). A 12-trial full-battle stress run (both recon modes) confirmed no regressions.

### 2026-09-08 — v83 follow-up 6: the player's mortar was categorically unspottable — fixed before the planned rewrite

While setting up a pre-rewrite characterization baseline (see below), a question came up about whether squads ever fire on enemy mortars — investigating it surfaced that the player's own mortar had a much bigger problem: `_refresh_visibility` (battle_manager.gd) categorically skipped it, permanently, no matter what — "a well-sited, camouflaged crew on a reverse slope isn't something rifle squads happen to notice by scanning the horizon," the ONLY way to ever get a fix on it being counter-battery fire-detection. The user corrected this directly: "enemy squads should shoot at the player mortar when they can... It should try to avoid being shot at. But if it gets into range, they shoot at it. This has to be fixed before the rewrite."

The categorical block is gone — the player's mortar now goes through the exact same live visibility roll as every other unit. What replaces the blanket immunity is the SAME hidden/exposed treatment the artillery spotter already has (`CombatResolver.roll_spot`/`effective_detection_range`): `GameConfig.MORTAR_HIDDEN_DETECTION_RANGE` (250m, same value as the spotter's own) replaces the normal detection range outright when the mortar is in cover (TREES — a mortar can never be inside a BUILDING at all), and `MORTAR_EXPOSED_CONCEALMENT_MULTIPLIER` (0.8, likewise matching the spotter's) applies a mild penalty even in the open. Applied symmetrically to the enemy's own mortars too, which had no special concealment treatment at all before this (just the generic per-terrain multiplier every unit gets) — the same siting/camouflage doctrine applies to both sides equally.

This is a genuinely separate, second layer underneath the hard geometric one `has_direct_los` already provides: a mortar masked by a hill's own elevation is still fully protected regardless of any of this (the spotting roll never even happens — LOS is blocked outright, unchanged by this fix). The new probabilistic layer only ever matters once LOS is genuinely clear, deciding how hard the crew is to notice given real camouflage discipline versus none. "Try to avoid being shot at, but if it gets into range, they shoot at it" — both things are now literally true, whereas before only the first half was ever possible for the player's own gun.

**Verified:** a direct test confirmed a mortar at point-blank range in the open, given enough rolls, is now genuinely reachable by `roll_spot` (previously mathematically impossible regardless of roll count); confirmed the hidden-in-cover detection range is real and wired correctly (verified against an actual `FOREST_PATCHES` zone, not just an arbitrary open point); and confirmed that once spotted, an enemy squad in range correctly picks the now-visible player mortar via the ordinary `_pick_target` path — no separate mechanism needed, since mortars have always been the top targeting priority once merely targetable.

### 2026-09-08 — Pre-rewrite doctrine characterization baseline

Ahead of a planned rewrite of the game's tactical decision-making — moving from many individually hand-tuned thresholds toward decisions grounded in general principles ("win the battle," "keep our people alive," "cause enemy casualties," with the further constraint that a unit's own behavior can only be based on what that unit would actually know, allowing for some communication between squads) — the user asked for current behavior to be captured under test first, so the rewrite's actual effect can be measured rather than guessed at. Git tag `before-tactical-rewrite` marks the commit immediately prior. Two design decisions, confirmed with the user before building: this should be a baseline-capture-and-comparison report, not a pass/fail regression gate (the rewrite is expected to change specific numbers on purpose — that's the point), and it should cover every identifiable doctrinal behavior broadly rather than just aggregate outcomes.

Built `scripts/tests/characterize_doctrine.gd` plus two thin instrumented subclasses, `TestBattleManager`/`TestUnit` (and a no-op `TestCombatLog` to avoid the real log's UI-node dependency in a headless run) — kept permanently in `scripts/tests/`, unlike this session's usual throwaway `_smoke_test_*` convention, since these are meant to be re-run after the rewrite for comparison. Each override exists purely to COUNT what a decision point actually did (squad-vs-mortar targeting frequency, mortar hold-vs-flee outcomes, ammo cook-off rate, resupply run lifecycle, enemy flanking arrival rate, retreat threat-avoidance activation, wounded-evacuation carried-vs-abandoned split, drone sorties/losses), always delegating to `super()` for the real behavior, so the logic under test is byte-for-byte what ships. Everything that persists in final unit state (surrender, retreat-depth escalation) is read directly with no instrumentation needed; everything transient (a one-shot flag already cleared by the time the battle ends, a decision that leaves no trace at all) needed a live hook. Drives `BattleManager` directly rather than through `main.gd`'s SubViewport/camera (irrelevant to battle outcomes), which also made trials roughly 4x faster than the session's earlier full-battle stress tests.

Building this surfaced two real findings before a single line of the rewrite was written: player squads essentially never fire on an enemy mortar (0 occurrences across every trial batch) purely because enemy mortars sit deep in their own rear and nothing drives a player squad to advance that far — while the player's own mortar does engage enemy mortars — and, far more significantly, the player's own mortar was never targetable by the enemy AT ALL via the normal path (see the follow-up entry directly above this one, fixed as a result).

Output: `docs/designs/pre-rewrite-baseline.json` (raw per-metric data, for future automated diffing) and `docs/designs/pre-rewrite-baseline.md` (human-readable summary) — 100 trials per recon mode. Re-running the identical script after the rewrite and comparing the two reports by eye is the intended workflow; a changed number is informative, not automatically a failure.

### 2026-09-08 — v83 follow-up 7: the enemy flanking maneuver never actually flanked

The first real baseline run surfaced a second finding, more serious than the mortar-visibility one: across all 200 trials, `0.0%` of enemy squads assigned the flanking route (from v83's own enemy-flanking change) ever completed it — every single one got stuck. Confirmed directly with a standalone repro: a flanking squad closes to *exactly* `ENEMY_SURROUND_STANDOFF_RADIUS` (300m) from its flank waypoint and then stops forever.

The bug: `_next_advance_point` (battle_manager.gd) stops requesting any further movement once a squad is within `ENEMY_SURROUND_STANDOFF_RADIUS` of whatever `_enemy_advance_objective` currently returns — correct behavior for the REAL objective (a squad shouldn't walk on top of the mortar/village, it should stand off and support/besiege from there), but while `flanking_route_active` is still true, that objective IS the flank waypoint itself, not the real one. `ENEMY_FLANK_WAYPOINT_ARRIVAL_RADIUS` had been set to 100m — deliberately *smaller* than the 300m standoff, on the reasoning "this is a pass-through waypoint, tighter makes sense" — which was exactly backwards: a smaller arrival radius than the standoff radius is unreachable, since the squad's own movement halts at the (larger) standoff distance before it can ever get within the (smaller) arrival distance. The squad reaches the flank, and then simply never pivots to actually use it.

Fixed by setting `ENEMY_FLANK_WAYPOINT_ARRIVAL_RADIUS` to 350m — comfortably larger than the 300m standoff radius, so arrival is always detected first, before the standoff-driven halt could ever kick in.

**Verified:** a direct repro of the exact stuck scenario now shows the squad detecting arrival and pivoting off the flank waypoint well before reaching standoff distance, then genuinely closing on the real objective afterward (confirmed by checking its distance to `VILLAGE_CENTER` decreases over subsequent steps, not just that the flag flipped). This second fix is exactly why the baseline is worth having: it caught something no amount of "did it crash" stress testing would ever have noticed, since a permanently-stalled squad isn't a crash, it's just quietly wrong.

One more wrinkle, caught by the regenerated baseline itself: right after this fix, the "flanking maneuver" stats read `0.54 per battle assigned` and `274%` arrived — a percentage over 100 is impossible, so the harness's OWN instrumentation had a bug, not the game. `TestBattleManager.flanking_units_seen` erases a unit the moment its arrival is counted (to avoid double-counting); reading `.size()` at battle-end to report "how many were ever assigned" therefore only reflects units still mid-flight at that exact moment, undercounting anyone who already arrived — while `flanking_arrivals` (a plain monotonic counter) correctly counts every arrival across the whole battle, producing a nonsensical ratio between a shrinking snapshot and a running total. Fixed by adding a separate, real monotonic counter (`flanking_units_assigned_total`) incremented exactly once per unit the first time it's ever seen flanking, independent of the working set used for arrival-dedup. Re-verified with a fresh 5-trial run (sane values, both proportions under 100%) before regenerating the full 100-trial baseline a final time.

### 2026-09-08 — A battle-outcome scoring function, and a plan for the rewrite itself

With the baseline capturing current behavior, the actual design of the rewrite began. Agreed principles, in order:

**Scope.** Enemy behavior is explicitly out of scope for now — "do the rewrite in such a way that it could be applied to the enemy at some future time. But for now, we won't." The framework should be team-parameterizable (not hard-coded to "player") but only ever driven for the player's own side in this pass. **Pilot area**: retreat/hold-or-flee decisions (squad retreat threshold, mortar hold-vs-abandon, wounded evacuation) — the clearest existing place where "keep people alive" and "hold the position / cause enemy casualties" are already in real tension.

**Knowledge locality**, worked through concretely rather than assumed.
