A three-tier model: (1) *personal/organic* — a unit's own state (own casualties, own ammo — worked example: a mortar's round count stays local, nothing about a retreat decision needs another unit to know it), known only to itself unless a decision downstream actually needs it; (2) *reported situational awareness* — what's realistically radioed for shared tactical picture: enemy contact reports (already modeled via `_known_enemy_positions`, gated on `is_visible`), and — the piece genuinely missing from the current codebase — whether a *neighboring friendly unit* is still fighting or has gone quiet, which matters a lot for a real hold-or-flee call (a unit is far more willing to hold if it isn't isolated); (3) *command-level aggregate* — force-wide totals only a commander synthesizes (the existing general-retreat trigger), not needed for any individual unit's own local decision.

**A fixed battle-outcome scoring rubric**, the concrete operationalization of "win the battle / keep our people alive / cause enemy casualties":

| Outcome | Score |
|---|---|
| Position held | +20 |
| Position lost | -20 |
| Friendly death | -10 |
| Enemy death | +5 |
| Friendly captured | -8 |
| Enemy captured | +8 |
| Friendly heavily wounded | -4 |
| Enemy heavily wounded | +2 |
| Friendly walking wounded | -1 |
| Enemy walking wounded | +0.5 |
| Friendly mortar lost (the gun itself) | -5 |
| Enemy mortar destroyed or abandoned on ground the player holds | +2.5 |

Deaths are deliberately asymmetric (-10 vs +5 — a friendly life costs more than an enemy death is worth, real risk-aversion); captures are symmetric (±8). The friendly-mortar and enemy-mortar terms are each *on top of* whatever their crews already cost via the death/wounded terms above — they specifically price the loss/denial of the weapon system itself. The enemy-mortar term's "or abandoned on held ground" half ties directly into the AAR's own long-established "holding lets you confirm what was left behind" mechanic from earlier in this doctrine's history — a withdrawn crew only counts as confirmed out of action once the ground it stood on is actually held.

**Explicitly excluded: any "recon asset lost" term.** The stated principle: *"We are scoring the final result of the fight. Not intermediate steps. The decision should be based on final outcomes, not immediate localized results... there would be every chance to reconstitute the drone team. A dead drone team member is no better or worse than a dead soldier in a squad."* Losing recon capability mid-battle is an intermediate, replaceable effect (it changes how the rest of *this* fight goes, but resolves itself before the next engagement) — only the actual personnel losses matter for the score, and those are already fully counted via the generic death/wounded terms. This is the litmus test for any future proposed term: a genuine final outcome (a life, a scarce hard-to-replace weapon system, holding ground) belongs in the score; a replaceable/intermediate capability effect (recon coverage, ammo on hand) does not.

**Two separate scores, not one — and they're allowed to disagree.** Ground truth feeds the score actual decisions get evaluated against internally; a SEPARATE computation, fed the player's own best-guess/estimated figures (the exact same fog-of-war machinery the AAR already uses — `_compute_side_stats(enemy_units, not held, true)`), is what would justify a *displayed* verdict label. *"The labelling of a successful defense should be based on best guesses, not actual results. So it may not always match up."* This is a direct, deliberate extension of the AAR's existing honest-uncertainty epistemics (confirmed vs. estimated casualty splits, "partly estimated" wording) to the headline verdict itself — a commander's own after-action read, built on imperfect intel, can legitimately be wrong about how the fight actually went.

Implemented in `scripts/tests/characterize_doctrine.gd` as `_score_battle()` plus `_mortar_out_true`/`_mortar_out_estimated` (the latter mirroring `_compute_side_stats`'s own per-unit confirmation logic exactly), computing both scores per trial and reporting them broken out by the *existing* held+exchange_ratio verdict labels — not yet used to redefine those labels, since that requires seeing the real score distribution first. An early small-sample run already showed the two scores diverging in the expected direction (the estimated score consistently reads more pessimistic than the true score, since it can't fully credit enemy losses it can't confirm) and hinted that the current verdict labels may not rank cleanly against the true score — worth checking properly against the full 100-trial run before touching any production verdict logic.

Per the user's standing instruction, the `before-tactical-rewrite` git tag keeps moving forward through all of this scoring/validation work — it should land on whatever commit is the true last one before actual tactical decision-making code changes begin, not before.

### 2026-09-08 — The rewrite's first piece: unifying drone sweep and flank-watch into one destination-picker

With the baseline and scoring rubric in place, the user picked drone pathing as the first actual tactical change: "the first tactical change should be drone pathing." The scope was negotiated over several follow-ups rather than fixed up front. Starting from "which of the two decision layers do you mean" (the outer tiered priority argmax in `_drone_search_target`, or the separate mechanisms that pick WHERE within a tier), the user widened it: "Drone pathing in general. A particular path might accomplish multiple goals, which is fine. We want drone pathing that leads to victory" — rejecting strict tiered competition in favor of letting one flight serve more than one purpose. But immediately bounded that ambition: "this does have to be kept to what a person can reasonably think... not a full multilinear regression" — a ranked main goal with modest incidental credit for secondary value picked up along the way, not an exhaustive weighted sum over every consideration. And finally, explicit permission to actually attempt the harder version without needing to solve it completely: "be willing to play at the more difficult layer, but don't feel you have to solve it completely, as a person could not do that either."

Before this final scope landed, a Plan agent had already validated a concrete formula for a narrower version of the same idea (unifying the three separate WHERE-mechanisms — sweep grid, flank-watch, vicinity-circle — under one shared low-level function, each still on its own separate candidate set). That work caught a real, non-obvious bug and gave a concrete, geometry-checked reason to leave one of the three mechanisms out of the unification — both of which carried forward into the final design once the scope was reconciled against the user's later messages:

- **The parking-lock bug.** A naive value/recency/distance formula, re-evaluated fresh every tick the way `_drone_search_target`'s other tiers already are, has a structural flaw: a candidate the drone has just arrived at and is sitting on has zero distance cost, and if its recency penalty gets reset to "just visited" continuously while parked there (rather than stamped once, on arrival), the recency discount alone can never overcome that zero-cost advantage against real, distant alternatives — the drone locks onto wherever it happens to be for a long time instead of the brief "just left, come back later" discount this was supposed to produce. Fix: keep the mechanism *sticky* (return the same committed candidate every tick while still en route, exactly like the two old separate mechanisms already did), and only re-run the full argmax on arrival — at which point the just-left candidate is unconditionally excluded from being re-picked, rather than trusting the recency decay alone to make it lose.
- **Vicinity-search stays separate.** Its 70°-step, deliberately-not-a-divisor-of-360° circling pattern exists specifically so it never retraces the same points around a spotted squad; representing that as a small, static candidate array either defeats the anti-repetition design (too few points) or produces adjacent points closer together than the arrival radius (too many), causing false-immediate-arrival. It's also reactive to an already-spotted, specific threat rather than routine idle patrol, so — unlike sweep and flank-watch, which really are the same underlying activity — folding it in wasn't a clean fit anyway. Left untouched.

**What actually changed** (`scripts/battle_manager.gd`, `scripts/game_config.gd`): the 25-cell sweep grid (`_sweep_candidates`) and the mortar's 8-bearing flank-watch (`_flank_watch_candidates`) now feed ONE shared candidate pool (`{key, point, value}` dictionaries), picked by one function (`_pick_best_drone_destination`) using `value * recency_multiplier - distance_cost`. `_drone_routine_recon_target` wraps this with the sticky-until-arrival behavior described above. `TARGET_PRIORITY_FLANK_WATCH` — previously a fixed 30, competing directly against squad-danger (max 10) and even high-priority retreating-enemy (25) as its own outer tier in `_drone_search_target` — is retired; flank-watch candidates instead carry their own value (`DRONE_FLANK_WATCH_BASE_VALUE = 0.5`, pitched near the top of the sweep grid's own row-weight range) inside the merged pool, so flank-watch's overall priority now rides on the same fluctuating `TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR * _mortar_existence_confidence()` score sweep always competed with, rather than a fixed floor of its own — a deliberate consequence of treating the two as one activity rather than two. `DRONE_SWEEP_RECENTLY_VISITED_COOLDOWN_S`/`_MIN_WEIGHT_MULTIPLIER` were renamed to `DRONE_DESTINATION_*`, since the recency mechanism is now shared by both candidate kinds rather than sweep-only. A new `DRONE_DESTINATION_DISTANCE_COST_PER_PX` (0.0005) makes distance a real, comparable factor against a full row-weight step rather than negligible or dominant.

**Verified:** a throwaway smoke test confirmed a drone forced to "arrive" repeatedly does not re-lock onto the same candidate (5 consecutive re-picks cycled through 5 distinct sweep cells) and that a flank-watch bearing and a sweep cell genuinely compete in one pool rather than two silently-separate mechanisms. A full 200-trial run (100 per recon mode) against the existing `characterize_doctrine.gd` baseline harness showed no crashes and no stuck drones; DRONE_TEAM's headline numbers moved by about the same amount SPOTTER's did too between the two independent runs (held 59%→57% vs. SPOTTER's untouched 34%→38%) — consistent with ordinary run-to-run noise at n=100 trials, not a signal of a real behavioral regression from the merge.

### 2026-09-08 — Follow-up: the drone spotted enemies but didn't visibly react, and fixated once it found one

Playtesting the merge above surfaced two related problems the trial-count-based comparison above couldn't have caught (aggregate win/score stats are far too noisy at n=100 to detect a specific behavioral pattern — see the note at the end of this entry). First: *"The drone sees enemies but then fails to scout the region to look for more... The discovered enemy should cause the pilot to make inferences about where other enemies might be."* Second (diagnosed while investigating the first): *"if the drone sees an enemy on its sweep, this should impact the drone's behavior. It does not seem to."*

**Root cause of the fixation.** Before the merge, flank-watch had its own fixed outer-tier priority of 30, unconditionally beating the squad-tracking tier's own max of 10 whenever a real gap existed to check. The merge folded flank-watch's relevance into the SAME `TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR * _mortar_existence_confidence()` value sweep's outer gate always used — but that confidence value decays over real tactical time (floored near 5.0 once no mortar fire has been seen in a while, per `MORTAR_CONFIDENCE_DECAY_TAU`/`MORTAR_CONFIDENCE_FLOOR`) and typically decays well before a battle ends. Once it dropped below 10, an already-spotted squad's own tracking priority could out-bid the *entire* routine-recon tier — including flank-watch — indefinitely, so the drone just parked on one contact and never returned to broader search or flank protection. This was a real, unintended side effect of the merge, not a pre-existing issue: watching the mortar's blind side is a standing duty that has nothing to do with how confident anyone is that a *second* mortar exists, so tying the two together was the actual mistake.

**Root cause of "no visible reaction to a sighting."** The pre-merge (and initially post-merge) answer to "a spotted squad rarely travels alone" was `_drone_vicinity_search_point` — a slow, fixed-radius circle around that ONE squad's own exact position, re-centered every tick. That's a literal-minded stand-in for inference, not an actual one: it doesn't reason about where else the enemy might be, it just orbits the one spot already confirmed, and (per the fixation bug above) could end up doing that indefinitely. The user, thinking out loud rather than proposing a finished design ("just throwing out ideas, am not married to them"), reframed the whole thing as a small set of standing goals a real operator would hold at once — "find enemies, especially mortars," "make sure no one is sneaking up on our mortar," "assess how likely the second one is or isn't" — which mapped directly onto the codebase and clarified exactly what should and shouldn't decay together.

**The fix**, all in `scripts/battle_manager.gd`/`scripts/game_config.gd`:
- **Flank-watch gets an independent standing floor again** (`GameConfig.DRONE_FLANK_WATCH_STANDING_PRIORITY = 30.0`, restoring the old fixed value but now applied correctly): the routine-recon tier's outer competing value is `max(sweep's confidence-scaled value, 30.0 if flank-watch currently has any open bearing)` instead of one blended number. The two standing goals ("is a second mortar still plausible enough to justify a broad sweep" vs. "is there currently an unwatched gap toward our mortar") are judged independently, exactly as the user's framing suggested, while the *inner* pick (once the tier wins) still freely chooses whichever specific candidate — sweep cell or flank bearing — is actually most valuable, so a single trip can still serve both.
- **The dedicated vicinity-circle mechanism is retired entirely**, replaced by `_contact_search_bonus`: every ACTIVE enemy unit seen live gets recorded (`_recent_enemy_contacts`, position + timestamp, not just the single most dangerous one) and its influence spreads outward as a value bonus on nearby candidates in the SAME shared routine-recon pool, tapering with both distance (`DRONE_CONTACT_BONUS_RADIUS`) and age (`DRONE_CONTACT_BONUS_EXPIRY`, matching the existing mortar-fire-lead expiry idiom) rather than being a flat step. This is the literal implementation of "a discovered enemy should shift where we look next": a sighting now measurably raises the value of sweep cells and flank bearings near it, competing fairly against everything else in the pool (so it can't cause the same lock-on the circle did), instead of committing the whole drone to one fixed-radius orbit.
- **The single most dangerous visible squad is still flown at directly** (no more circling it either) — that tier's actual job is "keep real-time eyes on an active, closing threat," a genuinely different question from "where might more of the enemy be," which the contact bonus now answers on its own.

**Verified:** new smoke tests confirmed the contact bonus is full value at the exact sighting, zero beyond its radius, and zero once fully expired; that a real sighting measurably raises a nearby sweep cell's own value; that with a friendly mortar's flank-watch qualifying, a single visible enemy squad at maximum danger no longer wins the outer tier and causes the drone to fixate on it; and that the same maximally-dangerous squad, with no friendly mortar in the picture at all, IS flown at directly (tier 4 still works as intended on its own). A further 200-trial run showed no crashes; the headline win-rate/score numbers moved by roughly the same amount the untouched SPOTTER mode's OWN numbers moved between runs (SPOTTER's held-rate alone ranged 34%→38%→45% across three independent 100-trial runs with zero code changes to it at all) — confirming aggregate outcome stats are too noisy at this sample size to be useful evidence either way for a fix like this one; the targeted behavioral smoke tests are what actually validate it.

### 2026-09-08 — Follow-up: the drone was leaving the map to the north and south

The very next playtest surfaced a third, more mechanical bug, unrelated to the tiering/scoring logic above: *"it sometimes goes above or below the map. It should not. These are sectors of other units. However, going to the left or right of the visible region MIGHT be OK... only if there was reason to suspect that relevant enemies might be in those areas."*

**Root cause.** `_flank_watch_candidates`'s bearing probes are computed as `mortar.global_position + Vector2.RIGHT.rotated(bearing) * GameConfig.MORTAR_FLANK_THREAT_RADIUS` (1500m) across all 8 compass directions, with no map-boundary awareness at all — this specific geometry predates this session's rewrite entirely, but restoring flank-watch's standing priority in the previous entry means the drone now actually visits these probes far more often than it used to, which is almost certainly why this became visible now rather than earlier. 1500m is large enough relative to the map's own 3500m height that a mortar anywhere within roughly the northern or southern third of the map sends at least one probe straight off the map's vertical edge.

**The fix — an asymmetric boundary, matching the user's own north/south-vs-east/west distinction exactly:** a new `_clamp_to_drone_operating_area(point)` clamps y unconditionally to `[0, MAP_HEIGHT_M]` (north/south of the map is another unit's sector, never the drone's business regardless of any reasoning) but clamps x to `[-WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_M, MAP_WIDTH_M]` — the already-modeled west flank stays reachable (real ground a genuine contact or flanking threat can legitimately draw the drone into, per the earlier west-flank feature), while nothing further west, or east of the map's own edge, is modeled or ever should be suspected. Applied in two places: `_flank_watch_candidates` clamps the actual FLIGHT point for each probe (while still judging whether a bearing is screened/already-known against the true, unclamped direction — the real compass geometry, not where the drone can physically stand), and `_drone_search_target` applies the same clamp to whatever position ultimately wins, as a cheap universal backstop for every other tier (squad-tracking, retreating-enemy, mortar-lead, joint-hunt, sweep) even though none of those currently have a known way to produce an out-of-bounds point on their own.

**Verified:** a direct test confirmed the clamp helper's own boundary behavior (clamps above/below the map, allows a point inside the west flank unchanged, clamps a point past the west flank back to its own edge, clamps a point past the east edge to the map's own width); a mortar planted 50m from the north edge, and separately 50m from the south edge, both produced flank-watch candidates that stayed within the map's height despite the raw 1500m-radius bearing math; and a full single-battle run in DRONE_TEAM mode tracked the drone's actual position every tick and confirmed it never left `[0, MAP_HEIGHT_PX]` vertically over the whole battle.

### 2026-09-08 — A directional prior: "absent contact, the enemy is more likely to be coming from the east"

A fourth playtest observation, and this one prompted a step back rather than another drone-specific patch: *"At the start of the battle, the launched drone sometimes goes in the wrong direction. It should head for areas where the enemy might be coming from... I'm wondering if this might be symptomatic of a lack of thinking about where the enemy is likely to be at a particular time. If so, we probably need a module for that before we think about drone pathing."*

**Confirming the diagnosis.** The player's mortar/drone team starts around x≈1400m (`PLAYER_MORTAR_DEFAULT_POSITION`); the enemy spawns and marches in from x≈4950m (`ENEMY_SPAWN_X`) along the road. At battle start nothing has been visited yet, so the merged routine-recon pool's tie-break is pure distance cost — and the sweep grid's best row (the road's own y-band, value 0.6) has 5 columns spanning that whole 1550–4950m stretch, ALL sharing the exact same value. The nearest one to the drone's own starting position (≈1550m, right next to the player's own side) wins by a wide margin over the genuinely useful ones further east. So the very first flight went to the point closest to the drone, not the point closest to where the enemy actually is — confirmed directly: before this fix, the computed first pick was the westmost road-band cell; nothing in the sweep grid's column treatment (as opposed to its row treatment, which already biases toward the road's y-band) had ever encoded "the enemy's known approach is from the east."

**The user's key correction, mid-design:** an earlier draft of this fix (not built) would have made the "likely enemy region" grow inward over elapsed tactical time — but that assumes the commander knows roughly when the enemy's advance began and how fast it moves, which is knowledge a real commander doesn't have (per this doc's own knowledge-locality principle). The actual, honest piece of standing knowledge is simpler and time-independent: *"they do know that if no enemy has been seen, they are much more likely to be coming from the right."* A static directional prior, not a ticking clock.

**The fix** (`scripts/battle_manager.gd`/`scripts/game_config.gd`): a new `_enemy_approach_likelihood(point)` returns a smooth gradient from `GameConfig.ENEMY_APPROACH_LIKELIHOOD_MIN` (0.1) at the map's west edge up to `_MAX` (1.0) at the east edge (`ENEMY_SPAWN_X`/`MAP_WIDTH_PX`) — a genuine, doctrinal "public knowledge of the terrain" prior (same idiom already used for the road-band row-weighting), not a claim of exact intelligence. Multiplied into `_sweep_candidates`'s existing row-weight value as a SEPARATE, independent axis (row = which y-band, this = which x-position), so the two biases compose rather than either replacing the other. `_contact_search_bonus` is still ADDED on top afterward, unchanged — real evidence of a deep contact still wins outright regardless of what this default prior alone would say, which is exactly how "penetrated deeply" gets handled: through actual sightings, not an assumed clock. Deliberately NOT applied to `_flank_watch_candidates` — flank-watch's whole purpose is covering the mortar's non-obvious blind sides (including the ones a real flanking maneuver would use, like the modeled west-flank swing), so biasing it toward the "expected" direction would undermine the one thing it exists to catch.

**Verified:** a direct test confirmed the gradient's own shape (correct value at both edges, monotonically increasing west to east, clamped beyond either edge); and, using the actual default deployment positions, that the very first sweep pick — previously the westmost road-band cell, right next to the player's own mortar — now goes to the eastmost one instead, comfortably toward the enemy's actual approach. A further full-battle run in both recon modes showed no crashes.

### 2026-09-08 — Tracking possible and known enemy mortar locations

The east-favoring gradient above wasn't enough on its own: *"the drone did a lot of looking in the wrong direction. Also, it wasn't hunting for mortars. Possible and known mortar locations should also be tracked. For drone pathing, these will be important."*

**Diagnosis, this time backed by an actual instrumented run** (10 full battles, tallying which tier won each tick and which grid row a sweep pick landed on) rather than arithmetic alone, since the previous entries' hand-worked numbers had already proven fallible. Two real findings: (1) the general sweep DOES already lean east overall (276 east / 206 mid / 66 west sweep picks) and doesn't dominate the drone's time disproportionately (flank-watch: 100 picks, sweep: 548, live-mortar-watch: 125, out of ~850 total decisions) — so the previous entries' fixes were working as intended, not the main problem here; (2) the real gap is that `DRONE_SWEEP_ROW_WEIGHTS` (`[0.05, 0.15, 0.6, 0.15, 0.05]`) puts 60% of the sweep's attention on the road's own row and crushes the two rows flanking it to 0.15 each — but `GameConfig.ENEMY_MORTAR_POSITIONS_M` (and real indirect-fire doctrine generally) puts mortars OFF the road, in those very rows the weighting discounts hardest. So even sweep picks that correctly went east were still disproportionately checking the road corridor itself, not the bands where a mortar would actually be sited — a structural reason "wasn't hunting for mortars" was true even though the east/west balance looked fine.

**The fix** (`scripts/battle_manager.gd`/`scripts/game_config.gd`) tracks both halves the user asked for, reusing existing machinery wherever it already fit rather than building parallel mechanisms:
- **Known locations**: `_update_recent_enemy_contacts` now also folds in `_last_detected_mortar_fire` (muzzle-flash/trajectory detections) whenever the mortar itself isn't currently visually spotted — which is the normal case, since a mortar is essentially never actually seen the way a squad is. `_last_detected_mortar_fire` already stores `{"position", "time"}` in exactly the shape `_recent_enemy_contacts` uses, so this is a direct merge, not a new mechanism: a real detection now continues to inform the general search at a lingering, decaying value (via the existing `_contact_search_bonus`) even after it's gone stale for the single top-priority "fresh lead" tier, rather than vanishing from consideration entirely the instant `DRONE_MORTAR_FIRE_LEAD_EXPIRY` passes.
- **Possible locations**: a new `GameConfig.DRONE_MORTAR_HUNT_ROW_WEIGHTS` (`[0.05, 0.35, 0.05, 0.35, 0.05]`) is a genuinely separate row bias from `DRONE_SWEEP_ROW_WEIGHTS` — squads concentrate near the road, but a real mortar deliberately avoids sitting draped over the same open ground a rifle squad marches down, wanting concealment and standoff while staying close enough to the enemy's rear to support the advance. ADDED to the existing row weight (not replacing it) in `_sweep_candidates`, so the road remains the single highest-value row overall (worth checking for both purposes) while the flanking bands are no longer crushed to a token weight for mortar-hunting purposes specifically. Doctrinal public knowledge, same idiom as the road-band and approach-direction biases — not secret intelligence about any specific position.

**Verified:** a direct test confirmed the road row is still highest overall but the off-road rows are now meaningfully close (roughly 77% of the road row's value, versus 25% before) rather than crushed; and that a mortar detected only via fire (never visually seen) is correctly folded into the recent-contacts pool and produces a real, positive search bonus nearby. Re-running the same instrumented 10-battle diagnostic confirmed the shift is real in practice, not just in the formula: off-road row picks went from a small fraction of sweep activity to nearly matching the road row itself (175 off-road vs. 210 road, out of the same sweep-pick pool), and — the more telling number — live-mortar engagement (the top-priority tier that fires when an enemy mortar is actually spotted) roughly tripled across the same 10 trials (125 → 351), consistent with the drone actually finding mortars it previously would have missed.

### 2026-09-08 — The sweep didn't know ground already under friendly observation is confirmed empty

Two debug overlays built around this time (a drone-pilot reasoning readout and an enemy-location heat map, both purely visual/read-only tools — see the memory file rather than this doctrine log for their own details, since neither changes any actual decision) needed a genuine "is the enemy known NOT to be here" concept for the heat map specifically: *"We know the enemy is not in areas we can see. This includes whether the drone can see the area or our troops can see it."* That became `BattleManager._point_currently_observed` (checks the drone's aerial sensor and every active ground unit's ordinary detection range + line of sight + elevation bonus) feeding `estimated_enemy_likelihood`, the heat map's own read-only estimate.

The very next report showed the gap this created: *"Right at the start of the battle, the drone was exploring areas where the enemy was known not to be."* `_point_currently_observed` had only been wired into the heat map's VISUALIZATION, never into `_sweep_candidates` — the function that actually picks where the drone flies. At battle start, the sweep grid's westmost column sits close enough to the player's own deployed squads/mortar that they can already see it's empty themselves, but nothing in the real decision said so; only the debug picture knew.

**The fix:** `_sweep_candidates` now applies the same `DRONE_SWEEP_CLEARED_WEIGHT_MULTIPLIER` discount (reused, not a new constant — this is the same kind of fact as a permanent confirmed kill, just from live observation instead) whenever `_point_currently_observed` says some other friendly asset can already see a cell. Safe to treat as "confirmed empty" rather than merely "probably empty": by the time the routine-recon tier is even being evaluated, any real live enemy nearby would already have been claimed by a higher-priority tier (squad-tracking or mortar-live) earlier in `_drone_search_target`, so there's no real scenario where a currently-observed sweep cell secretly still has an enemy in it.

**Verified:** using the actual default deployment positions, the westmost sweep column (nearest the player's own units) is confirmed to be currently observed at true battle start, and its value comes out well below (under 30% of) what the row/approach weighting alone would give it — while the overall best pick still correctly favors the east, unaffected. A full-battle regression in both recon modes showed no crashes.

### 2026-09-08 — The same "wrong direction" report, once more — this time it was the distance-cost constant

The fix above wasn't actually the fix: *"Nope, right at the start, the drone is still sweeping unlikely areas, instead of going for the hot areas towards the right."* Diagnosed this time by directly instrumenting the exact real battle-start decision (drone position, every flank/sweep candidate's raw value, distance, and net score) rather than trusting an isolated unit test again — the earlier verification had only exercised `_sweep_candidates` on its own, never the actual merged sweep+flank pool `_drone_search_target` really competes over.

That instrumentation found it immediately: at true battle start, `flank:45` (value 0.5, ~294px from the drone's launch point near the mortar) narrowly beat the single hottest sweep cell (value 0.64, ~610px away, out at the enemy's actual likely approach) by net score — 0.353 vs. 0.339. `GameConfig.DRONE_DESTINATION_DISTANCE_COST_PER_PX` (0.0005) was calibrated back when the routine-recon pool's value spread was narrow (`DRONE_SWEEP_ROW_WEIGHTS` alone, 0.05–0.6) — but `_enemy_approach_likelihood` and `DRONE_MORTAR_HUNT_ROW_WEIGHTS`, both added afterward, widened that spread considerably (up to ~0.64 combined). Flank-watch candidates sit close to the mortar by construction, which happens to also be close to the drone's own launch point — giving them a structural distance advantage that, at the old rate, was enough to override a real, large value gap at long range. The whole POINT of the east-approach bias was to send the drone toward the far side of the map when nothing else was known; the old distance cost was undoing exactly that at the one moment it mattered most.

**The fix:** halved `DRONE_DESTINATION_DISTANCE_COST_PER_PX` to 0.00025. Re-running the same instrumented check confirmed the real `_drone_search_target()` now picks the hot east cell with a comfortable margin (0.49 vs. 0.43) instead of the nearby flank bearing. Verified this didn't reopen the original parking-lock problem the constant exists to prevent (still rotates through distinct sweep cells across repeated arrivals, no lock-on) and a full-battle regression in both recon modes showed no crashes.

**Reusable lesson**: this is the second time in this same arc that a plausible-looking direct unit test missed the actual bug because it tested a function in isolation rather than the real, full decision pipeline it's embedded in (the first was the tier-frequency diagnosis a few entries up, which used hand-arithmetic instead of measurement; this one used a real but incomplete test). When a fix is reported as not working, re-verify against the EXACT call path the game actually uses end to end, not a plausible-looking proxy for it.

### 2026-09-08 — A mortar aware of an approaching enemy should fire or evade

A step away from drone pathing, back to the mortar itself: *"A mortar that is aware of an approaching enemy should either fire at them or take evasive action."* Investigated first (an Explore pass over every mortar firing/relocation trigger, not a guess) before touching any code, since this project has already been burned twice in this session by fixing the wrong thing.

The investigation found every mortar self-preservation trigger was purely reactive: relocate after being hit (`Unit._mortar_crew_holds_position`, only reachable from `take_hit`), relocate when out of ammo (`_update_mortar_safety_relocation`, both sides, unconditional), or relocate when actually spotted with nothing to shoot (`_update_friendly_mortar_concealment` — **player-only**, a real asymmetry: the enemy mortar had no equivalent at all). Nothing anywhere reacted to merely *seeing* an approaching threat that hadn't yet hit the mortar, spotted it, or starved it of ammo. Tracing the actual firing path (`_pick_target`) further found the specific mechanism that lets this manifest: the existing ammo-conservation "hold fire" probability (`scarcity * (1 - urgency)`) applies to ANY squad target regardless of how close it's gotten — a low-ammo mortar can keep probabilistically declining to fire on a squad that's closing the whole time, with no distance-based override and no fallback evacuation plan once it's basically pinned.

**The fix** (`scripts/battle_manager.gd`/`scripts/combat_log.gd`), two pieces matching the user's own "either/or":
- **Fire**: `_pick_target` now forces `hold_fire_chance` to zero whenever a squad candidate is within `GameConfig.MORTAR_CREW_OVERRUN_DANGER_RANGE` (the same threshold the crew's own post-hit hold-or-flee roll already uses) — conserving ammo for later stops making sense the instant "later" might not come. Gated on actually having a round to fire (`mortar_rounds_remaining > 0`), since this only overrides the CHOICE to hold fire, not the fact of having nothing left — without that guard, anything calling `_pick_target` directly (see below) would misread a genuinely empty mortar as "has a shot" just because a threat was close.
- **Evade**: `_update_friendly_mortar_concealment` generalized into `_update_mortar_threat_response`, now running for BOTH sides (fixing the asymmetry) with a second trigger alongside the original "spotted with nothing to shoot": NOT spotted, but a known enemy within that same overrun range, and still nothing worth shooting (`_pick_target` returns null) — relocate rather than wait. In practice this second trigger is a narrow backstop (the fire-override above already covers the common case), but it completes the "either fire or evade" principle literally rather than leaving a gap for edge cases like a mortar simultaneously engaging a higher-priority enemy mortar target while a squad also closes in.

**A related report from the same session, checked but not a bug**: *"the enemy squad walked right over the mortar, but I could not see the enemy firing at the mortar either."* Checked `is_targetable()` for any categorical exclusion (the earlier "player mortar categorically unspottable" bug from before this whole rewrite arc was exactly this shape) — found none; spotting is governed by `CombatResolver.roll_spot`, which scales its chance by `delta` (a per-tick rate, not a guaranteed/instant spot even at zero range). A squad passing quickly near/over a mortar in cover (a short hidden-detection range) can plausibly never roll a successful spot in that narrow window. Consistent with the user's own hedge ("I may have missed it") — flagged as expected fog-of-war variance, not fixed.

**Verified:** direct tests confirmed the hold-fire override never lets a mortar hold fire on a squad within overrun range even at maximum ammo scarcity; a mortar with zero rounds and a closing, unspotted threat correctly relocates; the original "spotted, nothing to shoot" trigger still works; a mortar with a live shot available does NOT flee; and — the asymmetry fix — an ENEMY mortar in the identical zero-ammo/closing-threat scenario now also relocates. A full-battle regression in both recon modes showed no crashes.

### 2026-09-08 — Spotting was running on the wrong clock entirely

A follow-up request — *"verify the probability of the drone spotting enemy mortars... sometimes it seems to fail to spot them when it flies overhead... determine [if that's appropriate]"* — turned into the most consequential fix in this whole session, and it retroactively corrects the previous entry's "not a bug" verdict on the enemy-squad-not-firing report too.

**The bug**: `BattleManager._process` calls `_update_spotting(delta)` — the RAW real engine delta — while every other rate-based system in that same function (movement, ammo timing, resupply) correctly uses `scenario_delta` (`delta * _current_time_scale()`). `GameConfig.SPOT_CHANCE_PER_SECOND` (0.15) was clearly calibrated against the tactical clock, like everything else in this game, but was actually being multiplied by real wall-clock seconds. At `TIME_SCALE_NORMAL` (60x — the guaranteed minimum whenever a drone is airborne, via `_drone_actively_searching`), that's a flat 60x under-count.

Confirmed empirically before touching anything: a drone and an enemy mortar placed exactly on top of each other — zero distance, guaranteed range and line of sight, stationary, in the open — went completely unspotted for 10 real seconds, which is **30 simulated tactical minutes**. A more realistic brief close pass (3 real seconds ≈ 3 tactical minutes of proximity, roughly what a drone flying through a sweep cell near a mortar would actually get) spotted successfully in only **9 of 200 trials (4.5%)**. This isn't narrow to drones or mortars — every `CombatResolver.roll_spot` call in the game (squad-vs-squad, spotter, drone-vs-anything, and the ground-notices-drone check) runs on this same mistimed clock, and the ENTIRE pre-rewrite baseline captured earlier this session was recorded under this exact bug.

**The fix**: pass `scenario_delta` instead of `delta` at the one call site (`battle_manager.gd`), threading the renamed parameter through `_update_spotting`/`_refresh_visibility`/`CombatResolver.roll_spot`/`_roll_ground_notices_drone` for clarity, and renamed `SPOT_CHANCE_PER_SECOND` to `SPOT_CHANCE_PER_TACTICAL_SECOND` so the unit is no longer ambiguous at the call site.

**Verified**: the same co-located drone/mortar now gets spotted on literally the first tick; the 3-tactical-minute close-pass scenario went from 4.5% to 100% across 200 trials; a full-battle regression in both modes showed no crashes. A quick 20-trial-per-mode comparison against the pre-rewrite baseline showed held-rate/exchange-ratio/duration staying in a broadly similar range (reassuring — this wasn't secretly propping up the whole game's balance), but a real, substantial shift in HOW OFTEN contact actually happens once units are in range: enemy fire on the drone went from 0.00/battle in every prior baseline run to 0.20/battle, and mortar-vs-mortar counter-battery engagement roughly quadrupled in frequency. The drone is now a genuinely exposed asset once spotting works correctly, not a near-invisible one — a real, felt difficulty/pacing change worth being aware of even though aggregate win/loss numbers didn't obviously break. A full 100-trial re-baseline is recommended before drawing firm conclusions from any future characterization work, since everything captured before this fix used the wrong clock.

**Retroactive correction to the previous entry**: the "enemy squad walked over the mortar without firing — checked, not a bug" verdict from the mortar fire-or-evade entry above was reached by reading `roll_spot`'s formula correctly but before this timing bug was found — "a per-tick rate, not guaranteed" was true, but badly understated just how rare a successful roll actually was, for the same reason this whole entry describes. That observation was very likely this same bug, not just ordinary fog-of-war variance.

### 2026-09-08 — A scrubbable post-battle history view, and retreating units can still fire back

Two smaller, unrelated additions logged together since both landed right after the spotting fix above.

**Post-battle history scrubber** (`scripts/battle_history_viewer.gd`, new file; `main.gd`; `battle_manager.gd`): *"After the battle, I want a history option. One should be able to drag this forwards and backwards to re-examine particular moments in the history of the battle."* Clarified up front whether the replay should show the player's own fog-of-war knowledge at each moment or full ground truth — the user chose full ground truth. `BattleManager` now records a lightweight snapshot (`{team, kind, x, y, state}` per unit, no live node references) every `HISTORY_SNAPSHOT_INTERVAL_S` (5 tactical seconds) plus one final snapshot the instant the battle ends, kept in `battle_history()`. Snapshots deliberately don't reference live `Unit` nodes: some units (a despawned resupply run, a destroyed drone) may not exist as live nodes at all by the time the battle is over, so a scrubbable review has to stand on recorded data rather than puppeting node lifecycles it doesn't control. `BattleHistoryViewer` is a self-contained `Node2D` that redraws from whichever snapshot index an `HSlider` is set to, echoing `Unit._draw()`'s own team/kind color and state-dimming logic but WITHOUT any `is_visible`-based hiding, since this view is unconditional ground truth by design (unlike the AAR report itself, which stays honest to what was actually known at the time). A new "Review Battle History" button on the AAR screen swaps the live battle nodes out for the viewer plus slider/clock label/back button.

**Retreating units can still fire back**: *"In the latest battle, at about 8:06, our squads start leaving the village. They then pass right by an enemy squad without apparently shooting. I don't understand either action."* Investigated both halves before changing anything: the squads left individually, not together, because retreat is an independent per-unit threshold check, not a coordinated withdrawal order — expected, not a bug. But retreating units never firing at all, even at point-blank range while walking right past an enemy, was a real gap the user then explicitly asked to close: *"yes, retreating units could still fire if enemies are close. This applies to both sides."* `_tick_fire`'s state gate (`battle_manager.gd`) was previously a flat "must be ACTIVE" check; it's now split so a RETREATING **SQUAD** specifically (a retreating MORTAR crew has abandoned the gun — nothing left to fire with) sets a `fighting_withdrawal` flag that lets it reach the normal `_pick_target` range/LOS check and bypasses the "too busy to fire" gate that would otherwise apply while it has an active move order.

**Verified:** the history slider correctly renders unit positions/states at arbitrary recorded moments including the exact final tick, and a destroyed/despawned unit's last-known snapshot state (not a live-node crash) is what's shown after that point. For the fighting-withdrawal fix, `_fire_flashes.size()` growth (the only reliable "did it actually fire" signal — `fire_timer` resets even on a null target pick, a pre-existing quirk unrelated to this change) confirmed a retreating squad now fires on a close enemy while still moving away, a retreating mortar crew still never fires, and ordinary ACTIVE-state firing is unaffected. A full-battle regression in both recon modes showed no crashes.

### 2026-09-08 — Shoot-and-scoot wasn't guaranteed to clear the counter-battery blast radius

*"When doing shoot and scoot, the mortar is frequently getting hit anyway. This might be appropriate. Or it might be that the 'scoot' part is not going far enough. Please investigate and compare against real-world artillery duels."* Real-world doctrine (shoot-and-scoot displacement to "a sufficiently safe distance," counter-battery response typically a couple of minutes) roughly matches this game's own numbers (`COUNTER_BATTERY_BLAST_RADIUS` 150m, `COUNTER_BATTERY_DELAY_MIN/MAX` 1-3 tactical minutes) — so the question was whether `_relocate_mortar`'s actual chosen destination reliably cleared that radius, not whether the abstraction itself was wrong.

It didn't, and the mechanism was traceable to a specific function: `GameConfig.nearest_hidden_point` tries `_reverse_slope_candidate` first (a point 75% of the way down a nearby hill's radius, on the far side from the average threat bearing — added in an earlier round so friendly mortars would seek real elevation-based masking). That candidate is anchored to the HILL's geometry and the threat's bearing, not to the mortar's current position — `from` only affects which hill is nearest/qualifies, not where the resulting point actually is. A mortar already dug in near a hill's reverse slope (a normal, encouraged posture — this is exactly where the earlier round's fix would have sent it) can fire again and get back almost the same point next time, since nothing about the candidate computation asks "how far is this from where I just fired." Sampling 216 realistic mortar positions around every hill on the actual map with a roughly-opposite threat: the reverse-slope candidate qualified 89% of the time, with a median distance from the firing position of exactly 150m, a 10th percentile of just 5m, and 51% of samples landing INSIDE the 150m blast radius. The ring-search fallback (tried only when reverse-slope doesn't qualify) was never the problem — its nearest ring is 250m, already 100m past the blast radius by construction.

**The fix** (`GameConfig._reverse_slope_candidate`): reject a reverse-slope candidate outright if it's closer than `COUNTER_BATTERY_BLAST_RADIUS` to `from`, letting `nearest_hidden_point` fall through to the ring search instead. A hill-adjacent point that's essentially where the mortar already is isn't a real scoot even if it happens to break line of sight.

**Verified:** re-running the identical 216-sample geometry test with the fix in place dropped within-blast-radius relocations from 51.3% to 2.3% — and the handful remaining sit exactly at the 150m boundary, where the game's own linear hit-chance falloff already reads 0%. A full 5-battle regression showed no crashes.

### 2026-09-08 — A "Play" button for the post-battle history, including fire

Direct follow-up request on the history scrubber from a few entries up: *"The history should also have a 'play' button... show everything that was happening at the time, including fire."* Two real gaps versus just dragging the slider by hand: nothing animated automatically, and the recorded snapshots (`{team, kind, x, y, state}` per unit) never captured shots being fired at all — the live game's own `_fire_flashes` fade on real `elapsed_time`, tuned for watching the actual battle, and aren't kept around after the battle ends anyway.

**The fix:**
- **New recording** (`battle_manager.gd`): `_history_fire_events` — same shape as a `_fire_flashes` entry (`from`, `to`, `team`, `is_mortar`) but keyed on `scenario_elapsed_time` (the tactical clock the position snapshots already use) rather than real time, appended at both existing `_fire_flashes.append` call sites (squad direct fire, mortar launch — the muzzle flash, not the mortar's later impact, matching what the live view itself shows). Exposed via `battle_history_fire_events()`, alongside the existing `battle_history()`.
- **Playback** (`battle_history_viewer.gd`): `playback_time` is now the single source of truth for "what moment is on screen," whether reached by dragging the slider (`set_index` snaps it to a recorded snapshot's exact time and pauses — a manual scrub always pauses, the same convention as any video player's seek bar) or by `advance_playback(delta_real)`, called every frame from `main.gd`'s existing `_process` while playing. Replay runs at a fixed pace, `GameConfig.HISTORY_PLAYBACK_TIME_SCALE` (60x — 1 real second = 1 tactical minute, the same felt pace as the live game's own `TIME_SCALE_NORMAL` "worth watching closely" tier), independent of whatever time scale actually applied live at each moment (that wasn't recorded per snapshot, and doesn't need to be — a replay is a deliberate re-watch, not required to match the original session's pacing exactly). `_index_for_time` binary-searches for the latest snapshot at or before `playback_time`; at `HISTORY_SNAPSHOT_INTERVAL_S` (5 tactical seconds) and this playback rate, a new snapshot arrives roughly every 80ms of real playback time — smooth enough that no position interpolation between snapshots was needed. Pressing Play while sitting at the final moment restarts from the beginning rather than doing nothing.
- **Fire rendering**: a self-contained echo of `BattleManager._draw()`'s own flash rendering (same tracer/arc-tracer/muzzle-circle drawing, same team coloring), but fading over `GameConfig.HISTORY_FIRE_FLASH_DURATION_TACTICAL_S` (18 tactical seconds — chosen to look like the live flash's 0.3-real-second fade would at the 60x replay rate) measured against `playback_time` rather than real time. Drawn regardless of whether currently playing, so scrubbing the slider to land near a shot's moment shows it too, not just Play specifically.
- **UI** (`main.gd`): a "Play"/"Pause" toggle button next to the existing slider/back button; `_process` drives `advance_playback` and keeps the slider (via `set_value_no_signal`, to avoid re-triggering the manual-scrub-pauses-it handler) and clock label in sync every frame while playing, and flips the button back to "Play" the instant playback reaches the end on its own.

**Verified:** a real recorded battle (251 snapshots, 97 shots) confirmed `setup()` starts paused at the final moment; `toggle_play()` at the end restarts from the beginning; `advance_playback` moves `playback_time` forward at exactly the documented rate and resolves the correct snapshot index via the binary search; a manual `set_index` call pauses and snaps to the exact recorded time; playing all the way through stops automatically at the final moment without overshooting it; and a fire event reads as "in its flash window" exactly at its own recorded time and correctly falls outside that window once comfortably past it. A full project load and a full-battle regression both showed no errors.

### 2026-09-08 — The drone's opening flight was checking ground the enemy couldn't possibly have reached yet

*"In this battle, right off the bat, the drone went backwards and down. This is wrong."* Investigating this needed to see the actual live decision, not just theorize from the code — a headless test against the DEFAULT deployment showed the drone correctly heading due east at t=0, so whatever was happening depended on specifics of this particular battle's deployment. After some unsuccessful attempts at automating a screenshot of the paused game, the user brought the actual running window forward and read out the on-screen drone-pilot debug overlay directly, which is what actually cracked it: at 06:02:56 (under 3 tactical minutes into the battle), the drone's live reasoning read *"checking an unwatched gap toward our mortar's flank... Target: (1100, 3250)m"* — deep in the map's southwest corner, on a 5000×3500m map where the enemy spawns at the far EAST edge (x=4950m) and has to march there. At that elapsed time, the enemy could only have covered a few hundred meters from its own start line — nowhere near capable of threatening that corner. The drone's very first meaningful flight was checking ground that was, by simple physics, impossible for the enemy to be anywhere near yet.

Root cause: `_flank_watch_candidates()` scores every compass bearing around the mortar at a flat base value, with no plausibility check at all — unlike `_sweep_candidates()`, which already discounts by `_enemy_approach_likelihood()` (a static west-to-east prior). That asymmetry was deliberate from an earlier round: flank-watch stays direction-agnostic on purpose, specifically so it can still catch a genuine flanking maneuver — including through the west flank — later in a real fight, which a permanent directional bias would undermine. So this wasn't a simple oversight to patch silently; it was a real design tension between "ignore direction so late-battle flanking is still caught" and "don't waste the opening flight on somewhere physically impossible." Given the trade-off, this was put to the user rather than decided unilaterally; the chosen fix (of four options, including "this is correct behavior" and "just reuse sweep's directional bias") was a time-gated discount, not a permanent directional one.

**The fix**: new `BattleManager._flank_watch_plausibility(point)` — models a straight-line travel time from `GameConfig.ENEMY_SPAWN_X` at `GameConfig.ENEMY_ADVANCE_SPEED` (a deliberately generous, i.e. fast, lower bound — an actual flanking route only takes longer than a straight march, never less), and ramps a multiplier linearly from `GameConfig.DRONE_FLANK_WATCH_EARLY_DISCOUNT_MIN` (0.1, same floor and "still possible, not ruled out" reasoning as `ENEMY_APPROACH_LIKELIHOOD_MIN`) up to 1.0 as `scenario_elapsed_time` reaches that travel time. This is public, doctrinal knowledge (where the enemy started, roughly how fast infantry marches) — the same category of non-omniscient prior `_enemy_approach_likelihood` already relies on — not a secret peek at the enemy's actual position. Applied as a multiplier on `DRONE_FLANK_WATCH_BASE_VALUE` in `_flank_watch_candidates()`, with `_contact_search_bonus` still added afterward unchanged (a real recent sighting should count at full weight regardless of the time-plausibility prior, same pattern sweep already uses for its own directional prior).

A second, unrelated but related-in-spirit fix landed in the same investigation: getting to this diagnosis required physically bringing the paused game window to the foreground and reading the debug overlay by eye, which desktop automation in this environment couldn't do reliably. Since `main.gd` already wrote this exact same debug data to `debug_state/drone_pilot_snapshot.json` — but only while the on-screen overlay (`d` key) happened to be toggled on — the write is now unconditional, decoupled from the visual toggle entirely (a battle running is enough). This makes `debug_state/drone_pilot_snapshot.json` a reliable, always-current, direct-file-read way for future investigation to see the drone's live position/reasoning/top candidates, without needing a screenshot or the player to have the overlay open at all.

**Verified:** a headless test reproducing the reported deployment shape (mortar/drone team pushed toward the deep rear) confirmed the actual `_drone_search_target()` picks a sweep cell, not the flank bearing, at t=0 — the exact reported bug, fixed. Direct checks on `_flank_watch_plausibility` confirmed every bearing sits at the floor at literal t=0 (zero elapsed time, nothing could have moved yet, regardless of direction — not a bug), a bearing near the enemy's own spawn recovers plausibility far faster than a deep-rear one once real time passes (90 tactical seconds in: 0.554 vs 0.129), and the deep-rear bearing fully recovers to 1.0 once enough time has passed that the enemy could plausibly have marched there — confirming this is a temporary early-battle gate, not a reintroduced permanent directional bias. A full 10-battle regression (both recon modes) showed no crashes.

### 2026-09-08 — The camera widens instead of panning back and forth when there's action at both ends

*"Sometimes the screen oscillates back and forth when there is action at both ends. This is distracting. Don't let it happen."* Traced to `GameConfig.compute_camera_target_x`: its two branches each edge-chase one side back into view (pan west if something relevant sits past the left edge, pan east if something sits past the right), but the camera's own pan range (`CAMERA_MIN_X` to `CAMERA_DEFAULT_X`) is only 300px wide — genuinely simultaneous west-flank-and-far-east action has no single camera position that satisfies both branches at once, so each frame's fix undoes the other's.

The user's own proposed fix — "zoom out... zoom back in when it's no longer needed... don't let zooming oscillate either" — was refined once discussed further: rather than an actual Camera2D zoom (which shrinks every unit on screen), the fix physically WIDENS the map viewport instead, reclaiming the casualty dashboard's own screen footprint (a happy fit: the dashboard is 320px wide, and exactly `GameConfig.WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_PX` (300px) of extra width is what's needed to show the ENTIRE modeled world — west flank through the map's true east edge — at once, at full scale, with zero panning required). The user confirmed this reading before implementation ("reclaim its screen space to widen the map view" over two other readings offered).

**The fix:**
- **Detection** (`GameConfig.camera_relevant_span_px` + `camera_wants_wide_view`): the same `_camera_relevant_positions()` list the pan logic already uses, reduced to its own max-minus-min span. Two-threshold hysteresis (`CAMERA_WIDE_VIEW_ENTER_SPAN_PX` 900px / `_EXIT_SPAN_PX` 650px, a real gap between them) decides whether the wide state should be on THIS frame given the span and whatever it already was last frame — a span sitting anywhere between the two thresholds just keeps the current state, which is what stops the WIDE STATE ITSELF from flapping the same way the panning alone used to.
- **Camera position while wide**: a fixed constant, `CAMERA_WIDE_VIEW_CENTER_X` (the midpoint of the full west-flank-to-east-edge range) — not dynamically tracked, because once the viewport is widened to show that entire range, every possible relevant position is already visible regardless of exactly where it sits, so there's nothing left to track.
- **The reveal** (`main.gd._apply_wide_view_state`): `casualty_dashboard.modulate.a` fades smoothly (`CAMERA_WIDE_VIEW_FADE_SPEED`) rather than snapping invisible, so the widened map is revealed gradually from behind it. The actual `map_container`/`map_viewport` width change is a discrete step, not animated at that same rate — continuously resizing a SubViewport's backing render target every frame would reallocate GPU resources for no real benefit, unlike a plain float lerp. Widening happens immediately on entry (still-fading dashboard covers the reveal); narrowing back only happens once the dashboard is fully opaque again, so the viewport's real edge is never exposed uncovered in either direction. The dashboard's `mouse_filter` also flips to `IGNORE` while faded so clicks reach the map underneath instead of an invisible panel.
- Retreat/pause buttons and the combat log, unaffected siblings positioned in the same sidebar column, simply draw on top of the widened map behind them while this is active (they were already added to the scene tree after `map_container`) — left alone rather than relocated, since only the casualty dashboard's specific footprint was what needed reclaiming.
- `BattleManager._resupply_entry_point_for` (governs which map edge a resupply run visibly enters from) previously assumed a hardcoded `MAP_WIDTH_PX` half-width; now reads a newly pushed-down `current_camera_view_width` (same pattern as the pre-existing `current_camera_x`) so a resupply run still enters from the REAL edge of whatever's currently on screen, wide view or not.

**Verified:** direct tests on `camera_wants_wide_view` confirmed it won't enter below the enter threshold, will above it, and — the actual anti-oscillation guarantee — stays in whichever state it already had anywhere inside the hysteresis band; running a deliberately jittery span sequence through it start-to-finish produced exactly 2 state changes (one real entry, one real exit) instead of flapping on every jitter. `_resupply_entry_point_for` was confirmed to use the pushed-down view width correctly in both the normal and wide states, landing exactly on the true west edge (`-WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_PX`) once wide. A full project load and a 10-battle regression (both recon modes) showed no crashes. The actual on-screen fade/widen visual wasn't hand-verified in this pass (this project's UI can't easily be screenshot-tested from this environment — see the drone debug-snapshot entry above) — worth a look in the running game.

### 2026-09-08 — Correction: the casualty dashboard shouldn't disappear at all, permanently widen instead

*"I don't like the removal of the casualties pane. We should keep it. Maybe just make the window wider in the first place, so panning is not an issue."* Right after the entry above shipped. The whole runtime-toggle mechanism it described — hysteresis, a fading dashboard, a resizing SubViewport, `current_camera_x`/`current_camera_view_width` pushed down every frame — solved the oscillation but only by making the dashboard periodically disappear, which the user didn't want even temporarily. The simpler fix the user proposed removes the problem at its root instead: if the window (and the map viewport) is wide enough to show the WHOLE modeled world — west flank through the map's true east edge — from the very start, the camera never needs to pan or resize at all, so there's nothing left to oscillate and nothing to hide.

**The fix**: reverted essentially the entire previous entry's mechanism.
- `project.godot`'s `window/size/viewport_width` raised from 1360 to 1660 (exactly `+WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_PX`, 300px).
- `map_container`/`map_viewport` are now permanently `GameConfig.CAMERA_VIEWPORT_WIDTH_PX` (`MAP_WIDTH_PX + WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_PX`, 1300px) wide, set once in `_ready()` and never resized again.
- `map_camera.position.x` is now a fixed constant, `GameConfig.CAMERA_CENTER_X` (the midpoint of the full range) — never panned, because the viewport already shows the entire range at all times, so nothing is ever off-screen to pan toward.
- The sidebar column (doctrine panel, retreat/pause buttons, casualty dashboard, combat log) shifted right by the same 300px, now starting at the new `GameConfig.SIDEBAR_X` (replacing six separate hardcoded `1020` literals with one named constant while touching each of those lines anyway) — the casualty dashboard is never hidden or faded; it just lives 300px further right than before.
- Deleted as genuinely dead code, not just unused: `compute_camera_target_x`, `_camera_relevant_positions`, `CAMERA_MIN_X`/`_DEFAULT_X`/`_VIEW_MARGIN_PX`/`_FOLLOW_LERP_SPEED`, `current_camera_x`/`current_camera_view_width`, and everything from the immediately-preceding entry (`camera_relevant_span_px`, `camera_wants_wide_view`, the `CAMERA_WIDE_VIEW_*` constants, `_apply_wide_view_state`, `_wide_view_active`) — none of it has a reason to exist once the camera never moves.
- `BattleManager._resupply_entry_point_for` simplified to the two literal true edges (`-WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_PX` / `MAP_WIDTH_PX`) instead of reading a per-frame pushed-down camera position/width — there's no longer a "whatever's currently on screen" to track, since the whole world is always on screen.
- The mouse-driven elevation readout's screen-space bounds check (`main.gd._process`) updated from `MAP_WIDTH_PX` to `CAMERA_VIEWPORT_WIDTH_PX`, so hovering over the now-permanently-visible west flank still shows a reading instead of blanking out past the old, narrower boundary.

**Verified:** a direct test confirmed `_resupply_entry_point_for` now returns exactly `-WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_PX`/`MAP_WIDTH_PX` for the player/enemy side respectively (the fixed true edges, no camera state involved at all), a full project load showed no compile errors after removing all the now-dead symbols, and a 10-battle regression (both recon modes) showed no crashes. The actual on-screen layout (wider window, dashboard in its new position, no panning) wasn't hand-verified in this pass — worth a look in the running game, same caveat as the reverted entry above.

### 2026-09-08 — A mortar mid-relocation couldn't fire even at a perfect, in-range, ammo-available target

*"Right now, the mortar is moving towards an enemy squad. It has plenty of ammo, but it is not shooting at it. This is dead wrong."* `_tick_fire`'s own move-target gate is a single blanket rule: any unit with an active move order can't fire, full stop, with exactly one carved-out exception (`fighting_withdrawal`, a retreating SQUAD specifically — see the earlier "retreating units can still fire" entry). A mortar never got an equivalent exception, so ANY move order — a post-shot shoot-and-scoot hop, a threat-response displacement, a hunt toward a suspected enemy mortar — made it mechanically unable to fire for the entire walk, regardless of ammo or how good the shot was. Notably, "can't fire while moving" isn't wrong in general for a mortar (a crew can't lob shells while carrying the tube) — the real gap was that nothing ever asked "should I interrupt this walk because a good shot just showed up," the way `_update_friendly_mortar_hunting`/`_update_enemy_mortar_positioning` already do ("now in range — stop closing and get to work") for the one case they cover.

**The fix** (`_tick_fire`): the move-target gate now only blocks a MORTAR from proceeding to `_pick_target` when it's a squad-style ordinary move (unchanged for squads); a mortar always reaches target selection regardless of its move state. If `_pick_target` finds a real target while the mortar was still mid-relocation, the move is cancelled (`has_move_target = false`, `activity = STATIONARY`) right before firing — the same "stop and get to work" idiom, generalized from the two mortar-hunting functions to ordinary firing. Deliberately uniform across every reason the mortar might currently be moving, including an urgent counter-battery evasion already in progress (`evading_counter_battery` is consumed the instant `_relocate_mortar` is called, so there's no flag left to distinguish "this specific walk was safety-critical" after the fact) — a finer per-reason distinction is real but explicitly out of scope for this narrow fix; it's exactly the kind of thing the mortar decisionmaking rewrite below should reason about properly instead of a bolted-on special case.

**Verified:** a direct test reproducing the report (mortar with 20 rounds, fire timer ready, an active move order toward an arbitrary point, an enemy squad well within `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE`) confirmed the mortar now fires and its move order is correctly cancelled; a second check confirmed an ordinary (non-fighting-withdrawal) SQUAD move order still correctly blocks fire, unchanged. A 10-battle regression (both recon modes) showed no crashes.

**Looking ahead — a mortar decisionmaking rewrite, the natural next piece of the tactical rewrite.** The user's own framing: drone pathing's move to a unified, principle-driven destination-picker (see the 2026-09-08 entries starting with "Drone pathing rewrite" earlier in this doc) is "working well" — could the mortar's fire/relocate/hold decision get the same treatment? This is a good fit, not a tangent: mortar behavior today is exactly the kind of tangle the rewrite exists to replace — shoot-and-scoot, ammo hold-fire, threat-response relocation, safety relocation, and mortar-hunting are each their own independently-triggered mechanism, and this very bug (no interrupt-to-fire) is a symptom of that architecture rather than a one-off oversight. `[[tactical_rewrite_principles]]` memory now records the user's stated goal ordering for this piece: **preserve self, destroy enemy mortars, destroy dangerous squads, destroy other squads** — a ranked priority list, the mortar's own equivalent of the drone's tiered priority structure. Scoped as its own deliberate design pass, not started as part of this fix (per the user's own chosen pacing: quick fix now, design the rewrite next).

### 2026-09-08 — Mortar decisionmaking rewrite: one authoritative per-tick decision, tiered by the user's stated goals

*"Now start the rewrite."* Investigated before designing (verified every claim against the actual code, not taken on faith) and found the current architecture wasn't just messy — it was actively getting the user's own stated priority order backwards, with a real, previously-undiscovered bug as a direct consequence.

**What investigation found, before any code changed:**
1. **Hunting outranked self-preservation — the exact inversion of "preserve self" being goal #1.** In `_process`, the two mortar-hunting functions ran BEFORE `_tick_fire`, while the safety-relocation and threat-response functions ran AFTER — both gated on `not has_move_target`. Whenever a hunt lead existed, hunting claimed the mutex first and self-preservation never got a look in that tick. Confirmed directly against the actual call order, not assumed.
2. **Shoot-and-scoot could be silently defeated by hunting.** Neither hunting function checked `has_move_target` before overwriting a mortar's move order (documented as intentional — "re-aims every single tick" — for the hunting case specifically, but with an unintended side effect): a mortar could fire, shoot-and-scoot to a concealed position, and have that very displacement overwritten on the next tick by a hunt move toward the enemy, if a hunt lead happened to exist. Confirmed directly.
3. **`_pick_target` is not idempotent** (two `randf()` rolls — the ammo hold-fire chance, the weighted target pick) **and was being called 2-3 times per mortar per tick**, so the same mortar could get different answers to "do I have a shot" from different callers in the same tick — the mortar's own version of the flicker/thrash class of bug the drone rewrite hit.
4. **A reloading mortar (`fire_timer > 0`, the overwhelming majority of ticks) must still correctly know it has a shot** even though `_tick_fire` itself never reaches `_pick_target` on those ticks — a naive "just cache what `_tick_fire` resolved" approach would have made a reloading mortar think it had nothing to shoot and relocate constantly.
5. **The four stated goals don't map onto four symmetric tiers.** A mortar never chases squads by design (`MORTAR_MAX_RANGE` already dwarfs the range at which a squad becomes dangerous, and an existing reckless-destination check keeps the crew behind its own infantry screen) — tiers 1-2 govern movement, tiers 3-4 govern target *selection* and ammo willingness, already living inside `_pick_target`.

**The fix — replacing five independently-triggered functions racing over an informal `has_move_target` mutex with one authoritative per-mortar-per-tick decision:**
- **`_mortar_shot_this_tick(m, opposing)`** — the one place anything asks "does this mortar have a shot right now." Memoizes `_pick_target`'s result per mortar per tick (`_mortar_tick_shot`, cleared at the very top of `_process`, before `_tick_fire` runs) but computes it *lazily* if nothing has yet, so a reloading mortar still gets a correct answer when asked later in the same tick. Every mortar-relevant `_pick_target` call site now routes through this instead of calling it directly.
- **`_issue_mortar_move`/`_clear_mortar_move`** — the single writer/clearer of a mortar's move order (`move_target`/`has_move_target`/`move_queue`/`move_speed`/`movement_predictable`, plus a new `_mortar_move_intent[m]` tag — `"scoot"/"evade"/"conceal"/"out_of_ammo"/"linkup"/"hunt"`), replacing five repeated five-line blocks.
- **`_decide_mortar_action(m)`** — the tier ladder, run for every ACTIVE mortar on both sides via the new `_update_mortar_decisions()`. Step 0 resolves the tick's shot once via the memoized accessor; if there's a real shot, done — stand and fight (this is exactly the non-regression the earlier interrupt-to-fire fix bought, now formalized as the ladder's first check). Step 0b — the actual fix for finding 2 and 3 above — keeps an in-progress SELF-PRESERVATION walk (scoot/evade/conceal/out_of_ammo/linkup) sticky against being overwritten by a lower tier (hunt) or restarted by a same-tick coin-flip, the mortar's own version of the drone rewrite's sticky-until-arrival commitment. Then, in the user's own stated order:
  - **Tier 1 (preserve self)**: out-of-ammo (linking up with a close inbound resupply run instead, if the survival math favors it, or relocating for safety otherwise — precedence matches the old code exactly), a genuinely new trigger (a just-landed counter-battery near-miss now proactively displaces the crew instead of the flag just sitting there unused on a hold-position mortar, which was every enemy mortar), then spotted-with-nothing-to-shoot or an unwatched close threat.
  - **Tier 2 (destroy enemy mortars, movement half)**: the targeting half already always won in `_pick_target`; this is the "advance to bring a known enemy mortar into range, stop once there" behavior, now correctly gated by step 0b so it can no longer steal a tier-1 walk. Kept as ONE shared skeleton with two named per-team policy functions (`_mortar_hunt_fix_for`/`_mortar_hunt_destination_for`), not a merged body — the two old hunting functions differ in 8 real ways (the player's crew additionally avoids being seen while closing, stays within range of home, stays behind its own infantry screen, and caps how far it'll gamble on an unconfirmed lead; the enemy's simpler version has none of that), and a real merge would either become an unreadable per-team branch or silently hand the enemy concealment-aware routing it doesn't have today — an actual enemy-behavior change, out of scope for this pass.
  - **Tiers 3-4 (destroy dangerous/other squads)**: pure target-selection inside `_pick_target`, described next.
- **The one real targeting change**: a new `GameConfig.MORTAR_DANGER_HOLD_FIRE_OVERRIDE` (1.0) lets an especially dangerous squad override ammo-conservation hold-fire regardless of distance from the mortar — a genuinely different axis from the existing overrun-range override (which measures danger to the *crew*, a tier-1 question) since this one measures danger to the *force* (`_squad_danger_priority`'s own distance-to-nearest-friendly). Sliding with the danger score, not a threshold, matching this codebase's existing "five shots remaining should never be a magical number" principle. Routed through a new `_mortar_candidate_danger(unit, target)`, extracted from the existing `_mortar_target_value` so the override and the target-ranking weighting can never disagree on what "dangerous" means — critically, so a RETREATING squad near a friendly (pulling out, not fighting) can never be misread as a reason to burn the last rounds, the same trap the target-ranking code already avoided.
- **Visibility**: a new `mortar_decision_debug_snapshot()` (mirroring `drone_pilot_debug_snapshot()`), written unconditionally every frame to `debug_state/mortar_decision_snapshot.json` (both sides — an out-of-band developer file, not player-visible, same precedent as the history viewer's own deliberate ground-truth omniscience) via a generalized `main.gd._write_debug_snapshot(path, snap)` now shared with the drone's own write path. This is the direct payoff of "actual visibility" — the exact tool that would have made the original mortar-not-firing report trivially diagnosable without reading a line of code.

**Verified**, in four phases (pure-plumbing refactor, the tier ladder, the targeting change, visibility), each with its own headless smoke tests before moving on: a reloading mortar still correctly reports having a shot; the memoized shot accessor is idempotent within a tick and clears correctly between ticks; tier 1 never fires when a real shot exists; a dry mortar relocates or links up correctly; spotted-with-nothing-to-shoot relocation works on both sides (the earlier asymmetry fix didn't regress); **shoot-and-scoot survives an active hunt lead** — direct repro of finding 2, now fixed; **tier 1 beats tier 2 head-to-head** — spotted, no shot, and a valid out-of-range hunt lead all at once correctly displaces rather than hunts, the headline ordering fix from finding 1; a forced 20-tick visibility flicker issues exactly one move order, not one per flip; the danger override correctly overrides hold-fire for a squad in contact with a friendly, correctly does NOT for the same squad retreating or for one beyond `SQUAD_DANGER_RANGE`, and the pre-existing overrun-range override still wins absolutely; the debug snapshot is well-formed, JSON-serializable, and shows sensible, changing tier attribution across a full battle. A final 24-battle regression (both recon modes × both shoot-and-scoot settings) completed with no crashes. A full instrumented re-baseline against `pre-rewrite-baseline.json` (mirroring `characterize_doctrine.gd`'s own methodology) is recommended before drawing conclusions from aggregate stats, same caveat as every previous rewrite piece — this pass relied on targeted, direct verification of each specific behavior instead, which this session has repeatedly found more trustworthy than aggregate win/score numbers at reasonable trial counts anyway.

### 2026-09-09 — Retreat stopped short of the now-permanently-visible west flank

*"Retreat should complete at the left edge of the full map, not the left edge of the old smaller map."* A leftover dependency on a camera model this project already retired. The v83 west-flank design (`PLAYER_SAFE_X`/`PLAYER_EXTENDED_SAFE_X`, `Unit.retreat_extended`, `BattleManager._still_under_pressure`) deliberately kept most player retreats stopping at the old map's own edge (`PLAYER_SAFE_X`, ~12px past x=0) and only escalated a unit still under live pressure at that line into the west flank proper (`PLAYER_EXTENDED_SAFE_X`, 100m short of the true world edge) — reasonable at the time, since the camera only ever panned into the west flank on demand (`compute_camera_target_x`), so most retreats never needed to reveal it. That premise was removed by this session's own permanent-wide-viewport change (see the "camera view permanently" entry above): the west flank has been on screen at all times since, with no panning at all — so a retreat stopping at the old shallow line now just reads as stopping in the middle of clearly visible ground for no reason.
