**The fix**: collapsed the two-tier line to one. `PLAYER_SAFE_X` now holds the value `PLAYER_EXTENDED_SAFE_X` used to (100m short of the true world edge) and every player retreat targets it directly — `_step_retreat`'s escalation branch, `Unit.retreat_extended`, and `BattleManager._still_under_pressure` are removed outright as dead code, not superseded. `characterize_doctrine.gd`'s `player_retreat_extended` stat (percentage of retreats that escalated to the deep line) is removed along with it — the distinction it measured no longer exists. Enemy retreat (`ENEMY_SAFE_X`, its own home edge) is untouched, as before.

**Verified**: a direct test confirmed `PLAYER_SAFE_X` now sits out in the west flank (between the true world edge and x=0) rather than near the old map's own edge, and that a retreating unit with no known enemy anywhere near it — previously guaranteed to stop at the old shallow line — now walks all the way out and is marked WITHDRAWN exactly at the new line. Grepped the full `scripts/` tree afterward to confirm no remaining references to `retreat_extended`, `PLAYER_EXTENDED_SAFE_X`, or `_still_under_pressure`. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — Doctrine panel text spilling off the right edge of the window

*"When setting doctrine, part of the words at the far right are cut off."* `DoctrinePanel` extends plain `Control`, not `Container` — its `custom_minimum_size = Vector2(320, 500)` is a hardcoded number, never actually checked against what its own children need. Two rows inside it needed more than that: the retreat-threshold row packed a 264px-wide label next to a 140px-wide slider in an `HBoxContainer` (404px combined), and the shoot-and-scoot `CheckBox`'s own text ("Shoot and scoot (relocate after every shot)") alone measured 357px. Confirmed directly (a headless probe instantiating the real panel and reading `get_global_rect()`/`get_combined_minimum_size()` on every node after layout, rather than guessing from the source): the root `VBoxContainer` — anchored to fill the panel, but never shrinkable below its own content's minimum size — was actually drawn 408px wide against the panel's nominal 320px, and nothing anywhere clips the excess. This bug predates this session's camera work by unrelated width and shifted with it by the same amount (`SIDEBAR_X` and the window's own width were both widened by exactly the same 300px in the "camera view permanently" change), so the overflow past the window's right edge was ~68px both before and after — always there, just now noticed.

**The fix**: reflowed both rows to fit inside 320px instead of enlarging the panel (the sidebar's own width budget, `window width - SIDEBAR_X` = 340px, wouldn't have fit the checkbox's full text either way). The retreat-threshold label and slider now stack vertically instead of sharing a row — each comfortably fits 320px alone. The checkbox's own text shortened to "Shoot and scoot," with the "(relocate after every shot)" detail folded into the descriptive note already sitting below it, since a `CheckBox` draws its label on one line with no wrapping support.

**Verified**: re-ran the same headless layout probe after the change — the root `VBoxContainer`'s actual rect is now exactly 320px wide (matching the panel), with a combined minimum width of 264px, comfortably under budget; every row (label, slider, checkbox, both notes) renders within the panel's own bounds with nothing left to spill past it.

### 2026-09-09 — Variable-size attacking force: 1-3 enemy mortars, roughly 3x that in squads

*"Let's make the size of the attacking enemy variable. It could be anywhere from 1-3 mortars and 2-10 squads. The number of squads should be roughly 3x the number of mortars, but this should not be exact."* Applies regardless of recon mode (`_spawn_enemy_units` doesn't branch on it — the user confirmed this explicitly should hold for both). Previously the enemy force was a fixed 6 squads (`ENEMY_SQUAD_Y_OFFSETS_M`, 6 hand-placed y-offsets spanning -420m to +420m) and 2 mortars (`ENEMY_MORTAR_POSITIONS_M`, 2 hand-placed rear positions spanning 1300m-2500m in y) every single battle.

**The fix**: a new `BattleManager.roll_enemy_force_size()`, called once per battle from `_spawn_enemy_units`, rolls a mortar count uniformly in `[ENEMY_MORTAR_COUNT_MIN, ENEMY_MORTAR_COUNT_MAX]` (1-3), then a squad count targeting `ENEMY_SQUAD_PER_MORTAR_RATIO` (3.0) times that, jittered by a random `±ENEMY_SQUAD_COUNT_JITTER` (2) and clamped into its own separate `[ENEMY_SQUAD_COUNT_MIN, ENEMY_SQUAD_COUNT_MAX]` range (2-10) — the jitter is what makes the ratio "roughly," not exactly, 3x, and the separate clamp is what keeps a jittered outlier (e.g. 1 mortar rolling squads down to 1) inside the stated 2-10 floor/ceiling. The old fixed-position arrays became two generator functions, `GameConfig.enemy_squad_y_offsets_m(n)`/`enemy_mortar_positions_m(n)`, spreading `n` positions evenly across the exact same span the original hand-placed layouts used — 6 squads and 2 mortars still land almost exactly where they always did (the two counts that used to be the only options), any other count spreads that same width more or less densely, and a lone squad/mortar sits at the center/midpoint rather than at either extreme.

**Verified**: 2000 rolls of `roll_enemy_force_size()` confirmed every mortar count (1-3) actually appears, squad counts spread well beyond just the three exact ratio targets (3/6/9), the average ratio across all rolls lands at 3.02 against a 3.0 target, and only 19% of individual rolls hit that target exactly — genuinely "roughly," not exactly. Confirmed `enemy_squad_y_offsets_m(6)` and `enemy_mortar_positions_m(2)` reproduce the original hand-placed values exactly, and `enemy_mortar_positions_m(1)` lands at the original span's midpoint. 20 full `start_battle()` calls confirmed the real spawned unit counts always land inside both stated ranges. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode, forces now varying battle to battle across the whole range) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — An untouched mortar crew brings the gun when it retreats

*"When the mortar team retreats, if the team has not been hit, it should typically take the gun with it, not abandon it. This would change only in extremely dangerous circumstances."* `_tick_fire` treated EVERY retreating mortar as unarmed, unconditionally: "a retreating mortar crew has abandoned the gun the moment they started retreating, so there is nothing left to fire with regardless of how close anyone gets." That's the right call for a crew that's just taken a hit and chosen to flee rather than hold (`Unit._apply_crew_casualties`/`_mortar_crew_holds_position` already made that specific, already-nuanced decision) — but the exact same "abandoned" treatment also applied to a perfectly healthy mortar crew simply obeying a general retreat order, which real crew-served-weapon doctrine wouldn't do: you don't leave a functional tube behind just because you're pulling back, only because staying with it is genuinely too dangerous.

**The fix**: a new `Unit.mortar_gun_abandoned` flag, decided once, the moment retreat actually starts (`order_retreat`), not read as a blanket assumption at fire time. A crew that's already taken a hit (`crew_casualties > 0`) always abandons it — unchanged from before. An untouched crew rolls it fresh via `_mortar_gun_abandoned_on_unhit_retreat`: risk scales with the same `MORTAR_CREW_OVERRUN_DANGER_RANGE` proximity `_mortar_crew_holds_position` already uses, squared so a merely moderate threat still doesn't cost the gun — only something that's actually closed to real overrun range reliably does. `_tick_fire` now checks the flag instead of the kind: a retreating mortar that kept its gun fires exactly like a squad's fighting withdrawal (can still hit an in-range target on the way out); one that left it behind fires at nothing, same as every retreating mortar did before this change. `casualty_dashboard.gd`'s status text ("abandoned, crew fleeing" / "fleeing, gun abandoned") now only shows for a mortar that actually left the gun — a crew that kept it reads "falling back with the gun" instead, for both the player's own row and the enemy's last-known one.

**Verified**: 500 unhit crews ordered to retreat with nothing known nearby kept the gun 500/500 times; 500 unhit crews ordered to retreat with an enemy right on top of them (maximum overrun risk) abandoned it 500/500 times; 100 already-hit crews abandoned it 100/100 times regardless of how safe things looked, confirming that path is untouched. A direct `_tick_fire` check confirmed a retreating mortar with the gun kept actually fires and spends a round at an in-range target, while one with the gun abandoned fires at nothing and its ammo count never moves. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — Ammo conservation had no way to know a specific reason to hold back existed

*"Mortar ammo usage is unwise. I frequently see it firing away at squads when there are enemy mortars known to exist but currently out of range. This should only be done when the enemy squad is in a dangerous position."* Followed by a real refinement once the first design was described: *"Squad fire might also be done if ammo is plentiful. But that was not the case in what I just saw."* `_pick_target`'s existing sliding-scale hold-fire chance (`scarcity * (1.0 - urgency)`) already unifies ammo conservation in one place — no scattered per-tick mechanism to rewrite here, unlike the drone/mortar-movement rewrites earlier in this doc — but `scarcity` is purely a function of raw rounds remaining. It has no way to know that a specific, currently-unreachable, higher-value target exists: an enemy mortar out of `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE`, tracked by the exact same fix the movement side of tier 2 already hunts toward (`_mortar_hunt_fix_for`). A mortar sitting on a comfortable load never hesitated on an ordinary squad regardless of what it knew about an enemy mortar elsewhere, simply because scarcity itself started at 0.0 on a full tube.

**The fix**: `_mortar_ammo_scarcity` gained an optional `reserve` parameter — rounds treated as already spent, for squad-targeting purposes only, computed before scarcity itself is. `_pick_target` calls `_mortar_hunt_fix_for(unit)` (already computed elsewhere in the tier ladder, cheap and side-effect-free to call again) and reserves `GameConfig.MORTAR_AMMO_RESERVE_FOR_ENEMY_MORTAR_TRUSTED` (6 rounds) for a confirmed fix or `_UNTRUSTED` (3) for a bare suspected lead, out of a 20-round `MORTAR_STARTING_AMMO` load. This is a slice off the top, not a flat "always hold" rule — the second piece of feedback: a generous load barely notices 6 rounds reserved (scarcity only rises to 0.3, still spends fairly freely) and a mortar already partway through its load reads as meaningfully scarcer than its raw count alone would say. The existing danger-override (tier 3, dangerous squads) and overrun-range override (crew self-preservation) both still apply downstream unchanged — either can still force the shot regardless of how much the reserve has inflated hold-fire chance.

**Verified**: at a moderate 8/20 rounds, a distant, undangerous squad got engaged 41% of the time with no known enemy mortar, only 11% with a known trusted fix out of range — the reported behavior, fixed. At a full 20/20 load with the same trusted fix, engagement stayed at 70% — plentiful ammo isn't held back hard, addressing the follow-up correction directly. An untrusted lead landed in between (24%), reserving less than a trusted fix but still more than no fix at all. A squad placed in genuine contact with an enemy unit (`_mortar_candidate_danger` at its maximum) was engaged 100% of the time regardless of the reserve, confirming the danger-override still wins outright. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — The drone team's ground crew now considers evasive action against a closing threat

*"If the drone team sees an enemy coming towards it, it would consider taking evasive action, especially if the enemy appears likely to come very close."* The ground crew (`Unit.Kind.DRONE_TEAM`) had no proactive self-preservation at all — genuinely passive, sitting at its deployed position for the whole battle, moving only reactively: an actual hit (`_apply_crew_casualties`, unconditional retreat for this kind — no equivalent to the mortar's own hold-or-flee roll, since there's no "someone else takes over" option for a ground-control link) or a general retreat order. Unlike the mortar's own tier-1 self-preservation (a hard proximity threshold — within range, always relocate, no roll), the user's own framing here — "would consider... especially if... likely to come very close" — asked for a genuinely sliding, probabilistic response, not a hard trigger, so this one intentionally doesn't just copy that precedent.

**The fix**: a new `BattleManager._update_drone_team_evasion()`, called once per tick, DRONE_TEAM mode only, and only while the crew is ACTIVE and not already mid-relocation (`has_move_target` — once moving, it just walks there rather than re-rolling toward a new pick every tick). Only a known enemy actually CLOSING counts — heading roughly toward the crew's current position, not merely sitting nearby while moving away or across — via a new shared `_estimate_unit_velocity(u)` helper (extracted, behavior-preserving, from `_mortar_aim_point`'s own lead calculation, which now just calls it). Risk slides with proximity against a new `GameConfig.DRONE_TEAM_EVASION_RANGE` (1200m, matching `SQUAD_DANGER_RANGE`'s own "starts to be a live threat" scale — wider than the mortar's tighter 600m `MORTAR_CREW_OVERRUN_DANGER_RANGE`, since this is meant as an early, proactive watch rather than a last-second one), and the actual roll uses risk *squared* — a threat merely inside the watch range barely registers, one that's actually closed to real danger range reliably triggers, matching "especially if... very close" rather than an even chance across the whole range. On a successful roll, the crew relocates to `GameConfig.nearest_hidden_point` (same concealment search the mortar's own threat relocation uses, `avoid_buildings: false` this time — a ground team has no reason to avoid ducking into one) while staying fully ACTIVE throughout, not transitioning to RETREATING — this is a tactical reposition, not leaving the fight, mirroring the mortar's own "relocate, don't retreat" self-preservation tier.

**A real latent bug this surfaced and fixed along the way**: `_send_drone_home` snapshots `drone_team.global_position` into a returning drone's `move_target` once, at the moment RTB is ordered, never updated again — harmless as long as the ground crew never moves, which was always true until this feature. `_update_returning_drones` now re-aims that `move_target` at the crew's current position every tick, the same "always chase the current position" fix already established for the mortar resupply run, so a drone flying home mid-evasion still finds the crew wherever it actually ends up instead of orphaning itself at a stale point.

**Verified**: a threat well within range and heading directly at the ground crew triggered evasion 238/300 times (79%); the same threat's own kind sitting near the outer edge of the watch range triggered only 1/300 (0.3%); a threat just as close but heading away never triggered at all, confirming the directional gate actually works, not just proximity. Once relocating, 20 further calls in the same tick window never picked a new destination — no re-roll thrashing. A returning drone given a deliberately stale `move_target` was correctly re-aimed at the ground crew's actual current position on the next `_update_returning_drones()` call. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode, including DRONE_TEAM itself) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — A mortar with a live shot never even considered a known enemy mortar worth maneuvering for

*"There was a situation where enemy squads are approaching and in range, but still distant. An enemy mortar was slightly more distant, out of range, and being monitored by a friendly drone. The mortar chose to fire on the enemy squads. It would have made more sense to try to move to a location where it could take a shot at the mortar, do so, then move again for self preservation."* Root cause: `_decide_mortar_action`'s step 0 ("does this mortar have a shot right now") always won outright the instant `_pick_target` found ANY target — including an ordinary squad — with no comparison at all against tier 2's own known-enemy-mortar opportunity, even though tier 2 is supposed to outrank tier 3/4 (squads, dangerous or not) per the user's own stated priority order from the original mortar rewrite. Tier 2 was only ever reached when `_pick_target` returned null (no shot at all), never when it returned a real but lesser one.

The user's own framing, mid-design: *"I find myself thinking that rather than model the specific behavior, we need more general code that thinks at a higher level... the desired behavior would be to attempt to destroy [the enemy mortar] if that can be done with relatively low risk."* Followed by a further, more general correction once a first (mortar-hunting-specific) version of the fix existed: *"rather than code to mortar hunting specifically, friendly units should have an idea of the value of an enemy target and act accordingly."*

**The fix**: `_mortar_target_value` (mortar-only, squad-only) generalized and renamed to `_enemy_target_value(assessing_unit, target)` — the value of destroying/neutralizing ANY target, from any friendly unit's perspective, not a notion bolted onto one specific behavior. An ACTIVE mortar now reads as a fixed high value (`GameConfig.TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR`) in this function directly, alongside the existing squad case (casualty pips + `_target_danger_to_force`, itself renamed from `_mortar_candidate_danger` for the same reason). `_pick_target` now uses this shared value scale for a new decision, inserted before the existing ammo-scarcity logic: when a known/suspected enemy-mortar fix exists (`_mortar_hunt_fix_for`, the same signal tier 2's movement side already hunts toward) and it's actually safe to pursue — this crew hasn't been spotted, and nothing is already close enough to force a shot regardless (self-preservation, computed once up front and reused for both this and the existing overrun-override further down) — the chance of holding the available shot to go pursue the known opportunity instead scales as `known_target_value / (known_target_value + best_available_value)`. `TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR` so thoroughly outweighs any single squad's own value that this reliably (not absolutely, still a genuine probabilistic roll) favors the pursuit whenever it's live and safe — that falls naturally out of the value gap GameConfig already encodes, not a hardcoded "always prefer the mortar" rule. When the roll holds the shot, `_pick_target` returns null exactly as the ammo-conservation path already does, so `_decide_mortar_action`'s own tier ladder falls through to tier 2's existing movement logic completely unchanged — no new movement code needed at all. A spotted crew's existing "fire first, then get to cover" behavior is untouched (the new check is gated on being unspotted), and the crew-overrun self-preservation override still wins outright regardless of any opportunity, exactly as before.

**Verified**: the reported scenario (a trusted, drone-covered enemy-mortar fix out of range, an ordinary undangerous squad in range, mortar unspotted, nothing forcing a shot) held the available shot 96% of the time — reliably pursuing the known mortar instead, as intended. With no known enemy mortar at all, behavior was completely unaffected (0% hold beyond the pre-existing baseline). A spotted crew with the same trusted fix showed only the pre-existing ammo-reserve mechanic's own ~30% hold rate (that one doesn't require moving, so correctly still applies) — nowhere near the 96% the new pursuit preference produces, confirming a spotted crew's fire-first behavior is genuinely untouched. A threat within crew-overrun range forced the shot 100% of the time regardless of the known opportunity. An end-to-end check confirmed `_decide_mortar_action` actually reaches and acts on tier 2's own movement logic once the preference holds the shot. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — The sidebar assumed exactly 2 enemy mortars, and showed ones the player hadn't actually found

*"The sidebar should not assume that there are two enemy mortars. Only show mortars that are known to exist. Number could go as high as however many there are."* `CasualtyDashboard` pre-allocated a hardcoded `MAX_MORTARS_PER_SIDE = 2` mortar-status row for both sides — stale the moment the enemy's own force size became variable (see the "variable-size attacking force" entry above, `GameConfig.ENEMY_MORTAR_COUNT_MAX = 3`): a 3rd enemy mortar simply had no row to go in at all, silently dropped. Separately, every enemy mortar with an allocated row showed unconditionally, including one the player had never sighted or even caught firing once — rendered as a gray "status unknown" row rather than being hidden, which still reveals that a mortar exists there purely from the row's presence, the exact omniscience leak the rest of this dashboard's enemy-side fields already avoid (`_enemy_mortar_status_text` already only ever shows last-KNOWN state, never the live truth).

**The fix**: enemy row allocation now reads `GameConfig.ENEMY_MORTAR_COUNT_MAX` directly instead of a separate local constant that can drift out of sync again; the player's own side (always exactly one mortar, per `_spawn_player_units`) gets its own `MAX_PLAYER_MORTARS = 1`. `_update_mortar_rows` gained an `is_enemy` flag: for the enemy side only, a mortar is dropped from the list entirely (not shown as "unknown") unless the player's side has actually discovered it — `Unit.player_has_been_sighted` (set by `_refresh_visibility` for ANY player-side observer that spots it, a drone's own detection included, not just ground-unit LOS) OR a new `BattleManager.mortar_ever_detected_firing(mortar)`, a permanent version of the existing `mortar_recently_detected_firing` that never expires (has this mortar EVER been caught firing, not just recently) — a mortar heard from once, long enough ago that the RECENCY-gated check no longer reads it as "currently active," still correctly shows as a known-but-unknown-status gray row rather than disappearing again. The player's own mortar needs no such filter at all — there's no fog of war on your own units.

**A layout consequence, measured rather than assumed**: `CasualtyDashboard`'s own fixed height (`custom_minimum_size`) was sized against a measured worst case with only 2 enemy mortar rows (478px real height, `500` declared). A third wrapped mortar row measured directly at +47px on its own, projecting the new worst case to ~525px — grown to `550` with the same kind of real margin the original sizing used. `main.gd`'s `CombatLog` (positioned below the dashboard at a fixed y) moved down by the same 50px the dashboard grew (575→625), and the window itself grew from 700 to 760px tall to make room — safe to do in isolation since `GameConfig.MAP_HEIGHT_PX` (the map viewport's own fixed height) is completely independent of the window's actual height in `project.godot`; growing the window only gives the sidebar column more vertical room below the map, without touching the map/world's own scale at all (documented directly on `MAP_HEIGHT_PX` now, since the two numbers no longer being equal is easy to misread as a bug otherwise).

**Verified**: a scenario with 3 enemy mortars (one sighted, one only ever detected firing and now long expired for recency purposes, one genuinely never discovered) correctly allocated 3 rows but showed only 2 — the never-discovered one hidden entirely, not rendered as "unknown." Once all 3 became known, all 3 rows showed (no silent cap at the old hardcoded 2). The player's own mortar row always showed regardless of any sighting state. A direct layout measurement confirmed the dashboard's real content height (with a hand-built worst-case sample) fits comfortably within the new `550` budget, and the new `CombatLog` position plus its own fixed height fits within the new `760`px window. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — The drone didn't distinguish a mortar worth watching from one nobody had any plan for

*"Drone does not always have to hang over an enemy mortar, especially if we are not going to target it in the foreseeable future. That said, I think the value of an enemy mortar is still not high enough relative to a squad. One particular case: if there are multiple mortars and we are likely to target one of them, the drone should be at the one we are likely to shoot at."* `_drone_search_target`'s tier 1 ("live mortar contact") scored ANY currently-visible ACTIVE enemy mortar at a flat, unconditional `TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR` — the highest score on the whole board — regardless of whether any friendly asset had the slightest plan to actually engage it. With more than one visible at once, it also just returned the first one found in array order, with no notion of which one the friendly force was actually oriented toward.

Asked mid-design whether the surrounding code was solid or "a collection of special cases": it's the same disciplined tiered value-competition pattern as the mortar's own decision ladder — one function, each tier a comparable score, highest wins, every exception in the ladder documented with why — not scattered special-cased functions. The gap here was real but narrow: one tier's score didn't yet account for actionability.

**The fix**: a new `BattleManager._is_priority_hunt_target(target)` checks whether ANY active friendly mortar's own `_mortar_hunt_fix_for` (the exact resolver tier 2's movement and the mortar's own fire-vs-pursue decision already use) currently points at that specific unit — reusing existing, already-tested machinery rather than inventing a parallel heuristic. `_mortar_hunt_fix_for` itself now threads a `"unit"` reference through its player-side branches (previously position/trust only) so this match is possible at all. A new `_priority_visible_enemy_mortar()` replaces the old "first visible mortar wins" lookup: among all visible ACTIVE enemy mortars, one matching `_is_priority_hunt_target` is preferred outright over one nobody has a plan for — directly answering "if there are multiple mortars and we're likely to target one, be at that one." Tier 1 now scores the priority case at the full (and, per the second piece of feedback, bumped) `TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR` (100 → 150), and a non-priority one at a new, much lower `GameConfig.DRONE_NON_PRIORITY_MORTAR_WATCH_VALUE` (6) — deliberately below `TARGET_PRIORITY_SQUAD_MAX` (10) so a squad already in real contact can win the drone's attention, but still above 0, since general awareness of a found mortar's position still has some standing value even with no near-term plan for it.

**A real interaction caught before it shipped**: naively bumping `TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR` site-wide also inflated tier 6's own "might an undiscovered SECOND mortar be out there" sweep term (`TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR * _mortar_existence_confidence()`), which would have let pure speculation about a mortar nobody's even found yet out-bid a squad already in real, visible contact — exactly backwards from what this fix is for. Split into its own `GameConfig.TARGET_PRIORITY_UNDISCOVERED_MORTAR_SWEEP`, held at the original, pre-bump 100: the bump only applies to mortars that are actually actionable (seen live and matching a hunt-fix, a fresh fire-detection lead, or an active joint hunt commitment), not to speculative sweep value for one that hasn't been found at all.

**Verified**: a visible enemy mortar matching no friendly mortar's hunt-fix read as non-priority; the same mortar, once a joint hunt commitment pointed at it, flipped to priority. With two visible enemy mortars and the hunt-fix pointing at the SECOND one in array order, `_priority_visible_enemy_mortar` correctly picked that one, not the first. End-to-end: a genuinely dangerous squad (maximum danger score) beat a visible mortar nobody had a plan for; the same squad still lost outright to a mortar a friendly mortar was actually oriented toward. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — Restarting after a battle couldn't change the reconnaissance setup

*"'Set new doctrine and try again' can go back to the screen where the user chooses level 0 or level 1. Change the title of the prompt, too."* The AAR report's restart button went straight to `_show_deployment()`, skipping level select entirely — the recon mode (spotter vs. drone team) chosen at launch was locked in for the rest of the session; only doctrine (unit placement, retreat threshold, mortar behavior) could change between attempts.

**The fix**: `restart_button.pressed` now connects to the existing `_show_level_select()` (the same function launch itself uses — no new screen-transition logic needed) instead of `_show_deployment()`, so trying again offers a genuine choice of setup again, not just doctrine. Retitled to "Choose new setup and try again" to actually describe what it now does.

**Verified**: picking a recon mode, simulating a restart, and confirming it lands back on level select (not deployment) with the deployment screen fully torn down; picking the OTHER mode on that second pass actually took effect (`recon_mode` updated, deployment screen rebuilt for it) — proving this is a real choice each time, not a screen shown cosmetically while the mode stays locked from before.

### 2026-09-09 — A mortar fell back from a threat that couldn't actually see it

*"Right now in the current battle, the mortar is falling back, staying where it can hit enemy squads at extreme range. It would be a much better tactic to look for a shot at an enemy mortar. It has spotted two."* Followed, mid-diagnosis, by two sharpening corrections: *"This may have to do with 'preserve self' versus 'hunt enemy mortar'. Some risk to self is absolutely worthwhile to kill a mortar. In this case, the self risk would be pretty low,"* and then the actual root cause: *"The reason the self risk would be low is that we don't think the enemy has scouting on our position. I don't know if the code is aware of that part, but it's the key consideration."*

It wasn't. `_decide_mortar_action`'s tier-1 "threat closing" check (`_known_enemy_positions` filtered to `MORTAR_CREW_OVERRUN_DANGER_RANGE`) was pure proximity — any known enemy unit merely nearby triggered an immediate relocate-for-safety, with no check for whether that enemy could actually see the crew's position at all. A real crew, well concealed, doesn't panic just because someone happens to be marching past somewhere out of sight — the real question is whether the enemy has any way to actually find them, not whether they're geometrically close. `_pick_target`'s own, separate overrun-range override (forcing a shot regardless of ammo conservation once a candidate closes to the same range) had the identical gap, caught while verifying this fix rather than reported directly — a nearby-but-blind candidate was still treated as "about to overrun the position."

**The fix**: both checks now additionally require `GameConfig.has_direct_los` from the threat back to the mortar's own position — extracted into a new, directly-testable `BattleManager._unwatched_threat_closing(m)` for the tier-1 case (previously inlined). A stronger, deterministic bar than waiting on the real, gradual, probabilistic spot roll: genuinely blocked line of sight (terrain in the way) means no route to being found exists right now at all, not merely "hasn't happened yet." The REACTIVE overrun checks elsewhere (`_mortar_crew_holds_position`'s post-hit hold-or-flee roll) deliberately keep the old, LOS-agnostic proximity test — by the time either of those runs the crew has already been hit, so something can already reach them regardless of this specific range, a different question than "is anything about to find us."

**Verified**: a known enemy squad within overrun range but with line of sight genuinely blocked by the village building read as NOT closing (`_unwatched_threat_closing` false) — the crew correctly stayed put instead of panicking. The same distance with clear line of sight still read as closing, unchanged (regression check). End-to-end, reproducing the report directly: a blind, nearby squad plus two known (out-of-range) enemy mortars, one under an active joint hunt commitment — the mortar correctly proceeded to the hunting tier instead of falling back from a threat that couldn't see it. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — The drone wasn't told about a mortar's own solo hunt, only a team-wide one

*"A few minutes ago in tactical time, the drone was hovering over enemy mortar A, while the mortar was moving for a shot on enemy mortar B. This is wrong. The drone should have been supporting what the mortar was doing."* Followed by the generalization: *"The drone should be supporting important friendly action. In this case, that would have been the shot on mortar B."*

Root cause: `_drone_search_target`'s tier 3 ("joint mortar hunt") only ever watches `_joint_mortar_hunt_target` — and `_update_joint_mortar_hunt` only ever forms that commitment for a TRUSTED lead (a confirmed live sighting, or a fire-detection lead a drone is already covering), by design: an untrusted lead is meant to be a real, deliberate solo gamble the mortar takes on its own, not a team-wide commitment. But `_mortar_hunt_fix_for` — the SAME resolver the mortar's own tier 2 uses to decide where to walk — has no such restriction; it happily walks toward a bare, untrusted lead too. The drone's own tier 1 (`_priority_visible_enemy_mortar`, from the earlier "drone watches the mortar we can act on" fix) only helps pick among CURRENTLY VISIBLE mortars; it can't redirect the drone toward one that isn't visible at all, which an untrusted lead — by definition not confidently placed — often isn't. The result: the mortar could commit to and start walking toward an untrusted lead on mortar B while the drone, with no signal at all that this had happened, kept watching whatever else scored highest on its own (mortar A, via a closer, more range-eligible fire-detection lead).

**The fix**: a new tier, inserted right after the joint-hunt one, generalizing its own principle rather than special-casing this one instance — the comment on it says so directly: the drone should back up whatever important friendly action is actually committed to and in progress, not just a formally-promoted shared one; today's only such action is a mortar's own hunt, so that's where it lives for now, but a future second kind of committed action belongs in the same tier, not a copy of it. Checks every ACTIVE friendly mortar's own `Unit`-keyed `_mortar_move_intent == "hunt"` (set the instant `_decide_mortar_action`'s tier 2 actually commits to the move) and, if set, reads its own `_mortar_hunt_fix_for` position directly — the exact same resolver the mortar itself already trusted, so the drone can never end up disagreeing with what the mortar actually chose. Scored at the same full `TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR` the joint tier uses (the two tiers naturally agree and never conflict when a lead IS trusted, since both then point at the identical position).

**Verified**: with mortar A the best in-range fire-detection lead (what the drone's own tier 2 would otherwise pick) and mortar B — out of range, more recently detected overall — what `_mortar_hunt_fix_for` actually resolves the friendly mortar's own hunt to, the drone correctly followed the mortar to B once its intent was set to `"hunt"`, not tier 2's own in-range-only pick of A. With the SAME setup but a non-hunt intent (mid shoot-and-scoot), the new tier correctly stayed out of it, falling back to tier 2's pick of A unchanged. The pre-existing trusted/joint case (mortar not currently visible, a real joint commitment) still resolved via its own original tier, unaffected by the new one running immediately after it. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — A mortar that had drifted from its own lines could never hunt again

*"Mortar is failing to close on and engage enemy mortars, saying 'no acceptable route'. But it should be finding an acceptable route. The enemy is not likely to be near friendly setup region."* Confirmed via the newly-merged decision inspector's own captured state: the mortar's actual position at the moment of that decision was (81m, 455m) — hard against the map's own west edge, and well outside any plausible friendly-squad Y-band (squads cluster near the village around y≈1700-1900m).

Root cause: `_friendly_mortar_hunt_destination_is_reckless` rejected a candidate destination outright if it fell ahead of the frontmost active friendly squad's x, OR outside the vertical band those squads occupy — an ABSOLUTE rule, with no notion of where the mortar itself currently stands. That's the right call for a mortar starting from a normal position near its own line. It's the wrong call for one that's already drifted well outside that footprint — through an earlier, entirely unrelated self-preservation relocation (shoot-and-scoot, evading a threat, whatever pushed it there had nothing to do with hunting) — since ANY hunt destination for an already-outside-the-band mortar reads as "outside the band" under an absolute rule, permanently locking it out of ever hunting again until something else (never itself, since this check gates its own hunt movement) moved it back inside first.

**The fix**: the check now compares the destination's exposure against the mortar's OWN current exposure, not an absolute line — rejecting a destination only if it's newly ahead of the squads' front (when the mortar wasn't already ahead) or newly outside their Y-band (when the mortar wasn't already outside it). A mortar already outside the footprint is free to move as long as it isn't making its own position worse; one starting normally positioned keeps the exact original behavior, since "already ahead"/"already outside" are both false for it by construction.

**Verified**: a mortar already well outside the squads' band (matching the reported position) was correctly allowed a destination that stayed outside the band but wasn't ahead of the line — no longer permanently stuck. The same mortar was still correctly blocked from a destination that would put it newly ahead of the line, drift or not. A mortar in an ordinary, normal position was still correctly blocked from newly advancing ahead of the line or leaving the band, confirming the original behavior is untouched for the common case. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — The dashboard's enemy personnel total revealed the enemy's true order of battle, not what had actually been scouted

*"The right window should not show the user the number of enemy personnel that exist. It should only show the number we know are there."* `_compute_side_stats`'s `pips_total` accumulation was, by original design, unconditional for every non-drone, non-resupply-run unit regardless of whether the player had ever sighted it — the function's own doc comment even spelled this out as deliberate: a never-sighted unit contributed to the known TOTAL order of battle but not to casualties. That's backwards from real fog of war: how many personnel the enemy fields AT ALL is exactly the kind of fact a real commander only has as good as their own reconnaissance, not a number handed over for free just because `BattleManager` itself is omniscient.

**The fix**: `pips_total`'s accumulation now also gates on `Unit.player_has_been_sighted` — but ONLY for the specific case this dashboard uses (`estimated == true and is_enemy_side == true`); a unit never sighted at all is excluded from the total, while one sighted at some point and later lost track of (withdrawn, retreating) still counts, since its existence was genuinely confirmed even though its current condition wasn't. `casualty_percent`'s own division needed no separate change — it was already reading `pips_lost`, which independently already skips any never-sighted unit for this same estimated-enemy case (a pre-existing, correct guard on the numerator); the two now agree by construction, not by coincidence.

**Blast radius checked directly**: `casualty_stats(ENEMY)` (the live dashboard, and the only intended target of this fix) is affected. `_end_battle`'s own unheld-position AAR figure (`_compute_side_stats(enemy_units, not held, true)`) is also affected when the position wasn't held — correctly so, since an AAR for a position you didn't hold shouldn't hand you the true order of battle either. The AAR's own unconditional `true_enemy_stats` (always full omniscience, used for the historical record regardless of fog of war) and `battle_manager.gd`'s two internal omniscient calls (enemy commander retreat-threshold logic) are both untouched, since neither ever passes `estimated=true, is_enemy_side=true`. The player's own `casualty_stats(PLAYER)` is unaffected — `player_has_been_sighted` is only ever populated for enemy units, and the new gate is itself conditioned on `is_enemy_side`.

**Verified**: an enemy side with one sighted squad, one never-sighted squad, and one sighted-then-withdrawn squad — the estimated (dashboard) total counted only the two confirmed ones, correctly excluding the never-sighted one entirely; the same call with default omniscient parameters counted all three, confirming the internal/AI-facing path is untouched. The player's own side, with a squad that had never triggered `player_has_been_sighted` (a flag that's simply never populated for player units), still counted in full, confirming the gate can't accidentally leak onto the player's own totals. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — Friendly squads left the village to "screen" a mortar flank threat that was actually retreating

*"I saw friendly forces leaving the village when they didn't need. There was only one enemy squad known to exist. They may have been pulling out to protect the mortar? Not sure."* Confirmed: `_update_friendly_squad_positioning`'s mortar-protection tier pulls the nearest idle squad out to screen the single closest known enemy within `MORTAR_FLANK_THREAT_RADIUS` (1500m — well beyond `SQUAD_DANGER_RANGE`'s own 1200m) of the friendly mortar that has no squad already covering the direct lane to it. `_nearest_unscreened_mortar_threat` sourced its candidate list from `_known_enemy_positions`, which treats RETREATING exactly like ACTIVE ("still a threat" — correct for the mortar's own self-preservation checks, where a withdrawing unit can still shoot back on the way out). But a RETREATING squad isn't preparing to overrun anything; it's leaving the fight. This is the same class of bug already fixed twice this session on the mortar's own side (`_target_danger_to_force`'s and `_mortar_target_value`'s RETREATING exclusion, `_smoke_test_retreating_danger.gd`) — this particular consumer had just never picked it up.

**The fix**: `_nearest_unscreened_mortar_threat` now takes the actual enemy `Unit` array instead of a bare position list, and only counts a candidate that's `Unit.Kind.SQUAD`, `Unit.State.ACTIVE`, and visible — matching the same ACTIVE-only standard the rest of the codebase already applies to squad danger. A RETREATING (or MORTAR-kind, or no-longer-visible) enemy can no longer pull a squad out of position to screen it.

**Verified**: a single enemy squad, RETREATING, sitting well within the flank-threat radius with an open lane to the mortar — `_nearest_unscreened_mortar_threat` now correctly returns no threat (`Vector2.INF`), leaving the screening squad free to stay in the village. The identical squad, still ACTIVE at the same position, still correctly triggers the screening response — confirming this is a RETREATING-specific exclusion, not a break of the underlying mechanic. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — A scouted enemy mortar could get silently dropped, leaving the mortar shooting squads with nothing held back

*"Just now, the mortar used either all or all but one of its rounds on advancing enemy squads, even though the enemy was known to have a mortar. The mortar was currently out of range, but still, this is VERY wrong. We seem to be going in circles on this type of behavior,"* followed, once the underlying mechanism was named directly, by *"It should NOT even be an issue of a fixed ammo reserve. Hunting the mortar is more important, even if it takes some time to line up a shot,"* and then the fully generalized version: *"the mortar seems to prefer shooting at available squad targets over trying to find a way to get a shot at a mortar we have scouted, even when this latter course can be done at relatively low risk (as the enemy has not scouted us). This is backwards."*

Root cause, traced directly (not guessed at): `_mortar_hunt_fix_for`'s player-side branch would only ever surface a lead as a usable "fix" in two cases — a live team-wide `_joint_mortar_hunt_target` commitment, or a bare UNTRUSTED lead. A TRUSTED lead (a currently-visible enemy mortar, the strongest kind of knowledge there is) with no joint commitment yet formed for it fell through a third, silently-discarding branch: `if lead.is_empty() or lead.trusted: return {}` — treating "trusted but not (yet) a team commitment" as if nothing were known at all. `_update_joint_mortar_hunt` declines to form that commitment for entirely legitimate reasons (the resulting hunt point would land outside `MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_RANGE_FROM_HOME`, or read as reckless per the mortar-drift fix earlier this session) — but whenever it declined, this mortar's OWN pursuit of that exact same lead was thrown away too, not just the team's drone-escorted version of it. With no fix at all, both consumers went dark: `_pick_target`'s ammo-conservation pursuit-hold (gated on a real `enemy_mortar_fix`) never got a chance to run, and `_decide_mortar_action`'s tier-2 hunting movement had nothing to walk toward — leaving ordinary squad engagement as the mortar's only live option, every tick, regardless of how valuable or safe pursuing the known mortar would have been.

**The fix, in two parts.** First, `_mortar_hunt_fix_for` no longer discards a trusted lead just because the team commitment didn't form: it falls through to the lead directly, passing its real `trusted` value through unchanged. This can't send the mortar anywhere the team path itself would have refused — `_mortar_hunt_destination_for` independently re-applies the exact same home-range and reckless checks for the movement side regardless of trust, so a lead outside safe bounds still correctly produces "no acceptable route," it just isn't discarded before ever getting that far. Second, per the explicit instruction to stop treating this as an ammo-reserve problem: `_pick_target`'s pursuit-hold (the probability of holding a squad shot to preserve ammo for a known, valuable mortar opportunity) is no longer gated on `not unit.is_visible` — being spotted doesn't make the enemy mortar any less worth pursuing, and the old worry that holding fire while spotted would strand an exposed crew doing nothing is already handled elsewhere: if the hold leaves the mortar with no shot, `_decide_mortar_action`'s own tier 1 ("Spotted... relocating for cover") reacts to that same spotted state on the very same tick regardless of whether a shot was taken. The separate fixed-reserve mechanism (`MORTAR_AMMO_RESERVE_FOR_ENEMY_MORTAR_TRUSTED`/`_UNTRUSTED`, a flat 6 or 3 rounds taken off the top) is removed outright — an arbitrary round count was never really doing the conservation work; the value-based pursuit-hold comparison (`TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR` vs. the best available squad, already a huge margin) is what actually should, and does the job on its own.

**Verified**: `_mortar_hunt_fix_for` on a currently-visible enemy mortar with no joint commitment now correctly returns a trusted fix pointing at that exact unit (previously empty). `_pick_target`, given that same out-of-range trusted lead plus an ordinary in-range enemy squad, correctly held fire to preserve ammo for the mortar — even with the friendly mortar itself already spotted, the case the old code explicitly excluded. A squad closing to genuine overrun range still correctly forced a shot regardless of the known mortar opportunity, confirming self-preservation still wins outright. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — Default doctrine: shoot-and-scoot on, retreat threshold 50%

*"Mortar shoot and scoot should default to on. Casualties for retreat should default to 50%."* Two starting-doctrine defaults, changed directly (not a bug fix — a deliberate default-setting change):

- `DoctrinePanel`'s "Shoot and scoot" checkbox now starts checked (was unchecked).
- The "baseline" (Original doctrine) `CommanderProfile` preset's `retreat_threshold` changed from 0.30 to 0.50 — this is what `DoctrinePanel._on_profile_selected(0)` actually applies to the retreat slider on startup (the slider's own literal initial value, updated to match for source clarity, is overwritten by this the instant the panel builds its profile section either way). `Unit.retreat_threshold`'s own bare class-default was updated to match (0.30 → 0.50) for the same reason — real gameplay always overwrites it from doctrine before a battle starts, but a freshly-constructed `Unit` with nothing else set should still read the current intended default, not a stale one.
- `characterize_doctrine.gd`'s own `RETREAT_THRESHOLD` constant (and its `shoot_and_scoot: false` default) is a deliberately FROZEN pre-rewrite baseline value, not a live mirror of the panel's default — left untouched, per that file's own stated purpose of comparing against a fixed historical snapshot; its comment was updated to stop claiming to track the panel's current default.

**Verified**: a fresh `DoctrinePanel` instance reports `get_retreat_threshold() == 0.50` and `get_mortar_doctrine().shoot_and_scoot == true` immediately after `_ready()`, with no other interaction — confirming both new defaults take effect exactly the way a player starting a fresh battle would see them.

### 2026-09-09 — The mortar structurally never reached its own "destroy enemy mortars" tier

*"Right now, the mortar is pulling back. It should be looking for shots at enemy mortars."* Followed by a broader, sharper version once the pattern repeated across several reports: *"the mortar seems to prefer shooting at available squad targets over trying to find a way to get a shot at a mortar we have scouted, even when this latter course can be done at relatively low risk (as the enemy has not scouted us). This is backwards,"* and, after a first partial fix, an explicit instruction not to declare victory on code-reading alone: *"Please, test for mortar behavior in the early part of the battle. Under current rules, it should usually find itself hunting enemy mortars. If it isn't, something is wrong,"* and *"Don't report this as 'fixed' until you see that happening."*

Diagnosed with a purpose-built instrumented harness (`TestBattleManagerScript`, real battles, no UI) rather than by reasoning about the code alone — direct per-tick traces of `_mortar_reasoning`/`_mortar_hunt_fix_for`/`_pick_target`'s own recorded evidence, weighted by actual tactical seconds elapsed (a plain tick count wildly over-weights whichever stretch happens to run at `TIME_SCALE_NORMAL`, since `TIME_SCALE_FAST_FORWARD` can cover many tactical minutes in a single macro-tick). The very first measurement: 0.0% of all tactical time with a known enemy mortar was ever spent in the "Destroy enemy mortars" tier, across 16 trials. Three separate, compounding root causes, each individually enough to explain a real report:

1. **A RETREATING-but-still-visible enemy mortar was invisible to every hunt-lead source.** `_known_enemy_mortar_lead()`'s live-visibility check (and its two other sources, added below) all required `state == Unit.State.ACTIVE` — but a mortar crew that's just taken a hit decisive enough to flee doesn't abandon the gun (`order_retreat`'s own reasoning: "it's just walking it somewhere else"), and `Unit.is_targetable()` itself already allows firing at a RETREATING unit. The instant the player's own mortar wounded an enemy mortar crew enough to make it flee — arguably the moment it most deserved finishing off — every lead-resolution path started reporting nothing was known about it at all.
2. **No memory of a mortar's last-seen position once it stopped being currently visible or recently-firing.** `_known_enemy_mortar_lead()`'s two existing sources (live visibility, then a short-lived muzzle-flash detection window) both forget a mortar entirely the instant it goes quiet for longer than `MORTAR_FIRE_DETECTION_EXPIRY` — extremely common, since a real mortar isn't obligated to fire every single reload cycle. A trace showed exactly this: an enemy mortar directly engaged for several shots, then went quiet; the very next tick, the fix disappeared, and 13 rounds went to ordinary squads with zero ammo conservation before the mortar next happened to fire and briefly reappear.
3. **Shoot-and-scoot's own post-fire relocation was uninterruptible by hunting, for however long the walk actually took.** `_decide_mortar_action`'s step 0b treats an in-progress "scoot" exactly like a genuine emergency (evade/conceal/out-of-ammo/linkup) — sticky until arrival, no lower tier allowed to interrupt it. A single scoot leg, at real m/s speeds over a real map, can take several minutes of tactical time to complete — a trace showed a single scoot blocking tier 2 for over 300 tactical seconds straight. With shoot-and-scoot now defaulting to ON (see the entry directly above), this became the dominant lockout: a routine precaution was outranking the very "destroy enemy mortars" priority the user set at the top of this whole rewrite.

**The fix, matching each cause.** (1) `Unit.is_targetable_state()` (ACTIVE or RETREATING) factored out and used throughout `_known_enemy_mortar_lead()` in place of a bare `state == ACTIVE` check — the same standard `is_targetable()` and `_known_enemy_positions()` already apply elsewhere. (2) New `Unit.player_known_position`/`player_known_position_time`, populated by `_update_player_intel()` the same tick-scoped way `player_known_pips`/`player_known_state` already are (only while `is_visible`, frozen thereafter) — `_known_enemy_mortar_lead()` gained a third, UNTRUSTED, non-expiring fallback to it once the first two sources come up empty, so a mortar that's gone quiet is still a real, pursuable lead, just not a confidently-placed one. (3) `"scoot"` removed from step 0b's sticky-intent list — only genuine emergencies stay uninterruptible now; falling through can't actually disrupt an ongoing scoot's own movement either way, since every tier below only overwrites `move_target` when it finds something more important to do, and reports "Holding" otherwise while the scoot keeps walking untouched. A small related cleanup: `_update_mortar_decisions()` was leaving `_mortar_reasoning` frozen at its last value forever once a mortar stopped being ACTIVE (destroyed/retreating/withdrawn) — now overwritten with an honest "No longer in action" note, so the Decision Inspector doesn't keep showing a stale "Target available" for a crew that's long gone.

**Also added**: a "Copy" button on the Decision Inspector panel (`DecisionInspector`), copying its displayed text to the clipboard — directly requested mid-diagnosis to make pasting inspector output back for review less tedious, and likely to matter again for the same reason.

**Verified, empirically, not just by re-reading the code**: the same instrumented harness, re-run after each fix, showed the "Destroy enemy mortars" tier going from a hard 0.0% to 4.9%, then to 14.9% of all known-enemy-mortar tactical time after all three fixes, with the dominant recorded detail shifting to "In range of a known enemy mortar" — a completed hunt, not just an attempt. A **new permanent regression test**, `scripts/tests/test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd`, keeps this measurement in the repo going forward (asserting at least a 2% floor, not today's exact number, since tuning is expected to move it) — this was genuinely hard to pin down across several rounds of reports, worth guarding against silently regressing again. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — A mortar position could stockpile ammunition without limit

*"I also wonder if there should be a limit to how much ammo the friendly mortar can reasonably have with it? Would too much ammo slow it down? If there is a surplus of ammo, would the resupply run wait in a rear area? Please do real-world research and implement appropriate rules."* Confirmed directly: `_resolve_resupply_run_arrivals` added `MORTAR_RESUPPLY_ROUNDS` unconditionally on every delivery, with no ceiling at all — a mortar that kept requesting and receiving waves without ever running dry could accumulate an unbounded stockpile.

**Research** (scoped to this game's own early-Russia-Ukraine, man-portable-mortar setting per `[[project_scenario_setting]]` — the user separately confirmed the crew is man-portable, on foot, not vehicle-mounted, matching the existing walking-pace `MORTAR_RELOCATE_SPEED`): a single medium mortar HE round runs close to 9-10 lb (4-4.5 kg) fully assembled; the tube, base plate, and bipod are already a multi-man carry on their own. Real logistics doctrine (US ATP 3-21.90 and general ammunition-supply-point practice) prescribes a unit's "basic load" — the quantity kept on hand to sustain it until the next resupply, limited by what can actually be transported and stored at the position — with the bulk of any surplus deliberately staged/cached farther back at an ammunition supply point rather than pushed all the way forward "just in case." No single authoritative "rounds per tube" figure could be pinned down in the time available, so the actual cap below is a judgment call grounded in that doctrine's shape, not a cited number — consistent with how every other probability/threshold in this file is already handled. Also confirmed: 81/82mm is not the smallest infantry mortar in general use — 60mm is the standard lighter "light mortar" class (used at platoon/company level; Ukraine itself fields the Polish-made LMP-2017 60mm) — relevant context, not something this change acts on.

**The fix**: new `GameConfig.MORTAR_MAX_AMMO_ON_HAND` (30 — a modest 1.5x the 20-round starting load, reflecting a slightly-larger-than-initial ready pit rather than a second full load sitting exposed next to the gun), applied symmetrically to both sides since this is a real physical constraint, not a fog-of-war rule. Answering the user's second question directly: **yes**, a resupply wave now holds back in the rear rather than making the trip — `_update_mortar_resupply`'s arrival handling checks the cap right before committing a physical run to the trip (requests themselves stay ungated on ammo level, per that function's own existing, deliberate reasoning: the supply chain should start moving the moment a fight looks likely, not after the guns run dry) and simply never spawns the `RESUPPLY_RUN` unit when the position is already stocked up, logging "resupply held in the rear — position already well-stocked" instead. Delivery is ALSO capped at arrival (`_resolve_resupply_run_arrivals`), not just at dispatch — a run already in transit could still arrive to find the position closer to full than expected if the mortar simply didn't fire much in the meantime, so it delivers only what actually fits rather than overshooting the ceiling. On the first question — whether excess ammo would slow the crew down — decided this doesn't need a separate mobility penalty: the cap itself already keeps the carried load within a modest, realistic band (never more than 1.5 loads), so there's no meaningful surplus left to model a speed penalty for.

**Verified**: a wave due to arrive while the mortar was already at the cap correctly held back — no physical run ever appeared, ammo unchanged. A run already in transit, arriving at a mortar that had barely fired since departure (5 rounds short of the cap), delivered only the 5 rounds that actually fit rather than the full wave, landing exactly at the cap with the run still properly consumed. A wave arriving well below the cap dispatched normally, confirming the ordinary case is untouched. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — The drone ignored the player's own general retreat entirely

*"Even during a general retreat, the drone team would, when possible, support the retreat by scouting for safe routes."* Confirmed directly: `player_general_retreat_ordered` (set by `order_general_retreat`) was never once read anywhere in `_drone_search_target()` — only the ENEMY's mirror flag (`enemy_general_retreat_ordered`) had any effect on drone priorities (finishing off retreating contacts, discounting the routine sweep). Once the player called a general retreat, the drone just kept running its ordinary tier ladder — hunting mortars, tracking squads, sweeping for contacts — as if nothing had changed, even though `order_general_retreat` (unlike the enemy's own version, which deliberately exempts mortars) pulls EVERY ACTIVE player unit into retreat, mortar included, so most of those tiers have nothing real left to support anyway.

**The fix**: a new tier, `_retreat_route_scout_target()`, checked right after the mortar-support tiers and before ordinary squad-tracking. Once `player_general_retreat_ordered` is true, it picks the retreating (or not-yet-caught-up-to-its-order) player unit with the LONGEST remaining walk to its own current destination — the one most exposed for the longest, and so the most worth clearing a path for — excluding `RESUPPLY_RUN` (not a combat unit anyone's escorting) and `DRONE` (already airborne). The scouted point sits `GameConfig.DRONE_RETREAT_SCOUT_LOOKAHEAD_FRACTION` (0.6) of the way along that unit's own remaining route, not its current position — its own eyes already cover where it's standing right now; the point of sending the drone ahead is ground it hasn't reached yet, the way a real reconnaissance element screening a withdrawal covers the routes the main body is using rather than trailing behind it. Scored at the same `TARGET_PRIORITY_MORTAR` level as the mortar-support tiers — high enough to beat ordinary squad-tracking (this codebase's own existing precedent already has procedural priorities like flank-watch outranking squad-tracking's own ceiling, not just ad hoc for this case) but still yielding to anything that already legitimately reached that same top tier.

**Verified**: with no general retreat ordered, returns nothing regardless of unit state. With one ordered but no unit holding an active move order, still returns nothing (nothing left to screen). With several retreating units at once — including a RESUPPLY_RUN and a DRONE deliberately placed farthest away to confirm they're excluded — correctly picked the SQUAD with the longest remaining route, at exactly the configured 0.6 lookahead fraction toward its destination. End-to-end, with nothing else competing, `_drone_search_target()` actually selected the "Screening the retreat route" tier and the computed lookahead point, not a stale tier from before the retreat was called. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — The "Damage by unit" AAR report read as if a unit's own casualties were what it suffered

While reading a real AAR report to answer *"what destroyed the friendly mortar,"* the top summary line — `"Player Mortar 1: 15 casualties / 20 shots / 1 CB strikes"` — reads naturally as "this mortar took 15 casualties," but `UnitCombatStats.damage()` actually accumulates `casualties` on the ATTACKER's own row (casualties it INFLICTED on whoever it hit), confirmed by cross-checking that the 15 exactly matches the sum of that unit's own "Against ..." lines further down. What actually destroyed the mortar (a real answer, found in the DETAILS section instead) was a combined effect: 2 crew casualties from one enemy squad's direct fire and 2 more from an enemy mortar's counter-battery fire, together accounting for the full 4-person crew — not visible anywhere in the misleading top-line number. Asked directly whether to fix the wording: *"OK, change the wording to make it more obvious."*

**The fix**: `UnitCombatStats.report_lines()` reworded throughout to make "inflicted, not suffered" explicit rather than implied — the top note now reads "people this unit killed or wounded ON THE ENEMY (what it dealt out, not what it took)," the per-unit summary line says "N casualties inflicted," the DETAILS section gained an explicit pointer ("each row is what THAT unit dealt out; to see what a unit received, look for it under 'Against ...' in another unit's own row below"), and each detail row now reads "X inflicted: ..." instead of a bare "X: ...". No data or structure changed, wording only.

**Verified**: `test_unit_doctrine.gd`'s own existing report-content check (`report.contains(shooter.display_name())`) still passes unchanged. A small standalone print confirmed the new wording reads correctly end to end. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — Playback speed dropdown (1/4x–4x)

*"I want a playback speed dropdown. It can range from 1/4 speed to 4x speed."* New `BattleManager.playback_speed` (default 1.0), multiplying `delta` exactly once at the very top of `_process` — before it's used for `elapsed_time`, `scenario_delta`, or anything else — so the entire simulation (tactical clock, movement, combat, and the real-time-based visual/animation timing like fire-tracer fade) speeds up or slows down uniformly, the same way a video player's speed control works, rather than needing every individual timing calculation in the file to separately know about it. `is_paused`'s own early-return in `_process` sits ahead of the multiply, so pausing still freezes everything outright regardless of the selected speed — pausing at 4x is exactly as frozen as pausing at 1x.

**The fix**: a `main.gd` `OptionButton` (`speed_dropdown`), five choices — 0.25x/0.5x/1x/2x/4x, doubling steps reading naturally the way camera zoom or audio playback controls usually do — each item's own text carrying its label ("Speed: 1x", etc.) rather than a separate `Label` node needing its own slot in `_clear_all`'s cleanup list. `item_selected` sets `battle_manager.playback_speed` directly from a parallel `SPEED_OPTIONS` array. Placed in the open strip above the map next to the existing "Inspect AI (i)" button rather than the sidebar's own button row (`retreat_button`/`pause_button`), which measured comments elsewhere in this file already establish has no spare width left for another control.

**Verified**: driving `BattleManager._process` directly with a real player/enemy unit present (so the battle doesn't end after one tick) confirmed 4x playback advances the scenario clock exactly 4x as fast as 1x, and 0.25x exactly 1/4 as fast; pausing at 4x still produced zero advance. End-to-end through the real `Main` scene (`_on_recon_mode_chosen` → `_on_start_pressed`, matching this project's own established UI-test pattern), the dropdown existed with exactly 5 choices defaulting to 1x/`playback_speed == 1.0`, selecting 4x and 0.25x each actually updated `battle_manager.playback_speed` to match, and `_clear_all()` correctly freed and nulled it on restart. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — A deliberate mortar-on-mortar shot wasn't counted as counter-battery

*"Mortar shots at enemy mortars should be reported as counter battery strikes in the AAR."* Confirmed: `_record_shot` (the one place `UnitCombatStats.shot()` gets called for every ordinary firing attempt, mortar or squad) always passed `counter_battery = false`. The ONLY path that ever recorded a shot as counter-battery was the separate, purpose-built reactive mechanic in `_resolve_pending_counter_battery` — blind return fire at a detected muzzle flash, with no confirmed target. A mortar that could actually SEE and deliberately target an enemy mortar through the normal `_pick_target`/`_tick_fire` pipeline (already the highest-priority candidate whenever it's a live, in-range option) was just counted as an ordinary shot — even though "one indirect-fire weapon firing on another" is exactly what counter-battery means, confirmed-target or not.

**The fix**: `_record_shot` now computes `is_counter_battery := unit.kind == Unit.Kind.MORTAR and target.kind == Unit.Kind.MORTAR` and passes it through to `unit_combat_stats.shot()`. Scoped to the FIRING unit also being a mortar — a squad's rifle fire happening to land on a mortar crew within its own engagement range is ordinary contact fire, not a mortar duel, and shouldn't inflate the CB-strike count.

**Verified**: a mortar shot at an enemy mortar registered as `counter_battery` (not `shots`); the same mortar shooting at a squad still registered as an ordinary `shots`; a squad shooting at an enemy mortar was correctly NOT counted as counter-battery. `test_unit_doctrine.gd`'s own existing combat-stats coverage still passed unchanged. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — Squads consolidating against encirclement walked to open ground

*"Right now, the three friendly squads are sitting bunched up in open ground. This is weird."* Traced to `_reposition_for_encirclement`: once a squad reads as genuinely at risk of being surrounded (known enemies spanning enough arc around it, most already dug into cover), it consolidates toward the geometric average position of its own side's other ACTIVE units (or the mortar alone, if it's the last one) — a bare coordinate average, with no notion of terrain at the destination at all. Once two or more squads trigger this in the same stretch of the fight, each is walking toward roughly the same point (the others' shared centroid), and that point has exactly as much chance of being open ground as anywhere else on the map — turning a safety-driven consolidation into several squads standing shoulder to shoulder in the open, letting a single round catch all of them at once (the exact splash-spread risk `_bunched_ally` elsewhere in this file already exists to reason about), which is worse than not consolidating at all.

**The fix**: the capped consolidation step now redirects through `GameConfig.nearest_cover_point`, centered on the step's own raw destination and excluding anywhere too close to a known enemy — the same "look for actual cover, not a bare point" treatment `_relocate_for_risk`/`_relocate_mortar` already give every other safety-driven move in this file. Falls back to the original raw point untouched if genuinely nothing better is nearby (`nearest_cover_point`'s own no-candidates convention), so this never makes a squad worse off than before, only better when real cover exists nearby.

**Verified**: a squad with two known enemy contacts spread widely enough around it (both actually sitting in real cover, matching the function's own "surrounded by cover, not just outnumbered in the open" gate) and one ally positioned so their bare geometric midpoint fell in genuinely open ground still correctly triggered the consolidation, but the resulting `move_target` landed in real cover, distinctly different from — not just near — the raw open-ground rally point. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — Total casualties by type added to the exact damage report

*"The exact simulation results can report total casualties by type for both sides."* `UnitCombatStats.report_lines()`'s "DAMAGE BY UNIT — EXACT SIMULATION RESULTS" already listed casualties inflicted per unit, but nothing summed them up per side — a reader had to add up every row by hand to get "how many total casualties did my side inflict" or its mirror, "how many did the enemy inflict" (which, in a closed ledger, is exactly what the OTHER side suffered — this report already being the one true, omniscient view, unlike `_end_battle`'s own separate AAR casualty lines, which fog-of-war-limit the enemy figure when the position wasn't held).

**The fix**: each side's block in `report_lines()` now ends with a `TOTAL INFLICTED: N killed, M wounded (T total)` line, summed across exactly the same SQUAD/MORTAR rows already listed above it (drone airframes destroyed appended only when nonzero, matching the per-row convention of only mentioning it when relevant).

**Verified**: a scenario with two player units (a squad killing 2/wounding 1, a mortar wounding 4, on two different enemy squads) and no enemy-side damage produced `TOTAL INFLICTED: 2 killed, 5 wounded (7 total)` for the player side and `TOTAL INFLICTED: 0 killed, 0 wounded (0 total)` for the enemy side, correctly summed across both contributing units. `test_unit_doctrine.gd`'s own existing combat-stats coverage still passed unchanged. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — A general retreat could still be waiting on a resupply run that hadn't left the rear

*"If we are making a general retreat, the mortar resupply, if it hasn't entered the map yet, can be cancelled."* `order_general_retreat` already pulls the mortar itself into RETREATING along with every other ACTIVE player unit, but a resupply wave still just a scheduled arrival time in `_mortar_resupply` (not yet a physical `RESUPPLY_RUN` unit on the map — see `_update_mortar_resupply`'s own dispatch-time check) kept running on its own schedule regardless, eventually spawning a run to chase a position that was abandoning the fight.

**The fix**: `order_general_retreat`'s own per-unit loop, right where it already orders a mortar to retreat, now also erases any pending `_mortar_resupply` entry for it — nothing physical to recall, just a scheduled request that's cancelled before it ever leaves the rear. Deliberately scoped to only the not-yet-dispatched case: a run already a real `Unit` on the map when the retreat is ordered is left alone (a separate, already-in-motion trip, matching the user's own "if it hasn't entered the map yet" framing) and isn't affected by this at all — it keeps moving under its own existing logic. No further request gets created afterward either, since `_update_mortar_resupply_requests`'s own existing `state != ACTIVE` gate already excludes a retreating mortar. Enemy-side general retreats are untouched — the enemy's own mirror deliberately keeps its mortar fighting through a general retreat (mortars "continue the fire mission"), so its resupply still matters.

**Verified**: a mortar with a pending, not-yet-dispatched resupply wave had that wave's entire scheduling record erased the instant a general retreat was ordered, with a combat-log entry confirming the cancellation; a resupply run already physically on the map at that same moment was left untouched, still present afterward; a mortar with no pending resupply at all produced no error. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-09 — A 2-hour resupply wait was a rare tail event, not a common one

Asked directly what the resupply delay's maximum possible wait actually was (`GameConfig.sample_resupply_delay` is log-normal — genuinely unbounded above, not a hard cap), then: *"Let's extend that. Have 2 hours be about the 75th percentile."* — followed by *"The median can still be 60 minutes"* and, anticipating a possible conflict, *"or close, if exactly 60 is inconvenient with the distributions you are using."* No conflict, as it turned out: `sample_resupply_delay(median, sigma) = exp(log(median) + randfn(0, sigma))` has a median of exactly `median` regardless of `sigma` (by construction — `randfn(0,sigma)` has median 0, so `exp(log(median) + 0) = median`), so widening the spread to move the 75th percentile out to 2 hours doesn't touch the 60-minute median at all.

**The fix**: `MORTAR_RESUPPLY_DELAY_SIGMA` raised from 0.5 to 1.0 — solving `60 * exp(Z₀.₇₅ · sigma) = 120` for `sigma` (Z₀.₇₅ ≈ 0.6745) gives ≈1.028; 1.0 is close enough to land almost exactly on the target while staying a clean, round number. `MORTAR_RESUPPLY_DELAY_MEDIAN` itself is untouched at 60 minutes. The comment documenting the "middle two-thirds" range was updated for the new, much wider spread (~22 to ~163 minutes, up from ~36 to ~100).

**Verified**: 20,000 samples from `sample_resupply_delay` at the new parameters gave an empirical median of 59.5 minutes (target 60) and a 75th percentile of 117.1 minutes (target ~120) — both within simple sampling noise of the intended targets.

### 2026-09-09 — The drone latched onto a retreating enemy mortar instead of the one still in action

*"Drone is tracking a retreating enemy mortar. Would be more likely to search for the in-action enemy mortar that is known to exist."* Direct consequence of this session's own earlier RETREATING-lead fix (see the "structurally never reached its own 'destroy enemy mortars' tier" entry above): `_known_enemy_mortar_lead`'s live-visibility branch now correctly treats a visible RETREATING mortar as a real, trusted lead — good for the friendly mortar's own solo pursuit of a fleeing crew, but it had two knock-on problems. First, the branch returned whichever visible mortar it hit FIRST in `enemy_units` order, with no preference for one still actually fighting over one already fleeing. Second, and more directly the reported symptom: `_update_joint_mortar_hunt`'s TEAM-WIDE commitment (the one that pins the drone's own reconnaissance priority, not just the mortar's movement) accepted ANY trusted lead, including a RETREATING one — and since a RETREATING mortar that's still visible keeps re-qualifying as "trusted" every tick, the instant the commitment cleared for going non-ACTIVE it could immediately re-form on that exact same mortar, effectively pinning the drone to a fleeing, no-longer-dangerous contact indefinitely.
