**The fix, in two parts.** (1) `_known_enemy_mortar_lead`'s live-visibility check now prefers any visible ACTIVE mortar outright, only falling back to a visible RETREATING one when no ACTIVE mortar is visible at all — so a still-fighting mortar wins the lead over a fleeing one regardless of array order. (2) `_update_joint_mortar_hunt` now refuses to form (or re-form) the team-wide commitment for a lead whose unit isn't ACTIVE, even if `_known_enemy_mortar_lead` reports it trusted — a fleeing crew is a mop-up, not an ongoing threat, and tying the drone's shared attention to it starves the search for a still-dangerous mortar that hasn't even been found yet. The friendly mortar's own solo pursuit of a fleeing target (via `_mortar_hunt_fix_for`, used directly by its own movement tier and the drone's separate "supporting friendly action" tier) is untouched by either change — this only ever gates the escorted, team-wide commitment and the base lead-preference order.

**Verified**: with a RETREATING mortar listed BEFORE an ACTIVE one in `enemy_units`, both visible, `_known_enemy_mortar_lead` correctly returned the ACTIVE one, not the first-in-array RETREATING one. With only the RETREATING mortar visible, it still correctly fell back to reporting it as a trusted lead (confirming the earlier fix wasn't regressed). With the joint commitment already pinned to a RETREATING mortar and no ACTIVE alternative visible, `_update_joint_mortar_hunt` correctly cleared it to null rather than re-latching onto the same fleeing contact. The permanent `test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd` regression guard still passed (16.1%, comfortably above its 2% floor). A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-10 — A clearly-dominant squad target still lost the weighted roll more often than not

*"Recently, enemy squad 7 was very dangerous and should have been getting a lot of attention from the mortar as no enemy mortars are thought to be alive. It was not."* Diagnosed directly from the mortar's own Decision Inspector data, not guessed at: both enemy mortars correctly read "no longer targetable" (confirming the premise), and Enemy Squad 7 genuinely scored highest among six squad candidates (13.57, next-best 11.22) — the mortar's own weighting had already correctly identified it as the top threat. It still lost this particular roll to a 9.38-scoring squad, because `_weighted_mortar_target_pick`'s draw weight was each candidate's bare `_enemy_target_value` — a LINEAR weight, giving the 13.57 only a 24.3% chance of winning any single roll (13.57 / 55.86 total) despite being the clear standout. Not a bug in the strict sense (this is exactly the documented "a lower score can win" design, deliberately not a hard "always pick the max" rule) — but the linear weighting meant a target scoring nearly 4.5x the field's minimum still lost more often than it won.

**The fix**: the draw weight is now each candidate's `_enemy_target_value` SQUARED, not the bare score — still a genuine weighted-random choice (never a deterministic max-pick), but a real standout now dominates the roll much more reliably than "best odds among several comparable options." Recomputing the reported case: Squad 7's share rises from 24.3% (linear) to about 31.6% (squared) given that particular score spread — a meaningfully stronger lean toward the actual top threat, while a true outlier scenario (as tested) shows an even larger shift. The displayed "original target value" scores in the Decision Inspector are unaffected — those come from `_enemy_target_value` directly, independent of this internal draw weight — only which candidate the roll actually favors changes. Deterministic profiles/explicit unit doctrine are untouched: squaring is a monotonic transform, so the "always pick the single highest score" branch picks the identical winner it always did.

**Verified**: with two candidates scoring 3 and 9 (danger isolated to zero so the score is exactly the pip count), 20,000 draws picked the higher-scoring one 89.6% of the time — matching the score-squared prediction (90.0%, 81/(81+9)) and clearly distinct from the old linear prediction (75.0%, 9/(9+3)). `test_decision_ai.gd`'s full battle coverage across all four commander profiles still completed with no failures. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-10 — "Hidden" only meant out of line-of-sight, not actually far away

*"Just now, mortar with no ammo moved very close to known enemy locations. Was this caused by the 'possible enemies' heatmap?"* Checked directly: no — the heat-map (`estimated_enemy_likelihood`) is never referenced anywhere in mortar relocation logic; it only ever feeds the drone's own search/sweep prioritization. The real cause was in `GameConfig.nearest_hidden_point` (the shared destination-picker behind the mortar's evade/conceal/out-of-ammo relocation, the drone team's own ground-crew evasion, and the general self-risk relocation choice set): a candidate qualified as "hidden" purely by being out of direct line-of-sight from every known threat, with no minimum distance requirement at all. A candidate one wall or terrain-fold away from a known enemy — genuinely LOS-blocked, but only a stone's throw from where the enemy actually stands — read as just as "safe" as one genuinely far away, which is a real problem for a crew that's just spent its last round and can't shoot back if discovered.

**The fix**: added a minimum standoff distance to the per-candidate check — a point must now be BOTH LOS-blocked AND at least `MORTAR_CREW_OVERRUN_DANGER_RANGE` (600m) from every known threat to qualify as hidden. That constant's name is mortar-specific but the reasoning it already encodes ("close enough that a small shift ruins it") is general to any ground unit relocating for safety, which is why it's reused here rather than adding a near-duplicate. Falls through to farther search rings (or, if truly nothing qualifies, `from` unchanged) exactly as before — this only makes candidates harder to accept, never picks a worse one than the old logic would have.

**Verified**: with a unit and a threat placed on opposite sides of the village building (LOS genuinely blocked between them), a reachable candidate existed that the OLD logic would have accepted at only 472m from the threat — confirmed by independently replicating the search and finding it. The fixed function correctly rejected that candidate and returned a point 900m away instead, still genuinely LOS-blocked. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-10 — The heat-map's positional guess applied at full trust from the very first tick

Same conversation, a related but separate point: *"if we scout rear areas and don't find anything, it should take time for the 'possible enemy locations' heatmap to increase the odds in rear areas. The enemy does not teleport."* — one of three related ideas (the other two, a nonzero-but-low baseline specifically for rear infiltration and a dedicated drone rear-area overflight tier, were deliberately deferred: *"1 and 2 for now"*). `_heatmap_recently_cleared_multiplier` already discounted a spot right after a friendly asset confirmed it clear, ramping back to full trust over `HEATMAP_RECENTLY_CLEARED_COOLDOWN_S` (5 tactical minutes) — but a point with NO record of ever being checked skipped that discount entirely, reading at full positional trust (`_row_bias_at_y`/`_enemy_approach_likelihood`) from the very first tick of the battle, before either side has had any real time to move anywhere.

**The fix**: a point with no clear-time on record is now treated as if it had been confirmed clear at scenario time zero, rather than automatically trusted — `_heatmap_last_cleared.get(key, 0.0)` instead of an early `return 1.0`. This phases the entire heat-map in over the same, already-established cooldown window used for "just checked, unlikely to already be re-occupied" — nothing reads as fully suspicious until real tactical time has actually passed, uniformly, without needing any new per-region "how far is this from the front" reasoning (left for the deferred, fuller rear-overflight design).

**Verified**: a never-observed point read at exactly `HEATMAP_RECENTLY_CLEARED_MIN_MULTIPLIER` at scenario time zero (not full trust), ramped linearly to the halfway point at half the cooldown window, and reached full trust once the cooldown fully elapsed — the same shape a point explicitly cleared mid-battle already used, confirmed side by side in the same test.

### 2026-09-10 — The heat-map's own recovery clock ignored how far the cleared ground actually was

Immediate follow-up to the entry above: *"5 minute window could be reasonable for a small region. But it is too short when a wide area has been checked. Heatmap evolution needs to understand enemy movement capabilities. People carrying loads on foot, and trying to avoid detection, are not going to travel 1km in 5 minutes."* Correct — the fixed `HEATMAP_RECENTLY_CLEARED_COOLDOWN_S` (300 tactical seconds) was sized for "one grid cell's own width" (per its own original comment), but applied UNIFORMLY regardless of how much ground had actually just been cleared. A drone sweeping a wide swath at once cleared many cells together; five minutes later every one of them snapped back toward full suspicion together too, including ones a kilometer from anything ever actually seen — 1km in 5 minutes is 12 km/h, plainly not a covert, load-bearing foot infiltration pace.

**The fix**: the cooldown is no longer a flat constant — it's now `distance to the nearest currently-known enemy position / GameConfig.HEATMAP_INFILTRATION_SPEED`, a new, deliberately slow (~1 km/h, well under the existing `REPOSITION_SPEED`'s ordinary 1.8 m/s) rate representing someone moving cautiously, carrying a load, specifically trying not to be seen — a real "how long would it actually take to walk here" claim rather than a one-size-fits-all window. `_known_enemy_positions` (the same fog-of-war-respecting set every other "what do we actually know" computation in this file already uses) supplies the reference point nearest ground already cleared. A point near a known contact still recovers in a couple of minutes (plausible — the enemy is right there); a point a kilometer from anything ever seen now takes roughly an hour, not five minutes. With no known enemy anywhere on the map at all, the cooldown is effectively infinite — nothing to infiltrate FROM yet — so previously-cleared ground simply stays at the floor discount until real contact happens somewhere.

**Verified**: two points cleared simultaneously, one 50m and one 1km from a single known enemy — at the old flat window's own 5-minute mark, the near point had already recovered to over 90% trust while the far point remained under 15%, and the far point only reached full trust once the actual travel-time math (~1 hour at the new infiltration speed) had elapsed. With no known enemy on the map at all, a cleared point stayed pinned at the floor multiplier indefinitely, confirmed after an arbitrarily long elapsed time. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-10 — Spotting gained two missing real-world factors: signature persistence and sensor-cone geometry

*"Rather than always having the drone always spot enemies within visual range, it should be probabilistic... Research how good such detection actually happens in practice... For enemies in cover, keep in mind that they have probably moved recently, which would increase the chances of detection... Detection odds would also depend on whether the drone is flying directly over an enemy, or whether it is further out in the field of view."* Checked first, since the premise deserved verifying before acting on it: spotting was ALREADY fully probabilistic (`CombatResolver.roll_spot`, a per-tactical-second roll gated on LOS, range, terrain concealment, and a flat 3x bonus for a currently-moving target — nothing here is "always spot within range"). Real-world research (military camouflage/concealment doctrine, current Ukraine-battlefield drone-reconnaissance reporting) confirms the QUALITATIVE shape already encoded — concealment matters enormously, exposed troops die fast, camouflaged/dug-in positions can hold up — but yields no clean, citable NUMBERS for exactly how much harder a concealed target is to spot or how long a movement signature lingers; those figures don't appear to be published anywhere accessible. Two genuine, previously-missing pieces were identified and implemented as judgment calls in that same spirit, rather than left unaddressed for lack of a citation:

1. **Recently-moved signature was all-or-nothing, not decaying.** The existing `MOVING_SPOT_MULTIPLIER` (3x) applied only on the EXACT tick a target's `Activity` read MOVING — the instant it stopped, the bonus vanished completely, as if dust settled and a thermal signature faded instantaneously. New `Unit.seconds_stationary` (maintained every tick in `_tick_movement`, reset to 0 the instant a unit is MOVING) lets the bonus decay linearly back to 1.0 over `GameConfig.RECENT_MOVEMENT_SIGNATURE_DECAY_S` (4 tactical minutes, a judgment call at the same rough order of magnitude as this file's other short-lived "how long does recent evidence stay meaningful" windows) instead of cutting off the instant movement stops.
2. **Distance falloff was a flat line regardless of sensor type.** The same linear "closer = easier" falloff applied to a ground observer's eyesight and a drone's overhead sensor alike. A real overhead sensor's footprint is a cone, not a flat disk: near the center (close to directly overhead) the look angle is steep and resolution stays near its best across a wide area; toward the edge of that same footprint, the identical target is seen from a far more oblique angle, where residual foliage or structure much more easily breaks the sightline well within nominal detection range. The falloff is now squared specifically for a DRONE observer (a ground observer's own falloff is about eye/optics range, not sensor-cone geometry, so it stays linear) — directly implementing "directly overhead vs. further out in the field of view."

Deliberately left unchanged: `DRONE_CONCEALMENT_MULTIPLIER`'s existing TREES/BUILDING values. That table already encodes a considered, explicitly-documented prior design call — a natural canopy is more porous to a straight-down view than to a lateral ground one, so concealment that works at ground level is deliberately weaker against a drone — and the research done here didn't surface anything specific enough to justify overriding that reasoning; it's a separate question from the two gaps above, not something this pass touched.

**Verified**: statistically (30,000-roll empirical trials), a target that had JUST stopped moving (`seconds_stationary = 0`) was spotted at effectively the same rate as one still actively moving, while one that had been stationary for ten times the decay window was spotted at well under half that rate. For a drone specifically, halving the distance to a target roughly QUADRUPLED the spot rate (measured ratio ≈0.243, matching the predicted 0.25 for a squared falloff) rather than the ~2x a linear falloff would produce. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-10 — Idle squads had no reason to ever leave open ground

*"Friendly squads are currently bunched up in the open even though a lot of enemy squads are approaching. This does not make sense."* Diagnosed directly from a squad's own Decision Inspector entry, not guessed at: "No movement order; observing or waiting for a firing opportunity," with nine enemy squads all listed as known but still "Out of range." Not a bug in an existing relocate function (the `_reposition_for_encirclement` cover-seeking fix from earlier this session was a red herring — that specific trigger needs most nearby threats already dug into cover, which doesn't describe squads still approaching in the open) — the actual gap was structural: an idle player squad had NO mechanism at all telling it to get off open ground before contact. The enemy's own mirror, `_alert_enemy_squads`, only ever triggers reactively (once a shot is actually fired at that side), which is fine for an attacking force already closing the gap, but leaves a defending squad standing exposed for however long it takes the enemy to close the last stretch into engagement range.

Generalized further once the first fix landed: *"As a general matter, squads want cover when possible, unless there is a reason not to have it. A squad that is moving is an example of such a reason, but not the only one."* Confirmed directly: should an idle squad with NO known enemy anywhere nearby also proactively seek cover, or is "nothing known nearby" itself a legitimate reason to stay put? — *"Unconditional — always seek cover when idle."* Saved as a standing design principle (`[[feedback_squads_prefer_cover]]`) since it's broader than this one fix.

**The fix**: `_update_friendly_squad_positioning` gained a new tier, checked right after self-preservation (encirclement) and before mortar-flank-screening: any idle squad (no active order, no live target, not already at-risk from encirclement) not already in cover simply moves to the nearest one (`GameConfig.nearest_cover_point`), unconditionally — no detected-threat gate at all. Standing in the open is now something that needs an actual reason (already covered by every exclusion already in this same loop), not the default absent one.

**Verified**: an idle, exposed squad with zero known enemies anywhere on the map still correctly moved to a genuine nearby cover point (not the deployment principle intended only for imminent threats — deliberately broader, per the follow-up). A squad already standing in real cover was left untouched. A squad with a live target in range stood and fought rather than diverting. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-10 — Added a Scheduled Retreat order: plan ahead, and let ammo and cover decisions anticipate it

*"I want a new order option for the commander. It is a scheduled retreat at a later time. After such an order, the mortar would realize its ammunition has little value beyond the scheduled retreat time and would plan fire accordingly. Squads that saw their position becoming vulnerable would pull back."* Two design questions resolved directly rather than guessed at before implementing: how the player sets the time — *"Relative delay, but allow the delay to be changed by a slider"* — and what "vulnerable" should mean — *"Reuse the existing check, but the squad might or might not actually retreat. It might just pull back to better cover. That can be up to the AI. But the willingness to pull back should in general increase if an overall retreat is pending."*

**The fix.** `BattleManager.order_scheduled_retreat(delay_seconds)` records a target time (`scenario_elapsed_time + delay`, re-callable — adjusting the slider and re-confirming just reschedules rather than stacking multiple pending retreats); `_check_scheduled_retreat()`, polled every tick, fires the existing `order_general_retreat()` once that time arrives. A new `_scheduled_retreat_urgency()` ramps 0→1 over `GameConfig.SCHEDULED_RETREAT_URGENCY_WINDOW_S` (20 tactical minutes) as the scheduled time approaches (0 with nothing scheduled or still far off, 1 once due), and feeds two independent, deliberately separate consumers:

1. **Ammo planning** — a new `_scheduled_retreat_ammo_discount(mortar)` compares time remaining until the scheduled retreat against how long the mortar would take to expend its own remaining rounds at its natural reload rate, sliding from 1.0 (no discount, plenty of time to use it all anyway) down to 0.0 (already due — every hold-fire reason should be overridden, there's no "later" left to save rounds for). Applied multiplicatively in `_pick_target` to both `hold_for_pursuit_chance` and `hold_fire_chance` — a mortar that would ordinarily conserve its last few rounds becomes steadily more willing to spend them as the scheduled pull-out gets close, exactly the requested "ammunition has little value beyond the scheduled retreat time."
2. **Squad vulnerability** — per the second answer, this deliberately does NOT force any squad into an actual `order_retreat()` state change; it reuses the existing, already-tested `_reposition_for_encirclement` self-preservation check (repositioning to better cover, the AI's own call whether that's enough or a fuller withdrawal follows separately) and simply makes it fire more readily as urgency rises. Its three gate thresholds (`FRIENDLY_ENCIRCLEMENT_DETECT_RADIUS`, `_MIN_COVERED_FRACTION`, `_ANGLE_THRESHOLD_DEG`) are now each `lerp()`'d toward new, more lenient `_URGENT` counterparts by `_scheduled_retreat_urgency()` — with nothing scheduled, urgency is exactly 0 and every threshold lerps back to its original value, unchanged.

UI: a "Retreat in:" label, a 5–120 minute slider (5-minute steps, default 30) with a live value readout, and a "Schedule Retreat" button, added to `main.gd` alongside the existing playback-speed dropdown — pressing it calls `order_scheduled_retreat(slider.value * 60.0)`. Distinct from the existing immediate `retreat_button`; re-pressing after adjusting the slider reschedules rather than queuing a second order. (Range/default later narrowed — see the 2026-09-10 follow-up below.)

**Verified**: `order_scheduled_retreat`/`_check_scheduled_retreat` correctly left `player_general_retreat_ordered` false until the scheduled instant and correctly fired it once scenario time reached that point, and were confirmed idempotent once already ordered. `_scheduled_retreat_urgency` measured 0 with nothing scheduled, ~0 at the start of its window, ~0.5 halfway through, and exactly 1.0 at and past the scheduled instant. `_scheduled_retreat_ammo_discount` measured 1.0 with nothing scheduled, ~1.0 when remaining time exactly covered expending all remaining ammo, ~0.5 at half that time, and exactly 0.0 once already due. `_reposition_for_encirclement`, given a fixed scenario (three known enemy positions, one genuinely in cover, spaced to clear the encirclement angle check regardless of urgency) that fell just short of the normal 50%-covered threshold, correctly declined to fire at urgency 0 and correctly fired once urgency reached 1 (the same scenario clearing the relaxed 20% threshold). A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

**Follow-up, same day**: *"Once a retreat is scheduled, the UI should reflect that, showing the scheduled time. When scheduling a retreat, it can round to the nearest minute. Also, the commander should have the option to countermand his own scheduled retreat order. Lastly, as the retreat time gets closer, the mortar should get more and more willing to use its ammo on available targets."* The last point was already exactly what `_scheduled_retreat_ammo_discount` does (verified above) — it's a monotonic function of time remaining for any fixed ammo count, so willingness to fire only ever increases as the scheduled time approaches. The other three were genuinely new:

- `order_scheduled_retreat` now rounds the resulting `scheduled_retreat_time` to the nearest whole minute (`round((scenario_elapsed_time + delay_seconds) / 60.0) * 60.0`) — the slider's own delay was already whole minutes, but adding it to the continuous `scenario_elapsed_time` could otherwise land on some arbitrary `:MM:SS`.
- New `BattleManager.cancel_scheduled_retreat()` — clears `scheduled_retreat_time` back to `INF` and logs it, a no-op if nothing is scheduled or if the scheduled retreat already fired (there's no undoing an already-ordered withdrawal, only the pending clock).
- `main.gd` gained a second UI row, hidden until something is actually scheduled: a status label reading "Retreat scheduled for `<clock_string>`" and a "Cancel Scheduled Retreat" button, both toggled live in `_process` off `not is_inf(scheduled_retreat_time) and not player_general_retreat_ordered` — so they disappear again the instant the scheduled retreat actually fires (at that point it's just the ordinary general retreat already in progress, nothing left to cancel).

**Verified**: scheduling with a deliberately non-round elapsed time still produced a `scheduled_retreat_time` an exact multiple of 60. `cancel_scheduled_retreat` cleared a pending schedule without itself ordering a retreat, was a harmless no-op with nothing scheduled, and left `player_general_retreat_ordered` untouched (still true) if called after the scheduled retreat had already fired. Scheduling, cancelling, then scheduling again still fired correctly at the new time. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-10 — Drone numbers climbed past the size of the fleet

*"I just saw the drone number on the map go up to 'drone 8'. There are only four drones. If a drone is launched again, the number should reflect that."* Confirmed directly in `_make_unit`: every unit kind's on-screen number came from `_unit_name_counts`, a per-kind counter that only ever increments, which is correct for units that live for the whole battle (a squad, a mortar) but wrong for a drone — `GameConfig.DRONE_FLEET_SIZE` is 4 physical airframes, but `_launch_drone`/`_launch_backup_drone` spawn a brand-new `Unit` instance for every sortie of the same airframe (see the fleet's own battery-pool model: airframes are reused, `Unit` objects are not). A battle with enough shoot-downs and relaunches would keep counting upward past 4 sorties total, showing "Drone 5," "Drone 6," and, as reported, "Drone 8" — even though at most 4 airframes are ever in the air or on the ground at once.

**The fix**: `_make_unit` special-cases `Kind.DRONE` with a new `_next_drone_number()`, which scans `player_units` for currently-live drones (active, backup, or already turned for home but not yet landed/freed — exactly the set whose number is legitimately still on screen), and returns the lowest number in `1..DRONE_FLEET_SIZE` not currently in use, instead of an ever-incrementing serial. Every other unit kind's numbering is unchanged.

**Verified**: launching a full fleet of 4 drones produced numbers 1–4 exactly. Losing one and relaunching reused its freed number rather than continuing to 5. Ten repeated loss/relaunch cycles (well past the fleet size in total sorties) kept every live drone's number within `1..DRONE_FLEET_SIZE` throughout.

### 2026-09-10 — A retreating mortar walked straight into enemy squads its own side had already seen

*"Mortar was just destroyed because it charged enemy squads."* The pasted Decision Inspector snapshot showed the crew already RETREATING (a prior hit had knocked it to 2/4 strength and out of ammo) and holding an "Ordered destination" well away from its own position — the retreat WAS aimed somewhere, just not somewhere safe. Traced to `_known_enemy_positions(Unit.Team.PLAYER)`, the shared helper behind every player-side safety/avoidance computation in this file (retreat cover-point exclusion, the final retreat dash's steer-away, encirclement detection, mortar self-preservation, drone re-search avoidance): it required the enemy unit's raw, real-time `is_visible` flag, not the persistent "has the player's own side ever actually seen this" memory (`Unit.player_has_been_sighted`/`player_known_position`) this same session already built for exactly this kind of question elsewhere (`_known_enemy_mortar_lead`'s own last-known-position fallback, the AAR's enemy-status panel). A squad the player had genuinely spotted moments earlier but that had since stepped out of direct line-of-sight — behind terrain, into trees — dropped out of `_known_enemy_positions` entirely, so `GameConfig.nearest_cover_point`'s own danger-radius exclusion had nothing to exclude it with, and a retreating mortar could be routed to duck for cover right on top of (or directly past) a threat its own side already had every reason to remember was there.

**The fix**: `_known_enemy_positions`, for the PLAYER side specifically, now reports a spotted enemy's position from the persistent snapshot (`player_known_position`, frozen at whatever it last was while `is_visible`) rather than requiring current visibility, gated on `player_has_been_sighted` and the unit's real (not remembered) state still being a genuine threat (ACTIVE/RETREATING — a contact confirmed destroyed after being seen is correctly dropped regardless of memory). The ENEMY side's own equivalent call is deliberately untouched — that fog-of-war memory model is one-directional by design (see the same fields' own doc comments), so enemy-side avoidance still reads plain real-time `is_visible`, matching this project's established "enemy may differ" principle.

**Verified**: an enemy squad, once sighted, continued to count as a known position after `is_visible` went false and it moved elsewhere, correctly still reporting its OLD (last-seen) position rather than its current real one; being destroyed after being sighted correctly dropped it regardless of memory; the mirror check for the enemy side confirmed a hidden player unit still drops out of the enemy's own known-positions list immediately (no memory model there, unchanged). A direct repro of the reported scenario — a mortar ordered to retreat with the only nearby cover sitting right on top of a squad that had been sighted there but had since blinked out of visibility — correctly routed the retreat to a cover point outside the danger radius instead of straight into it. The permanent `test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd` regression guard still passed (now 20.2%, up from 8.1% — an expected, positive side effect of the mortar itself no longer losing track of a known enemy mortar the instant it's briefly obscured). A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-10 — Enemy squad count of 2 wasn't a coincidence — the boundary clamp double-counted it

*"An enemy initial squad count of 2 seems to happen pretty often. Is that just a coincidence? I would want to see each possible initial squad count equally likely."* It wasn't a coincidence — worked out the actual distribution directly rather than guessing. `roll_enemy_force_size` rolled MORTAR count first (uniform 1-3), derived a squad-count TARGET as `round(mortars * 3)` (giving 3, 6, or 9), then added a uniform ±2 jitter and clamped into `[2, 10]`. For mortars=1 (target 3), jitter -2 and -1 BOTH clamp to the same floor of 2 — so squads=2 draws from two separate jitter outcomes instead of one, making it (and, symmetrically, squads=10 off the mortars=3 target) exactly twice as likely as squads=3, 6, or 9. Computed the full marginal: `{2: 13.3%, 3: 6.7%, 4: 13.3%, 5: 13.3%, 6: 6.7%, 7: 13.3%, 8: 13.3%, 9: 6.7%, 10: 13.3%}` — genuinely uneven, not sampling noise.

Asked directly how to fix it, since keeping mortar count as the primary roll can't reach true uniformity (3 mortar values × 5 jitter values can't map evenly onto 9 squad-count values) without decoupling the two entirely: *"Generate the squad count first, then the mortar count. The mortar count should be roughly 1/3 of the squad count. But it can vary from that, randomly."*

**The fix**: `roll_enemy_force_size` now rolls SQUAD count first — uniform across its own full `[ENEMY_SQUAD_COUNT_MIN, ENEMY_SQUAD_COUNT_MAX]` range, no clamping involved since nothing is added to it — and derives mortar count from that (`round(squads / ENEMY_SQUAD_PER_MORTAR_RATIO)`, jittered by a new `ENEMY_MORTAR_COUNT_JITTER` (±1), then clamped into mortar count's own narrow `[1, 3]` range). Clamping still happens, but now on the derived, secondary value with only 3 possible outcomes — a minor skew there isn't something a player tracks the way they immediately notice "the enemy always seems to start with 2 squads."

**Verified**: 90,000 simulated rolls put every squad count from 2 to 10 within 2.8% of the uniform 11.1% expectation (previously 13.3% vs 6.7% — a real, doubled skew). Mortar count stayed within its own `[1, 3]` range throughout, and still correlated with squad count as intended (average derived mortar count rose from 1.34 at squads=2 to 2.67 at squads=10 — the "roughly 1/3, but can vary" relationship preserved). A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-10 — A resupply run vanished the instant it delivered instead of driving back out

*"When a resupply run reaches the mortar, it should not disappear. It should have to return to the edge of the map. Then it should disappear."* Followed up directly: *"The left edge that is"* — the player's own rear, confirming the existing `_resupply_entry_point_for` (west edge for the player, east edge for the enemy — each side's own true map edge, at the mortar's y) is exactly the destination to send it back to, not just "off the map somewhere."

**The fix**: new `Unit.resupply_delivered` (false for the whole outbound leg) flips true the instant `_resolve_resupply_run_arrivals` hands off rounds to the mortar — which now, instead of freeing the run on the spot, reassigns its `move_target` to `_resupply_entry_point_for(mortar)` and lets it walk itself back out. `_update_resupply_run_targets` skips re-tracking a delivered run's destination onto the mortar's live position (it did that for the OUTBOUND leg specifically so the mortar could close some of the distance itself — irrelevant once delivery is done), so the homeward leg is a fixed point, set once. The run is only actually removed once it physically reaches that edge. Two knock-on fixes were needed to keep this consistent: `_active_resupply_run_for` (feeds the dashboard's "resupply en route" status, resupply-linkup pursuit, and urgency-based ammo conservation) now excludes a delivered run — its job for that mortar is done, so a second request shouldn't read "one's already coming" off a truck that's just driving away — and `_on_resupply_run_state_changed`'s narration skips `log_resupply_run_destroyed` for a run shot down AFTER delivering, since that log's own wording ("lost before reaching the position") would be simply false for a run that already got there.

**Verified**: a run that reaches its mortar delivers rounds and correctly stays in `player_units`/`enemy_units` rather than being freed immediately; its destination becomes the correct edge for its own side (west/`-WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_PX` for the player, east/`MAP_WIDTH_PX` for the enemy); a later call to the outbound-leg retargeting function confirmed it no longer re-locks the destination back onto the mortar; the run is only actually removed once it reaches that edge point. The pre-existing abort path (mortar lost before delivery) was confirmed unaffected — still removed immediately, never redirected. A run destroyed after delivering was confirmed to skip the now-inapplicable "lost before reaching" log line while still being correctly removed from the roster. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-10 — Idle squads seeking cover in the same tick all converged on the same spot

*"Everybody appears to be stuck in place. Time is moving, but the units are not."* Followed by a pasted Decision Inspector snapshot showing one squad, still ACTIVE, sitting well out in the rear (`x: -674m` — deep in the west flank, PLAYER_SAFE_X is -1400m) with "No movement order; observing." Then: *"The squads are all bunched up, too. It does not look to me like it is correct."* Investigated the "stuck" report first: reproducing an extended real-time run confirmed the battle wasn't actually frozen — it resolved on its own — consistent with `TIME_SCALE_FAST_FORWARD` (already existing, deliberate compression whenever nobody's in contact) simply compressing a long quiet stretch into what looked like a pause, then squads reaching wherever they were headed and having nothing further to do. Confirmed directly afterward: *"They did un-freeze eventually. It is still curious that they bunched up."*

The bunching WAS real, and traced directly: `_update_friendly_squad_positioning`'s unconditional "seek cover" tier (added earlier this session) processes every idle, uncovered squad in the SAME tick, and called `GameConfig.nearest_cover_point` with only `_ally_positions_for(u)` (other squads' CURRENT positions) as the exclusion list — never each other's freshly-picked cover destinations from earlier in the SAME pass. `order_general_retreat`'s own mass-retreat handling already solved this identical problem with a `claimed` accumulator (threading each unit's chosen destination into the next one's exclusion list, specifically because "several units [choosing] in the SAME instant... independently [pick] nearest cover point... and reliably [pick] the same one" — its own doc comment, word for word the bug reported here) — the newer seek-cover tier just never got the same treatment.

**The fix**: `_update_friendly_squad_positioning` now threads its own `claimed: Array[Vector2]` accumulator through the seek-cover loop, exactly mirroring `order_general_retreat`'s pattern — each squad's chosen `cover_dest` is appended and passed into every subsequent squad's own `nearest_cover_point` call this same tick.

**Verified**: reproduced directly first — 4 idle squads placed at the identical open-ground starting position (guaranteeing an identical "nearest cover" ranking for all of them) collapsed onto the same single cover zone 3 times out of 4 with the fix reverted, confirming the bug's real mechanism, not just a plausible theory. With the fix applied, the same 4-squad scenario deterministically spread across 4 distinct cover zones, zero repeats. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-10 — Scheduled Retreat slider narrowed to 30 minutes, default 10

*"The 'retreat in' slider can max out at 30 minutes and default to 10."* Straightforward follow-on to the Scheduled Retreat feature added earlier this session (5–120 minutes, default 30). `main.gd`'s slider now runs 5–30 minutes (still 5-minute steps) defaulting to 10, with the value label's own initial text updated to match. No backend change — `order_scheduled_retreat` itself has no opinion on the delay's range, so `_scheduled_retreat_urgency`'s 20-tactical-minute ramp window and `_scheduled_retreat_ammo_discount` are unaffected.

### 2026-09-11 — A relocating mortar could walk clean off the edge of the map

*"OK, right now, I can't see our mortar on the map."* Pasted Decision Inspector data showed the mortar out of ammo, correctly in a "Preserve self" relocation, but at `x: -1904m` — well past `PLAYER_SAFE_X` (-1400m) and past the true west edge of the modeled world (`-WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_PX`, -1500m) entirely. Traced to `GameConfig.nearest_hidden_point`'s ring search (the fallback path behind every ground unit's evade/conceal/out-of-ammo relocation once no hill's reverse slope qualifies): its candidates are built by projecting outward from the unit's own current position — `from + Vector2(cos(theta), sin(theta)) * radius_px` — with no map-edge clamp at all. A mortar repeatedly evading a threat approaching from one side (the east, in this report) has every one of those evasions biased the same direction (away from the threat, i.e. further west each time), and nothing ever stopped that from compounding past the map's actual west edge — right past `main.gd`'s own camera `limit_left`, which is hard-set to that same `-WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_PX` boundary. Past that line, the unit is not just far away, it's somewhere the camera can never scroll to: invisible, not "off in the distance."

**The fix**: new `GameConfig.clamp_to_operating_area(point)` — the map's real bounds (the modeled west flank through the core map's east edge horizontally, 0 to the map's height vertically), the same rectangle `BattleManager._clamp_to_drone_operating_area` already enforced for drone search targets (now refactored to just call the shared function instead of duplicating the same two `clamp()` calls). Applied to both of `nearest_hidden_point`'s candidate sources: the ring-search projection (the actual culprit) and `_reverse_slope_candidate`'s hill-anchored point (analytically incapable of reaching this specific bug on this map's current hill layout, but the same unclamped-projection reasoning applies, so it gets the same treatment on principle).

**Verified**: a 300-trial fuzz across west-flank mortar positions with an east-side threat, before the fix, reliably produced results past the west edge (worst case -1718m, well beyond the -1500m boundary) — a direct reproduction, not just a plausible theory. With the fix applied, the same fuzz never once exceeded the map's real bounds, while still confirming the ring-search path specifically was being exercised (some results landed farther west than any hill-candidate formula could ever reach alone, given the two west-flank hills' own maximum possible offset works out to about -1305m). The permanent `test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd` regression guard still passed. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-11 — This map now depicts a real place: Moshchun, Kyiv Oblast

*"I want a new map. But this time, base it on the real world! Pick a village that came under Russian attack in 2022. The right side of the map should be the region the attack came from, regardless of compass headings. Compass North should be shown somewhere, but it does not have to be pointing up. The map should show the region as it existed before the Russian attack, not how it looks today. Show the actual name of the village."* — the first genuinely new-content request of this rewrite, rather than a fix to existing decisionmaking.

**Research, not a guess.** Chose Moshchun (Bucha Raion, Kyiv Oblast) after checking several candidates for how well-documented and appropriately-scaled they were: a real battle fought 5-21 March 2022, credited as one of the engagements that stopped the Russian drive on Kyiv and contributed to the eventual full withdrawal from Kyiv Oblast, and — directly relevant to this game's own mechanics — fought with mortar shelling, artillery, and infantry defense (even period-appropriate drone use). Confirmed the real geography before touching any code: the village sat on the Irpin River; Russian forces attacked from the northwest, having come through the Chornobyl exclusion zone via Ivankiv, Dymer, and Borodyanka, trying to force a crossing to reach Kyiv via Pushcha-Vodytsia beyond; a single-lane dirt-trail bridge was the real crossing point near the village itself. A professional military historian's own published analysis explicitly notes no more detailed tactical study of the battle's exact layout has surfaced — so beyond the river's real position/orientation and the village's real name, the specific building/forest layout is a reasonable, similarly-scaled approximation in the same spirit as the rest of this file's terrain, not a surveyed reconstruction.

**Two decisions confirmed before implementing, not assumed**: (1) whether the Irpin River should be a real, hard obstacle or purely cosmetic — *"2"* (real obstacle: blocks movement except at the bridge, blocks LOS like a building); (2) how to source the pre-war layout — *"1"* (best-effort real approximation, not a from-scratch fictional layout). A follow-up ("it does not have to be a village... avoid significant urban areas... mostly countryside") was already satisfied by Moshchun (real population ~1,000, surrounded by farmland and pine forest, nothing urban about it).

**The orientation required no change to the map's own existing geometry.** This game already has the enemy attacking from the map's own east/right and the defender's rear to the west/left — exactly the real attack's own axis (Russians from the northwest, Ukrainians falling back toward Kyiv to the southeast) — so "the right side is the region the attack came from" was already true by construction; only a real compass needed adding on top. Worked out the rotation directly: real bearing 315° (northwest, the attack's origin) needed to land on screen-right; solving for where real North (bearing 0°) lands under that same rotation puts it at screen-bearing 135° — down-and-to-the-right, not up. `main.gd`'s new compass rose (bottom-right of the map, alongside the existing scale bar) draws its "N" arrow at exactly that diagonal.

**The river as a real obstacle — the actual engineering work.** New `GameConfig.RIVER_X_M`/`RIVER_BRIDGE_Y_M`/`RIVER_BRIDGE_HALF_WIDTH_M` define the river as two full-height blocking rects flanking a single ~120m-wide passable gap (a real chokepoint, not a single-file pinhole) — sized at the river's ordinary width, not the much wider flood the historical defenders deliberately triggered mid-battle by breaching the Kozarovychi dam upstream, since this map depicts the region as it stood BEFORE the attack, and that flooding was a specific defensive act during the fighting itself. `is_river_at`/`path_crosses_river` reuse the same `_line_crosses_rect` check BUILDING zones already use. `has_direct_los` now also blocks across the river (ground-level sightline, matching the confirmed choice); `has_aerial_los` deliberately does NOT — a drone looking down from altitude sees straight across it, same reasoning already applied to hills. Movement enforcement is new: `BattleManager._river_route`, called fresh every tick from `_step_toward_target` for every ground unit (a DRONE is explicitly exempted — it flies over), detects a move_target that would require crossing the river outside the gap and reroutes through `GameConfig.nearest_river_crossing` first, queuing the real destination right behind it — reusing the existing multi-leg `move_queue` mechanism rather than inventing a new one. `_step_retreat`'s own straight dash gained a matching `_sidestep_river`, mirroring the existing `_sidestep_building` pattern, for the rare case of a unit already on the wrong side needing to fall back across it.

**Two real bugs found and fixed along the way, not just the feature itself.** (1) The first version inserted a dedicated road waypoint exactly at the river crossing — but the enemy's own per-squad spread mechanism (`enemy_squad_y_offsets_m`) shifts EVERY waypoint by up to ±420m in y for an off-road squad, and a shifted "bridge" waypoint lands well outside the actual ~120m gap: a destination sitting INSIDE the impassable river, which a unit can never validly arrive at. Confirmed directly: this produced an infinite reroute-arrive-reroute loop that made zero forward progress, stalling squads at the near bank indefinitely. Fixed by removing that dedicated waypoint entirely — the existing long road segment already spanning the river is enough on its own; `_river_route` (checked fresh every tick, not baked into the road) correctly redirects whatever y a given squad's own shifted path would cross at. A general defensive check was added on top: if a move_target is ever found to already be inside the river (from any source, not just the road), `_river_route` now redirects to the crossing directly rather than risk reproducing the same loop — confirmed live: three existing forest patches happen to geographically overlap the river's own x-band, so a cover-point pick could plausibly hit this same case independently of the road fix. (2) While chasing that first bug, found a second, genuinely pre-existing one, unrelated to the river itself but newly exposed by the bridge funneling several squads to the same contact point at once: `_next_advance_point`'s candidate generation (an enemy squad's "first contact, leave the march route" reaction) has no map-edge clamp, and its rush distance is deliberately UNCAPPED for that first alert-triggered move — for a flanking squad still far from its own west-flank waypoint, rotating that large a radius across the full angle spread landed a candidate with a negative y (no such place exists on the map), which then got weighted-picked as the squad's actual order. Fixed with the same `GameConfig.clamp_to_operating_area` this session's earlier mortar-visibility fix already established as the shared answer to "a projected candidate needs to stay on the map."

**Verified**: direct geometry checks (river blocks on both sides of the gap, gap itself is open, LOS blocked on the ground but not from the air); a squad ordered straight across gets its move_target rerouted to a point on the bridge and its real destination queued right behind it; a drone's order is untouched; a path that never crosses the river is left alone. The road-waypoint infinite-loop bug was reproduced directly (a squad stalled at the exact same position for 1800+ tactical seconds, zero net movement) before being fixed, and confirmed resolved after (the same squad crossed cleanly and continued its march). The advance-point clamp bug was reproduced directly (a flanking squad's candidate landing at a literal negative y) and confirmed fixed across 200 repeated trials with zero out-of-bounds results after. Five full battles were run end-to-end post-fix: most successfully forced the river crossing with real losses along the way (a genuine chokepoint fight, not a free pass), one small attacking force failed to force it within the battle's own time limit — a plausible outcome given the real battle's own two-week duration, not a bug. The permanent `test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd` regression guard still passed. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes, and produced a healthy, varied spread of outcomes (59% held, exchange ratios and casualty rates in normal ranges) rather than a degenerate one-sided result.

### 2026-09-11 — Follow-up: the map wasn't actually data, and the terrain hadn't actually changed

*"It looked like the same map again, just with the village name."* Fair: the river, the compass, and the name were all real additions, but the underlying hills/forest/building layout was left exactly as it was before — a river and a label aren't enough to make a place feel different. *"I also wonder if you might need some refactoring? The program should treat the map as data. Draw_terrain should draw whatever the data have. It should not be tied to a specific map."* Confirmed directly: `TERRAIN_ZONES`, `FOREST_PATCHES`, `HILLS`, `ROAD_WAYPOINTS_M`, the river constants, and every faction spawn/deployment position were individually-named top-level `const`s in `GameConfig`, referenced by name from `draw_terrain` and every terrain-lookup function — there was no single "the map" object, the map WAS the code, which is exactly why it had been easy to bolt a river onto the old layout without ever touching the layout itself. Scoped directly rather than assumed: *"Data object, one map"* — decouple the drawing/lookup code from one hardcoded layout, but don't build a map-selection mechanism nobody's asked for. Two more follow-ups landed in the same pass: *"You should update the terrain, including forested regions"* and *"also the actual shape of the village."*

**The refactor.** New `GameConfig.CURRENT_MAP: Dictionary` holds everything that's actually content — name, hills, terrain zones, forest patches, road, river, and both sides' spawn/deployment data — and every function that used to read a named constant (`draw_terrain`, `elevation_m`, `is_building_at`, `path_crosses_building`, `_all_cover_zones`, `nearest_cover_point`, `has_direct_los`, `has_aerial_los`, `nearest_hidden_point`, `is_river_at`, `path_crosses_river`, `nearest_river_crossing`, `road_waypoints_px`, `enemy_squad_y_offsets_m`, `enemy_mortar_positions_m`, plus `battle_manager.gd`'s spawn/objective code and `deployment_screen.gd`'s zone drawing) now reads the same field off `CURRENT_MAP` instead. Map DIMENSIONS (`MAP_WIDTH_M`, `WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_M`, `PIXELS_PER_METER`) deliberately stayed as ordinary engine constants, not map data — nearly every range/speed constant in the file is computed from them at compile time, so making them swappable would cascade into a rewrite of the whole file for a variable-battlefield-size capability nobody asked for; what actually varies per map is the content laid out WITHIN that fixed coordinate system, which is what moved.

**The actual redesign.** Village (`terrain_zones[0]`) reshaped from a 450x380m block into a 300x500m elongated ribbon — Moshchun was originally Pylnia, a sawmill town strung out along the Irpin River, not a compact village center, and no detailed public survey of its exact pre-war footprint exists to do better than that. Repositioned to keep the mortar's own default position (a mortar can never occupy a building) clear of the new footprint, and checked against the two hill-slope forest patches already deliberately placed near it for clearance. Forest rebalanced to reflect the real geography rather than being spread evenly: three patches that geographically overlapped the river were removed outright (the real crossing sat in open floodplain, not woods — where the historical attack got bogged down), three more attacker-side patches near the road were thinned, and four new, deliberately larger patches were added on the defender side representing Pushcha-Vodytsia, the real, extensive pine forest the historical attack was trying to reach past Moshchun.

**Verified**: 18 direct checks confirmed every relocated function still reads correctly through the new indirection (river blocking/gap, road waypoint conversion, cover-zone lookup, enemy spawn/spread math) and that the reshaped village is genuinely elongated and still clear of the mortar's default position; a full battle ran end-to-end against the new data with no errors. All three permanent regression suites (`test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd`, `test_decision_ai.gd`, and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`) passed after updating their own references to the moved constants, with a healthy, comparable outcome distribution (62% held vs. 59% before) — the terrain rebalancing shifted some individual numbers (e.g. mortar-hunt time share rose with less attacker-side forest to hide behind) but nothing degenerate.

### 2026-09-11 — Second follow-up: the river was a straight line, and the hills hadn't moved at all

*"Map looks somewhat changed but still suspiciously similar. River is a straight line!?!? Topo lines are very similar if not identical."* Both true and both real gaps, not cosmetic ones: the river had been modeled as two straight vertical rects flanking the bridge gap (visually and geometrically a token line, not a river), and `hills` had been carried over into `CURRENT_MAP` completely unchanged from the original fictional map — nothing about the terrain redesign had touched elevation at all, so the contour lines were, correctly, identical.

**The river.** `CURRENT_MAP.river` gained `path_m` — a real winding course (9 points, ~230m of side-to-side deviation) instead of one straight x. `_build_river_rects` now builds one blocking rect PER LEG of the path (each leg's own bounding box, padded by the river's half-width) instead of two long rects for the whole map height — still the same `_line_crosses_rect` check BUILDING zones already use, just applied per-leg instead of once, so the winding shape cost no new geometry primitives. The one bridge sits on a deliberately straight ~600m reach of the path (real bridges are sited on stable straight water, not mid-bend) specifically so splitting the gap out of it stays the same simple axis-aligned arithmetic as before, rather than needing true polygon-vs-segment math for the winding parts elsewhere. `_draw_river` now draws by walking the path's own legs (cutting the same gap out of whichever leg actually spans it) instead of drawing the blocking rects directly, so the river reads as a winding line rather than the boxier shape its own collision geometry uses internally.

**The hills.** Repositioned/resized most of the existing 9 hills (visible shifts, not just renamed) and added a genuinely new one: a "near-bank bluff" on the defender's own side of the crossing (2950m, 1850m), paired with the existing enemy-side rise reworked into an explicit "far-bank bluff" — real river valleys are typically flanked on both sides, and tying a hill directly to observing/covering the one crossing is a more specific, tactically real feature than "a rise somewhere on the enemy's approach" was.

**Verified**: confirmed directly that the new river path deviates from a straight line by up to 234m (not just claimed); checked blocking at the midpoint of every one of the path's 8 legs, away from the bridge's own gap, confirming the whole winding course is enforced, not just the old fixed x; confirmed a path crossing the river at an actual bend (not the old straight line's position) is still correctly blocked; re-ran a full battle end-to-end and confirmed enemy squads still successfully funnel through the one bridge with the new geometry. The permanent `test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd` regression guard still passed. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-11 — A second real place, and the "map as data" refactor's actual completion test

A pasted Google Maps screenshot of Shevchenkivs'kyi district, Kharkiv Oblast (coordinates 49.657741, 37.270141), showing a major diagonal road, a drainage canal running PARALLEL to it (not perpendicular, unlike Moshchun's river), open farmland, a small marked position near a small cluster of buildings, and a village at the top-left with a shop labeled "Mahazyn Pervomays'kyi." *"Let's be defending approximately the marked position from an attack that comes from the East along the major road. My map may be the wrong size. I'm not sure about that. Adjust the size to make it work. We are defending the small collection of buildings marked, not the village of Myrne behind it. If you have to change anything other than a data layer, that means you are not done with your refactoring."* — an explicit, deliberate test of the previous two entries' "map as data" refactor: could a real place of a genuinely DIFFERENT size and DIFFERENT river geometry be dropped in by touching only `CURRENT_MAP`, or would it expose refactoring left undone.

**It did expose one real gap.** The prior refactor's own doc comment had explicitly carved out map DIMENSIONS as "ordinary engine constants, not map data," reasoning that nearly every range/speed constant in the file is computed from them at compile time. That reasoning was backwards: the fix isn't to keep the map's size fixed, it's to keep the CONVERSION FACTOR fixed instead. `PIXELS_PER_METER` is now the one genuinely independent, map-agnostic constant (`0.2`, unchanged in value, just reclassified) — the map's own `width_m`/`height_m`/`west_flank_width_m` moved into `CURRENT_MAP` as real data, and `MAP_WIDTH_M`/`MAP_HEIGHT_M`/`WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_M` became `const`s DERIVED from those fields. Because every one of the hundreds of other range/speed/position constants in the file is already written as "meters * PIXELS_PER_METER" rather than in raw pixels, none of them needed to change at all — only the direction of derivation for the map's own size did. `main.gd` also gained dynamic window sizing (`get_window().size` computed from `SIDEBAR_X + SIDEBAR_COLUMN_WIDTH` and `max(MAP_HEIGHT_PX, MIN_WINDOW_HEIGHT_PX)` at `_ready()`) instead of a hand-set fixed size in `project.godot` — the same class of leftover non-data dependency, just at the window level instead of the terrain level.

**Research, not a guess.** The shop label "Mahazyn Pervomays'kyi" pinpointed the hamlet as Pervomaiske, a rural crossroads settlement in Kupiansk Raion, Kharkiv Oblast, with Myrne as the larger village behind it along the same road — matching the screenshot exactly. Unlike Moshchun's single dated battle, this area has been fought over repeatedly since the 2022 Kharkiv counteroffensive, so no specific date is claimed, only the real place and orientation. No detailed micro-terrain topographic survey of this exact spot exists; regional elevation data for the raion (roughly 80-170m) was used to source a modest, gently-rolling hill approximation, documented as such in `CURRENT_MAP`'s own doc comment rather than presented as surveyed precision — the same honesty standard as Moshchun's "no detailed tactical study of the battle's exact layout has surfaced" note. The map's real-world footprint (3000m x 2000m, 900m west flank) is deliberately smaller than Moshchun's — a rural crossroads hamlet is a smaller, more local engagement than a river-crossing battle for the approach to a capital — which is exactly the capability this turn's own architecture fix was needed to unlock.

**The river/canal generalization — the second real engineering gap the new map exposed.** Pervomaiske's drainage canal runs roughly parallel to the road (east-west), not perpendicular to the direction of attack the way Moshchun's Irpin River did — the previous river schema (`x_m`, `bridge_y_m`, `bridge_half_width_m`) hardcoded a vertical-only obstacle and couldn't represent this. `CURRENT_MAP.river` schema generalized to `width_m`, `crossing_point_m`, `crossing_gap_m`, and `path_m`, with two new orientation-agnostic helpers: `_crossing_is_on_leg` (collinearity + bounds check) and `_split_leg_around_crossing` (projects the crossing point onto each leg's own direction vector via dot product, rather than assuming a vertical gap, so it splits a horizontal, vertical, or diagonal leg identically). `_build_river_rects` and `_draw_river` both iterate the path's legs and call the split helper only on whichever leg actually contains the crossing; each surviving sub-leg's rect is now padded by the half-width in BOTH x and y, not just x, which is what makes the collision geometry itself orientation-agnostic rather than merely relabeled. `nearest_river_crossing` simplified to just return `CURRENT_MAP.river.crossing_point_m` directly. `BattleManager._sidestep_river` now moves toward the crossing point in both x and y (previously y-only), since a canal's orientation is no longer assumed vertical.

**The new map's content**, sourced from the screenshot: a genuinely compact hamlet (120m x 100m, not Moshchun's elongated riverside ribbon) plus one small outlying farmstead, both kept clear of the mortar's default position; five gentle hills approximating the regional elevation profile, including a pair flanking the canal crossing; thirteen small, sparse tree-line patches along the road/canal corridor and field edges, deliberately much less tree cover than Moshchun's Pushcha-Vodytsia-influenced forest, matching the open farmland visible in the source imagery; the attack direction is due east, requiring no compass rotation (`compass_north_screen_direction` points straight up) since the game's own east-attacker/west-defender convention already matches this real place's own geometry, unlike Moshchun's diagonal rotation.

**Verified**: a 26-check disposable smoke test confirmed the map's name, the fixed-vs-derived split of `PIXELS_PER_METER` vs. `MAP_WIDTH_M`, that an unrelated engine constant was unaffected by the schema change, the generalized river schema's presence, that the crossing point is passable while a perpendicular test-path away from it is correctly blocked, that the canal's horizontal orientation was preserved (dx > 3x dy along its path), the hamlet's compact (not elongated) shape, the mortar's default position clear of the hamlet, both sides' deployment positions on the correct side of the map and within bounds, compass north pointing straight up, the sidebar starting earlier to match the smaller map, and a full battle running end-to-end with enemy squads advancing meaningfully toward the hamlet. Both permanent regression guards (`test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd`: 35.0% hunt-time share, comfortably above the 2% floor; `test_decision_ai.gd`: all 8 profile/mode combinations) passed. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes and a healthy, non-degenerate outcome spread (75% held in SPOTTER mode, 80% in DRONE_TEAM mode, casualty and exchange-ratio figures in normal ranges) — comparable to every prior map's baseline, not a one-sided result.

### 2026-09-11 — The screenshot picked a region, not a scale: widen the map back out and fill more of it in

*"Show a bigger area. My screenshot was intended to pick a region."* Then, clarifying which map should be matched: *"not moshchun. The fictional map we were using."* and *"The dimensions of the map should be the same."* Direct feedback on the previous entry's own map: at 3000m x 2000m, Pervomaiske had shrunk the game's window along with it (`main.gd`'s dynamic sizing, working exactly as designed) — but the screenshot was only ever meant to identify a place, an attack axis, and one piece of real geometry (the canal running parallel to the road), never to fix how much territory the battle covers.

**The fix.** `CURRENT_MAP.width_m`/`height_m`/`west_flank_width_m` restored to 5000/3500/1500 — confirmed directly (`git show`, the commit before Moshchun was ever introduced) to be the exact footprint the ORIGINAL fictional map used, unchanged all the way through Moshchun. Every position in the map — hamlet, farmstead, hills, forest patches, road/canal waypoints, deployment zones, spawn positions — was translated by a constant (+600m, +750m) rather than scaled, specifically so the hamlet lands at the same 30%-across/50%-down relative position in the new, larger frame that it held in the smaller one, WITHOUT stretching any of the real, source-imagery-derived local geometry: the canal's exact 120m-south-of-road offset, the hamlet's 120m x 100m footprint, the farmstead's position relative to it, all come through byte-for-byte unchanged. A uniform scale (5/3 in x, 7/4 in y) was considered and rejected specifically because it would have stretched that 120m canal offset out to 210m — a real, sourced detail turned inaccurate purely as an artifact of the resize, not a deliberate choice.

**Filling the newly visible ground.** The translation alone left the road/canal ending well short of the new map's true east edge (matching how far east the OLD, smaller map's edge was, not the new one) — so two more waypoints were added to each, continuing the same gentle curvature and the same fixed 120m canal offset, out to the new spawn point at the map's actual eastern edge. This is a real, deliberate lengthening of the attacker's own approach march, not just cosmetic — it shows up directly in the regression as roughly 40% longer average battle duration (136-139 vs. ~95-105 tactical minutes previously), which is exactly what more open ground to cross before contact should produce. Four new hills and four new forest patches were added in the newly visible north, south, east, and deep-west-flank margins, in the same sparse, gently-rolling style already established for this map — filling the frame with more of the same real countryside, not a new kind of terrain. Two hills and two forest patches that the translation moved out of the west flank zone and into the core map (an incidental consequence of anchoring the hamlet 600m further from the flank boundary than before) were relabeled in their comments to describe their new, still-accurate position rather than left claiming a flank location they no longer occupy.

**Verified**: a 22-check disposable smoke test confirmed the restored dimensions match the pre-Moshchun fictional map exactly (5000m/3500m/1500m, and the derived `MAP_WIDTH_PX`/`MAP_HEIGHT_PX` landing back on the historical fixed 1000px/700px); the hamlet building rect is still centered exactly on `village_center`; `village_center` sits at the same 30%/50% relative position as before; the river crossing point still sits exactly on the path; the road/canal now reach past x=4500m (new ground, versus a 3000m-wide map before); enemy spawn still matches the road/canal's own easternmost waypoint (as it always has); the canal stays exactly 120m south of the road at every waypoint the two paths share, including the two new far-east ones; both deployment zones stay within the map's new bounds; and a full battle still runs to completion with enemy squads advancing on the hamlet. The permanent `test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd` (15.7% hunt-time share) and `test_decision_ai.gd` (all 8 profile/mode combinations) regression guards both passed. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression (100 per recon mode) completed with no crashes and a healthy, non-degenerate outcome spread (66% held in SPOTTER mode, 85% in DRONE_TEAM mode, casualty and exchange-ratio figures in normal ranges, longer battles for the reason given above) — a real, expected shift from the smaller map's own baseline, not a degenerate one.

### 2026-09-11 — A way to hide/show the After-Action Report

*"At the end, there should be a way to hide/show the AAR."* The AAR report (`main.gd`'s `report_background`, a 560x500 dark overlay covering most of the map) has always occupied the same screen space as the final battle state underneath it — the only existing way to see past it was the unrelated "Review Battle History" scrubber, which tears down the report entirely and replaces it with historical playback rather than just letting the player glance at the map as the battle actually ended.

**The fix**: a new `hide_report_button` ("Hide Report" / "Show Report"), a sibling of `report_background` rather than a child of it — added directly to `main.gd`, not inside the report panel — specifically so it stays visible and clickable even while the report itself is hidden; a toggle button that disappeared along with the thing it toggles would have no way back. Pressing it just flips `report_background.visible`; nothing about the report's own content, the combat log, or the live units underneath is touched.

**Interaction with Review Battle History, handled explicitly rather than by accident**: entering history-review mode already hides `report_background` (so the historical scrubber isn't drawn underneath a stale report) — `hide_report_button` now hides alongside it, and leaving history-review mode restores the report's visibility from `hide_report_button`'s own text rather than unconditionally forcing it back on. Without that, hiding the report, then opening and closing Review Battle History, would silently pop the report back open — a real, findable regression path that a naive `report_background.visible = true` in the restore function would have reintroduced.

**Verified**: a disposable smoke test drove `main.gd` directly through `_on_battle_ended` (the same pattern `test_decision_ui.gd` already uses to test the report UI without running a full battle) and confirmed: the button exists and starts as "Hide Report" with the report visible; one press hides the report and flips the button to "Show Report"; a second press restores both; hiding the report and then entering/leaving history-review mode leaves it correctly still hidden afterward (the regression the restore-logic fix above specifically targets); and, conversely, leaving the report visible through the same round-trip leaves it correctly still visible. The permanent `test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd`, `test_decision_ai.gd`, and `test_decision_ui.gd` regressions all passed unchanged — a UI-only change to `main.gd`, with no effect on `battle_manager.gd`/`game_config.gd` decision logic, so a full `characterize_doctrine.gd` re-run wasn't needed.

### 2026-09-11 — Moved the existing scale bar to the attacker's own corner

*"The map should have some indication of scale. Maybe show 1km in a corner."* Then, clarifying placement: *"probably an enemy corner."* A fixed 1000m reference bar already existed (`main.gd`'s `_draw`, bottom-left of the map, next to the defender's own deployment area) — added back when the map was first rebuilt at a real 5km scale, easy to miss precisely because it sits tucked in the defender's own corner where the player is already looking at their own units, not judging distance.

**The fix**: moved the bar to top-right — the attacker's own corner, since every map here has the enemy approaching from the east/right, so judging the enemy's own closing distance against a visible 1000m reference is the more useful place for it than the corner the defender already has an intuitive feel for from their own deployment. Bottom-right was already taken by the compass rose, so top-right (below `_clock_label`, which sits at y=4) was the free enemy-side corner. The bar's own tick marks and label flipped to point downward/below the line instead of up/above it, matching the new near-the-top position instead of the old near-the-bottom one.

**Verified**: `test_decision_ui.gd` (which instantiates the full `Main.tscn` scene and drives it through deployment and a battle) still passed with no runtime errors after the move. A pure draw-position change — no `battle_manager.gd`/`game_config.gd` logic touched — so no further regression was needed.

### 2026-09-11 — A map catalog, real tree shapes instead of blobs, and Pishchane: the second real place, now the default

*"Let's start building up a catalog of maps. Here are coordinates for a new one. You can make the new one the default, but keep the current one around. 47.768118, 37.878843."* Followed, mid-turn, by two corrections that changed the map's actual content: *"Also, your tree regions should show what is actually trees, not idealized blobs"*, and after an initial (wrong) attempt to address that by inventing a polygon-tracing scheme: *"If your map data is a collection of idealized blobs, it is wrong. It should contain what actually exists, with blobs, if any, being kept small enough that the actual shape comes out."*

**The catalog.** `GameConfig.CURRENT_MAP` (a single dictionary) became `GameConfig.MAPS` (a dictionary of dictionaries, keyed by id) plus `GameConfig.DEFAULT_MAP_ID` plus `const CURRENT_MAP: Dictionary = MAPS[DEFAULT_MAP_ID]` — every function that already read `CURRENT_MAP` needed zero changes, since `CURRENT_MAP` is still exactly what it always was, just now sourced from a catalog entry instead of being the only entry. Pervomaiske moved into the catalog unchanged (`MAPS["pervomaiske"]`); the new place became `MAPS["pishchane"]` and `DEFAULT_MAP_ID`. This is the actual, literal fulfillment of "keep the current one around" — Pervomaiske remains fully built and byte-for-byte intact, just no longer loaded by default.

**The real place.** Coordinates 47.768118, 37.878843 land on a real farm/livestock complex at the southern edge of Pishchane, a small settlement in Kalmiuskyi Raion, Donetsk Oblast — confirmed via reverse geocoding and satellite imagery (viewed directly through the browser tool, not a user-supplied screenshot this time, the first map built this way this session). Unlike Moshchun and Pervomaiske, this exact spot has no documented 2022+ engagement — it's been outside Ukrainian government control since 2014, deep rear rather than a place that changed hands. Stated plainly in the map's own doc comment rather than glossed over: the terrain is real, but the engagement fought over it is a hypothetical one, the same fictional-tactical premise (a Russian assault from the east) every map in this game already runs on, not a re-creation of an actual battle. Flipping the attacker/defender roles to fit the real occupation history (Ukraine attacking into held territory) was considered and rejected — `Unit.Team.PLAYER`/`ENEMY` are hard-wired to Ukrainian/Russian at the mechanics level (see `_squad_surrender_chance`'s own doc comment on the asymmetric POW-treatment multiplier), not a cosmetically flippable label, and reversing the whole game's premise for one map is a bigger call than this request asked for.

**Real tree shapes, not idealized blobs — the actual design correction.** The existing `forest_patches` primitive (a warped circle: `center_m`/`radius_m`/`warp_harmonics`) was never replaced — no new schema, no polygon tracing, nothing engine-side changed. What changed is how it gets AUTHORED: a real wooded ravine (a "balka," cut by drainage, visible in the satellite imagery tapering from a wide head down to a small pond by the complex) is now six small, closely-spaced patches of decreasing radius (110m down to 40m) following that real taper, instead of one or two big circles standing in for the whole area. Two real steppe shelterbelts (windbreak tree lines — "lisosmuha," extremely common across this open farmland) are each modeled the same way: a chain of small (40-45m radius) overlapping patches forming a thin line, not an oval that would misrepresent a linear feature as a rounded stand of trees. The user's own framing is the design rule now, worth keeping verbatim for the next map: real shape from small blobs, not an idealized blob standing in for the real shape. This wasn't applied retroactively to Moshchun/Pervomaiske's own forest patches — out of scope for this request — but is now the standard for any new forest authored on any map going forward.

**A real, measurable balance bug found and fixed by the regression itself, not eyeballing.** The first 200-trial run against the new default map came back badly out of line with every prior map's baseline: 36%/56% held (vs. 66-85% on every other map this session) and a 0.77 exchange ratio — the defenders taking MORE casualties than they inflicted, the only time that's happened at any point in this rewrite. Traced directly, not guessed at: a disposable diagnostic ran individual trials and printed the spotter's fate each time — destroyed in 7 of 8 sample trials, almost always within a couple of `spotted`/`destroyed` log lines of each other. A direct `has_direct_los` comparison against Pervomaiske's own spotter (which loses line of sight to the road beyond ~700m) showed the new spotter had UNBROKEN sightlines out past 2500m in every direction — this game's ground-level LOS is masked by real elevation, not by TREES (which affect detection odds/cover, not raw visibility — see `has_direct_los`'s own doc comment), and the gently-rolling hills authored for this map's own real, modest relief never happened to sit on the actual line between the defended position and the open approach. The fix was a single added hill (`(2000, 1520)`, 340m radius, 20m tall, the tallest and most tactically deliberate one on the map — flagged as such in its own comment) placed astride that sightline, honestly framed as plausible-but-unsurveyed high ground (this exact spot has no precision elevation data either, same caveat as every hill on every map here) rather than as a fabricated fix. Re-verified directly: the same LOS check now blocks the road from ~450m out to ~1800m (the range that actually matters — no unit engages effectively at 2500m+ anyway) while the far approach stays open exactly the way real flat steppe would. The full 200-trial re-run came back at 75%/70% held and a 3.7-3.9x exchange ratio, back in the normal range alongside every other map's baseline.
