**Everything else about the new map**, sourced from the same imagery: the complex itself and a smaller outbuilding just south of it (the actual defended terrain_zones), Pishchane's own residential streets north of both (real, present, off the main east-west attack axis entirely — modeled as real terrain rather than forced onto that axis just to have somewhere to put it), a diagonal (not due east-west) road/canal-adjacent axis reproduced as a steady y-drift the same way Pervomaiske's own local diagonal geometry already was, and no river — the only real watercourse here is the small pond by the complex, too local to model as a map-spanning obstacle, which is also what prompted the one genuine engine generalization in this pass: `_build_river_rects`/`_draw_river` now check `CURRENT_MAP.has("river")` and simply skip (empty rects, nothing drawn) rather than assuming every map has one — the map-as-data refactor's own logical conclusion, since "no river here" is exactly as valid a piece of map data as "a canal here" or "a river there."

**Verified**: a 25-check disposable smoke test covered the catalog structure (both maps present, Pervomaiske's own river still intact, Pishchane correctly has none), the optional-river engine change (no crash, never blocks, on a map with no river key at all), the complex centered on `village_center`, the forest's own small-patches property (≥20 patches, none over 120m radius, the ravine chain actually tapering, the shelterbelt patches close enough to read as a line), deployment zones within bounds, no default unit position landing inside a building, the road spanning rear to near the east edge, and a full battle running to completion. Both permanent regression guards passed (`test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd`: 19.7% hunt-time share; `test_decision_ai.gd`: all 8 profile/mode combinations; `test_decision_ui.gd`, which instantiates the full scene, also passed unchanged). The full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression — the one that actually caught the balance bug in the first place — came back healthy after the fix, as detailed above.

### 2026-09-11 — Elevation relative to sea level, and a contour interval that actually fits the map

*"Thanks, but elevation is defined relative to sea level."* Followed by: *"Also, contour lines can be drawn every 5 or 10 meters I think. The current map has very few of them, so probably every 5 on that one."* Then, refining that into the actual right fix rather than another authored number: *"The code should probably look at a map and choose a delta for elevation drawing, based on the difference between the highest and lowest points on the map."*

**Elevation now reads as a real ASL figure.** `elevation_m()` returned "0m" for flat ground and just the local hill contribution above that — accurate as a relief measurement, but not what "elevation" means on a real map of a real place. Each catalog entry gained its own `elevation_baseline_m`, the midpoint of the regional ASL range already researched and cited in that map's own doc comment (Pervomaiske: 125m, from its stated 80-170m range; Pishchane: 185m, from its stated 150-220m range) — `elevation_m()` now adds this once, on top of the existing per-hill sum. Confirmed safe everywhere else elevation gets used, not just assumed: `has_direct_los` and `_build_contour_cache` only ever COMPARE or DIFFERENCE two elevation readings, and a shared additive constant cancels out of both identically — the hover label now shows a real sea-level number, but line-of-sight masking (including the reverse-slope hill added in the previous entry) is mathematically untouched.

**Contour spacing is now chosen from the map's own actual relief, not a single fixed number.** The old `CONTOUR_INTERVAL_M` (10.0, one value for every map ever loaded) is gone; `_build_contour_cache` now measures the real spread (highest sampled point minus lowest) across its own sampling grid — a value it was already computing anyway, just not keeping — and `_choose_contour_interval_m` picks 5m/10m/20m/25m from that, the same way a real topographic sheet's contour interval is chosen per area rather than fixed across every kind of terrain. This mattered concretely: Pishchane's hills are individually modest (9-20m), but several overlap enough that the map's TRUE combined relief runs closer to 30m in places — real Gaussian-hill stacking the old fixed-bucket-by-single-hill-height approach would have missed entirely. Contour levels themselves also had to become baseline-aware: they're now the first round multiple of the chosen interval at or above the map's own lowest sampled elevation (e.g. Pishchane's lines land on 190m, 195m, 200m...), not "one interval above zero" — a map whose baseline isn't itself a multiple of the interval would otherwise have silently drawn no lines, or lines that don't read as round real-world numbers the way an actual contour sheet's would.

**Verified**: a disposable smoke test confirmed flat, hill-free ground reads within 1m of each map's own cited baseline (185m Pishchane, 125m Pervomaiske — checked by temporarily swapping `DEFAULT_MAP_ID` and re-running, not just eyeballed); a hill's own peak reads at or above baseline-plus-its-declared-height (a floor, not an exact match, since nearby overlapping hills only ever add more); every generated contour level is a real number at or above the map's baseline; and the dynamically-chosen interval actually responds to a map's true combined relief rather than any single hill's own declared height (5m for Pishchane's ~30m real combined relief once overlap is accounted for, matching Pervomaiske's own gentler stacking too). Confirmed live in the running game (launched directly, not just headless) that the elevation hover label now reads a real three-digit ASL figure instead of a small relief-only number. Both permanent regression guards and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run all passed unchanged (65%/75% held, exchange ratios in the normal 3-3.7x range) — a purely cosmetic/display change, no effect on any decision-making logic.

### 2026-09-11 — Enemy squads/mortars numbered by discovery order, not spawn order

*"In all user-visible information, including the map and the AAR, enemy squads and mortars should be numbered in the order they are discovered."* Confirmed directly first: `_make_unit` assigned "Enemy Squad N"/"Enemy Mortar N" at SPAWN time, via an incrementing counter keyed by team+kind — a real, internal army-list order the player has no way to know and no reason to expect matches what they've actually seen. A disposable diagnostic from an earlier entry this session had already surfaced this by accident ("Enemy Squad 3 was spotted" logged before "Enemy Squad 1 was spotted" — direct evidence the numbers never tracked discovery at all).

**The fix.** Enemy SQUAD/MORTAR units are now left unnumbered at spawn (`unit_label` stays plain "Squad"/"Mortar") and get their real number assigned by a new `_assign_discovery_number`, called at the moment the PLAYER'S OWN side first spots one (`_refresh_visibility`, gated on `target.team == Unit.Team.ENEMY` so the symmetric enemy-spots-player call never touches it) — strictly before that spotting is logged or the unit is first drawn on the map, so the very first "was spotted" line and the very first visible frame already carry the assigned number. Two additional real reveal channels, not just visual spotting, needed the same hook: a bunching-spillover victim (picked purely by proximity to the actual target, per `_bunched_ally`'s own doc comment — never has to be individually spotted to get named in the log) and a mortar whose muzzle flash triggers counter-battery detection (`_refresh_visibility`'s own doc comment already calls this out as "a separate, complementary channel... even without a current visual sighting"). Any enemy squad/mortar the player never spots at all during the fight still needs a real number by the time the AAR's full battlefield sweep (holding the ground) or the deliberately omniscient Damage-by-unit report names it — `_number_remaining_undiscovered_enemies()`, called once at `_end_battle()`, hands out whatever numbers are left, in roster order, to anything still unnumbered; there's no real "discovery order" for a unit nobody ever found, so this is "next available," not a claim the player found it first.

**A real crash found and fixed along the way, not just the feature itself.** `UnitCombatStats` used to store each unit's name as a frozen string, captured once at registration — wrong for a name that can change mid-battle (a unit numbered well after its own first shot was recorded), so rows were changed to hold a live Unit reference instead, resolving `display_name()` at report time. That's safe for SQUAD/MORTAR, which stay in the roster even destroyed specifically so the AAR can reference them — but a DRONE is a fresh Unit per sortie and DOES get freed once shot down or returned home (`_next_drone_number`'s own doc comment already says so), and the live game crashed on exactly that: "Damage by unit" report generation calling `.display_name()` on an already-freed drone. Fixed with `is_instance_valid()` plus a `label_fallback` captured once at registration — the live name wins whenever the reference is still good (picking up any renumbering), the fallback covers a reference that's gone.

**Verified**: a disposable smoke test drove a full battle and confirmed every enemy squad/mortar starts genuinely unnumbered (plain "Squad"/"Mortar") while every player unit is numbered immediately as before (unaffected); the assigned numbers land as a real 1..N sequence per kind with no gaps or duplicates; every enemy squad/mortar is numbered by battle's end regardless of whether it was ever actually spotted; the observed order units first appear under a real number matches actual spotting order, not spawn order; and the Damage-by-unit report contains no unnumbered "Enemy Squad"/"Enemy Mortar" line. The freed-drone crash was reproduced directly first (confirmed crashing on the pre-fix code, matching the exact error the user hit live), then confirmed fixed. All three regression suites (`test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd`: 19.7%; `test_decision_ai.gd`: all 8 combinations; `test_decision_ui.gd`, which exercises the Damage-by-unit tab directly) passed, and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression came back healthy (64%/74% held, 2.1-4.5x exchange ratio) — a naming-only change, no effect on decision logic.

### 2026-09-11 — A real map picker, and coordinates so a player can look the place up themselves

*"Let's have a drop-down in the setup with the available maps. Have the dropdown give the place name, but also have the map show coordinates so the user can look up the location on google maps."* The first genuine test of the map catalog as a CATALOG rather than a single hardcoded default — selecting a map at runtime, not just changing which one `DEFAULT_MAP_ID` points to.

**The architectural piece that had to change first.** `CURRENT_MAP` and everything derived from it — `MAP_WIDTH_M/HEIGHT_M/PX`, `WEST_FLANK_WIDTH_M/PX`, `CAMERA_VIEWPORT_WIDTH_PX/CENTER_X`, `SIDEBAR_X`, `PLAYER_SAFE_X`, `ENEMY_SAFE_X` — were all `const`, evaluated once when the script first loaded. That was fine as long as "which map is loaded" was itself a compile-time fact (a hardcoded `DEFAULT_MAP_ID`); it stops being fine the instant a player can pick a different one at runtime, since a GDScript `const` can never be reassigned. All twelve became `static var`s instead, recomputed by one `_recompute_map_derived_state()` — called automatically once at script load via Godot 4.4's `_static_init()` hook (so every existing call site keeps working exactly as before, with zero syntax changes anywhere else in the codebase — a `static var` reads identically to a `const` from outside the class), and again by the new `GameConfig.set_active_map(id)` every time the player picks a different one. The river/contour caches (`_river_rects_built`, `_contour_cache_built`, etc.) get cleared in the same place — both are genuinely "build once, reuse forever" caches, which is exactly wrong the moment "forever" can mean "until the player picks a different map."

**main.gd's own share of the same problem.** Window size, `map_container`/`map_viewport` size, and `map_camera`'s position/limits were all set once, inline, in `_ready()`. Pulled out into `_apply_map_dimensions()`, called once at startup and again by a new `_on_map_chosen` handler whenever `LevelSelectScreen`'s own new `map_chosen` signal fires — so picking a different map resizes the window and re-bounds the camera live, still on the level-select screen, not just the next time the game happens to restart. (Today's two catalog maps happen to share the exact same 5000m x 3500m footprint, so this doesn't visibly change anything yet — but the point of the fix is that it would, correctly, the moment a differently-sized real place joins the catalog, instead of silently mis-sizing the window for it.)

**The dropdown itself**: `LevelSelectScreen` gained an `OptionButton` listing every `GameConfig.MAPS` entry by its own place name (`Pervomaiske`, `Pishchane`) — never the internal catalog id — defaulting to whichever map is already active rather than always the first entry, so reopening this screen never silently re-picks a map different from the one already loaded. Selecting a new one takes effect immediately (no separate "Choose" button to confirm first) — there's nothing to commit to first the way recon mode has, since picking a place doesn't shape anything downstream the way recon mode does.

**Coordinates, so a player can go verify the place themselves.** Each catalog entry gained a `"coordinates"` field — Pervomaiske's own screenshot-cited 49.657741, 37.270141, Pishchane's user-given 47.768118, 37.878843 — appended to the existing location readout (`main.gd`'s `_location_label`, already always on screen): `"Pishchane, Kalmiuskyi Raion, Donetsk Oblast (47.768118, 37.878843)"`. The same real numbers this map was actually built from, not a re-derived approximation.

**Verified**: a disposable smoke test drove the actual `Main.tscn` scene, confirmed the dropdown starts on the currently-active map (not always index 0) and lists exactly the catalog's own entries, then simulated selecting "Pervomaiske" exactly as a player click would and confirmed: `GameConfig.CURRENT_MAP` actually switched, the location label updated to Pervomaiske's own coordinates, `map_container`'s width and the camera's left limit both updated to the newly-selected map's own values, the window resized correctly, and deployment — entered afterward — built against the newly-selected map's own data, not whatever was active at startup. A separate smoke test exercised `GameConfig.set_active_map` directly: switching to Pervomaiske and back correctly rebuilt the river cache both times (no stale "no river" state carried over from Pishchane, no stale river geometry carried into it), and an unknown map id was confirmed to be a safe no-op. All three regression suites and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run (65%/78% held, 2.3-3.4x exchange ratio) passed against the still-default Pishchane map, confirming the const-to-static-var conversion didn't change ordinary single-map behavior.

### 2026-09-11 — A second, unpatched case of the same same-tick bunching bug

*"Right now, the friendly squads are way too bunched up. You can deal with it after the drop down."* Investigated directly rather than guessed at, once the map picker above was done: `_reposition_for_encirclement` (a squad's own "I'm surrounded, pull back toward my own side's center of mass" response) computes its rally point as the bare average of every other active friendly unit's position, then asks `GameConfig.nearest_cover_point` for the nearest real cover to a capped step toward it — but passed a hardcoded empty array as `avoid_positions`, never the `claimed` accumulator the sibling cover-seeking tier one loop iteration above it already threads through for exactly this reason. Several squads reading "surrounded" in the same tick, from the same shared and only slowly-changing enemy picture, naturally average to nearly the same rally point and independently ask for the same nearest cover — the identical mechanism, and nearly the identical code, as the "idle squads seeking cover in the same tick all converged on the same spot" bug fixed earlier this session, just in a sibling function nobody had given the same treatment yet.

**The fix.** `_reposition_for_encirclement` now takes `claimed` and passes it straight through to its own `nearest_cover_point` call, exactly like the cover-seeking tier already does; its return type changed from `bool` to `Vector2` (the chosen destination, or `u.global_position` unchanged if it didn't act — `nearest_cover_point`'s own established no-op convention) so the caller loop can both tell whether it fired and append the result to `claimed` for whichever squad gets processed next in the same pass, the same two things the bool return couldn't give it.

**Verified**: a disposable smoke test proved the mechanism directly, isolating it from `nearest_cover_point`'s own genuine randomness (a weighted pick among its top few candidates, seeded per-call) rather than fighting it — with the RNG seeded identically before each call, the same squad in the same situation with nothing claimed reproduces the identical destination twice (confirming the test's own determinism assumption), while marking that exact destination as claimed before a third, otherwise-identical call produces a different one. Reverting the fix (put back the hardcoded empty array) was confirmed to make this same test fail — the third call then reproduces the same destination regardless of what's claimed — proving the test actually exercises the bug, not just passing by construction. All three regression suites and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run (71%/77% held, 2.78-4.0x exchange ratio) passed unchanged.

### 2026-09-11 — High ground now makes direct fire more effective, not just easier to spot with

*"Are you having the effectiveness of fire vary based on who has the high ground? I think it should. Please research recent real-world data and implement your findings."* Checked first, not assumed: elevation already had exactly one effect in this engine — `CombatResolver.effective_detection_range`'s `ELEVATION_ADVANTAGE_THRESHOLD_M` bonus, purely about SPOTTING range. `hit_probability` itself — cover, movement, range, drone-directed accuracy — had nothing elevation-aware in it at all. The user's instinct was correct: fire effectiveness genuinely didn't vary by who held the high ground.

**The research, and why the result isn't a flat "elevation = more accurate" number.** Searched specifically for quantitative data (matching this file's own established standard — the 1948 ORO close-range dataset, the FM 7-90 mortar-casualty figures already cited elsewhere) before implementing anything. Two real, distinct effects turned up, and only one of them survives as an actual combat-effectiveness claim: (1) the "rifleman's rule" ballistic effect (an unadjusted uphill/downhill shot flies high, since gravity only acts over the horizontal component of a slanted shot) is real physics, but it's a shooter's AIM-CORRECTION problem that trained soldiers account for, not a demonstrated hit-probability swing in real combat data — no equivalent of the ORO/FM 7-90 numbers exists for it, so it isn't modeled. (2) Plunging/elevated fire defeating cover built to stop a flat, level shot IS a well-documented doctrinal fact — it's the entire reason a "reverse slope defense" exists (positioning behind a crest specifically to deny the enemy the ability to see and fire down INTO the position) and why infantry doctrine instructs against occupying ground observable from higher terrain. This is qualitative, real doctrine rather than a precise number, same honesty standard as this file's own elevation_baseline_m/contour-interval entries.

**The mechanic**: a new `GameConfig.ELEVATION_COVER_DEFEAT_FRACTION` (0.4) — when a direct-fire attacker is genuinely higher than the defender (the SAME `ELEVATION_ADVANTAGE_THRESHOLD_M` gate the existing detection bonus already uses, so "high enough to matter" means one consistent thing everywhere in the file), the defender's own terrain-cover multiplier is blended 40% of the way toward 1.0 (neutral, "as if no cover") — cover that would normally cut incoming fire to 20% effectiveness only cuts it to 52%. Capped at neutral, never past it: elevation lets an attacker see and shoot INTO a position, it doesn't make holding that position more dangerous than standing in the open would already be (open ground's own separate exposure penalty is a different mechanism entirely). Applies only to direct (squad) fire — a mortar's plunging trajectory already does this regardless of the tube's own elevation, which is exactly what its own, already-weaker `MORTAR_COVER_MULTIPLIER` table represents — and only to a stationary defender, since a moving one already gets no cover benefit to defeat in the first place. Reused directly by `RiskForecast` and every other caller of `hit_probability` for free, since they all funnel through the one function — a squad's own self-risk evaluation now correctly reads an elevated enemy position as more dangerous to approach.

**Verified**: a disposable smoke test found a real TREES-covered point on the default map far enough from a real hill to isolate the effect from range/close-combat terms, then hand-computed the expected chance from the pre-existing formula alone and confirmed the actual function's output matches the elevation-adjusted formula EXACTLY (0.064 → 0.166 for this specific case) — not just "different," but the precise predicted number. Confirmed a moving defender is unaffected (no cover to defeat), mortar fire is unaffected regardless of the tube's own elevation, and firing UP the hill gets no reciprocal bonus (an attacker-relative-to-defender check, not "whoever's on the hill always wins"). All three regression suites passed, and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run (67%/70% held, 2.43-3.01x exchange ratio) stayed within the normal range.

### 2026-09-11 — A right tail on assault size, and a looser squad-to-mortar ratio

*"Next, let's add a right tail to the size of the enemy assault. Up to 15 squads and 5 mortars, with the squad count still being chosen first. But 85% of the time, it should still be in the 2-10 squad count. Also, allow the number of mortars to more frequently vary from 1/3 of the squad count. Stuff like 8 squads and 1 mortar should happen occasionally."*

**Squad count becomes a two-piece mixture, not a single wider uniform range.** Widening the existing uniform `[2, 10]` roll straight out to `[2, 15]` would have thinned out the ordinary 2-10 band the player actually sees most battles (each count only 1/14 likely instead of 1/9) just to make room for a rare, much larger one — not what "add a right tail" asked for. Instead, `roll_enemy_force_size` now rolls a coin first: `ENEMY_SQUAD_COUNT_TAIL_CHANCE` (15%) of the time, squads come from a separate uniform tail range (`ENEMY_SQUAD_COUNT_MAX+1..ENEMY_SQUAD_COUNT_TAIL_MAX`, i.e. 11-15); the other 85% of the time, the roll is exactly what it always was (uniform 2-10). This is a real, if simple, statistical modeling choice — a mixture of two uniforms, matching how a real assault is usually a company-minus-sized probe with an occasional much larger one, not a smooth gradient between the two.

**Mortar count gets a wider jitter.** `ENEMY_MORTAR_COUNT_JITTER` doubled from 1 to 2, and `ENEMY_MORTAR_COUNT_MAX` raised from 3 to 5 (matching 1/3 of the new 15-squad ceiling) — the derived-from-squads formula and its "rolling squads first so the mortar clamp can't distort the player-visible squad count" reasoning are otherwise unchanged. A wider jitter is what actually makes "8 squads, 1 mortar" possible at all: the old ±1 jitter around round(8/3)=3 could only ever land on 2-4, never dipping anywhere near 1.

**A real, secondary layout consequence, found and fixed rather than ignored: CasualtyDashboard now scrolls.** The dashboard pre-allocates one status row per possible enemy mortar (`ENEMY_MORTAR_COUNT_MAX`), and its own panel height was a hand-measured constant sized for the old worst case (3 rows, 550px). Naively bumping the constant to fit 5 rows (measured directly: 734px) would have meant growing the dashboard, pushing CombatLog down by the same amount, and growing the window itself again — a rare, large-assault worst case most battles never reach shouldn't force a permanently taller window for every battle. Per direct steer mid-fix ("if the right bar needs to become scrollable, that is OK"): the dashboard's own content now lives inside a `ScrollContainer` instead of directly in the panel, so the panel stays fixed at its original, already-tuned 550px (comfortably fits the common 1-3-mortar case) and only the rare 4-5-mortar worst case scrolls internally — CombatLog's position and the window's own minimum height are both back to their original, unchanged values.

**Verified**: a 20,000-trial statistical smoke test confirmed the observed squad-count range reaches the new 2-15 span, mortar count reaches the new 1-5 span, the ordinary/tail split lands within 2 percentage points of the intended 85%/15%, and "8 squads, 1 mortar" genuinely occurs (not just theoretically possible). A separate smoke test forced all 5 enemy mortar rows to their cited worst-case wrapped text and confirmed the panel's own measured size stays pinned at exactly 550px regardless — scrolling absorbs the rest rather than the panel growing. All three regression suites passed, and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run showed a real, expected shift (54%/66% held, down from the prior ~65-77% baseline, average player casualties up correspondingly) — not a degenerate result, but the direct, intended consequence of the new tail: some battles are now genuinely larger and harder, exactly what "add a right tail" was asked to do.

### 2026-09-11 — An out-of-ammo mortar had no more reason to flee slowly than a mortar with nothing wrong at all

*"Mortar was just destroyed because it failed to evade an enemy squad. It had no ammo. Hence no reason not to evade. You can do that next."* Traced directly in `_decide_mortar_action`'s tier-1 ladder, not guessed at: an out-of-ammo mortar's own relocation call, `_relocate_mortar(m, "out_of_ammo")`, and the dedicated "spotted or a threat is closing" relocation call one branch below it both funnel into the exact same underlying `nearest_hidden_point` search — but neither one ever told it "this is urgent." `_relocate_mortar`'s own `urgent` flag was wired to exactly one signal, `Unit.evading_counter_battery` (set only by an actual recent counter-battery near-miss) — a threat closing to overrun range with a clear shot, or the mortar simply being spotted, never set it. An out-of-ammo mortar being closed in on got the exact same unhurried, nearby-ring relocation (`CONCEALMENT_SEARCH_RINGS_M`, ordinary `MORTAR_RELOCATE_SPEED`) as one doing routine shoot-and-scoot housekeeping with nothing actually threatening it at all — even though, per the user's own framing, a crew with zero rounds left has no reason left to trade escape speed for concealment quality.

**The fix.** `_relocate_mortar` gained a `force_urgent` parameter, ORed with the existing `evading_counter_battery` check — "urgent" already meant, and still means, "the ground right around me is compromised, search farther out (`CONCEALMENT_SEARCH_RINGS_URGENT_M`) and move faster (`MORTAR_RELOCATE_SPEED_URGENT`)," exactly the same real posture a threat with a clear shot at overrun range calls for. `spotted`/`threat_closing` (previously computed only for the dedicated tier below) are now computed once at the top of the tier ladder and threaded through: the out-of-ammo relocation now passes `spotted or threat_closing` as `force_urgent`, and the dedicated spotted/threat-closing tier passes `true` unconditionally (reaching that branch at all already means the condition holds). The out-of-ammo resupply-linkup path picked up the same fix for the identical reason: it used to walk TOWARD an inbound resupply run even with a threat actively closing in, when survival already outranks logistics for a crew that can't shoot back either way — the linkup branch is now also skipped whenever `spotted or threat_closing`, not just during counter-battery evasion.

**Verified**: a disposable smoke test built a real out-of-ammo mortar with a real enemy squad inside `MORTAR_CREW_OVERRUN_DANGER_RANGE` holding direct LOS on it (confirmed both facts directly, not assumed), ran the actual decision function, and confirmed the resulting relocation used `MORTAR_RELOCATE_SPEED_URGENT` (0.52), not the ordinary `MORTAR_RELOCATE_SPEED` (0.44). Reverting just the one changed argument (temporarily hardcoding `false` in place of `spotted or threat_closing`) was confirmed to reproduce the exact old behavior — the same scenario then relocates at the ordinary, non-urgent speed — proving the test actually exercises the fix rather than passing by construction. All three regression suites passed, and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the same broadly-normal range the previous entry's force-size widening had already established (some added noise expected from the larger battle mix, not a new regression from this fix specifically).

### 2026-09-11 — Mortar range was too short to reach across the current maps, and the permanent hunt-regression test went blind to why

*"Enemy mortars under mortar fire seem to fire remarkably few CB strikes back at our mortar."* Measured directly rather than guessed at: a disposable diagnostic tallied real counter-battery log lines across 15 battles and found the enemy's own CB rate against the player (5.8%) actually matched its intended design rate (`MORTAR_COUNTER_BATTERY_SCOOT_CHANCE`, 6%, suppressed by the player's own default shoot-and-scoot doctrine — working as designed) — but the PLAYER's own CB rate against the enemy (9%) fell far short of ITS intended 22% (`MORTAR_COUNTER_BATTERY_HOLD_CHANCE`, since enemy mortars never scoot). A second diagnostic explained why directly: it sampled the real distance between the two sides' mortar positions at the moment of every enemy shot and found the MEDIAN distance (3779m) already exceeded `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE` (3500m) — cross-mortar counter-battery was geometrically impossible more than 80% of the time, regardless of any percentage tuning.

**Researched before touching the number**: the existing 3500m figure was never actually cited to a real mortar system, just "a real light/medium mortar tops out well short of the whole map." Real, recent (2023-2024) reporting on 82mm mortars in this exact war cites Ukrainian-produced shells reaching 4500m and Russia's currently-issued 2B24 82mm light mortar rated to 6000m — both meaningfully above the uncited 3500m, which sits closer to the older Soviet 82-BM-37's own 3040m. `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE` raised to 5000m, the conservative end of that cited 4500-6000m range rather than the high end — the same "cite a real range, land conservatively within it" practice this file already uses for `DRONE_DIRECTED_MORTAR_ACCURACY_MULTIPLIER`. Re-running both diagnostics confirmed the fix: the player's own CB rate rose to ~21.6%, right at its intended 22%, and the enemy's stayed at its own already-correct ~6%.

**A real, previously-passing permanent regression test broke as a direct, honest consequence — investigated rather than papered over.** `test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd` dropped to 0.0% (its own floor is 2%). Traced directly: the test's own "known enemy mortar" gate checked ONLY visual sighting (`is_visible`/`player_has_been_sighted`), but `_mortar_hunt_fix_for`/`_known_enemy_mortar_lead` — the ACTUAL mechanism deciding whether a mortar is worth hunting — also accepts a muzzle-flash/counter-battery detection lead (`mortar_ever_detected_firing`), independent of visual sighting entirely. Confirmed directly: every enemy mortar in the failing trials showed `ever_detected_firing=true` while `is_visible`/`player_has_been_sighted` stayed false for the WHOLE battle — real mortar duels, resolved entirely through detected-firing leads, exactly the way real counter-battery radar (not eyeballs) actually locates an enemy mortar. The test's own measurement was simply blind to this channel; it happened to pass before only because visual sighting occasionally also occurred by coincidence. A second, related gap: once "known" was fixed, `hunt_seconds` STILL read 0%, because with mortars now routinely already in range of each other from the moment either is discovered, the player's mortar resolves straight through the faster "Target available" tier (direct engagement, `_pick_target`'s own overriding priority for a mortar candidate) instead of ever needing the "Destroy enemy mortars" MOVEMENT tier to close the distance first — both are the same underlying priority actually being honored, just resolved through a different, now-more-common path.

**The fix, to the test only, not the game**: "known" now also counts `mortar_ever_detected_firing`, and "prioritizing" now also counts the "Target available" tier when its own resolved target is a mortar. Re-measured at 52.4% (floor stays 2%) — the test's own doc comment and its `MIN_HUNT_TIME_FRACTION` comment were updated to record both the new measured value and why the definition had to broaden, so the next person to see this number move isn't left re-deriving this same investigation from scratch.

**Verified**: both diagnostics were re-run after the range fix to confirm the actual counter-battery rates converged on their intended design values (not just "changed"). The regression test fix was confirmed against the real mechanism it now matches (`ever_detected_firing=true` cases genuinely exist and are now counted; the tier-0/tier-2 split was confirmed by a direct tier-by-tier breakdown showing 0% still landed in "Destroy enemy mortars" alone but ~53% in "Target available" resolved against a mortar). All three regression suites and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run (57%/56% held, 1.87-3.57x exchange ratio) stayed within the same broadly-normal range already established this session, on top of the earlier force-size widening.

### 2026-09-11 — Why the enemy's own counter-battery rate reads low: two separate chances collapsed into one

Direct follow-up question after the range fix above: *"Why is the enemy's intended CB rate low?"* The enemy's own intended rate (`MORTAR_COUNTER_BATTERY_SCOOT_CHANCE`, 6%) was deliberately lower than the player's (`MORTAR_COUNTER_BATTERY_HOLD_CHANCE`, 22%) on the theory that a shoot-and-scoot crew is harder to counter-battery than one that holds position — but that reasoning doesn't survive contact with how real counter-battery actually works: a real CB response fires at a detected firing SIGNATURE (radar-cued back-azimuth/range from the shell's own trajectory, or a visual muzzle flash), not a prediction of where the crew will be standing by the time the response lands. The crew's own choice to move afterward doesn't change whether the initial CB response gets triggered at all — it only changes whether that response, once inbound, actually catches anyone there. Ukrainian shoot-and-scoot doctrine writing consistently frames the tactic exactly this way: avoiding EFFECTIVE counter-battery (surviving the response), not avoiding ATTEMPTED counter-battery (triggering one in the first place) — while Russian mortar crews that hold position after firing are reported as suffering poor survivability specifically because they stay put through the response window, not because fewer responses get triggered against them.

This codebase already models the "does scoot save you" half of that distinction separately and correctly: `_resolve_pending_counter_battery`'s own delayed-impact check already lets a mortar that's relocated since firing dodge the resulting strike. Keeping a second, lower SCOOT_CHANCE on top of that was double-counting the same real-world effect once as "try less often" and again as "survive more often when it does come."

**Fix**: `MORTAR_COUNTER_BATTERY_HOLD_CHANCE`/`_SCOOT_CHANCE` replaced with a single `MORTAR_COUNTER_BATTERY_CHANCE = 0.22` in `game_config.gd`, applied identically in `_resolve_mortar_counter_battery` (`battle_manager.gd`) regardless of `shoot_and_scoot`. Kept at the old HOLD rate rather than splitting the difference — a real CB response is attempted whenever a firing signature is detected, and 0.22 was already the more carefully-reasoned of the two original numbers (matching this file's own "cite a real number, don't average away the reasoning" practice).

**Verified**: a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression run afterward stayed within the same broadly-normal range already established this session (no degenerate all-win/all-loss collapse). Both permanent regression suites and the standard smoke-test-sensitivity check (temporarily reverting the constant back to a split value and confirming a real, expected difference in behavior) passed before the change was finalized.

### 2026-09-11 — The Damage-by-unit tab no longer gets echoed into the AAR text too

*"Now that 'damage by unit' is its own tab on the after-battle report, it does not need to be appended to the AAR."* `_end_battle()` (`battle_manager.gd`) used to build the AAR text by appending `unit_combat_stats.report_lines()` straight onto the end of the summary lines — a leftover from before the report panel grew its own separate "Damage by unit" tab (see the earlier "'damage by unit' is its own tab" work); once that tab existed, the full unit-by-unit breakdown was showing up twice: once on demand via its own tab, and a second time unconditionally scrolled onto the end of the plain summary tab. Fix: that one `lines.append_array(...)` call removed; the summary tab's `report_text` now ends at the existing win/loss/casualty/losses lines, and the Damage-by-unit tab keeps fetching `unit_combat_stats.report_lines()` fresh on demand exactly as it already did, unaffected by the removal since it never read from `report_text` in the first place.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test driving a real battle to its natural end and confirming (a) the real `battle_ended` signal's `report_text` no longer contains "DAMAGE BY UNIT", (b) the summary tab's on-screen text doesn't either, and (c) the Damage-by-unit tab still shows its own full report when pressed. Caught and fixed two unrelated bugs in the test itself along the way: an `await`-based coroutine was reading a non-existent `main.gd` member instead of locating the report `RichTextLabel` via `find_children`, and a `bm.battle_ended.connect(func(text): captured_report = text)` lambda was silently failing to write back to its enclosing scope — GDScript lambdas capture outer locals by value, so a plain-String local can never be reassigned from inside one; the fix mutates a captured Dictionary's field instead, which does work since dictionaries are reference types. All three permanent regression suites and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range (this is a pure reporting change with no combat-logic effect, so no behavior shift was expected or found).

### 2026-09-11 — Damage-by-unit report now lists mortars-then-squads in numeric order, not by damage dealt

*"In the Damage by unit report, we can list enemy mortars in numerical order, then squads in numerical order."* Both the per-side summary section and the DETAILS section of `unit_combat_stats.gd`'s `report_lines()` used to list units in whatever order the report's own `rows` dictionary happened to iterate them (summary: sorted by casualties dealt, which reshuffles every battle; details: raw spawn/registration order) — neither is a stable place to look a specific unit up, unlike the combat log or the map, which both already use the discovery-order numbering from `BattleManager._assign_discovery_number` ("Mortar 1", "Squad 3", etc.).

Fix: a shared `_by_roster_order` comparator sorts by team, then kind (mortars before squads, matching the user's stated order), then the unit's own trailing discovery number, parsed from its resolved display name (`_resolve_name`, already existing — falls back to a registration-time label for a freed unit). Applied to both the per-side summary lists (replacing the casualties-descending sort) and the DETAILS section (which previously had no sort at all).

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test constructing `UnitCombatStats` rows directly (fabricated dictionaries, no real `Unit` instances needed since `_resolve_name` already falls back to a plain label string when `unit_ref` is null) for a mix of out-of-order mortars and squads on both sides, asserting both the summary and DETAILS sections land in mortar-then-squad, ascending-numeric order. Confirmed the test actually exercises the sort, not just passes by construction, by temporarily disabling the DETAILS section's `sort_custom` call and confirming the same test then fails on six of seven rows. A pure reporting/formatting change with no combat-logic effect; all three permanent regression suites and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range.

### 2026-09-11 — A shot at an enemy mortar now forces an urgent scoot, not just an already-hit one

Direct follow-up once the counter-battery-chance consolidation above landed: *"Now that enemy CB fire is more frequent, the friendly mortar needs to be quicker and more consistent in scooting after it shoots at an enemy mortar."* Correct diagnosis — unifying the CB attempt rate raised the enemy's own real chance of returning fire against the player's mortar from the old, favorably-discounted `SCOOT_CHANCE` (6%) to the full `MORTAR_COUNTER_BATTERY_CHANCE` (22%), but the shoot-and-scoot displacement that's supposed to be the crew's actual protection against that fire had no way to know this in advance: `_tick_fire`'s post-shot scoot only goes "urgent" (`MORTAR_RELOCATE_SPEED_URGENT`, farther `CONCEALMENT_SEARCH_RINGS_URGENT_M`) when `Unit.evading_counter_battery` is already set — and that flag is only ever set REACTIVELY, once an incoming strike has already landed close enough to matter (`_resolve_pending_counter_battery`, 1-3 tactical minutes after the shot that drew it). By the time a crew "knows" to hurry, the shell that would confirm it either already missed or already hit — the information arrives too late to inform the one displacement that could have mattered.

Real mortar crews don't wait for confirmation either: firing on a located enemy mortar is understood as the highest-risk shot a crew can take, precisely because mortars are each other's top-priority target (already modeled — see `_resolve_mortar_counter_battery`'s own doc comment) — the crew displaces with urgency on the assumption that shot may draw fire, not after finding out it did.

**Fix**: in `_tick_fire`'s existing shoot-and-scoot block, the post-shot relocation is now forced urgent (`_relocate_mortar(unit, "scoot", target.kind == Unit.Kind.MORTAR)`) whenever the shot that was just fired was AT an enemy mortar specifically — regardless of whether `evading_counter_battery` happens to be set. A shot at an ordinary squad is unaffected, still ordinary-speed/ordinary-search unless a real counter-battery near-miss has already set the reactive flag.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test constructing two bare `Unit` mortars directly (no full battle needed — `_tick_fire` doesn't require `start_battle`'s own setup) and calling `_tick_fire` once with a visible enemy mortar as the only candidate, confirming the resulting `move_speed` matches the urgent rate (adjusted for destination terrain), versus a second case with an enemy squad as the only candidate confirming ordinary speed. Confirmed the test's own sensitivity by temporarily hardcoding `forced_urgent = false` and seeing the mortar-vs-mortar case fail as expected. All three permanent regression suites and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range (a pure reaction-speed change to an existing displacement, not a new decision path).

### 2026-09-12 — A mortar can't fire, return counter-battery, or start walking away instantly — setup and pack-up now take real time

Direct follow-up question after the last two entries: *"If an enemy mortar is on the move, would it have to stop moving to launch a counter battery? Should mortars have different states for 'set up' and moving?"*, then, after being asked to ground the answer in the real world and confirming the CB-rate impact was welcome: *"Setting up should take some amount of time, as should starting to move after firing. Base both of these on the real world."*

**Research**: a mortar tube can't fire while it's being carried, and a crew can't walk off with it until it's broken back down — the only real question is how long each direction actually takes. Real, recent reporting on Russia's currently-issued 2B24 82mm light mortar (the same system already cited for `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE`) states plainly: *"the transition from traveling to firing position, and vice versa, is accomplished in less than 30 seconds"* — one figure, from one already-cited real system, covering both directions symmetrically. A second, less specific source (the Soviet-legacy 2B14 Podnos, still in wide use) puts stored-to-usable closer to 60 seconds, corroborating the same order of magnitude without contradicting the more specific, already-established citation.

**No new state machine needed**: `Unit.activity` (`STATIONARY`/`MOVING`) and `Unit.seconds_stationary` (already tracked, reset to 0 the instant a unit is actually moving — see `CombatResolver`'s spotting-signature use of it) already draw exactly the "set up" vs. "moving" line the question asked about; a mortar that's never moved at all defaults to `seconds_stationary = 1e9`, so nothing about this delays a hold-position mortar's very first shot.

**Fix**: new `GameConfig.MORTAR_SETUP_TEARDOWN_TIME = 30.0` (tactical seconds), gating three things:
- `_mortar_shot_this_tick` — a mortar can't actually resolve a shot (for itself OR for anything else asking "does it have one," e.g. the mortar decision ladder) until `seconds_stationary >= MORTAR_SETUP_TEARDOWN_TIME`.
- `_resolve_mortar_counter_battery` — the SAME floor on the responding mortar, directly answering the opening question: a mortar mid-relocation (or only just arrived) cannot return counter-battery fire.
- The shoot-and-scoot post-fire displacement no longer starts walking the instant the round is away. `_relocate_mortar`'s destination/speed-picking logic was split out into a new `_mortar_relocation_plan` helper so it isn't duplicated; the scoot path now calls `_queue_mortar_displacement`, which computes the plan immediately (same threat picture as before) but holds it in a new `_pending_mortar_displacement` dict until `_resolve_pending_mortar_displacement` (called once per tick alongside the other pending-strike resolvers) finds the pack-up delay has elapsed and only then calls `_issue_mortar_move` for real. A pending scoot that's since been superseded by a higher-priority immediate order (a tier-1 self-preservation evade, most sharply — `has_move_target` already true by the time the delay elapses) is dropped rather than clobbering it.

Deliberately scoped to exactly what was asked: the pack-up delay only applies to the routine, procedural shoot-and-scoot displacement, not to a genuine tier-1 emergency evasion (spotted, threat closing, out-of-ammo, counter-battery-evade) — those still displace immediately, on the reasoning that a crew that panicked-and-ran wouldn't pause to break the tube down properly first (which is also already separately modeled: see `mortar_gun_abandoned`).

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test constructing bare `Unit` mortars directly: a just-arrived mortar (`seconds_stationary = 0`) has no shot and never returns counter-battery fire across 300 rolls at the 22% chance, while an identically-positioned but fully-settled mortar does both normally; a never-moved mortar (default `seconds_stationary`) is unaffected; a shoot-and-scoot mortar that just fired has NOT started walking and instead has a queued pending displacement, which only actually issues once scenario time is advanced past the delay; and a higher-priority order issued before that delay elapses is confirmed to win, with the stale pending scoot silently dropped rather than overwriting it. Confirmed each of the three gates' sensitivity by temporarily disabling it in turn and watching the matching assertion fail. All three permanent regression suites passed (`test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd`'s own measured mix of "Target available" vs. "Destroy enemy mortars" shifted, as expected — engagements now spend more time in the movement tier since instant re-engagement is no longer possible — but it stayed comfortably above its own floor) and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range.

### 2026-09-12 — Damage-by-unit report now breaks "wounded" into heavily wounded and walking wounded

*"The damage by unit summary should break apart seriously wounded and walking wounded."* The AAR's own top-level casualty lines (`_end_battle`) already distinguish killed / heavily wounded / walking wounded / captured, but `unit_combat_stats.gd`'s separate "Damage by unit" report only ever tracked a single combined `wounded` figure per unit — a real information loss, since "wounded" alone can't tell a reader whether a unit's toll was mostly people who kept fighting or mostly people who didn't.

**Fix**: `UnitCombatStats.before_hit`/`damage` now snapshot and delta `Unit.heavily_wounded_count`/`walking_wounded_count` the same way they already do for `killed_count` — one independent clamped delta per bucket, matching exactly how `Unit._categorize_casualties` actually sorts newly-lost people into the three buckets in the first place (a single hit, or even a mortar hit plus its own secondary ammo cook-off within the same `take_hit()` call, is captured correctly either way since this is a pure before/after snapshot). `row.wounded` itself is left alone, still the combined total — the existing `killed + wounded == casualties` reconciliation `test_unit_doctrine.gd` already asserts on doesn't need to change at all. `report_lines()`'s TOTAL INFLICTED line and each DETAILS row now print heavily wounded and walking wounded as separate numbers instead of one merged "wounded" figure, using this codebase's own established "heavily wounded"/"walking wounded" terminology (matching the AAR) rather than the user's own "seriously wounded" phrasing, for consistency with the rest of the report.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test running a real battle to completion and checking, for every unit row: `heavily_wounded + walking_wounded == wounded` and `killed + wounded == casualties` (the pre-existing invariant, confirmed still intact), with at least one row actually having real wounded so the check isn't vacuous; and that the rendered report text mentions both new labels. Confirmed sensitivity by temporarily zeroing both new deltas and watching every row's reconciliation check fail. `test_unit_doctrine.gd` (the existing permanent test that asserts directly against `UnitCombatStats` rows) and all three standard permanent regression suites passed unchanged; a pure reporting/bookkeeping addition with no combat-logic effect, verified with a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run.

### 2026-09-12 — The player's own mortar now hesitates to reveal itself on a bare squad before any enemy mortar is known about

*"That time the friendly mortar was destroyed because it started firing as soon as the drone spotted enemy squads. At that point, it should be thinking there might be enemy mortars. At least give the drone a couple of minutes to look."* A real, previously-reported failure mode, root-caused directly: `_pick_target`'s existing ammo-conservation hold-fire logic has never had any notion of "revealing this position is itself a cost" — a full-ammo mortar with a clean shot at a freshly-spotted squad has always taken it immediately, first contact or not, with zero regard for whether an entirely unaccounted-for enemy mortar might be sitting there ready to answer with counter-battery the crew never saw coming.

First proposed as a flat "give it a couple of minutes" timer, then explicitly corrected: *"Not a fixed length of time — more like 'how long do we need to understand what the enemy is doing.'"* That reframing matters: a real commander doesn't hold indirect fire back for an arbitrary clock, they hold it back for as long as the picture is still genuinely unclear, and keep re-assessing as evidence comes in.

**Fix**: reused, rather than reinvented — `BattleManager._mortar_existence_confidence()` already computes exactly this "how confident should we still be that an unaccounted-for enemy mortar is out there" estimate (built earlier this session for the drone's own routine-recon weighting): 1.0 at first contact, decaying over `MORTAR_CONFIDENCE_DECAY_TAU` (15 tactical minutes) as no enemy mortar fire is detected anywhere, hard zero once every enemy mortar is confirmed destroyed. New `GameConfig.MORTAR_UNKNOWN_ENEMY_HOLD_FIRE_CHANCE = 0.75` (the hold chance at that confidence's peak, a judgment call) scales by it, gating a new hold-fire check in `_pick_target`, right alongside the existing pursuit-hold: only applies to the player's own mortar (enemy targeting intentionally untouched, matching this doctrine's own "enemy may differ" principle), only when no enemy mortar has actually been found yet (no live mortar candidate, no fix, no lead — the moment ANY of those exist, the picture is no longer blind and the hold stops applying outright), and never when a squad is already within overrun range (self-preservation always wins regardless).

Deliberately probabilistic rather than a real clock, but tuned so its EXPECTED behavior still lands close to the original "couple of minutes" framing: at this mortar's own ~30-tactical-second fire-decision cadence, a 0.75 peak hold chance means roughly 4 checks (~2 minutes) before firing anyway even at maximum uncertainty — without ever actually being a timer. A lucky roll can fire sooner (a real commander sometimes has to take a fleeting shot), a run of bad luck holds longer, and the whole thing keeps loosening on its own as real evidence (or a growing absence of it) accumulates, exactly the "how long do we need to understand what's going on" framing asked for.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test constructing bare `Unit`s directly: with zero active enemy mortars anywhere in the roster (confidence forced to 0), the hold never triggers; with a real, unspotted enemy mortar present at first contact (confidence at its peak), a genuine mix of holds and fires resulted (46/60 held in one run, consistent with the 0.75 peak chance) rather than an absolute block; once that same mortar is actually spotted, the hold stopped applying entirely and it was chosen as the target outright every time; and a squad within overrun range always forced the shot regardless. Confirmed sensitivity by temporarily disabling the new check and watching the first-contact case fire 60/60 instead of holding. All three permanent regression suites and `test_unit_doctrine.gd` passed; `test_mortar_hunts_known_mortar.gd`'s own tier mix moved back toward its earlier ~50% "Destroy enemy mortars" figure (holding on bare squads leaves more room for genuine hunt-tier engagement, rather than resolving instantly through direct fire) — a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range.

### 2026-09-12 — Follow-up: the same hold still let a mortar get caught blind by a SECOND, undiscovered enemy mortar

*"Just now, mortar did not wait long enough for the drone to find nearby enemy mortars. It fired on a squad, then was killed by the nearby mortar."* A repeat of the same failure mode, despite the hold above already being in place — investigated rather than dismissed as bad luck, and found to be a real, distinct gap in that hold's own gating, not just an unlucky roll.

The hold above required `enemy_mortar_fix.is_empty()` — it stopped applying outright the instant ANY lead existed on ANY enemy mortar, treating "we've found one" as "the picture is no longer blind." That's wrong given this side's own force composition: `roll_enemy_force_size` can field up to `ENEMY_MORTAR_COUNT_MAX` (5) mortars in a single battle. A stale or even just a single trusted lead on ONE of them says nothing about whether a completely different, second mortar is still sitting there entirely unaccounted for — exactly the mortar that ended up landing the fatal shell here.

**Fix**: `_pick_target`'s new hold no longer checks `enemy_mortar_fix.is_empty()` at all — it now runs unconditionally (still gated on no LIVE mortar candidate and no overrun-range threat forcing the shot regardless) purely off `_mortar_existence_confidence()`, which was already the right signal for this all along (it decays off detected mortar FIRING events generally, not off whether any ONE mortar has been specifically found). It now runs as its own independent check even when the separate pursuit-hold (for the known lead) has already run and decided not to hold — two genuinely different questions ("is this squad worth more than chasing the mortar I know about" vs. "have I ruled out a mortar I don't know about at all") that were incorrectly short-circuiting each other before.

Also raised `MORTAR_UNKNOWN_ENEMY_HOLD_FIRE_CHANCE` from 0.75 to 0.9 (peak, at confidence 1.0) — the original figure meant a full quarter of first-contact decisions gambled on immediate exposure, too loose a bar for losing a mission-critical, hard-to-replace asset. At the same ~30-tactical-second decision cadence this raises the expected wait at maximum uncertainty from ~2 to ~5 minutes, still fundamentally probabilistic rather than a clock.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test constructing a scenario the original hold's own gate would have missed: a bare squad target, a known (untrusted) lead on enemy mortar A, and a completely separate, never-detected enemy mortar B. Confirmed the fix actually flows through pursuit-hold's own doctrine gate (forced `targeting = "nearest"` so the pre-existing pursuit-hold couldn't itself trivially satisfy the test) and verified the corrected hold applies (55/60 held) where the original, unpatched gate condition fires every single time (60/60) — confirmed by temporarily restoring the old `enemy_mortar_fix.is_empty()` condition and watching the test fail exactly as the reported incident would predict. All four permanent regression suites (including `test_unit_doctrine.gd`) passed and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range.

### 2026-09-12 — A general retreat could still walk a unit toward a hill the enemy was already known to be advancing on

*"Mortar just took casualties because it retreated towards the hill, which the enemy was known to be advancing on. This was after I ordered a general retreat."* Then, once asked to think at a higher level: *"awareness of what the enemy is doing, then acting accordingly."*

Root-caused directly: `GameConfig.nearest_cover_point`/`safest_cover_point` already hard-exclude any candidate within `DANGER_RADIUS` of a known enemy position (`_exclude_dangerous`) — but only of where that enemy IS right now, a single snapshot. `order_general_retreat` builds that snapshot once and hands it to every unit's own `Unit.order_retreat`. An enemy squad that was still just outside `DANGER_RADIUS` of a given hill at the exact moment the retreat order was given sails straight through that check — even though it had already been advancing steadily toward that same hill the whole time, and the (comparatively slow, cover-picking, not sprinting) retreat walk gives it plenty of time to actually arrive there before our own unit does.

**Fix**: new `BattleManager._known_enemy_positions_for_retreat(team)` extends the plain snapshot with a projected point for every currently-VISIBLE, currently-moving opposing unit — reusing `_estimate_unit_velocity`, the same honest, already-established extrapolation `_mortar_aim_point` uses to lead a shot at a target it can currently see, projected `GameConfig.RETREAT_ADVANCE_PROJECTION_TIME` (240 tactical seconds, a rough approximation of how long it actually takes to walk to a nearby cover candidate at `REPOSITION_SPEED`, not a cited figure) into the future. Deliberately never applied to a unit only known via a stale, no-longer-visible sighting (`player_known_position` without current `is_visible`) — there's no honest read on THAT unit's current heading, and using one would be reading information this side doesn't actually have. Fed straight into the existing, unchanged `nearest_cover_point`/`safest_cover_point`/`_exclude_dangerous` machinery — a candidate near where the enemy will plausibly BE gets excluded exactly like one near where they already are, with zero changes to those shared functions' own logic. Wired into `order_general_retreat` specifically (the exact reported trigger); other retreat paths (an individual squad's own casualty-threshold retreat, the enemy's own mirrored general retreat) are left on the plain snapshot for now, matching this project's incremental, reported-bug-driven fixing pattern rather than a blanket rewrite.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test: a synthetic enemy squad placed just outside `DANGER_RADIUS` of a target point but with a real `move_target` heading straight at it, confirming (a) the enriched position list genuinely adds a projected point beyond the plain snapshot, (b) the plain snapshot alone does NOT exclude that point (reproducing the reported bug's own precondition), and (c) the enriched list DOES exclude it once the advance is projected forward — using a second, unrelated candidate to sidestep `_exclude_dangerous`'s own "some cover beats none" fallback, which would otherwise trivially defeat a single-candidate test regardless of whether the real logic works. Confirmed sensitivity by temporarily short-circuiting the new projection step and watching the same exclusion fail. Confirmed the actual wiring (`order_general_retreat` calling the new helper, not just the helper existing in isolation) directly against the source. All four permanent regression suites passed and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range.

### 2026-09-12 — Two more general-retreat failures: an encircled squad's rally point dragged by a distant mortar, and the mortar itself picking over leftovers

*"Right now, everyone is abandoning the hilltop position. This is nuts. Again may need higher-level thinking. Probably the mortar is leaving, causing everyone else to leave."* Then, once the encirclement bug below was found and fixed: *"But why did the mortar go where it went? Everyone should be working together to protect each other, with the mortar being highest priority. The mortar's choice to go where it did seems of questionable rationality."*

**Bug 1 — the encirclement "consolidate with the group" mechanic averaged in the mortar.** `_reposition_for_encirclement` (added for the earlier bunching fix — see this doc's own 2026-09-11 entries) rallies a squad reading "surrounded by covered enemies" toward the center of mass of every other ACTIVE unit on its own side — deliberately including the mortar and spotter/drone team, per its own original doc comment. That's a category error: a small, easily-displaced crew-served weapon team isn't a rallying anchor for infantry consolidation the way another squad is. Once the mortar wandered somewhere far from the front (its own shoot-and-scoot business, or the leftover-picking bug below), it dragged the whole rally-point average away from an otherwise perfectly good hilltop position — and every squad reading the same "surrounded" signal in the same tick followed it there together, exactly matching "everyone abandoning the hilltop." **Fix**: `allies` now filters to `Unit.Kind.SQUAD` only; the existing "rally toward the mortar alone" fallback is kept, but only for the genuine last-squad-standing case, not as a routine ingredient in the average.

**Bug 2 — the mortar's own retreat destination was picking over squads' leftovers.** `_spawn_player_units` adds squads to `player_units` before the mortar; `order_general_retreat` iterates `player_units` in that plain order, and each unit's `nearest_cover_point` call excludes zones earlier units in the SAME retreat already claimed (`claimed`, an existing anti-bunching accumulator). With squads always processed first, the mortar — the single highest-priority asset to get clear, per the user's own stated priority — was always last in line for the best nearby cover, sometimes pushed to a conspicuously farther or more isolated pick once the good nearby options were gone. **Fix**: `retreat_order` now puts every mortar first, everyone else after; `claimed` itself is completely unchanged, only the order units draw from it.

**Verified** with two disposable smoke tests. For the encirclement fix: ally squads placed essentially on top of the encircled unit (so a squad-only rally average reads as "already home," under the function's own 10-unit no-op threshold) plus a mortar 2000 units away — confirmed the fix produces a genuine no-op (correct: nothing to consolidate toward that isn't already here) where the unpatched, unfiltered average gets dragged far enough by the distant mortar to trigger an unwanted relocation instead; confirmed by temporarily reverting the filter and watching the no-op fail exactly as predicted. For the retreat-priority fix: computed the mortar's own true, uncontested first-choice retreat destination (nobody else has claimed anything, but other allies' own current positions are still real input either way) and confirmed the mortar's ACTUAL destination from a real `order_general_retreat` call matches it exactly once seeded identically (isolating `nearest_cover_point`'s own legitimate weighted-random pick from the ordering effect being tested, and separately keeping `crew_casualties` nonzero so `_mortar_gun_abandoned_on_unhit_retreat`'s own randf() roll doesn't desync the two calls' RNG state) — confirmed sensitivity by reverting to plain iteration order and watching the mortar's actual destination diverge from its own ideal. All four permanent regression suites passed and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range.

### 2026-09-12 — A hold-position mortar in a mortar-dense kill box now scoots after every shot, regardless of doctrine

*"Mortar should have some awareness of how dangerous things are. Just now, it was firing away at a region with four enemy mortars. It was destroyed as a consequence. With four enemy mortars, it has to scoot after every shot."*

Neither existing displacement trigger reacted to this: `shoot_and_scoot` is a single, fixed per-battle doctrine choice, and firing AT an enemy mortar specifically already forces an urgent scoot (see this doc's earlier "Force an urgent scoot" entry) — but nothing looked at how many DIFFERENT enemy mortars are actually known to be in range right now, regardless of what's actually being shot at. The real risk compounds fast and non-linearly: with `count` independent enemy mortars each able to roll `MORTAR_COUNTER_BATTERY_CHANCE` (0.22) against the very same shot (`_resolve_mortar_counter_battery` loops every opposing mortar in range), the chance at least one answers is `1-(1-0.22)^count` — 22% for one, but already 39% for two and 63% for four, the exact case reported. A "hold position" doctrine is a standing preference for when that risk is low, not a rule to keep following once it's this severe.

**Fix**: new `BattleManager._known_enemy_mortars_in_range(pos)` counts enemy mortars this side actually KNOWS about (visual sighting, a lingering last-known position, or a muzzle-flash/trajectory detection lead — the same "known" bar `_known_enemy_mortar_lead` already uses, never omniscient ground truth) within `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE` of `pos`. New `GameConfig.MORTAR_DENSITY_FORCE_SCOOT_COUNT = 2` (two independently-confirmed mortars already means a worse-than-a-third per-shot CB chance — a direct read of the probability formula above, not an arbitrary count). In `_tick_fire`, meeting or exceeding that count now forces an urgent scoot after every shot regardless of `shoot_and_scoot`, exactly generalizing the existing "shot at a mortar forces urgent" trigger into "the ambient counter-battery risk is already severe forces urgent," since both are really the same underlying question. Player-only, matching this project's standing "enemy may differ" convention.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test: a hold-position mortar (`shoot_and_scoot = false`) with two KNOWN-but-not-currently-visible enemy mortars in range, firing at the only currently-targetable unit (an ordinary squad, not a mortar) — confirmed it's now forced to scoot urgently anyway, isolating this from the pre-existing "shot at a mortar" trigger by keeping both enemy mortars out of `_pick_target`'s live candidate pool entirely (`is_visible = false`, known only via `player_has_been_sighted`/`player_known_position`) and retrying past `_pick_target`'s own separate, unrelated probabilistic "unknown enemy mortar" hold until a shot actually lands. A second case with only ONE known enemy mortar in range confirmed hold-position doctrine is still respected (no forced scoot) when the density risk is genuinely low. Confirmed sensitivity by temporarily hardcoding the density check to `false` and watching the first case's forced scoot disappear. All four permanent regression suites passed and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range.

### 2026-09-12 — Diagnosed: the mortar's own routine relocation had no leash, and a screening squad chases wherever it ends up

*"Again the mortar is leaving the hill, going North, and everyone else is following. The sequence is irrational. Not sure what step should change."* Investigated with a disposable diagnostic rather than another guess: ran real battles logging every friendly mortar move alongside any squad move within the following 10 tactical minutes, and tallied the REASONS those squad moves actually cited. Of 20 "followed" squad moves across 12 battles, 9 (the plurality) were "Screen an open approach to the friendly mortar" — `_update_friendly_squad_positioning`'s own mortar-protection mechanic, which sends the nearest available squad to a block point computed relative to wherever the mortar CURRENTLY is. Only 1 was the encirclement-consolidate mechanic (already fixed two entries ago to exclude the mortar from its own average) — the dominant real "follow" cause was something else entirely.

**Root cause**: `_mortar_relocation_plan` (the routine safety-scoot/evade destination picker) searches purely outward from the mortar's OWN current position for the nearest hidden/cover point, with no notion of "home" or the main defensive line at all — unlike the mortar's HUNTING movement, which already caps how far a destination may end up from `_friendly_mortar_home_position` via `MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_RANGE_FROM_HOME` (real reasoning already established there: "a real crew won't range indefinitely far from where it was actually deployed"). A string of individually-reasonable safety scoots — each one just the nearest hidden point from whatever's currently known — can drift the crew steadily farther from the actual defensible position over a battle, with nothing pulling it back. Once far enough, the screening mechanic above sends a squad chasing after it too, pulling that squad off the hilltop for no better reason than following an already-adrift mortar. "Everyone abandoning the hill" is a direct downstream consequence of the mortar's own destination having never had a leash.

**Fix**: `_mortar_relocation_plan` now rejects (returns empty — a hold in place, not a bad move) any candidate destination beyond the SAME `MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_RANGE_FROM_HOME` already used for hunting, measured from the SAME `_friendly_mortar_home_position`. Self-preservation is not a reason to abandon supporting distance of the position it exists to help defend, any more than chasing an enemy mortar is — the same real-world reasoning, reused rather than duplicated as a second magic number. Player-only (there's no tracked home position for the enemy's own mortar to check against). A useful side effect: since the mortar can now never be more than that cap from home, the screening squad's own worst-case chase distance is bounded too, without needing a separate cap on that mechanism.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test: a mortar with `home == its own current position` gets a normal, unrejected relocation from a nearby real threat; the same mortar with home placed 1000 units away (chosen so even the search's largest possible candidate — the reverse-slope hill path's own 300-unit travel cap — still leaves it past the 500-unit leash regardless of direction) gets rejected outright, holding in place instead. Confirmed sensitivity by temporarily disabling the leash check and watching the far-from-home case return a real (wrong) destination instead of the expected rejection. All four permanent regression suites passed and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range.

### 2026-09-12 — Counter-battery strikes were invisible, live and in replay; the replay itself was missing labels, sub-icons, cover rings, and strength bars

*"When I replay the battle, I don't see the enemy mortar shot that took out our mortar. I don't think I saw it in the original run, either."* Then: *"Also, the replay should label and number units, just as the initial play does. It should look exactly the same other than controls."*

**Bug 1 — counter-battery had no tracer at all.** Every other shot in the game (`_resolve_fire_and_check_bunching`, `_launch_mortar_shot`) appends an entry to BOTH `_fire_flashes` (the live on-screen tracer) and `_history_fire_events` (what the post-battle replay draws) the instant it fires. `_resolve_mortar_counter_battery` never did — it registered the hit in `unit_combat_stats`, logged text to the combat log, and scheduled the delayed impact, but never actually drew anything, live or in replay. A player watching a real CB kill happen had no visual confirmation of it at all, and the replay had no record to show even if they went looking afterward. **Fix**: added the same two `_fire_flashes`/`_history_fire_events` entries, in the same shape, at the same "muzzle flash is visible at firing, not impact" moment every other mortar shot already uses — `from` the responding mortar, `to` the original firing mortar's position (the same `impact_position` already computed for the delayed hit resolution).

**Bug 2 — the replay was a same-color-blob stand-in, not a real echo of the live view.** `BattleHistoryViewer._draw_unit` drew only a plain circle/rect per unit — no label, no sub-icon detail (the mortar's dot, the spotter/drone-team "eye," the drone's rotor cross), no cover ring, no strength bar — all things `Unit._draw()` already shows live. The gap existed because the recorded history snapshot (`_record_history_snapshot`) only ever stored team/kind/position/state, nothing else. **Fix**: the snapshot now also records `unit_label`/`pips`/`max_pips` per unit per snapshot (terrain for the cover ring is deliberately NOT stored — it never changes, so the viewer just looks it up fresh from the recorded position instead); `_draw_unit` now mirrors `Unit._draw()`'s full drawing sequence end to end, offset by each unit's own recorded position instead of relying on a per-node local origin (this is one shared node drawing every unit in a single pass). `GameConfig.draw_cover_ring` gained an optional `center` parameter (defaulting to `Vector2.ZERO`, so `Unit._draw()`'s own existing call is untouched) so the replay can draw the ring at the right spot too. The replay's own deliberate, already-confirmed choice to show unconditional ground truth (no `is_visible`-based hiding, unlike the live view) is explicitly preserved — this is a rendering-detail fix, not a reversal of that earlier decision.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test: rolled `_resolve_mortar_counter_battery` up to 300 times until a strike triggered, confirming it now produces both a live `_fire_flashes` entry and a `_history_fire_events` entry (team/is_mortar checked); ran a real battle to completion and confirmed the recorded history's own unit dictionaries now carry `unit_label`/`pips`/`max_pips`; instantiated a real `BattleHistoryViewer`, fed it that real recorded history and fire-event list, forced a redraw, and confirmed it completes without error (Godot's own draw calls throw loudly on a bad reference or wrong-typed argument, so a clean run is real evidence the new drawing code is at least structurally correct against real data — an actual pixel-level visual check isn't available in this headless environment, so that final on-screen appearance hasn't been independently eyeballed). Confirmed sensitivity on both fixes by temporarily reverting each in turn and watching the matching assertion fail. All four permanent regression suites passed and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range (a pure visual/reporting change with no combat-logic effect).

### 2026-09-12 — Follow-up: the mortar was STILL fleeing far off-axis during a retreat — a completely different, still-unleashed code path, and a better fix than a leash

*"Again the Mortar is fleeing to the North, and the squads are following. Again this does not make sense."* Diagnosed directly rather than assumed fixed: instrumented every large (>= 400 units from home) mortar move across several real battles and logged `_mortar_move_intent` — every single one read `"unknown"`, with `Unit.last_order_reason` reading `"Withdrawal ordered."` That's `Unit.order_retreat`, called by `order_general_retreat` (or a squad's own casualty-threshold auto-retreat) — a code path completely separate from `_mortar_relocation_plan`, which the PREVIOUS entry's home-range leash only ever touched. `order_retreat`'s own `nearest_cover_point` call constrains retreat_dir (never walk back toward the enemy — X-axis only) but leaves the Y-axis (north/south) entirely unconstrained, so a retreating mortar could land anywhere along a huge vertical band as long as it was nominally "westward enough" and safe.

Asked directly whether a hard leash was the right fix here too: *"A more sensible play would have been for the mortar to retreat towards the expected resupply run."* Correct, and better than a leash — retreating IS supposed to go somewhere (unlike routine self-preservation scoot/evade, capping how far a genuine withdrawal may travel doesn't make doctrinal sense), it just wasn't going somewhere PURPOSEFUL. `BattleManager._resupply_entry_point_for` already defines exactly this: the real point a resupply run enters the map from, on the player's own map edge, at the mortar's own current Y — i.e., the actual friendly-rear direction, not a guess.
