**Fix**: new `GameConfig.retreat_cover_point_toward(from, reference_point, known_enemy_positions, retreat_dir, avoid_buildings)` — same structure as the existing `safest_cover_point` (hard `DANGER_RADIUS` exclusion, narrow to the nearest few real candidates so this doesn't trek across the map for a marginal directional gain) but re-ranks that pool by proximity to `reference_point` instead of "farthest from known enemies." `Unit.order_retreat` gained a MORTAR-specific branch computing the same resupply-entry formula `_resupply_entry_point_for` uses (independently, since `Unit.gd` doesn't have BattleManager instance state — but the formula only ever needed `team`/`global_position`, both already on the unit) and calling the new function instead of the direction-blind `nearest_cover_point`. Squads and the spotter/drone team are unaffected.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test: picked two real, non-BUILDING cover zones on the current map at least 300 units apart in Y, placed a test position closer (in raw distance) to zone B, and confirmed `retreat_cover_point_toward` still picks zone A when `reference_point` is set to A's own center — proving the reference bias actually overrides raw nearest-distance, not just agreeing with it by chance. The first version of this test's own end-to-end check ("does `order_retreat` actually call this") was itself unreliable — plain `nearest_cover_point` kept coincidentally landing on the same zone regardless of the fix, traced to `avoid_buildings`/`path_crosses_building` excluding the other candidate outright on this particular map, an artifact of real geometry rather than a signal either way. Replaced with the more robust technique already established this session for exactly this class of problem: seed the RNG identically, call `retreat_cover_point_toward` directly for the "expected" result, seed again identically, call the real `order_retreat`, and assert bit-exact equality (also fixing `_mortar_gun_abandoned_on_unhit_retreat`'s own extra `randf()` roll for an unhit crew from desyncing the two calls, the same fix an earlier mortar-retreat-priority test this session already needed) — confirmed sensitivity by temporarily disabling the new branch and watching the exact-equality check fail. All four permanent regression suites passed and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range.

### 2026-09-12 — A squad screening the mortar kept walking to a stale destination even after the mortar had already moved on

*"OK, a new issue: units are not reconsidering actions when they become senseless. It looked like the mortar went North, then the squads went North to cover it, and continued going North even when the Mortar was returning South."*

A real, general architectural gap, not specific to any one trigger: `_update_friendly_squad_positioning`'s "screen an open approach to the friendly mortar" responder-selection loop only ever considers squads with `has_move_target == false` — once a squad is dispatched to a `block_point` computed from the mortar's position AT THAT MOMENT, it's completely excluded from reconsideration until it physically arrives, however long that takes and however much the underlying situation changes in the meantime. With the mortar itself now moving more purposefully (the last several entries' home-leash and resupply-bias fixes), a screening squad could easily still be mid-walk toward where the mortar USED to be by the time the mortar has already reversed course — exactly the reported sequence.

**Fix**: every tick, before picking a possible NEW responder, any squad currently mid-walk with `last_order_reason == "Screen an open approach to the friendly mortar."` gets its own `move_target` freshly recomputed from the mortar's CURRENT position and the CURRENT nearest unscreened threat — the same "re-aim every tick rather than commit to a stale destination" idiom already established for the enemy mortar's own hunting movement (never invented specifically for squads before now). If nothing is unscreened anymore at all, the squad is released outright (`has_move_target = false`) rather than left walking toward a block point serving no purpose — from there it falls straight back into the same function's own ordinary cover-seeking tier on its next idle tick, same as any other squad reconsidering its options.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test: a squad given a deliberately stale `move_target` and tagged as screening, with a real visible enemy threat and the mortar sitting somewhere else — confirmed a single call to `_update_friendly_squad_positioning` re-aims the squad's `move_target` to the fresh block point computed from the mortar's actual current position, matching the exact formula by construction; then, with the threat no longer visible, confirmed the same squad gets released (`has_move_target` cleared) instead of continuing toward the now-pointless point. Confirmed sensitivity by temporarily disabling the new re-aim/release block and watching both assertions fail exactly as the reported symptom would predict. All four permanent regression suites passed and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range (the first attempt at this run crashed near the end on an apparent Godot engine-internal cleanup error unrelated to this change — 200 consecutive full battles in one process is a lot of cumulative churn — and was simply re-run rather than treated as a real regression, cleanly completing the second time).

### 2026-09-12 — A magnifier loupe for precise deployment placement

*"When doing setup, the circle for the unit obscures the terrain behind, and it is hard to tell what I am doing. I want a blow-up window, probably in the left part of the screen. It should show the terrain and the unit, but the unit's circle can be smaller, and maybe not fully opaque, while the terrain behind is shown zoomed in."* `UnitToken`'s own on-map circle (radius 16, alpha 0.9) is deliberately large and solid so it reads clearly at normal zoom — but that same size/opacity is exactly what makes it hard to see the ground it's actually sitting on while placing it.

**Design choice**: a second Camera2D sharing the main map's `World2D` (the obvious first idea for a "zoomed-in view of the same live scene") was rejected — it would zoom the SAME `UnitToken` node the main view already draws, scaling its circle UP right along with the terrain, the opposite of "smaller and more transparent." Instead, `DeploymentMagnifier` (new, `scripts/deployment_magnifier.gd`) redraws the real terrain itself from scratch (`GameConfig.draw_terrain`, the same function every other terrain view already calls) through its own `draw_set_transform` centered on the token's live position at 4x magnification, then resets to identity and draws a small (half-radius), semi-transparent, hand-drawn echo of the token's own icon language on top, plus a thin crosshair pinpointing the exact placement point and the same cover-ring feedback the live game already shows. A `Control` with `clip_contents = true` (not a bare `Node2D`) keeps the 4x-scaled terrain from spilling past the window's own edges — the actual reason this is a `Control` at all.

**Wiring**: `main.gd` creates/frees `deployment_magnifier` alongside the other deployment-phase UI (screen-space, top-left corner of the map — the "left part of the screen" asked for, chosen so it doesn't collide with the sidebar on the right), and every `_process()` tick polls `deployment_screen._dragging` (the existing single source of truth for "what's currently being dragged," already read directly the same way several other UI elements already poll child-screen state) to show/hide it and keep `focus_position`/`unit_kind` in sync live. Visible only while an actual drag is in progress — hidden otherwise, since there's nothing useful to preview without one.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test driving the real `main.gd`/`DeploymentScreen` flow: confirmed the magnifier starts hidden, becomes visible and correctly tracks both position and kind the instant a drag begins, and hides again once it ends. Confirmed sensitivity by temporarily breaking the polling line and watching all three drag-state assertions fail. No script errors occurred while the magnifier was actually visible and being redrawn each frame (Godot's own draw calls throw loudly on a bad reference), real evidence the drawing code is structurally sound against live terrain data — same limitation as the earlier replay-viewer work: this project has no browser-based preview for a native Godot UI change, and headless mode's dummy rendering backend can't produce a real screenshot either (confirmed directly: `get_viewport().get_texture()` returns null under `--headless`), so the actual on-screen pixel appearance hasn't been independently eyeballed. This change touches only deployment-phase UI code that `characterize_doctrine.gd` and the other permanent regression suites never exercise at all (they construct battles directly, bypassing deployment entirely) — a 200-trial regression would have been uninformative here, so `test_decision_ui.gd` (the one permanent suite that does drive the real deployment flow) was run instead and passed cleanly.

### 2026-09-12 — Counter-battery's own muzzle flash was shown at the wrong moment: when the ORIGINAL shot fired, not when the RESPONSE actually did

Follow-up to the earlier "counter-battery strikes were invisible" fix, once the player noticed the timing itself still didn't add up: *"The history from the last battle shows one enemy CB shot... at roughly 6:04. Our mortar was knocked out 3-4 minutes later. How?"* Then, after being told `COUNTER_BATTERY_DELAY_MIN/MAX` (1-3 tactical minutes) is the FULL detect-to-impact cycle, not a separate delay stacked on top of the shell's own flight time: *"I thought shots from this mortar take 30-45 seconds. Is it typically not used as counter battery?"* Then, once it was confirmed this is the exact same 82mm mortar doing both jobs (an ordinary shot's `MORTAR_FLIGHT_TIME` is just the ballistic leg of an already-fired round; the CB window is meant to ALSO cover the time to detect, decide, and actually get the responding tube laid and fired before that same flight begins): *"But the yellow flash should happen when the shot is fired."*

Correct, and a real bug the earlier fix introduced: the new flash/history entry was added inside `_resolve_mortar_counter_battery`, which runs the INSTANT the ORIGINAL shot fires — the same tick, not after any of the "detect, decide, lay, fire" time the function's own doc comment already described. The visual said "the enemy just fired back" a full reaction cycle before that crew had actually done any such thing.

**Fix**: `COUNTER_BATTERY_DELAY_MIN/MAX`'s existing 60-180 tactical-second range is now split, not replaced — `react_delay = total_delay - MORTAR_FLIGHT_TIME` (the detect/decide/lay/fire portion, no visible flash) plus the existing `MORTAR_FLIGHT_TIME` constant itself (the responding round's own flight, the same physics every other mortar shot in this file already uses) for the remainder, carved OUT of the established total rather than added on top of it — the overall time-to-possible-impact envelope this constant was already tuned around, and already discussed with the player as roughly consistent with real reporting on this war's own radar-cued response times, is unchanged. `_pending_counter_battery` entries now carry a `fire_time` (reaction elapsed) and a `fired` flag alongside the existing `impact_time`; `_resolve_pending_counter_battery` only draws the flash/history entry and logs "fire inbound" once `fire_time` actually arrives — from the responding mortar's own LIVE position at that moment (it may have moved since being triggered), not a position captured back when the mission was merely decided. A genuinely new edge case this surfaced, handled explicitly: if the responding crew is destroyed before its own `fire_time` arrives, the mission is simply dropped — no flash, no impact, nothing fires from a dead crew.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test: triggered a real strike and confirmed no flash/history entry exists immediately afterward; confirmed the reaction portion and flight portion are both real, non-trivial delays (flight portion matching `MORTAR_FLIGHT_TIME` exactly, reaction portion comfortably nonzero given `COUNTER_BATTERY_DELAY_MIN` (60s) always exceeds `MORTAR_FLIGHT_TIME` (40s)); confirmed the total trigger-to-impact time still falls within the original `[60, 180]` envelope; stepped scenario time to just before and just after `fire_time` and confirmed the flash appears exactly at the transition, from the responder's own position; confirmed impact still resolves correctly once `impact_time` elapses; and confirmed a responder destroyed before its own `fire_time` drops the mission cleanly with no flash or impact. Confirmed sensitivity by temporarily zeroing the reaction delay and watching the flight-portion/reaction-portion assertions fail exactly as predicted. Caught and fixed a real, unrelated fallout while running the permanent regression suite: `test_unit_doctrine.gd`'s own `test_damage_credit` manually constructs a `_pending_counter_battery` entry to test delayed-credit-to-a-since-destroyed-shooter behavior, and needed `"fired": true` added to match the dict's new shape (it's testing impact-stage behavior specifically, not the new reaction stage). All four permanent regression suites passed and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range.

### 2026-09-12 — A THIRD recurrence of "mortar goes far away, everyone follows, hilltop abandoned" — two more distinct root causes, one geometry-driven and one lifecycle-driven

*"Again the mortar is retreating North and the squads are following it, thereby abandoning the hilltop. This is dumb."* The same reported symptom as two earlier entries this session (encirclement rally-point averaging; squads-before-mortar retreat order; `_mortar_relocation_plan`'s home-range leash; `order_retreat`'s resupply-corridor bias; the screening mechanism's per-tick re-aim) — all of those fixes were real and are all still in effect, so this had to be a genuinely different mechanism. Told directly not to report this fixed without running multiple trials confirming the bad behavior actually stops, not just reasoning about why it should.

**First diagnostic finding (real, but turned out not to be the dominant cause):** an isolated, seeded test of `Unit.order_retreat`'s MORTAR branch from its own default deployment position on the current map found EVERY nearby cover zone excluded by one of two hard, all-or-nothing filters shared by `nearest_cover_point`/`safest_cover_point`/`retreat_cover_point_toward`: `retreat_dir` (a blanket "retreat toward home side" assumption, fixed per team, with no actual threat data behind it — the mortar's own deployment sits with essentially no cover at all on its nominal retreat-ward side) and `avoid_buildings`'s `path_crosses_building` check (the mortar is deployed inside/near a hamlet, and the straight line to nearly every nearby patch of trees happened to graze some building along the way). With both hard-excluding every close option, a single ordinary hit-triggered retreat was landing 1700-3100m away instead of the ~600m the nearest genuinely close cover would have cost.

**Fix 1**: both filters became soft preferences with a shared escape hatch — `GameConfig._prefer_retreat_direction` and the new `GameConfig._prefer_clear_path`, both called right after the existing `_exclude_dangerous` (which stays a hard veto; it's grounded in real known threats, unlike these two blanket assumptions). Each keeps only candidates within `RETREAT_DIRECTION_MAX_EXTRA_M` (500m, a judgment call) of the single nearest reachable candidate overall, falling back to the unfiltered set otherwise — checked candidate-by-candidate, not just "does at least one acceptable option exist," since an early version of this that only checked the LATTER still let a technically-compliant but still-2400m outlier sit in the same pool as a genuinely close 638m option, and the existing weighted-random top-pick had a real chance of choosing the outlier anyway.

**This fix is real and was kept**, but a 30-battle Monte Carlo tracking the mortar's own max distance from `_friendly_mortar_home_position` across full battles found almost no difference before/after (11/30 vs 10/30 trials still exceeded `MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_RANGE_FROM_HOME`, 2500m) — the single-order-jump defect this fixed is real but isn't what was actually driving the reported symptom across full battles; most of a mortar's total displacement over a battle is legitimate movement (hunting, linkup, or an actual, correct withdrawal walking off the map, which is SUPPOSED to end up far from home).

**Second diagnostic finding (the actual dominant cause):** tracing one specific high-drift battle move-by-move showed the mortar transitioning from ACTIVE straight to RETREATING (a real hit-triggered withdrawal, not a bug) and then continuing to walk well past its own most recent cover-point destination — exactly what a genuine withdrawal is supposed to do. The reported "squads follow it" symptom traced to `_update_friendly_squad_positioning`: it fetches `_friendly_active_mortar()` (correctly `null` once the mortar stops being ACTIVE) and used to `return` immediately when null — skipping the very loop, right below, that exists to release a squad already mid-walk to screen the mortar once nothing is left to screen. A squad already dispatched to a screening block-point at the moment the mortar left ACTIVE state was left frozen there permanently: never re-aimed, never released, and — critically — never told to reposition back to the position it was actually supposed to be holding. A mortar that's already pulling out of the fight needs no more screening than one with no threat left to screen against; both are "nothing left to do here."

**Fix 2**: moved the `mortar == null` check so the release loop always runs (a squad tagged `"Screen an open approach to the friendly mortar."` is released whenever there's no active mortar to screen OR nothing left unscreened), and only the NEW-responder-assignment logic below still bails out early when there's no mortar to protect at all.

**Verified**, for each fix independently, with the standard temporarily-disable/confirm-failure/restore sensitivity check: a synthetic squad tagged as screening, first confirmed still correctly maintained while a real unscreened threat and an ACTIVE mortar are present (baseline), then confirmed released the tick after the mortar flips to RETREATING — reverting just this change reproduced the frozen-forever squad exactly as reported. Then, per the explicit instruction not to call this fixed without multiple trials: ran the SAME 30 seeds' full battles before and after fix 2, counting ticks where a squad sits with a stale screening order while the mortar isn't ACTIVE — 29 total stale ticks across those 30 battles before the fix, 2 after (both a single transitional tick, not a persisting freeze). Overall squad-drift-from-deployment didn't drop dramatically in aggregate (554m vs 622m average max) — most squad movement in a battle is legitimate (encirclement response, ordinary cover-seeking) and this fix only removes the ONE specific broken persistence mechanism — but that mechanism's actual duration, the direct cause of "everyone follows the mortar and abandons the hilltop," collapsed by the measured amount. All four permanent regression suites passed and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range (SPOTTER 63% held / 3.96x exchange ratio, DRONE_TEAM 62% held / 3.21x — both consistent with prior runs this session).

### 2026-09-12 — The deployment magnifier hid itself the instant a drag ended

*"During setup, the magnified view can remain on screen until the action starts. Just show whatever was set up most recently."* The magnifier's own `_process` poll (see the earlier "magnifier loupe" entry) tied its visibility directly to `DeploymentScreen._dragging` being non-null — useful WHILE actively placing a token, but it vanished the instant the player released the mouse, even though checking the placement that was JUST made is exactly when a zoomed-in view is most useful.

**Fix**: `main.gd` gained `_magnifier_last_token`, set to whichever token is currently being dragged whenever one is, and otherwise carried forward from the previous drag. The magnifier's visibility and drawn contents now key off `dragging if dragging != null else _magnifier_last_token` instead of `dragging` alone — visible from the first drag onward, always showing the most recently touched token, through drops and fresh drags alike. Reset to `null` alongside `deployment_magnifier` itself in `_clear_all()` so a new deployment phase doesn't start by showing a stale token from the last one — the whole node is freed at that same point regardless (deployment ending IS "the action starts"), so there's no separate "hide once battle begins" case to handle.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test driving the real `main.gd`/`DeploymentScreen` flow: confirmed the magnifier starts hidden before any token is ever touched; becomes visible and tracks a token mid-drag; on release, STAYS visible showing that same token's own last position (the actual behavior under test); on dragging a different token, switches to tracking it; on that drag's own release, again stays visible on the new, most-recent token; and that leaving deployment entirely clears both `deployment_magnifier` and `_magnifier_last_token`. Confirmed sensitivity by temporarily reverting to the old `dragging != null`-only visibility check and watching both "stays visible after release" assertions fail exactly as the reported symptom would predict. This change touches only deployment-phase UI code — `characterize_doctrine.gd` and the other permanent regression suites construct battles directly, bypassing deployment entirely — so `test_decision_ui.gd` (the one permanent suite that exercises the real deployment flow) was run instead of the full 200-trial regression, matching the precedent set by the original magnifier entry; all four permanent suites passed regardless.

### 2026-09-12 — A FOURTH recurrence of "mortar retreats North" — the real root cause, missed by all three earlier fixes because none of them touched this function

*"The retreat to the North is back. Again. This is getting frustrating. I keep reporting this same issue."* Every earlier fix this session (encirclement rally-point averaging; squads-before-mortar retreat order; `_mortar_relocation_plan`'s home-range leash; `order_retreat`'s resupply-corridor bias; the screening mechanism's re-aim/release; the retreat_dir/path-crossing budget softening; the screening-release-on-mortar-inactive fix) was real, verified, and still in effect — none of them were wrong, they just weren't THIS. Pointed at the live, currently-running game's own debug snapshot (`debug_state/mortar_decision_snapshot.json`) rather than guessing again: the player mortar was sitting at `move_intent: "out_of_ammo"`, `"Continuing a displacement already under way"`, far from where any of the recent enemy contacts in `drone_pilot_snapshot.json` actually were — a real, live instance of the same complaint, not a synthetic worry.

**Root cause, found by tracing the mortar's OWN concealment function directly** (`GameConfig.nearest_hidden_point`, used by every tier-1 self-preservation trigger — conceal, evade, out-of-ammo relocation — via `_mortar_relocation_plan`; NONE of it goes through `order_retreat`'s cover-picking that the earlier retreat_dir/path-crossing fix touched, which is exactly why that fix barely moved the needle on full-battle drift): it tries `_reverse_slope_candidate` (the reverse slope of a nearby hill — real, durable LOS-blocking cover) FIRST, and returns it UNCONDITIONALLY the instant it finds anything at all, never comparing against what the ring search (`nearest_hidden_point`'s other half, sampling expanding rings around the unit's own current position) would have found. Directly confirmed for the mortar's own deployment position: the hill candidate sat 1129m away; a fully-hidden, ordinary tree-line spot the ring search found on its very first (250m) ring sat FOUR TIMES closer and was equally safe from every known threat — yet the hill won every time regardless, because nothing ever asked whether it was worth the extra 900m. With a specific hill sitting consistently north of the mortar's own position on this map, this made "the mortar concealment-relocates to the same spot to the north" the deterministic, repeatable OUTCOME of an ordinary "spotted, nothing to shoot" event — not a one-off, a structural bias baked into the function every other fix this session had left untouched.

**Fix**: `nearest_hidden_point` now computes BOTH the hill candidate and the ring-search candidate (the latter split out into its own `_ring_search_hidden_point` helper so it can run independently of whether the hill search found something) and picks the ring's result instead whenever the hill costs more than `CONCEALMENT_HILL_MAX_EXTRA_M` (300m, a judgment call) more distance than the ring's own nearest hit — the same "prefer the more durable option, but don't pay an unreasonable premium for it" budget pattern as the retreat_dir/path-crossing fix two entries back, applied to a genuinely different function this time. The hill's real advantage (durable masking that survives a threat shifting position, per that function's own doc comment) is still honored for a modest premium; it just no longer wins by default regardless of cost.

**Verified**: confirmed directly that the fix flips the mortar's own deployment-position pick from the 1129m hill to the 250m tree line once the ring search is actually given a chance to compete. Then, since a full-battle max-distance-from-home metric conflates "still fighting but pushed far away" (the actual complaint) with "already RETREATING/WITHDRAWN and correctly walking off the map" (not a bug — that's what leaving the fight means), restricted the same 30-battle Monte Carlo established for the earlier fixes to distance-from-home measured ONLY while the mortar is still ACTIVE: avg max drift dropped from 1714m to 691m, median from 1120m to 390m, and trials exceeding `MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_RANGE_FROM_HOME` (2500m) while still ACTIVE dropped from 10/30 to 1/30 (that one remaining case only marginally over, at 2592m). Confirmed sensitivity by temporarily reverting to the old unconditional-hill-preference behavior and watching the same direct check pick the far hill again. All four permanent regression suites passed and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed within the established normal range (SPOTTER 60% held / 3.56x, DRONE_TEAM 71% held / 3.40x — DRONE_TEAM's own held-rate landing a little higher than prior runs is plausible and welcome, not concerning: a mortar that stays in useful supporting distance instead of uselessly wandering off should help the defense, not hurt it).

Not claimed as the last possible cause of every future "mortar went somewhere odd" report — but it is the first fix this session to specifically target the mortar's OWN most-frequently-triggered self-preservation function, which none of the previous (also real) fixes ever touched.

### 2026-09-12 — Counter-battery timing rebuilt as real component times instead of one stored random range, plus a genuine balance consequence knowingly accepted

*"The time to take a counter battery shot should not be a directly stored value. Rather, it is the sum of whatever component times need to happen. They need to figure out where the shot came from. If moving, they need to set up. They fire. Then more time elapses before there is a hit. I don't know if the first two can happen simultaneously or not. Figure that out by referencing the real world. Also include any steps I may have missed. We want a state-based mortar, with time depending on the state the mortar is in."*

**Research, not assumption, on the "simultaneous?" question**: real fire-direction-center procedure has the FDO announce "FIRE MISSION" to the gun crews the INSTANT a call for fire comes in — the crew starts laying/prepping at the same moment the FDC computes the actual firing data, not after. Different people/systems doing different jobs; a crew physically emplacing a tube doesn't block whoever is figuring out where to aim it. So LOCATE (turning a detected shot into an actual firing solution) and SETUP (if the responder wasn't already emplaced) run in PARALLEL — combined with `max()`, not `+`.

**The composed model** (`BattleManager._resolve_mortar_counter_battery`):
```
react_delay = max(locate_time, setup_remaining) + lay_time
```
- **LOCATE** (`GameConfig.COUNTER_BATTERY_LOCATE_TIME_MIN/MAX`, 30-110s): the original shot's muzzle blast/trajectory gives its position away instantly (not visual spotting — see `MORTAR_FIRE_DETECTION_EXPIRY`'s own doc comment), but turning that into an actual usable grid coordinate takes real time. Modern radar-cued counter-battery does this in "a matter of seconds"; this project's early-war setting has no counter-battery radar at this echelon, so it's slower — but still small-unit radio cross-cueing, not a formal WWI-style sound-ranging network (historically 3-5 minutes to reach the intelligence officer, an entirely different, much larger process). A judgment call within that real range.
- **SETUP** (reuses the existing, already 2B24-mortar-cited `MORTAR_SETUP_TEARDOWN_TIME`, 30s): `max(MORTAR_SETUP_TEARDOWN_TIME - m.seconds_stationary, 0.0)` — only costs whatever time the responder genuinely has left to finish emplacing; an already-set-up mortar (the common case) pays nothing here.
- **LAY (+ fire)** (`COUNTER_BATTERY_LAY_TIME_MIN/MAX`, 5-15s): once both a location and a ready tube exist, actually laying onto that azimuth/elevation and firing — grounded on real reporting that an already-ready crew gets its first round downrange within about 10 seconds of a fire mission.
- **FLIGHT**: `MORTAR_FLIGHT_TIME`, unchanged, added after `react_delay` exactly as before.

**A missed step the user asked to be caught, and was**: the old model had a hard, previously-unquestioned gate — a responder still mid-relocation (`seconds_stationary < MORTAR_SETUP_TEARDOWN_TIME`) couldn't attempt to answer AT ALL, no matter how nearly finished it was. That's not what "if moving, they need to set up" means in the real world — a moving crew still eventually gets the tube down and fires back, it just takes the remaining time. The gate is gone; SETUP now folds the remaining time into the `max()` instead of disqualifying the responder outright.

**The other side of the request — already correctly modeled, verified rather than rebuilt**: *"having fired at an enemy mortar, there is time to become mobile and time to move."* `_queue_mortar_displacement`/`_resolve_pending_mortar_displacement` already do exactly this: `MORTAR_SETUP_TEARDOWN_TIME` (the same real, cited 30s) as the become-mobile delay before the crew starts walking at all, then genuine movement at `MORTAR_RELOCATE_SPEED`/`_URGENT` over the real chosen distance — not a flat stored number, real distance divided by real speed. No change needed; confirmed by reading the call site directly (`_tick_fire`'s `target.kind == Unit.Kind.MORTAR` branch, which forces exactly this scoot after shooting at an enemy mortar).

**A real balance consequence, measured and knowingly accepted, not silently shipped**: closing the moving-responder gate means a mortar that moves/hunts often (this session's DRONE_TEAM mode's enemy mortar apparently does, far more than SPOTTER mode's) can now actually hit back far more often than before — previously most of its own counter-battery opportunities against the player were silently discarded whenever it happened to be relocating at the moment. A direct 40-trial controlled comparison (same seeds, only this code changed) measured player mortar destruction in DRONE_TEAM mode going from 27.5% to 60% — confirmed via instrumentation to be entirely about ATTEMPT FREQUENCY, not response speed (zero of the destructions in either version happen while the player's own hold-position mortar is mid-relocation — a mortar that never moves is equally catchable regardless of how long the response takes). An initial attempt to retune `COUNTER_BATTERY_LOCATE_TIME_MIN/MAX` to preserve the old average reaction time for an already-emplaced responder did NOT meaningfully change this (confirmed empirically, not just reasoned about) — correctly, since delay length was never the actual lever here. Presented directly to the user with the measured numbers rather than either silently shipping a large unannounced balance shift or unilaterally deciding to soften it: **the user chose to keep it at full strength** — the old behavior was a real, unrealistic exploit (permanent immunity while moving, however briefly), and this is its honest, accepted consequence, not a bug to be tuned away. `COUNTER_BATTERY_LOCATE_TIME_MIN/MAX` were still retuned from an initial 20-90s to 30-110s so the decomposed model's own average reaction time for the common (already-emplaced) case matches the OLD flat range's average as closely as the real component reasoning allows — a legitimate refinement in its own right, independent of the balance question.

**Verified**: full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run — SPOTTER 68% held / 5.21x exchange ratio (healthy, consistent with this session's established range); DRONE_TEAM 64% held / 1.47x exchange ratio (held rate still healthy; the exchange-ratio drop is the accepted consequence above, reproduced consistently across two separate 200-trial runs and a 40-trial controlled comparison, not a fluke). Disposable smoke tests confirmed, with the standard temporarily-disable/confirm-failure/restore sensitivity check: (1) a fully-emplaced responder's `react_delay` exactly equals `locate_time + lay_time` (bit-exact, re-derived from the same RNG seed) with zero setup cost; (2) `impact_time - fire_time` exactly equals `MORTAR_FLIGHT_TIME`; (3) a responder at `seconds_stationary == 0` still answers at all (the core behavior change) with a `react_delay` of at least the full `MORTAR_SETUP_TEARDOWN_TIME` — reverting just the removed gate reproduces the old "skipped entirely" behavior and fails assertion (3) exactly as predicted. All four permanent regression suites passed.

### 2026-09-13 — A counter-battery response fired and then just sat there — the shoot-and-scoot trigger never reached this path at all

*"At approximately 6:04, the mortar did not visibly scoot after shooting. It was promptly destroyed."*

A real, previously-missed gap, and a plausible reason it was noticed right after the counter-battery timing rework above: a NORMAL shot's shoot-and-scoot trigger lives entirely inside `_tick_fire` (checks `unit.shoot_and_scoot or density_forces_scoot`, then `_queue_mortar_displacement`). A counter-battery RESPONSE's own shot fires through a completely different resolution path — `_resolve_pending_counter_battery`'s `fired = true` stage — which draws the muzzle flash, logs it, and stops there. It never reached `_tick_fire` at all, so a responding mortar's own shoot-and-scoot doctrine was simply never consulted for its own counter-battery shot: it always sat exactly where it fired from, doctrine setting or not. This bug predates the timing rework, but that rework made counter-battery responses fire noticeably more often (closing the moving-responder immunity gap — see the entry above), which is almost certainly why it became visible now rather than earlier.

**Fix**: `_resolve_pending_counter_battery`'s `fired = true` stage now runs the same `shoot_and_scoot or density_forces_scoot` check and calls `_queue_mortar_displacement` on the responder, same as `_tick_fire`. Always urgent — a counter-battery response is, definitionally, a shot at a known enemy mortar, the exact case `_tick_fire`'s own trigger already forces urgent for a normal shot.

**Verified** with a disposable smoke test: constructed a due counter-battery strike for a shoot-and-scoot responder, confirmed no displacement is queued before the response fires, then confirmed one IS queued (with `intent == "scoot"`) the instant `_resolve_pending_counter_battery` actually fires it. Confirmed sensitivity by temporarily removing the new block and watching the same assertion fail exactly as reported. All four permanent regression suites passed, and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed consistent with the already-accepted post-timing-rework baseline (SPOTTER 78% held / 4.21x — healthy, plausibly improved since a responding mortar now actually survives its own return shot more often; DRONE_TEAM 54% held / 1.61x — in the same range as the already-discussed, already-accepted consequence from the prior entry, not a new shift).

### 2026-09-13 — A queued scoot could be starved indefinitely by an ordinary hunt order

*"In the latest battle, mortar fired away at enemy mortars multiple times, with no visible scoot at all. Was destroyed as a result."* Told directly not to call this fixed without verification.

Different mechanism from the responder-scoot gap above — this is the ORIGINAL shooter's own routine post-shot scoot (`_tick_fire`'s existing trigger, already correctly queuing one every time it fires, confirmed by direct instrumentation before looking any further). The actual bug lived one step later, in `_resolve_pending_mortar_displacement`: its own doc comment claimed it only drops a pending scoot when "a higher-priority trigger... has since claimed this tick's move," but the code only ever checked `mortar.has_move_target` — true for ANY current move order, including tier 2's own ordinary "hunt" (explicitly LOWER priority than the self-preservation a routine scoot represents). A mortar actively re-engaging known enemy mortars — exactly what was reported — very plausibly picks up a fresh hunt order sometime in the 30-second window between firing and the scoot's own `ready_time`, silently starving the scoot completely, every single time, with no cap on how long this could keep happening.

**Fix**: the drop condition now checks the SAME sticky-intent list `_decide_mortar_action`'s own step 0b already uses for genuine tier-1 emergencies (evade/conceal/out_of_ammo/linkup) — only one of those actually outranks a routine scoot. A mere hunt (or anything else) now gets overwritten by the scoot instead of blocking it.

**Verified**: reproduced the exact reported mechanism directly — queued a scoot, assigned a hunt order in the window before its `ready_time`, confirmed the scoot was silently dropped (pre-fix) — then confirmed the same scenario now correctly issues the scoot, overwriting the hunt. A second case confirmed the fix didn't overcorrect: a genuine tier-1 evade claimed in the same window still correctly wins and the scoot still correctly stays dropped, exactly as before. Sensitivity confirmed by reverting only this change and watching the hunt-overwrite case fail while the evade case still passed. All four permanent regression suites passed. Full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`: SPOTTER 74% held / 6.06x — the healthiest SPOTTER result yet this session, consistent with the player's own mortar now surviving its hunt-heavy engagements better; DRONE_TEAM 55% held / 1.28x — continuing the same downward trend in exchange ratio as the prior two entries rather than reversing it. Worth stating plainly rather than glossing over: this makes three fixes in a row (closing counter-battery's moving-immunity gap, making CB responders scoot, and now this) that all correctly make mortar survival MORE symmetric and MORE realistic between sides, and DRONE_TEAM mode's own heavy mortar-vs-mortar dueling meta keeps translating that symmetry into a worse trade for the player specifically — not evidence any of the three fixes is wrong (each was verified independently, on its own real-world grounding, with its own sensitivity check), but a real, accumulating balance question distinct from any single fix's own correctness, and one the user may want to revisit deliberately (e.g. `MORTAR_COUNTER_BATTERY_CHANCE`) once done chasing individual mechanism bugs, the same choice already offered and deferred once already this session.

### 2026-09-13 — Idle mortars took no evasive action at all against a known threat — a standing precaution was missing entirely

*"Even one enemy mortar known in range should be enough."* Following directly from the fix above: *"In the latest battle, mortar fired away at enemy mortars multiple times, with no visible scoot at all. Was destroyed as a result."* Pointedly challenged mid-diagnosis — *"Why did you report this as 'fixed'?"* — when a further battle showed the mortar taking no evasive action at all despite multiple scouted enemy mortars, even after the hunt-starvation fix above.

Correctly pointed out: the previous fix addressed a real, verified, but narrower bug (a queued scoot getting starved by a hunt order). This was a genuinely different, deeper gap the hunt-starvation fix never touched: `_known_enemy_mortars_in_range`/`MORTAR_DENSITY_FORCE_SCOOT_COUNT` was — confirmed directly by grepping every call site — checked in exactly two places, both strictly REACTIVE to the mortar having just fired a shot (`_tick_fire`'s own post-shot trigger, and the responder-scoot fix two entries back). A mortar that ISN'T currently shooting at all — reloading, no valid target, holding fire — got zero benefit from knowing enemy mortars could already reach it, no matter how long that stayed true. This matches the reported symptom exactly and doesn't require having fired recently at all.

**First fix**: added a new standing (not shot-triggered) tier-1 self-preservation check to `_decide_mortar_action`, below the existing spotted/threat-closing/just-hit triggers (which still take priority) and above tier 2 hunting: if `_known_enemy_mortars_in_range(m.global_position) >= GameConfig.MORTAR_DENSITY_FORCE_SCOOT_COUNT` (initially reusing the existing constant), relocate as a non-urgent precaution. Verified directly (a mortar with no shot, not spotted, no threat closing, but 2 known-but-not-currently-targetable enemy mortars in range now correctly relocates instead of holding) and by sensitivity check (reverting reproduces "Holding, nothing to do" under the exact same conditions).

**Then corrected**: *"Even one enemy mortar known in range should be enough."* Right, and for a real, distinct reason rather than just lowering a number by request: `MORTAR_DENSITY_FORCE_SCOOT_COUNT`'s own existing threshold of 2 is justified by real compounding-probability math (1-(1-0.22)^count) for overriding an ACTIVE decision — a deliberate hold-position doctrine choice, right after firing, where overriding the player's own standing preference needs real justification. This NEW standing check only ever fires when the mortar is already doing nothing productive (no shot, not spotted, no threat closing, not out of ammo) — there's no cost to relocating in that idle window, so it doesn't need the same higher bar. Added a separate `GameConfig.MORTAR_STANDING_THREAT_COUNT = 1`, distinct from `MORTAR_DENSITY_FORCE_SCOOT_COUNT` (unchanged at 2, still correctly gating the post-shot override with its own real justification) — one confirmed enemy tube in range, with nothing else going on, is already a real, direct reason not to just sit there.

**Verified**: a mortar with just ONE known (not currently targetable) enemy mortar in range now correctly relocates as a precaution; sensitivity-checked by temporarily raising the constant back to 2 and confirming the same single-mortar case reverts to holding. All four permanent regression suites passed. Full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`: SPOTTER 67% held / 4.80x — healthy; DRONE_TEAM 63% held / 1.36x — held rate healthy, exchange ratio continuing in the same already-discussed range as the prior three mortar-survival entries, not a new shift.

### 2026-09-13 — The opposite failure: the standing precaution's own over-correction locked the mortar out of ever firing, plus a permanent test for both directions at once

*"Now we have the opposite problem. The mortar is always scooting and never shooting. Is it possible to test and avoid both types of failure?"*

**Root cause**: `_mortar_shot_this_tick` (step 0 of the tier ladder) requires `m.seconds_stationary >= MORTAR_SETUP_TEARDOWN_TIME` (30s) before it will even ATTEMPT to pick a target — a mortar that just arrived from ANY relocation hasn't finished re-emplacing yet, so `has_shot` reads false purely on that technicality, not because no target actually exists. The new standing-evade check from the entry above had no awareness of this at all: reaching tier 1 with `has_shot == false` (near-certain for the first 30 seconds after any arrival) plus a known enemy mortar still in range (all but guaranteed — `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE` is huge, and `player_has_been_sighted`/a detected firing position can sit "known" for a long time) relocated the mortar AGAIN before it ever finished settling, resetting `seconds_stationary` to 0 every single time — a permanent lock-out, the exact mirror image of the hunt-starves-scoot bug two entries back, this time starving fire-eligibility itself rather than a queued displacement.

**Fix**: added `m.seconds_stationary >= GameConfig.MORTAR_SETUP_TEARDOWN_TIME` as a required precondition on the standing check — the same floor `_mortar_shot_this_tick` itself already needs, so the mortar always gets a genuine chance to look for (and take) a shot before this precaution is even considered.

**Testing both directions at once, per the explicit request**: added a new permanent suite, `scripts/tests/test_mortar_evasion_balance.gd`, with one test per direction — an idle mortar with a known threat must still relocate (guards the original gap), and a mortar under a PERSISTENT known threat must still eventually settle long enough to become fire-eligible (guards this one). Getting the second test to actually catch the bug took real, honest debugging, worth recording since it's a genuine trap for testing this exact class of behavior:
- First attempt used `risk: "balanced"` doctrine (copied from an unrelated existing test's pattern) — every tier-1 discretionary check, including the one under test, only ever runs for `risk == "inherit"`, so the test wasn't exercising the mechanism at all. Fixed by leaving risk at its default, which is also the realistic case (mortar doctrine has no risk-override UI at all).
- Second attempt used an easy, always-visible, always-in-range enemy squad target for realism — but `_tick_fire`'s own (separate, correct, already-established) interrupt-to-fire behavior let the mortar find a shot almost immediately regardless of the bug, masking it entirely. Fixed by removing any reachable target, isolating the precaution's own behavior from that unrelated mechanism.
- Third attempt initialized the mortar at its default `seconds_stationary` (a huge sentinel, "already emplaced since before the battle began") — trivially satisfying fire-eligibility from tick zero and never exercising the actual danger state (just-arrived, clock at zero). Fixed by explicitly starting it at 0.
- Fourth attempt called `_process()` with a half-second delta — `_current_time_scale()` multiplies delta by 60x under live contact (or 300x with none), so each call jumped scenario time by MORE than the entire 30-second window being tested, "settling" it in a single step regardless of the underlying bug. Fixed by using a real per-frame delta (1/60 real second) and ensuring genuine "contact" (a visible-but-unreachable distant enemy squad, avoiding the second trap) so the simulation actually ran at normal, granular pacing.

With all four of those corrected, the test reliably fails against the buggy code (confirmed directly) and passes against the fix — a real, working sensitivity check, not just a test that happens to read green.

**Verified**: all five permanent regression suites (this new one included) passed, and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run stayed healthy: SPOTTER 78% held / 6.73x — the best result yet this session, consistent with the player's mortar now actually managing to fight through its own self-preservation checks; DRONE_TEAM 61% held / 1.59x — in the same already-discussed range as the prior three entries.

### 2026-09-13 — The real fix: relocation gated on "does the enemy know where I am," not on any event

*"Now we have the opposite problem. The mortar is always scooting and never shooting."* Followed by the user identifying the actual missing principle directly, unprompted: *"I've realized what the condition for scooting should be. It is not 'Did I just shoot?' It's 'does the enemy know my location with enough accuracy to hit me?' If one shoots, the enemy does learn one's location. So one has to scoot to change that. After the scoot, if one's location is unknown to the enemy, one can shoot again. One also might be able to shoot without scooting if the enemy has no mortars in range, or close."*

This replaces every event-based trigger this project had tried for mortar self-preservation so far — "did I just fire" (the original shoot-and-scoot doctrine), "how many enemy mortars exist somewhere on the map" (density override), "is the mortar currently idle" (the standing-threat entry two above) — with one persistent, physically-grounded state question, checked fresh every tick with nothing to reset or game.

**Root cause of "always scooting, never shooting"**: `_tick_fire` queued a displacement (`_queue_mortar_displacement`) after EVERY shot, and that function's own overwrite semantics mean a fresh call always replaces the pending entry outright. `reload_time` defaults to the SAME 30 seconds as the packing-up delay itself — so a mortar reloading and re-engaging just as fast as it could depart would perpetually re-queue (and thus reset) its own departure, never actually leaving. The real condition for needing to displace was never "did I just fire" — a crew that fired 10 minutes ago and has since moved on has nothing to fear from that old shot; the danger is specifically "the enemy's intel on my CURRENT position is both recent enough and precise enough to act on."

**The new model** — `BattleManager._mortar_should_relocate_for_safety(m)`, three real conditions, all required:
1. **Position compromised**: `mortar_recently_detected_firing(m)` (the same muzzle-blast/trajectory detection channel `_known_friendly_mortar_position` and the opposing side's own hunting already use — not a new concept) AND the mortar hasn't yet put real distance between itself and where that happened. Distance matters independently of time: reusing `COUNTER_BATTERY_BLAST_RADIUS` (150m) as the "still close enough to where I fired to be caught" threshold means moving even a little clears the danger immediately, regardless of how much of the detection's own 180-second relevance window is left. This directly implements "after the scoot, if one's location is unknown to the enemy, one can shoot again" — decoupling safety from a fixed timer.
2. **A real threat**: at least `MORTAR_STANDING_THREAT_COUNT` (1) known enemy mortars actually in range — "one also might be able to shoot without scooting if the enemy has no mortars in range, or close," verbatim.
3. **Doctrine/severity gate**: shoot-and-scoot doctrine, OR density past `MORTAR_DENSITY_FORCE_SCOOT_COUNT` (2) severe enough to override even a deliberate hold-position choice — both constants reused unchanged, just recombined under the new, correct gating condition.

**Fix, mechanically**: `_mortar_shot_this_tick` (shared by every mortar-relevant caller) now refuses to even look for a target while this condition holds — no new shot is attempted while a real displacement is owed, so there's no timer to reset in the first place. `_tick_fire`'s old post-shot queueing block is gone entirely. `_decide_mortar_action`'s tier-1 ladder gained the actual relocation logic in its place: redirect immediately (no packing-up delay) if already moving for some other reason (a hunt, most likely), or queue the real 30-second delay if currently stationary — narrated and queued only once per episode, not every tick spent waiting. The counter-battery responder's own fired-stage now just records `_last_detected_mortar_fire` for itself (its muzzle blast gives it away exactly like any other shot) and lets the same unified mechanism pick it up next tick, instead of duplicating a separate scoot-trigger.

**Testing took real, honest debugging** to get right, worth recording since each trap is a genuine hazard for testing this exact class of mechanic: a plain idle-mortar test needed correcting once the model changed (an idle mortar that's never fired must NOT evade just because a threat exists — the exact over-broad behavior being replaced); the over-evasion test's target needed to BE an enemy mortar rather than a separately-marked "known" one (any way of marking a second mortar "known" fed the same "hold fire, pursue it instead" logic that made the test mortar never engage the intended target at all — an artificial deadlock specific to a sparse test setup, not a real bug); establishing visibility needed an actual spotter unit (a mortar alone cannot establish or maintain LOS-based visibility on anything — `CombatResolver.has_live_observer` requires a real observer, re-checked every tick with a hard cutoff, no persistence); and the final assertion needed to track the mortar's position AT THE MOMENT of each shot rather than aggregate drift over the run, since an unrelated, legitimate mechanism (genuine visual spotting) could satisfy an aggregate-drift threshold by accident even with the core mechanism completely broken — confirmed directly by temporarily removing the fix and watching the aggregate-drift version of the test still pass. The corrected, position-at-shot-time version fails immediately when the fix is removed and passes cleanly when it's restored.

**Verified**: three permanent tests (compromised idle mortar still evades; uncompromised mortar does not needlessly evade; a mortar with a persistent target and a persistent known threat both relocates AND resumes firing afterward), each independently sensitivity-checked by reverting the specific piece of logic it covers. All five permanent regression suites passed. Full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`: SPOTTER 79% held / 5.92x; DRONE_TEAM 79% held / 4.31x — a dramatic jump from the 55-64% held / 1.28-1.61x range that had persisted across the three prior mortar-survival-symmetry entries, strongly suggesting this fix didn't just resolve the two reported bugs but resolved the accumulating balance question flagged (and deliberately left unaddressed) in the entry two above: the mortar now relocates exactly when the real-world condition actually calls for it, not too rarely and not too often.

### 2026-09-13 — Collateral damage: attacks are aimed at a unit but hit a location

*"Do you have cases where fire at unit A accidentally hits unit B? This should happen, including friendly fire. For example, if a mortar is close to squads, fire aimed at it might hit the squads, regardless of which side they are on."* Investigated directly rather than assumed: the only existing mechanic was `_bunched_ally`/`_resolve_fire_and_check_bunching`'s "bunching spillover" — squad-on-squad only, and restricted to allies of the specific intended target, so a shot at an enemy MORTAR never had any collateral mechanic at all, and even squad-on-squad spillover could never hit the shooter's own side. Confirmed and reported honestly before changing anything.

Refined through direct follow-up, each correcting the model further: *"Fire should hit a location. Anybody nearby has a chance of being hurt, especially with artillery."* / *"Friendly fire with squads is likely possible too, but probably less common as it's small arms fire."* / *"But fire at squads that are bunched up could absolutely hit a squad that was not the target"* (confirming the existing bunching case itself was correct and should stay) / *"Yes, ultimately damage should not directly depend on who was the target of the attack"* / *"Attacks are aimed at a unit but hit a location"* — the precise, final framing: aim is unit-based (leading a moving target, etc. — already modeled), but the actual EFFECT is location-based, and collateral risk shouldn't be gated on whether the specifically-aimed-at unit happened to be hit.

**The model**: `BattleManager._collateral_victim(impact_point, exclude, radius, chance_at, only_squads)` — any unit (either side, real HE fragmentation and even a stray small-arms round don't check IDs) within `radius` of the REAL impact point (the target's own position for direct fire; a mortar shell's actual `aim_point`, which can differ from the target by up to `MORTAR_EVASION_RADIUS` when a lead didn't pan out), nearest checked first, DRONE always excluded (airborne, same protection logic as everywhere else), one victim per shot.

Two weapon-type profiles, called from `_resolve_fire_and_check_bunching` based on `attacker.kind`:
- **Mortar/artillery**: reuses `MORTAR_EVASION_RADIUS` (40m, already described as "the beaten zone") as the outer edge, with a NEW `GameConfig.MORTAR_BLAST_COLLATERAL_MAX_CHANCE` (0.6) scaling down by distance the same way `_resolve_pending_counter_battery`'s own `impact_chance` already does — grounded on real cited 82mm HE figures (an ~8m near-certain-casualty core, ~26m lethal-fragment radius for the Type 67 82mm mortar). Any unit kind, any side.
- **Direct/small-arms fire**: the existing `BUNCHING_RADIUS`/`BUNCHING_SPILLOVER_CHANCE` (30m, flat 25%) — UNCHANGED numbers, since the user explicitly confirmed the existing squad-on-squad case was already right — but now checked against BOTH sides instead of only the target's allies, and restricted to squad-on-squad specifically (aimed rifle fire is far more discriminating than an explosive round's fragmentation; this is about troops crowded together, not any two units merely nearby).

Critically, the check runs regardless of whether the primary aimed-at unit itself was hit (the earlier version gated on `if not hit: return`, which was wrong per the user's own correction) — and applies to BOTH regular mortar-shot resolution and counter-battery impact resolution (a CB shell is physically the same kind of event; its own collateral check now runs unconditionally on whether it actually caught its specific target).

**Verified**: four sensitivity-checked cases — a mortar's blast can hit a unit on the SHOOTER'S OWN side (not just an ally of the target); collateral is possible even when the primary target is guaranteed to miss (`base_hit_chance = 0`); a DRONE is never a victim; and a counter-battery strike's own impact produces the same collateral behavior. Two real test-writing traps were caught and fixed along the way: the first "cross-side" test case didn't actually distinguish "restricted to the attacker's side" from "restricted to the target's side" (both would have passed it) until corrected to check the true original restriction; and `test_unit_doctrine.gd`'s existing `SplashBattle` test harness (which overrides the victim-picking function to force a specific outcome) needed its `bm.splash = null` reset moved earlier, since the new counter-battery collateral check now also consults it and the harness's forced override was leaking into a later, unrelated assertion. Added as a permanent test (`test_collateral_damage` in `test_unit_doctrine.gd`). All five permanent regression suites and a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run pass, healthy and consistent with the established post-redesign baseline (SPOTTER 70% held / 6.06x, DRONE_TEAM 81% held / 4.67x) — no meaningful shift, expected since collateral damage is a comparatively rare secondary effect, not a dominant driver of outcomes.

### 2026-09-13 — Ballistic dispersion, and fire accuracy that depends on who's actually watching it land

*"Is the mortar going close enough to friendly units that they might be hit by CB aimed at it? I genuinely can't tell."* Before answering that, a more fundamental gap surfaced: *"Right, and as a part of this, how much do actual impact points vary from the aim point? That should also be implemented, probably first even."* Investigated and confirmed directly: `_mortar_aim_point(target)` was fully deterministic — no randomness at all — and was treated as the literal impact point everywhere (the miss check, the collateral-damage radius). Real indirect fire never lands exactly where calculated; this was a real, load-bearing gap the friendly-proximity question couldn't be answered honestly without first closing.

Then, mid-implementation, extended further, unprompted: *"The quality of spotting probably impacts the accuracy of shots after the first one. Can the firing unit learn where the impact point was. This probably depends on whether there is a squad observing, a spotter, a drone, or nobody. All of that should be modeled. And modeled based on real world data if you can find them."*

**Researched real-world grounding** (see GameConfig.MORTAR_DISPERSION_* for citations inline): unguided 120mm mortar CEP runs ~136m at max range without an advanced fire control system (NATO figure) — scaled to this project's own, shorter 82mm `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE` as a ~3% CEP-of-range fraction rather than reused outright (a different caliber's number at a different max range isn't directly portable). Real adjust-fire doctrine (FM 6-30's successive-bracketing method) has an observer watch the fall of shot and radio a correction, converging over a handful of rounds — a trained forward observer can register a mean point of impact to roughly 20m. Ukraine-war reporting on drone-directed artillery found real-time video correction destroying a platoon defensive position in ~9 rounds versus 60-90 unobserved — an order-of-magnitude efficiency gain from the fastest, tightest-converging observation channel modeled here.

**The model**:
- `BattleManager._mortar_dispersion_offset(mortar, target, aim_point)` — sampled as a circular Gaussian (CEP = 1.1774 × σ is the standard CEP-to-standard-deviation conversion), not a uniform disc, so most rounds cluster near the aim point with a real long tail of wider misses. `aim_point` (what the crew calculated/led) and the new `impact_point` (where the round actually lands) are now separate fields on every pending mortar shot — the miss check and the collateral-damage radius both moved from the former to the latter.
- `BattleManager._mortar_fire_observation_quality(mortar, target)` — reuses the existing `CombatResolver.has_live_observer` LOS mechanic (no new geometry) against the mortar's own side's units, best tier wins: a DRONE beats a SPOTTER beats any other unit with live LOS ("squad," untrained self-observation) beats nobody at all ("none").
- `_mortar_fire_adjustment` (Unit → `{target, shots}`) tracks "walking fire" onto the CURRENT target only — each further OBSERVED shot at the same target converges dispersion geometrically toward a floor (drone: decay 0.15, floor 6m; spotter: decay 0.35, floor 18m; squad: decay 0.65, floor 30m); an unobserved shot teaches nothing, so the counter only advances when quality isn't "none." Switching targets resets the counter outright — a correction learned against one aim point says nothing about a different one, exactly like a real fire mission starting fresh.
- Deliberately scoped to regular mortar-target engagement only, NOT counter-battery: a CB strike is a single reactive shot at a last-known position with its own already-designed, already-cited distance-based falloff model (`_resolve_pending_counter_battery`'s `impact_chance`), not an iterative "walk fire onto a tracked target" scenario — extending dispersion+adjustment there wasn't asked for and doesn't fit the mechanic.

**A real bug this exposed, not caused**: adding new upstream random draws shifted `test_mortar_evasion_balance.gd`'s existing seeded RNG stream, and the specific committed seed started failing (the mortar getting stuck "scooting"/"concealing" for most of the test's tick budget). Traced directly rather than just re-seeding away the symptom: the test's own spotter was planted EXACTLY on top of the enemy mortar it was directing fire onto — harmless when every shot landed exactly at the aim point, but a real, if accidental, self-inflicted-casualty risk once shots can disperse slightly off-aim into the existing collateral-damage radius (a co-located "spotter" is realistically the last place a real FO would stand). Fixed at the root: moved the test's spotter to a 60px (300m) standoff, comfortably inside detection range but outside `MORTAR_EVASION_RADIUS`. Confirmed the same fix works across 13 different seeds, not just the one that happened to newly fail.

**Testing**: new permanent suite `test_mortar_dispersion.gd` (4 tests, each independently sensitivity-checked by disabling the specific mechanism it covers and confirming the same assertion then fails): no observer means dispersion never converges shot over shot; a spotter's repeated corrected shots converge substantially tighter than the unadjusted average; drone-observed fire converges to a hard, analytically-derived bound tighter than the spotter's own floor (not just a noisy relative comparison between two samples of what a broken implementation could make an identical distribution); switching targets resets the adjustment counter. All five permanent regression suites pass.

**A real, measured balance consequence — reported directly, not glossed over**: full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`, compared against the collateral-damage entry's own baseline immediately above — SPOTTER held 70%→86% (exchange ratio 6.06x→9.50x), DRONE_TEAM held 81%→89% (exchange ratio 4.67x→4.82x). Both are real improvements for the player, not noise, and there's a clear mechanical reason: the player's own recon investment (a SPOTTER unit in SPOTTER mode, drones in DRONE_TEAM mode) now pays off as mortar-fire ACCURACY, not just detection — the player's mortar reliably reaches the fast-converging "spotter"/"drone" quality tier, while the enemy mortar, with no equivalent dedicated asset, is mostly limited to the slower "squad" tier or "none." SPOTTER mode's jump is the larger of the two because a dedicated spotter is a much bigger accuracy asymmetry over ordinary squad self-observation than drone-vs-spotter is over each other. This wasn't the point of the feature (which was requested for realism and to inform the still-pending friendly-proximity question) but is a legitimate, real-world-grounded consequence of it, not a bug — recon assets making your own indirect fire measurably better, not just giving you better information, is exactly what real forward observers and drone spotting are for.

### 2026-09-13 — Friendly-fire casualties were invisible in the damage-by-unit report

*"In the current battle, the casualties sidebar says we have taken five casualties. However, the damage by unit shows... TOTAL INFLICTED: 0 killed, 1 heavily wounded, 3 walking wounded (4 total). The totals should match. But we see 4 in one case and 5 in the other."*

Investigated rather than assumed a math bug: the sidebar's casualty count (`BattleManager._compute_side_stats`) is ground truth — total pips actually lost across all player units, whoever caused it. `UnitCombatStats`'s "ENEMY UNITS: TOTAL INFLICTED" line only sums casualties credited to an *enemy* attacker. Since the collateral-damage feature two entries above made friendly fire real and side-agnostic, a player unit hitting its own side is entirely possible now, and any casualty from it is credited to the ATTACKER's own row (correctly — `UnitCombatStats.damage()` was never wrong), which means it shows up under "YOUR UNITS," not "ENEMY UNITS" — invisible to a reader comparing only the enemy total against the sidebar. Confirmed directly: asked the user to check the "YOUR UNITS" section of the same report, which showed exactly this.

**The user's own fix, applied precisely**: *"I think it should say that in the line shown below. However, if there is no friendly fire, don't pollute the line by adding that information."* — then, unprompted, added the same request for the side-level TOTAL INFLICTED line too.

**The model**: `UnitCombatStats` rows gained `friendly_fire_casualties`, incremented in `damage()` alongside the existing killed/heavily_wounded/walking_wounded/casualties tally whenever `attacker.team == target.team`. `report_lines()` appends `" (%d friendly fire)"` to a unit's summary line, and `", %d friendly fire"` to its side's TOTAL INFLICTED line, in both cases ONLY when the count is nonzero — a unit or side that never committed friendly fire reads exactly as it always did, no "(0 friendly fire)" noise. The report's own top-of-file "Casualties = ... ON THE ENEMY" framing was also corrected — no longer strictly true once collateral damage is side-agnostic.

**Verified**: new permanent test `test_friendly_fire_reporting` in `test_unit_doctrine.gd` — a real friendly-fire hit (same setup as `test_collateral_damage`'s own shooter-side-blast case) is tracked exactly on the attacker's row and shows up in both the per-unit line and the side total; a battle with zero friendly fire mentions it nowhere in the report. Sensitivity-checked by disabling the new tracking condition and confirming all three related assertions fail. All six permanent regression suites pass. Skipped the full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` regression — this change is purely additive to the reporting layer (a new tracked field plus report text) and touches no gameplay or decision logic, matching this project's established precedent for pure-reporting/UI changes.

### 2026-09-13 — Shoot-and-scoot was pacing back and forth between the same 2-3 hiding spots

*"Shoot and scoot doesn't just involve movement. It involves getting away from the area that is likely to be hit. Often, the friendly mortar moving back and forth after shooting."*

Investigated directly rather than assumed: built a diagnostic logging every real scoot destination across several full battles. Confirmed the report exactly — the DRONE_TEAM trials in particular showed the mortar cycling through the SAME 2-3 positions over and over (e.g., ~1018,2140m → ~845,2550m → ~815,2622m → back to ~1018,2140m, repeating for 13+ scoots), sometimes landing within 1-2m of a position it had just left.

**What was already correct, verified rather than assumed broken**: `_reverse_slope_candidate` already explicitly rejects any hill-based candidate closer than `COUNTER_BATTERY_BLAST_RADIUS` (150m) to wherever the mortar currently is, and `_ring_search_hidden_point`'s own nearest ring (250m) already clears that radius by construction — EACH INDIVIDUAL scoot genuinely did get clear of its own immediately-prior position. The bug was one level up: `nearest_hidden_point` has no memory of anywhere the mortar has already been. `_reverse_slope_candidate` in particular is anchored to a hill's own geometry and the threats' bearing, NOT to the mortar's current position — for an unchanged threat picture it returns the literal SAME point every time, which is exactly what produced the tight, repeating cycle.

**The fix**: a new short, rolling per-mortar history (`BattleManager._mortar_recent_positions`, `_remember_mortar_position`) of the last few relocation destinations, fed into `GameConfig.nearest_hidden_point`/`_ring_search_hidden_point`/`_reverse_slope_candidate` as an additional `avoid_positions` list — any candidate within `COUNTER_BATTERY_BLAST_RADIUS` of a remembered position is rejected, the same real justification already established for why that distance from the CURRENT position isn't safe applying just as well to a position the crew itself recently vacated. A ROLLING window, not permanent avoidance — real predictability risk fades over an engagement, and a fixed cap avoids the search running out of valid candidates entirely on a map with only a few good hiding spots relative to a static threat layout (confirmed as a real constraint by the diagnostic itself).

**A real refinement caught empirically, not left in**: the history is shared across every relocation reason (scoot, evade, conceal, ...), and an initial `MORTAR_RECENT_POSITION_MEMORY_COUNT` of 3 meant a single intervening evade/conceal move (reacting to an unrelated, immediate threat) consumed one of only 3 remembered slots, pushing a still-relevant scoot position out of the window early — traced directly (a debug trace of every issued move showed a scoot landing 33m from one just 3 "moves" — but only 2 real scoots — earlier). Raised to 5 for real headroom; re-verified on the same seeds that this cut repeat/near-repeat destination pairs from 8 down to 3 across six full battles, with the remaining ones now genuinely aged-out (5+ relocations back) rather than the immediate, wall-to-wall repetition originally reported. Not pursued further (e.g., separate scoot-only history) — diminishing returns on what's now a rare edge case rather than the dominant, systemic pattern originally reported.

**Verified**: new permanent test `test_mortar_avoids_recently_used_scoot_positions` in `test_mortar_evasion_balance.gd` — six successive relocation plans from the same mortar must never land within `COUNTER_BATTERY_BLAST_RADIUS` of another still inside the remembered-position window. Sensitivity-checked by disabling the recent-position feed and confirming the exact same scenario then fails on 4 of the 6 destination pairs (the mortar genuinely does re-converge on the same couple of spots without the fix). All six permanent regression suites pass.

**A real, mixed balance consequence — reported directly**: full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`, compared against the dispersion entry's own baseline two above — SPOTTER held 86%→76% (exchange ratio 9.50x→7.37x, both DOWN), DRONE_TEAM held 89%→99% (exchange ratio 4.82x→6.14x, both UP). Not a uniform improvement: forcing genuine position variety means the mortar sometimes settles for a real but less-ideal hiding spot instead of always converging on the single best one, which measurably cost SPOTTER mode some effectiveness while DRONE_TEAM mode benefited. This is the honest, real cost of the fix, not a hidden regression — the alternative (the mortar's own pathologically predictable pacing pattern) is the actual bug being traded away, and the user may want to revisit `MORTAR_RECENT_POSITION_MEMORY_COUNT` or the exclusion radius later with this tradeoff in mind.

### 2026-09-14 — A general retreat walked the mortar toward the enemy before it actually retreated

*"I hit 'retreat now'. The mortar started my moving forwards a fair distance. Then it retreated."*

Traced end-to-end rather than guessed at: `Unit.order_retreat` doesn't retreat directly — it first walks to nearby cover (`GameConfig.retreat_cover_point_toward` for a mortar), and only once that's reached (or if already in cover) does `_tick_movement` switch it over to `_step_retreat`'s actual dash toward the safe line. Both phases are real and correct in principle (duck into cover before making the exposed run home); the bug was in what "nearby cover" resolved to. Confirmed directly at the mortar's own actual map deployment point, independent of which direction the triggering enemy was in: `retreat_cover_point_toward` consistently picked a cover zone ~90-115m EAST (toward the enemy) as that first waypoint.

**Root cause**: `_prefer_retreat_direction`'s travel budget was always measured from `nearest_overall` — the single closest cover candidate in ANY direction — not from the nearest candidate actually on the correct side. Cover isn't laid out with retreat direction in mind, so whenever the closest zone of all happened to be behind the unit's own back (the common case, evidently, at the mortar's own default position), that nearby wrong-side point set an artificially tight budget that excluded a real, reachable correct-side option for being "too far past an unrelated nearby blip" — while `RETREAT_DIRECTION_TOLERANCE`'s small forward allowance (100m) let that same nearby wrong-side point win outright anyway. Both mechanisms were individually reasonable (documented, cited, built to fix a real 2km-trek bug previously) but combined to make "walk toward the enemy for a nearby cover point" the routine outcome here, not a rare edge case.

**The fix**: `_prefer_retreat_direction` now budgets the correct-side (strict) pass from the nearest candidate that's ALREADY on the correct side, not from whatever's nearest overall — a real correct-side option no longer gets starved by an unrelated nearby wrong-side one. This still protects the original 638m/2400m case (both correct-side, nothing closer) exactly as before, since the budget is nearest-correct-side + `RETREAT_DIRECTION_MAX_EXTRA_M`, which still excludes a distant outlier past the nearest reasonable one. Only when NOTHING at all qualifies on the correct side does it fall back to the original nearest-overall-based lenient pass (small wrong-side tolerance) — unchanged from before, so a unit with genuinely no correct-side cover anywhere still gets some nearby answer instead of none. Shared by `nearest_cover_point`/`safest_cover_point`/`retreat_cover_point_toward`, so this fixes squad and spotter/drone-team retreats identically, not just the mortar case that was reported.

**A real, reasoned edge case, not a new regression**: at the mortar's own default position, the nearest correct-side option turned out to be ~2400m away — essentially at the safe line itself, since this map's west flank buffer zone has no cover zones in it at all between the front and safety. Verified this isn't actually a "2km detour for no reason": `REPOSITION_SPEED` (1.8 m/s, used to walk to cover) and `PLAYER_RETREAT_SPEED` (2.0 m/s, used for the dash) are close enough that walking the whole way via the "go to cover" leg costs barely more time than a direct retreat dash would anyway — this is functionally just "retreating," not a wasted side-trip.

**Verified**: new permanent suite `test_retreat_direction.gd` (4 tests): a close wrong-side candidate must never outrank a reachable correct-side one; the original far-outlier-exclusion case still holds; falling back to a nearby wrong-side candidate when nothing at all exists on the correct side still works; an end-to-end reproduction at the mortar's real map position across 20 draws never picks a forward destination. Sensitivity-checked by reverting to the original single-budget logic and confirming 21 of the same assertions then fail, including the exact reported scenario. All seven permanent regression suites pass.

**A real, mixed balance consequence — reported directly**: full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`, compared against the scoot-oscillation entry's own baseline immediately above — SPOTTER held 76%→84% (exchange ratio 7.37x→8.85x, both UP), DRONE_TEAM held 99%→89% (exchange ratio 6.14x→5.48x, both DOWN). Not pursued further to explain the DRONE_TEAM direction specifically (this fix touches every retreating unit kind, not just the mortar, so the effect runs through squad retreats too, which dominate DRONE_TEAM mode's much higher volume of squad-vs-squad combat) — worth knowing about, and revisiting if the user wants to dig into why a strictly-more-correct retreat direction cost DRONE_TEAM mode some of its previously-measured 99% held rate.
