### 2026-09-14 — A resupply run would walk cross-map to deliver a handful of rounds to an already well-stocked mortar

*"Next issue is mortar resupply tactics. Would the run go to the mortar if the mortar already had rounds? Could this impact the mobility of the mortar team? Please research real world behavior... Hold off on actual rewrites for now. Just suggest what you think makes sense."*

Investigated directly rather than assumed: a mortar's resupply *request* is deliberately never gated on ammo level (the ~hour-plus transit time means waiting for a real shortage would already be too late — already well-grounded, unchanged). The gap was at *dispatch* time — the only thing that held a scheduled wave back from becoming a real, physical `RESUPPLY_RUN` unit was `mortar_rounds_remaining >= MORTAR_MAX_AMMO_ON_HAND` (30), i.e. the position had to be COMPLETELY full. With `MORTAR_RESUPPLY_WAVE_COUNT = 2` waves scheduled per request, a genuine degenerate case existed: wave 1 tops a position off to 30, and if the mortar barely fires before wave 2's own (independently, randomly sampled) arrival time, wave 2 still checks in at e.g. 29/30 and dispatches a full cross-map run to deliver exactly 1 round. Mobility itself is barely touched by any of this in code — the only ammo-gated movement behavior is tier-1 "linkup" (walking toward an inbound run instead of relocating for concealment), and that only fires when `mortar_rounds_remaining <= 0`, not merely "has some rounds" — but a real, mechanically-live cost exists anyway: a `RESUPPLY_RUN` is a genuine, targetable unit that walks toward the mortar's live position, and now that collateral damage is side-agnostic and shells disperse off their aim point, unnecessary run traffic near the mortar is real (if secondary) exposure that didn't used to matter before those two features existed.

**Real-world research** (see GameConfig.MORTAR_RESUPPLY_REORDER_POINT's own doc comment for the citations inline): FM 7-90 (Tactical Employment of Mortars) names three resupply methods — routine, emergency, and **prestock** — chosen by METT-T, where prestock means ammunition is staged at the position *in advance* based on anticipated need, not delivered on-demand mid-fight. Its "pull"/in-position technique has crews go back to a rear rearm point rather than push a vehicle forward, specifically to avoid exposing it, when the situation doesn't clearly call for the trip. "Periods of limited visibility are the best times to resupply." None of this describes topping off to full every time a scheduled wave happens to come up — it describes staging against anticipated need and treating each forward movement as a real cost.

**The fix**: new `GameConfig.MORTAR_RESUPPLY_REORDER_POINT = 10` (half of `MORTAR_STARTING_AMMO`, the user's own explicit call: *"let's hold it back until we are down to 10 rounds on-hand"*) replaces `MORTAR_MAX_AMMO_ON_HAND` as the dispatch-hold threshold in `_update_mortar_resupply`. Requesting stays exactly as proactive as before; only whether a scheduled wave actually becomes a physical, spottable run changed. A wave held this way isn't lost — it simply never spawns a unit for that check, same as before, and the existing per-cycle cleanup/re-request mechanism already handles a fully-held cycle gracefully (a fresh request cycle begins immediately since the sighting condition that triggered the first one is still true).

**Verified**: new permanent suite `test_mortar_resupply.gd` (4 tests): a mortar well above the reorder point holds the wave back; one exactly at the point dispatches (boundary case); one well below dispatches; a dispatched run's deliverable amount is a real, substantial delivery, not a token few rounds. Sensitivity-checked by reverting to the original `MORTAR_MAX_AMMO_ON_HAND` threshold and confirming exactly the "held well above the reorder point" case then fails, as expected — the other three cases (well below, exactly-at-reorder-point with old logic still under 30, meaningful-delivery) remain unaffected by the revert, correctly isolating what the fix actually changed. A real, unrelated flakiness trap was caught and fixed along the way: the test's own record initially scheduled 2 waves both already "due," so `MORTAR_RESUPPLY_FAILURE_CHANCE`'s independent 10% roll on EACH wave made an exact "1 run spawned" assertion nondeterministic (0, 1, or 2 dispatches all possible) — fixed by using a single-wave record per test, matching what each test actually needs to isolate. All eight permanent regression suites pass.

**A real, measured reduction in unnecessary trips — the fix's actual intended effect, not just a balance side-effect**: full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`, compared against the retreat-direction entry's own baseline immediately above — player-side resupply runs spawned dropped from 28→21 in SPOTTER mode (-25%) and 31→13 in DRONE_TEAM mode (-58%). Held/exchange-ratio moved modestly in both modes (SPOTTER 84%→80% held, 8.85x→6.87x; DRONE_TEAM 89%→91% held, 5.48x→4.67x) — not dramatic, and not chased further here, but reported alongside the resupply-count numbers rather than only citing the metric that looks best.

### 2026-09-14 — Shoot-and-scoot still oscillating after the "fixed" recent-position fix — a genuinely different, deeper mechanism

*"In the current battle, mortar 'scooting' seems to involve a lot of oscillation back and forth. As opposed to getting away from the launch point as fast as possible. Is it really oscillating? Or is that a graphical issue? Please make sure this is really fixed before you report it as 'fixed'. You've already reported this as 'fixed' once before."*

Taken at full rigor, per the explicit instruction. First question answered directly: real, not graphical — confirmed by logging the actual simulation position data (not rendering) across 30 fresh full battles. DRONE_TEAM mode in particular showed genuine, large-scale wandering: 27.7% of all real scoots moved the mortar measurably CLOSER to its own launch point than the previous scoot, and average path efficiency (net displacement from launch / total distance actually traveled across all scoots) was just 0.36 — the mortar was covering roughly 3x more ground than its own net progress away from danger.

**Two real fix attempts that measured as no better, reported honestly rather than kept quietly**: (1) making the routine "position compromised" scoot always use the farther-reaching urgent concealment rings (reasoning: shoot-and-scoot's whole purpose deserves the same urgency spotted/hit already get) — measured change: 27.7%→27.1% reversals, 0.36→0.39 efficiency, one trial actually got WORSE (11→24 reversals). Reverted. (2) Enforcing the existing home-range leash (`MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_RANGE_FROM_HOME`) INSIDE the concealment search itself instead of only checking the final result — a real, separately-justified improvement in principle, but measured as bit-for-bit IDENTICAL to the "before" numbers on every single trial. Neither theory was actually driving the reported symptom; both were plausible-sounding guesses that the data rejected. Kept the leash-in-search change anyway (a real, independent improvement — see below) but did not stop there or declare victory on guesswork.

**The actual mechanism, confirmed by tracing every real relocation-search call during one specific oscillating battle, not theorized**: the reverse-slope hill candidate was essentially ALWAYS rejected as "too far" once the mortar had moved on from its launch point (it's anchored to hill/threat-bearing geometry, not the mortar's own position — a fixed point that stops competing once the mortar is elsewhere). The ring search — which has no notion of which direction is actually away from where the danger started, only "hidden from CURRENT threats, not recently used, from wherever I am RIGHT NOW" — was the one producing the wandering. The deeper gap: evade, conceal, and scoot share NO memory of each other. Each is purely reactive to the current moment. The trace caught the exact mechanism directly: a scoot pushed the mortar to 2360m from launch, then a completely separate `evade` — reacting to some other threat, with its own purely-reactive search — walked it back to 1473m, and the NEXT scoot simply continued from THAT position, with zero awareness a prior escape had already gotten twice as far.

**The actual fix**: `BattleManager._mortar_max_distance_from_home` — a high-water mark, not a ceiling, on how far a relocation has put the mortar from `_friendly_mortar_home_position` during the CURRENT danger episode. Fed into `GameConfig.nearest_hidden_point` (threaded through to `_ring_search_hidden_point`/`_reverse_slope_candidate`, alongside the leash-in-search change from attempt 2 above, as a parallel `min_distance_from_home` floor) as a hard floor on the NEXT relocation, whichever of evade/conceal/scoot triggers it. Updated the instant a plan is accepted (not on arrival — so it protects mid-walk, not just after settling). Reset to nothing — deliberately NOT a permanent one-way ratchet — the moment `_decide_mortar_action` falls through every tier-1 self-preservation check with none of them triggering: once genuinely no longer compromised, a later, unrelated compromise event starts its own fresh escape, since there's no real reason a mortar must stay at maximum distance from home indefinitely after the actual danger that pushed it there has passed.

**Verified**: new permanent test `test_relocation_never_undoes_progress_from_a_different_threat` in `test_mortar_evasion_balance.gd` — a mortar already partway through an escape, given an artificially pre-set progress floor, must never accept a relocation closer to home than that floor even when reacting to a completely different, closer threat whose own natural pull is back toward home. A real test-writing trap caught along the way (the same class of trap this whole project has hit repeatedly): the first version placed the mortar exactly at its own home reference with an unrealistically high floor, which the ring search's own fixed radii could never geometrically satisfy regardless of whether the fix worked at all — passed and failed for the wrong reasons. Fixed by giving the mortar real, already-escaped distance from home and a threat positioned to naturally pull it back, isolating the actual mechanism. Sensitivity-checked: reverting the floor reproduces the exact reported failure mode precisely (required 300px, got 130px). All eight permanent regression suites pass.

### 2026-09-14 — Scoot distances grounded in real doctrine, then a cascade of conflicts that exposed the search's whole priority order was backwards

*"First, please put a scale onto the map. The scoots look excessively long currently."* Then, once a scale bar confirmed it: *"You might want to look into how far real teams for this type of mortar would typically move after shooting, assuming an ongoing assault made it urgent to fire again."*

**The map scale itself was a real, separate rendering bug**, not a missing feature: `main.gd`'s own `_draw()` already contained a 1000m scale bar and compass, but `main.gd extends Node2D` with `map_container` (the actual battle viewport) added as a child — Godot draws a node's own `_draw()` output BEFORE its children paint over it, so the scale bar had been silently invisible the whole time. Fixed by moving that drawing into a standalone `map_hud_overlay.gd` node added as a later sibling of `map_container`, so it now genuinely renders on top.

**Real-world research**: FM 7-90-equivalent mortar displacement doctrine cites 75-100m for an EMERGENCY displacement made under pressure to keep firing (short enough the same firing solution still roughly applies), versus a much shorter 25-30m for a PLANNED move to an alternate position with no immediate threat driving it. This project's own scoot trigger (`_mortar_should_relocate_for_safety`) is always the emergency case — a real, already-detected threat in range — so `COUNTER_BATTERY_BLAST_RADIUS` (previously 150m, no real citation) dropped to 75m (the lower bound of the cited emergency range, chosen deliberately over the upper bound since the trigger never represents anything less urgent), and `CONCEALMENT_SEARCH_RINGS_M`/`_URGENT_M` shrank from `[250,450,650]`/`[450,700,1000]` to `[75,100,130]`/`[100,130,160]` — 3-10x smaller, with the routine set's smallest ring sitting exactly at the new blast radius to preserve "a scoot clears the blast radius by construction."

**Implementing these numbers immediately exposed two real, previously-invisible conflicts, not cosmetic ones**: (1) `MORTAR_RECENT_POSITION_MEMORY_COUNT`'s own exclusion radius reused `COUNTER_BATTERY_BLAST_RADIUS` directly — sound when both were 150m, but once the blast radius shrank to 75m (comparable to the entire new ring reach), a handful of remembered positions could blanket nearly the whole reachable search area. Fixed with a dedicated, decoupled `MORTAR_RECENT_POSITION_EXCLUSION_RADIUS = 20m`. (2) The pre-existing `MORTAR_CREW_OVERRUN_DANGER_RANGE` (600m, shared by every ground unit's concealment search, guarding against a different real concern — personal overrun by advancing infantry, not counter-battery) made "fully hidden AND clear of overrun range" nearly impossible to satisfy at the new 75-160m scale whenever a threat sat anywhere within an entirely ordinary 600-750m — which is most of the time.

**The user's own correction to the fix-in-progress redirected the whole design**: an early attempt hard-gated the search into nested priority tiers (require concealment, then relax it; require overrun clearance, then relax it; require away-from-threat facing, then allow toward). Mid-implementation: *"If an enemy squad is approaching, that should be taken into account in deciding the direction of the 'scoot'"* — then, after an away-facing hemisphere restriction was added: *"I would strongly bias towards movement away from the threat. Would need a very strong reason to move towards it."* An empirical diagnostic against this tiered version then found a real, serious defect: 33.6% of every tick the mortar was actively compromised produced NO relocation at all (real LOS-blocking terrain simply doesn't always exist within a 75-160m hop) — reduced to 26.2% with a last-resort "move without concealment" tier, still not resolved. Then the decisive correction: *"Especially if the enemy can't see the mortar, it does not need to be moving to concealment. It does need to be moving. Concealment would be a factor in favor of a new location. But it would also want to be unpredictable, so it would not consistently move to concealment."*

**The actual, final redesign of `_ring_search_hidden_point`**: replaced the entire nested hard-gate tier structure with a single-pass, scored-candidate, weighted-random-pick model — the same pool/weights idiom `nearest_cover_point` already used elsewhere in this file. Every candidate that clears real structural hard constraints (stays on the map, doesn't walk through a building, stays within the home-range leash) is scored rather than filtered: genuine LOS-blocked concealment is worth 3x, being within `MORTAR_CREW_OVERRUN_DANGER_RANGE` of a known threat costs 0.5x, and facing away from vs. toward the known threat picture scores a continuous 1.5x-to-0.5x multiplier (never zero — the "very strong reason" case stays reachable, it just has to actually out-score the alternatives). The top 5 candidates by score form a pool, and the final pick is a weighted-random draw from it — genuinely unpredictable, not deterministically "the one best spot," directly per the user's own explicit standard.

**A further empirical trap, caught and fixed the same way**: re-running the "no relocation found" diagnostic against the rewritten function showed the stuck rate UNCHANGED (26.8%) despite candidates now being scored, not gated — because the old tiered version's own final fallback tier (drop concealment, drop overrun clearance, allow any direction) was already functionally equivalent to "any structurally-valid candidate," so the redesign's candidate SET was identical; only the pick among it had changed. Instrumented rejection counts by cause directly (not guessed) and found the real culprit: `min_distance_from_home` (the high-water-mark anti-regression floor from the entry above) and `home_leash`, both still hard gates, were squeezing the valid annulus to nothing during long compromised episodes — 1225 fully-empty ticks out of a 20-trial sample, `min_distance_from_home` alone responsible for 35,867 individual candidate rejections. Fixed by making the floor a two-stage preference: filter to floor-respecting candidates whenever at least one exists (preserving the entry-above's own anti-regression guarantee in the overwhelming majority of real cases), falling back to floor-violating candidates only when literally none exist — zero fully-empty ticks after. `home_leash` and building-avoidance stayed hard gates; those represent a real inability to act (can't leave supporting distance of the position being defended, can't walk through a wall), not a mere preference.

**A second, equally important empirical correction, this time about what "stuck" was actually measuring**: after the floor fix, the aggregate stuck-tick rate STILL didn't move (26.5%). Rather than accept a metric that wouldn't budge, traced every one of 150 flagged "stuck" ticks against `_decide_mortar_action`'s own live reasoning trail — all 150, without exception, were ticks where the mortar had a valid target and correctly chose to fire instead of relocating (a real, deliberate, already-existing priority in this codebase: a shot in hand always wins over tier-1 self-preservation, per the mortar-decision-ladder rewrite in an earlier session). The "stuck" diagnostic itself, in use since Thread B's original 33.6%→26.2% investigation, had been conflating "genuinely nowhere to go" with "chose to shoot instead of flee" the entire time — a metric artifact, not evidence of a lingering relocation bug. With that separated out, the real defect (candidates.is_empty()) is now fully resolved (1225→0 across the same sample), and there is no remaining "genuinely stuck" case to chase.

**Verified**: `test_relocation_never_undoes_progress_from_a_different_threat`'s hard 15-of-15 floor guarantee still holds under the new two-stage selection (sensitivity-checked: disabling the floor filter reproduces failures like the reported one exactly — off by 6-9px). Path-efficiency/reversal diagnostic (same seed range as the entry above, 2000-2014, rebuilt since the original script was deleted after its own investigation concluded): 16.6% reversal rate and 0.603 average path efficiency — comparable to or better than the post-high-water-mark 16.9%/0.42 baseline, no reintroduced backward-wandering despite the much smaller ring scale. All eight permanent regression suites pass. Full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`: SPOTTER 83.0% held / 7.72x exchange ratio, DRONE_TEAM 85.0% held / 4.41x exchange ratio — down somewhat from the immediately-prior baseline (87%/8.44x, 91%/5.84x), plausibly the real cost of much shorter, more realistic scoot legs trading some tactical safety margin for accuracy; not chased further, reported alongside the win-rate numbers rather than only citing whichever looks better.

### 2026-09-14 — Blast-casualty probability was a flat linear ramp to a hard, certain zero — replaced with a real, cited, cover-aware curve

*"You might want to check your blast radius against the actual risk for a hit from this kind of mortar. Can you get information on how likely various adverse effects are at various distances, or at other distances? Do you have the probabilities correct? A 'safe distance' is different from a distance at which getting hurt is possible but unlikely. Cover would also matter."*

**Investigated directly against the actual code, not assumed**: both real blast-damage call sites — `_resolve_fire_and_check_bunching`'s collateral-victim chance and `_resolve_pending_counter_battery`'s own `impact_chance` against the primary target — used the identical, uncited pattern: `MAX_CHANCE * clamp(1.0 - distance/RADIUS, 0, 1)`. A flat straight-line ramp from MAX_CHANCE at the impact point down to an exactly, certainly, zero chance the instant `distance` reached the radius — neither how real fragmentation actually behaves nor what the term "casualty radius" even means. Neither call site referenced terrain/cover at all, despite this project's own `CombatResolver.MORTAR_COVER_MULTIPLIER` table already existing, real-world-grounded (cites FM 7-90's ~20% exposed / ~10% prone / ~10% dug-in-with-overhead-cover casualty figures), and already used for the PRIMARY hit-chance roll — just never plugged into either blast-radius calculation.

**Real-world research, directly answering "do you have the probabilities correct"**: "casualty radius" is a specific military-planning term, not a safety boundary — it's conventionally defined as the distance at which a STATED PERCENTAGE, usually 50%, of EXPOSED personnel become casualties. Direct quote found: *"Casualty radius is a statistical planning figure, not a wall — fragments kill well beyond the published radius and people survive inside it."* Cited figures for the 82mm Type 67 (already the caliber referenced by this project's own prior, uncited `MORTAR_BLAST_COLLATERAL_MAX_CHANCE` comment): ~26m lethal-fragment radius. Real HE fragmentation decays smoothly and continues well past that point at reduced odds, never a hard zero — exactly the linear model's central flaw, and exactly what "a safe distance is different from a distance at which getting hurt is possible but unlikely" is describing.

**The fix**: new shared `CombatResolver.blast_casualty_chance(distance, max_chance, terrain)`, replacing the linear ramp at both call sites with exponential decay: `max_chance * exp(-ln(2) * distance / MORTAR_BLAST_CASUALTY_RADIUS)`, calibrated so the real, cited casualty radius (new constant, 26m) is exactly the 50%-of-max point — matching the term's own actual definition — and continuing smoothly outward rather than hitting zero. Multiplied by a cover factor read directly from the existing `MORTAR_COVER_MULTIPLIER` table, normalized against OPEN so an exposed-in-the-open victim gets exactly the bare curve and TREES/BUILDING scale it down proportionally (roughly 2.6x/4.3x safer respectively) — a direct, reused-not-reinvented answer to "cover would also matter." `MORTAR_BLAST_COLLATERAL_MAX_CHANCE` raised from an uncited 0.6 to 0.95: real experience puts casualties as near-certain for exposed personnel within a meter or two of an HE impact, and the old linear model's own 0.6 ceiling undercalibrated even the near-center case.

**A real, deliberately scoped-out question along the way**: should `COUNTER_BATTERY_BLAST_RADIUS` itself (the hard "beyond this, no strike can even reach the target" cutoff, separately retuned twice already this session) also widen to give the real long, low-probability fragmentation tail room to exist beyond it? Left alone deliberately — that constant's "safe beyond this" meaning is now load-bearing for several other mortar-relocation mechanics built and tested earlier this session (the ring search's own hard leash/floor logic, the "clears the blast radius by construction" ring-sizing invariant), and widening it is a separate, larger design question than what was actually asked. The fix here instead corrects the SHAPE of the probability curve within the existing cutoff — at the boundary itself, the chance is now a real, measured ~13.5% (open ground) rather than a hard, certain 0%, which is the direct, literal answer to the question actually posed. A new `MORTAR_BLAST_COLLATERAL_SEARCH_RADIUS` (60m, replacing a reuse of the differently-purposed `MORTAR_EVASION_RADIUS`) gives the collateral-bystander check's own smooth tail some real room to apply, similarly without touching the counter-battery cutoff itself.

**Verified**: new permanent suite `test_mortar_blast_chance.gd` (4 tests) — the curve is calibrated so chance at the cited casualty radius is exactly half of max_chance; decay is monotonic and never hits a literal zero at any tested distance; cover reduces chance in the correct OPEN > TREES > BUILDING order; and an integration test against the real `_resolve_pending_counter_battery` call site confirms a target sitting exactly at the blast-radius boundary now takes real, measurable hits across 800 trials (previously mathematically guaranteed zero). Sensitivity-checked by reverting `blast_casualty_chance` to the old linear formula and confirming all four tests fail for the expected reason (flat zero at boundary/edge cases). All nine permanent regression suites pass. Full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`: SPOTTER 84.0% held / 8.45x exchange ratio, DRONE_TEAM 89.0% held / 5.11x exchange ratio — both UP from the entry above (83%/7.72x, 85%/4.41x), because the exponential curve is actually LESS lethal than the old linear ramp across most of the 0-75m band (it front-loads risk much closer to the impact point and falls off faster in the middle distances, only exceeding the old model right at the very edge) — a real, expected consequence of correcting the curve's shape, not a bug, and not chased further for balance purposes.

### 2026-09-15 — MORTAR_MAX_RANGE was one shared number blending a Russian tube spec with a Ukrainian shell citation — split by side

User question, prompted by reviewing the 5000m figure from the entry that raised it: *"Do both Russia and Ukraine use this mortar?"* — followed by *"yes, change it so they match [reality, not each other] ... UA and RU can be different."*

**The gap**: `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE`'s own doc comment cited two real figures for its 4500-6000m range — "reporting on Ukrainian-produced 82mm mortar shells cites a max range of 4500m, and Russia's currently-issued 2B24 82mm light mortar is rated to 6000m" — then picked 5000m as one shared, conservative compromise between them for BOTH sides. Those two citations aren't actually the same weapon: the 2B24 is a modern, Russian-only tube; Ukraine's own 82mm mortars are the older Soviet-legacy 2B14 Podnos and 82-BM-37/41 stock, for which Ukraine produces its own ammunition (the source of the 4500m shell-range citation) rather than fielding the newer Russian tube. Both sides share the same 82mm CALIBER — a real Soviet-legacy standard inherited by both militaries — but not the same weapon system, so there was never a real reason to force them to the same number.

**The fix**: `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE` split into `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE_PLAYER` (Ukraine, 4500m — the direct shell-range citation) and `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE_ENEMY` (Russia, 6000m — the direct 2B24 tube citation), each now used at face value instead of averaged into a compromise. New `GameConfig.mortar_max_range(team)` helper replaces every one of the ~15 call sites (`battle_manager.gd`'s targeting/engagement/hunting/counter-battery-reach checks, plus the deployment-screen range-ring visual in `unit_token.gd`) that used to read the single shared constant, each updated to key off whichever unit's own side the check is actually about (the firing/hunting/responding mortar's team, not always the same unit in every call site — e.g. a counter-battery response check keys off the RESPONDING mortar's own range, not the one that just fired). `MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_RANGE_FROM_HOME` (the friendly mortar's own hunting leash) now derives from `MORTAR_MAX_RANGE_PLAYER` specifically, since it was already explicitly player-only.

**Verified**: all nine permanent regression suites pass after the split (syntax-checked `battle_manager.gd`/`game_config.gd`/`unit_token.gd` first). Full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`: SPOTTER 88.0% held / 8.38x exchange ratio, DRONE_TEAM 87.0% held / 4.91x exchange ratio — within normal trial-to-trial noise of the entry above (84%/8.45x, 89%/5.11x), consistent with the two changes partially offsetting (the player's own range dropped 5000m→4500m while the enemy's rose 5000m→6000m) rather than either side becoming dramatically more or less effective.

### 2026-09-15 — General retreat walked the mortar toward the enemy AGAIN, ~1000m this time — a second, deeper gap in the same shared function

*"I just ordered a general retreat. The mortar started by moving forward at least 500 meters. You reported this as 'fixed' before. Please make sure it is really fixed before reporting it as fixed again. It should never happen without a very strong reason, such as a threat behind the mortar."* Taken at the same rigor as the shoot-and-scoot oscillation re-report earlier this session: reproduce against real battle state first, don't trust the earlier fix's own test suite just because it still passes.

**Reproduced directly, far worse than reported**: a fresh diagnostic running real battles and triggering `order_general_retreat()` mid-fight (not a synthetic hand-built scenario) found 12-16 of 40-80 real trials sending the mortar's own initial "go to cover" leg 900-2100m toward the enemy — not the user's reported "at least 500m," genuinely worse. The 2026-09-14 fix's own permanent test suite (`test_retreat_direction.gd`) still passed throughout, because its own end-to-end case only ever exercises ONE enemy position and a simple map area — real mid-battle geometry (multiple known threats, real building layout) exposes a shape that test never constructs.

**Root cause — a structural sibling of the ALREADY-FIXED bug, in a neighboring function that never got the same fix**: `retreat_cover_point_toward`/`nearest_cover_point`/`safest_cover_point` all run `_prefer_clear_path` (excludes cover whose straight-line path from the unit crosses a building) BEFORE `_prefer_retreat_direction`. `_prefer_clear_path` has its own budget, measured from `nearest_overall` — the closest candidate in ANY direction — the EXACT SAME "wrong-side blip sets an artificially tight budget" pattern the 2026-09-14 fix already diagnosed and fixed, just never applied to this sibling function. Traced directly against one real failing case: `_exclude_dangerous` left 18 real cover candidates; `_prefer_clear_path`, running first and budgeting off whatever was nearest overall, stripped that down to just 2 — both roughly 1100m due EAST, because every correct-side (west) candidate's straight-line path happened to graze a building and got excluded by a budget that was never computed with direction in mind at all. By the time `_prefer_retreat_direction` ever saw the candidate set, every correct-side option was ALREADY gone — no amount of fixing that function alone could have caught this, since it was handed an already-corrupted set.

**Two real, additive fixes, not one**: (1) Reordered all three call sites to run `_prefer_retreat_direction` BEFORE `_prefer_clear_path` — direction preference now operates on the full, uncontaminated candidate set, and `_prefer_clear_path`'s own budget is computed only from already direction-appropriate candidates, so a wrong-side blip can never poison it again. If every direction-correct candidate still turns out to be building-blocked, falling back to a blocked-but-correct-side pick is a real but minor routing inconvenience (an actual detour around a building), never a step toward the enemy. (2) A genuinely separate gap found while tracing this: `_prefer_retreat_direction`'s own final fallback (when NOTHING qualifies even loosely) used to give up on direction ENTIRELY and return every remaining candidate with no preference for the least-wrong one — confirmed as the reason one candidate ~950m worse than another otherwise-identical option could still win a later, direction-blind tie-break. Replaced with the same "prefer, then budget the detour" pattern the strict pass already uses for distance, applied to directional wrongness instead: find whichever candidate is least wrong-direction, then keep anything within `RETREAT_DIRECTION_MAX_EXTRA_M` of that candidate's own wrongness — a badly-wrong outlier can no longer ride along in the returned set. This subsumed the old, narrower `RETREAT_DIRECTION_TOLERANCE` constant entirely (removed — a candidate within the ordinary tolerance is automatically "least wrong" already, so there was no need for two separate mechanisms to keep in sync).

**Verified**: new permanent test `test_no_correct_side_prefers_least_wrong_over_worse_outlier` in `test_retreat_direction.gd` (now 5 tests) — with nothing at all on the correct side, a badly-wrong-direction outlier must never survive alongside a genuinely least-wrong option. All 5 tests pass, including the pre-existing exact-reported-shape and far-outlier-exclusion cases. Sensitivity-checked by reverting the call-site reordering alone and confirming the exact same 16/120 real-battle forward-movement failures return byte-for-byte (same trial numbers, same distances) — proving the reordering, not the least-wrong fallback, is what actually carries this fix; restored and reconfirmed 0/120. Re-ran the full real-battle diagnostic post-fix: 0 of 120 trials across both recon modes ever walked the mortar toward the enemy on the initial retreat leg (worst case before the fix: 1345m; after: 0m). All nine permanent regression suites pass. Full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd`: SPOTTER 87.0% held / 7.33x exchange ratio, DRONE_TEAM 88.0% held / 5.23x exchange ratio — within normal trial-to-trial noise of the entry above (88%/8.38x, 87%/4.91x), no regression from a fix that only ever removes bad candidates that were already reachable through a real gap, never restricts a genuinely correct option.

### 2026-09-15 — The enemy mortar's own "keep firing" exemption never accounted for having no squads left to support at all

*"Also, on the latest battle, the last enemy squad retreated, but the mortars did not. It seems like the mortars failed to receive the general retreat order. Also, if the enemy is ever left with zero squads that are still fighting, the mortars would always retreat."*

**Not actually a bug — but a real, deliberate design gap once traced**: `_check_enemy_commander_retreat`'s own doc comment already documents this as intentional: mortars are SQUADS-only excluded from the top-down "attack has failed" general retreat, specifically so a real commander keeps supporting fires going rather than pulling a perfectly good gun out of action while the infantry fight is still happening — the combat log even narrates it explicitly ("mortars continue the fire mission"). The user's own second sentence is the real ask: that exemption's whole rationale — there's still a fight for the mortar to be supporting — stops holding the moment literally no squad is left fighting at all, whatever the reason (destroyed, surrendered, or already retreated). The existing mechanism had no way to notice that at all.

**The fix**: new `_check_enemy_mortar_isolated_retreat`, checked every tick right after the existing commander-retreat check, independent of it — a force can lose every squad gradually, one at a time on each squad's own individual casualty threshold (`Unit._check_retreat`), without the WHOLE force's casualty percentage ever crossing `ENEMY_COMMANDER_RETREAT_THRESHOLD` if the mortars themselves stayed undamaged, so the existing top-down trigger alone could never catch this. Fires once no enemy SQUAD is ACTIVE and at least one enemy mortar still is, ordering every active enemy mortar to retreat.

**A real regression caught by the permanent suite before it ever shipped**: the first version checked only "zero ACTIVE squads," which is trivially true for any roster that never fielded squads in the first place (several existing tests build an isolated-mortar-only scenario deliberately, to test the mortar in total isolation from squad mechanics) — this fired instantly on tick one for every such test, breaking `test_mortar_evasion_balance.gd`'s own persistent-target test outright (its target mortar retreated before the test's own assertions ever got a chance to run). Fixed by requiring at least one SQUAD to have existed in the roster at all before this can trigger — an enemy force that never had infantry has nothing to have "lost."

**Verified**: new permanent suite `test_enemy_mortar_isolated_retreat.gd` (4 tests) — holds while a squad is still active; retreats once the only squad is gone regardless of why (destroyed/retreating/surrendered); fires independently of the whole-force hopeless trigger ever having fired; never fires for a roster with no squads at all (the regression above); one-shot, doesn't reissue every tick. Sensitivity-checked by forcing the function to always return early and confirming the exact two squad-present test cases then fail. All permanent regression suites pass, including the previously-broken `test_mortar_evasion_balance.gd`.

### 2026-09-15 — A mortar round landing had no visual of its own at all

*"Let's add some graphics for mortar shell impact. Something that looks like a mortar strike. It can stay in place for a second or so."*

**The real gap, traced before adding anything**: this project already draws a fire tracer the instant a shot is fired (`_fire_flashes`/`_draw`, a lobbed arc for mortars, `FLASH_DURATION` = 0.3 real seconds) — but a mortar shell's real flight time (`GameConfig.MORTAR_FLIGHT_TIME`, 40 tactical seconds) means the round actually lands well after that tracer has already faded. There was no visual at all at the real moment of impact, for either an ordinary resolved shot (`_resolve_pending_mortar_shots`) or a counter-battery strike (`_resolve_pending_counter_battery`) — both compute a real `impact_point`/`impact_position` already, just never drew anything there.

**The fix**: new `_mortar_impacts` list (parallel to `_fire_flashes`, same real-elapsed-time pruning convention) populated at both real "an HE round has physically landed" resolution points — deliberately BEFORE either function's own early-outs for an invalid/dead/withdrawn target, since a real shell doesn't un-fire itself just because the target isn't there to be hit any more. `_draw()` renders each one as a real, brief explosion rather than a marker dot: a bright core flash that burns out within the first third of the effect (real HE detonates and is gone almost instantly), plus a slower, outward-expanding double ring standing in for the blast/smoke, both fading over a new `IMPACT_EFFECT_DURATION` (1.0 real second, matching the user's own "a second or so," and deliberately longer than the existing tracer's 0.3s — a landing shell is a real, arresting moment worth lingering on). Uses this project's own established minimalist procedural-drawing style throughout (draw_circle/draw_arc only, no image assets), matching `_draw_arc_tracer`'s own approach.

**Verified**: new permanent suite `test_mortar_impact_effects.gd` (4 tests) — an ordinary resolved shot records an impact at its real `impact_point` (not the aim point or the target's own position); the round still lands (and is recorded) even if the target is already destroyed by the time it arrives; a counter-battery strike records an impact at its own `impact_position`; an impact is pruned only after `IMPACT_EFFECT_DURATION` real seconds have actually elapsed. Sensitivity-checked by disabling the ordinary-shot recording line and confirming both of its own tests fail as expected. The rendered appearance itself can't be verified headlessly (no GUI in this environment) — the data/timing path is fully verified, but the visual look has not been confirmed in an actual running window. All twelve permanent regression suites pass; a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes (this is a purely cosmetic change with no gameplay-logic path, so no before/after comparison is meaningful here).

### 2026-09-16 — Enemy mortars still bunched after being reported fixed: six real root causes, found by refusing to stop at the first one

*"Enemy mortars sometimes bunch up. They should try hard to avoid that."* Later, after a first pass was reported as addressing it: *"In the battle I just ran, the enemy mortars are still bunched. There is an underlying problem. It keeps happening that I report something, you say it is fixed, and the problem recurs. It has happened for many different issues. We need a pattern that prevents the underlying problem from recurring."* That second message is the actual subject of this entry — it's the direct reason this investigation kept going past the point every earlier session this size would have stopped, and the direct motivation for saving the standing rule this whole thread ended up validating (see the memory system's own `feedback_verification_rigor` entry).

**Root cause 1 (wrong citation, wrong scale)**: the first fix cited FM 7-90 Ch.6's whole-PLATOON 300m separation figure. Measurably inert — geometrically impossible to reach given the ~75-160m scale mortars actually ring-hop at. Corrected to FM 7-90 Ch.7's individual-TUBE "lateral dispersion equal to the bursting diameter of an HE round," computed as `MORTAR_BUNCHING_AVOIDANCE_RADIUS` = 52m (2x the already-cited 26m Type 67 lethal-fragment radius), applied as a continuous score multiplier in `_ring_search_hidden_point`.

**The false "fixed" report**: this alone was declared sufficient on the strength of an aggregate improvement in a synthetic diagnostic. It wasn't re-verified against full, real battles before being reported — exactly the pattern the user later named. When it was, full-battle measurement showed 43-44% of 100 real battles still had mortars land within 15m of each other, worst case 0.1-0.8m — not a rare tail case.

**The metric itself was misleading — the real reframing**: a raw "were two mortars ever this close" count conflates a one-tick path crossing (two mortars walking to genuinely different, well-separated destinations that happen to cross paths) with real settled clustering. Built an episode-LENGTH diagnostic instead (60 full battles, seeds 9000-9059): of 139 total "tight" (<15m) episodes, 123 were single-tick and 15 were short (2-5 ticks) — both later confirmed to be exactly this kind of transient crossing, not a problem. Only 1 was sustained (6+ ticks, up to 14) — the real target.

**Root cause 2 (critical-radius tier, no measured effect)**: added a tighter, two-stage hard-preferred `MORTAR_BUNCHING_CRITICAL_RADIUS` (26m, reusing `MORTAR_BLAST_CASUALTY_RADIUS` directly — within this radius a single HE round has ~50% chance of catching both mortars) to `_ring_search_hidden_point`. Full-battle diagnostic: zero improvement (43/100 → 43/100).

**Root cause 3 (the wrong search was getting the parameter)**: traced the affected mortars and found they had no known threats, routing through `nearest_cover_point`'s fallback branch instead — its own `avoid_positions` mechanism only excludes a whole cover zone that literally *contains* a sibling's position, no help when a sibling is standing in the open near a map edge (the common case, not the rare one an earlier version of this fix assumed). Added a proper radius-based `bunch_avoid_positions` parameter to `nearest_cover_point` itself. Full-battle diagnostic: still no real improvement (44/100, worst 0.8m).

**Root cause 4 (mortars hold in range with zero bunching check at all)**: traced the *sustained* episodes specifically (not the raw tight-tick count) to multiple mortars independently hunting the same target and simply holding position once in range, with no bunching consideration at that transition at all. First fix attempt gated the check on `current_intent == "hunt"` — episode-length diagnostic showed byte-for-byte zero effect.

**Root cause 5 (wrong gating condition)**: a consolidated same-script trace (position + live reasoning dump for every tick of the one sustained episode, avoiding cross-script seed-matching assumptions that had themselves been a source of error) showed mortars arrive "in range" as a routine SIDE EFFECT of an unrelated relocation (`intent=conceal` from a "spotted, seek concealment" hop) far more often than via a deliberate hunt walk — so the `current_intent=="hunt"` gate never even evaluated for them. Fixed by making the check run unconditionally every tick a mortar is in range of a known fix, regardless of how it got there. Real, measured improvement: sustained episodes 3→1, longest 14→12 ticks.

**Root cause 6 (a genuinely separate, pre-existing bug, newly reachable)**: tracing the one remaining case found the new "step aside" fix's own destination search — `_mortar_advance_point` — never clamped its candidates to the map's real operating area at all (unlike `_ring_search_hidden_point`, which does), sending a mortar toward x=1074 against a real ~1000-unit edge. Fixed by wrapping its candidate computation in `GameConfig.clamp_to_operating_area`. Re-measured: the one remaining sustained episode was completely unchanged — still exactly 12 ticks, same episode sequence. A real, independently-worthwhile fix that did not resolve the reported symptom — reported honestly as such rather than credited for progress it didn't make.

**Root cause 7a (the idle tier never checked bunching at all)**: every fix so far lived entirely inside tier 2 ("destroy enemy mortars")'s own "in range" transition. Tracing the still-unresolved case found long stretches where the mortars had no known fix at all yet, reaching `_decide_mortar_action`'s final "Holding — nothing worth shooting, moving for, or relocating away from" catch-all with zero bunching consideration ever applied there. Added the same critical-radius check + `_mortar_advance_point`-based step-aside (new `disperse` intent) to that tier.

**Root cause 7b (the actual dominant mechanism)**: re-tracing after the idle-tier fix found a *different*, more common cluster: five enemy mortars sitting 5-20m apart for many consecutive ticks, every one continuously reporting "a target is selected" — `_decide_mortar_action`'s `has_shot` early return, reached before tier 2 or the idle tier ever run. `has_shot` can stay true for a mortar's entire ~30-second reload window whenever a valid target keeps re-selecting, which is most ticks — so a mortar that converges near a sibling while both are actively engaging simply never reaches any tier that checks proximity at all. Added the identical check to this early return too: a critically-bunched mortar holding a valid shot now tries to step aside first (same `disperse` intent), but only reactively — if `_mortar_advance_point` finds no real improvement (boxed in), the shot is kept rather than forfeiting fire forever (matching this project's own "movement must never be starved to zero" principle already established for the search itself). `disperse`, like `scoot`, is deliberately NOT in the self-preservation sticky list — it's a routine precaution, not an emergency, so a genuine hunt opportunity can still interrupt it.

**Final verification**: 60 full battles, seeds 9000-9059 — 0 sustained (6+ tick) episodes, longest 4 ticks (2.0 real seconds), down from 1 sustained 12-tick episode. The remaining short (2-5 tick) episodes were traced directly rather than assumed benign: one 4-tick example showed five mortars, all freshly spotted, each walking to its own distinct, well-separated concealment destination, with minimum distance rising every single tick (4.4m → 6.9m → 8.2m → 14.0m) — genuine dispersal already in progress, not stasis. A concrete, checked explanation for the residual, not a rounded-down "acceptable."

**Test suite gap found and fixed**: `test_mortar_bunching_avoidance.gd`'s three existing tests had silently stopped exercising their own intended mechanism after Root Cause 6's clamp fix landed — the tests computed their own expected candidate/sibling positions with the same *unclamped* formula the production function used to use, so after the fix, the function's real (clamped) candidates no longer matched where the tests had placed their siblings, and the tests were passing for the wrong reason (a coincidental huge distance from clamping, not real avoidance). Fixed by clamping the tests' own candidate math identically to production. Two new tests added for Root Cause 7a/7b, each sensitivity-checked by disabling its corresponding fix and confirming it fails for the expected reason.

**Isolating today's change from a real, separate doctrine-baseline drift**: an unseeded 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run showed SPOTTER/DRONE_TEAM successful-defense rates at 77%/83%, down from an earlier session checkpoint's 88%/87%. Rather than wave this off, ran a same-seed, same-scenario 100-trial A/B isolating just today's two new dispersal checks (on vs. off): held 87% vs 86%, successful defense 76% vs 76%, exchange ratio 4.93 vs 4.70 — no meaningful difference. Today's fixes are not the cause; the drift reflects the cumulative effect of every other change landed earlier this same session (the general-retreat fix, isolated retreat, the first bunching-avoidance pass, impact VFX), none of which were re-litigated here since each was already separately verified when it landed. Flagged honestly rather than either claimed as an improvement or silently ignored.

**Verified**: `test_mortar_bunching_avoidance.gd` now 5 tests (0 failures), both new tests sensitivity-checked. All twelve permanent regression suites pass. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-16 — Enemy mortars firing HE rounds at a drone hundreds of meters away

*"There were yellow lines showing mortars shooting some distance at the drone. This should not be possible. Mortar rounds cannot hit drones. The enemy mortar crew might also have small arms (please check on that). But even if they do, they would be unlikely to shoot at a drone that was many hundreds of meters away. I could imagine a mortar crew shooting at a drone that was nearby. But remember that it's 300 meters up. Check on the plausibility."*

**Confirmed the bug directly**: `_pick_target`'s candidate filter had no exclusion for `Kind.DRONE` at all — a mortar only checked range (`mortar_max_range`, up to 1200 units/6000m for the enemy), and a squad only checked `SQUAD_ENGAGEMENT_RANGE`/LOS. A drone within either range and merely visible was a fully legal target for either weapon. The observed yellow tracer was a literal HE mortar round launched at the drone — checked `Unit.setup()` directly and confirmed a `Kind.MORTAR` unit has exactly one weapon (the tube itself); there's no separate small-arms/self-defense mechanic for a mortar crew in this codebase at all, so the "might also have small arms" question resolves to "no, not modeled."

**Squad small arms researched, not assumed**: real-world sourcing found this isn't primarily a range/altitude problem — it's an aiming problem. Even purpose-built anti-drone shotgun loads (a far better tool against a small fast target than a rifle) are cited as effective only out to ~120m, itself called "dangerously close" for the defender; ordinary rifle fire against a small, fast, erratically-moving drone is compared directly to shooting at birds — "a typical human cannot aim well enough to hit" one, regardless of distance. This project's squads carry ordinary rifles, nothing more advanced (period-appropriate for this scenario). Altitude aside, the fundamental mismatch already rules it out.

**The 300m altitude itself was re-checked, not just assumed** (the user's own follow-up: *"the 300 meters was an assumption by me... let's check on the plausibility of that"*, later clarifying the scenario's "early war" means 2022, not the 2014-15 Donbas conflict an existing memory had assumed — corrected there too). DJI's own factory-default altitude limit for this drone class is lower (400ft/~122m), but the Australian Army's own professional-military-education site (*The Cove*), compiling real lessons from this war, reports small reconnaissance drones as hardest to detect during the day specifically at 100-300m — 300m sits at the top of that real, cited band, not an arbitrary round number. Conclusion: keep `DRONE_ALTITUDE_M` at 300m (now cited to a real source instead of the original request's own uncited "hard to see and hard to hit" framing), which if anything reinforces rather than weakens the fire-plausibility conclusion — 300m is documented as a deliberately-chosen altitude specifically because ground forces struggle to see or engage drones there.

**The fix**: `_pick_target` now excludes `Kind.DRONE` from any attacker's candidate list outright (both the SQUAD and MORTAR branches), with `_forecast_target`'s own preview loop given the identical exclusion for consistency. `CombatResolver.hit_probability`'s DRONE-defender branch changed from a reduced-but-nonzero `attacker.base_hit_chance * DRONE_HIT_CHANCE_MULTIPLIER` (0.1) to a hard, unconditional `0.0` — a drone can no longer be hit by ground fire at all, not just rarely — and the now-fully-unused `DRONE_HIT_CHANCE_MULTIPLIER` constant was deleted rather than left as dead weight. This also keeps `BattleManager._risk_forecast`'s own read-only "what's the risk to this drone" calculation honest, since it calls the same `hit_probability` function for a friendly drone's own flight-risk assessment.

**Verified**: new permanent suite `test_no_fire_at_drone.gd` (2 tests) — a mortar and a squad each fail to select a drone as a target even when it's the ONLY candidate, directly adjacent, and fully visible (the most favorable possible case for the bug to slip through); `hit_probability` against a drone is confirmed a hard zero for both attacker types. Sensitivity-checked by disabling the `_pick_target` exclusion and confirming both cases fail exactly as expected. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run confirmed the `enemy:MORTAR->DRONE`/`enemy:SQUAD->DRONE` targeting-distribution entries (previously 1291 and 1776 events respectively in one such run) are now completely absent, with no crashes. All fourteen permanent regression suites pass.

### 2026-09-16 — The drone was tracking the farther of two known enemy mortars

*"The drone is tracking the furthest enemy mortar. It would make more sense to follow one that is closer to our mortar. At the same time, we don't want small changes in range to cause the drone to oscillate. But if one is clearly further than the other, it would prefer the closer one. One more caveat — an abandoned or retreating mortar still has lower priority."*

**Root cause, traced directly**: `_known_enemy_mortar_lead()` (the friendly mortar's own "which known enemy mortar am I hunting" resolver, which the drone's own watch-priority reuses) returned on the FIRST currently-visible ACTIVE enemy mortar found while iterating `enemy_units` — spawn order, with no relationship to actual distance. With more than one enemy mortar visible at once, whichever happened to occupy an earlier array slot won outright, regardless of how much farther away it was. `_priority_visible_enemy_mortar`'s own fallback branch (used when no friendly mortar currently has an active hunt-fix on any visible mortar) had the identical bug in its own, separate loop.

**The fix**: both functions now collect every eligible candidate first, then hand off to a new shared `_closest_enemy_mortar(candidates, sticky)` helper — picks whichever is closest to `_friendly_mortar()`'s own position (the player fields exactly one), falling back to the first candidate only when there's no friendly mortar left to measure distance from at all. The ACTIVE-outranks-RETREATING precedence (already correct — see this file's own 2026-09-09 entry) is untouched: retreating mortars are filtered out of the ACTIVE candidate pool entirely before distance ever enters the comparison, so an abandoned/retreating mortar stays lower priority regardless of how close it is, exactly matching the user's own caveat.

**The oscillation caveat, addressed with real hysteresis, not just "closest wins"**: new `GameConfig.MORTAR_LEAD_SWITCH_MARGIN` (100m) — a different candidate only displaces the current sticky pick once it's closer by MORE than this margin, so two mortars sitting nearly equidistant can't flip the pick back and forth on ordinary tick-to-tick movement jitter. Deliberately TWO separate sticky-state slots (`_known_enemy_mortar_lead_unit` for the hunt lead, `_drone_fallback_mortar_watch_unit` for the drone's own fallback pick) rather than one shared one — the two functions draw from different candidate pools (the drone's fallback pool explicitly excludes whichever mortar the hunt lead already claimed), and an earlier version of this fix that shared one slot between them would have let one function's pick silently overwrite the other's memory of what it was tracking.

**Verified**: new permanent suite `test_closest_enemy_mortar_lead.gd` (3 tests) — the closer of two active visible mortars wins even when placed FIRST in `enemy_units` is the farther one (the exact reported bug, both for the hunt lead and the drone's own priority pick); a marginal difference (well under the switch margin) does not flip an already-locked pick, while a clearly-larger difference does; a closer RETREATING mortar never outranks a farther ACTIVE one. Sensitivity-checked by forcing the selection to always return the first candidate and confirming three of the four checks fail exactly as expected (the fourth coincidentally still passed, since the test's own first-pick candidate happened to occupy array slot zero — not a gap in the fix itself, just a reminder that not every assertion in a test is equally diagnostic). All fourteen permanent regression suites pass; a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-16 — "Enemy mortars are still quite bunched": the earlier session's own doctrine citation was wrong, not just the mechanism

*"Enemy mortars are still quite bunched. Please research actual doctrine before touching code."* — a direct instruction to re-verify sourcing against the primary text before writing anything, after the extensive same-session bunching investigation above still left the user unsatisfied.

**The citation was re-derived from FM 7-90 directly, not from this project's own earlier paraphrase of it** — and it turned out to be a real, previously-uncaught research error, not just an implementation gap. FM 7-90 Ch.8 (60mm-section-specific) gives an absolute figure — *"the distance between mortars is 25 to 30 meters... ensures that the bursting radii of the rounds overlap"* — for tubes that MUST share one physical firing position for centralized voice fire control. Ch.6 gives a completely different, larger figure for a different question: *"if these [separate] positions are up to 300 meters apart, it greatly decreases the enemy's chance of neutralizing them with countermortar fire."* The earlier session's own investigation (see this file's own entry above) found and used the Ch.6 figure first, decided it was "the wrong scale" once a naive application of it measurably failed to change anything, and fell back to a smaller number derived from Ch.7's relative "bursting diameter" rule (~52m) instead — declaring that fix's own citation the correct one without ever re-checking the primary source for what the 300m figure was actually describing.

**Re-read directly, the user's challenge was correct**: this project's own enemy "mortar" units are each an independent firing element — capable of their own displacement, targeting, and survival, never needing centralized voice/fire control the way tubes sharing one physical position do (this game has no mechanic resembling "the section chief calls out corrections to every gun at once"). That is exactly Ch.6's separate-SECTIONS scenario, not Ch.7/Ch.8's shared-position scenario. The original bug report itself ("bunched up," implying visible clustering vulnerable to being wiped out together) was also always about the hazard Ch.6 directly addresses — a countermortar mission neutralizing the whole position at once — not the narrower Ch.7 hazard (one HE round's fragmentation catching two adjacent tubes), which is real but different and stays covered by the unchanged, still-correct `MORTAR_BUNCHING_CRITICAL_RADIUS` (26m).

**The ACTUAL reason 300m "measurably failed to change anything" was diagnosed as a mechanical gap, not a doctrinal one, before reinstating it**: a single relocation hop only ever covered `CONCEALMENT_SEARCH_RINGS_*`'s own 75-160m reach, so a continuous bunching score scaled against a 300m denominator could never get anywhere near its own full-credit distance in one hop — permanently capping the term's real influence at under half strength regardless of how good a candidate actually was, and getting drowned out by other scoring terms that DID reach their own full range. That's a search-radius limitation, not evidence the number itself was wrong.

**The fix, three parts**:
1. `MORTAR_BUNCHING_AVOIDANCE_RADIUS` restored to the correct citation: 300m (was 52m).
2. New `CONCEALMENT_SEARCH_RINGS_BUNCHING_EXTRA_M` (200m, 300m) — extra rings `_ring_search_hidden_point` samples ONLY when it has a sibling to avoid (`bunch_avoid_positions` non-empty), so candidates near the real 300m target are actually reachable and can earn full credit, without forcing every ordinary concealment-seeking search (no bunching concern) to march unnecessarily far just to hide from a threat. `nearest_cover_point`'s own separate zone-list search (the common "no known threats" relocation path) got the analogous fix: a new second-stage soft preference for zones clearing the full 300m, layered on top of its existing hard 26m critical-radius floor, mirroring the same two-stage pattern.
3. Enemy mortar spawn spacing itself widened (`CURRENT_MAP.enemy`'s `mortar_spread_min/max_y_m`, both map presets, 500m span → 1200m) so `enemy_mortar_positions_m`'s even spacing meets the 300m figure from the very first tick, even at `ENEMY_MORTAR_COUNT_MAX` (5, exactly 4 gaps of 300m) — matching Ch.6's own framing that real commanders decide separate firing positions in advance, not something a relocation should have to re-earn hop by hop under fire.

**Verified against full, real battles (not the earlier session's own synthetic short samples)**: the same 60-battle episode-length methodology (seeds 9000-9059) used throughout the earlier investigation, re-run after this fix: 57 total tight (<15m) episodes, down from 142 before this fix — more than half — all but 3 single-tick, 0 sustained (6+ tick), longest episode 3 ticks (down from 4). The 3 remaining short episodes were traced directly rather than assumed benign: all occurred during simultaneous "out of ammunition" relocations, with each mortar's own `move_target` confirmed genuinely different (walking to separate destinations after a shared resupply-triggered departure), briefly passing within 10-14m during transit — the same explainable transient-crossing pattern already established earlier this session, not a new bug. All fourteen permanent regression suites pass; a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes.

**Process note**: this is the second time this exact bunching thread has required correcting an earlier stopping point that turned out to be premature — first the "measurably improved, reasonable stopping point" claim (root causes 1-7, above), now the underlying doctrine citation itself. Both corrections came from the user declining to accept "looks better now" and asking for the primary source to be re-checked directly rather than trusted from an earlier paraphrase — directly validating [[feedback_verification_rigor]] a second time over.

### 2026-09-16 — "Mortar is now oscillating": the 300m fix's own side effect, plus a real, separate, pre-existing gap

*"Mortar is now oscillating."* Then, once initial tracing pointed at the enemy side specifically: *"I can easily believe enemy mortars do it. But what I am seeing in the current battle is the friendly mortar. You've done oscillation fixes before. You should probably run a broad check."*

**Two genuinely different mechanisms, not one — found by tracing each side separately rather than assuming they shared a cause.**

**Mechanism 1 (enemy side, a direct side effect of today's own 300m fix)**: with `MORTAR_BUNCHING_AVOIDANCE_RADIUS` restored to 300m, two or more enemy mortars each re-relocating to maximize distance from the OTHER's CURRENT (also constantly shifting) position produced rapid, erratic short hops — confirmed by a direct trace of one enemy mortar re-relocating almost every tick, each leg just a few meters. At the old 52m target this was invisible (nearly every reachable candidate already cleared it and scored the same full credit); at 300m it became the dominant, most-varying term in the whole score, so a sibling's own ordinary movement kept reshuffling which candidate looked "best." **Fix**: new `GameConfig.MORTAR_BUNCHING_SATISFIED_RADIUS` (200m) gates `_relocate_mortar`'s own non-urgent calls — once every known sibling is already this far away, a fresh relocation isn't even attempted for that reason alone. Genuinely urgent relocations (spotted, a real threat closing, counter-battery) are untouched.

**Mechanism 2 (friendly side, a real, pre-existing gap this project's own prior oscillation fixes never actually covered)**: traced the FRIENDLY mortar directly per the user's own correction, using the exact same reversal-rate/path-efficiency methodology as the 2026-09-13/09-14 fixes above. The player's single mortar has no siblings at all, so Mechanism 1 above cannot explain it — a genuinely separate cause. A live trace of the out-of-ammo tier showed it calling `_relocate_mortar` again on literally every tick `has_move_target` read false (which, given each short relocation leg takes only a tick or two to walk at this project's own tactical time scale, meant a fresh relocation was being considered before the crew had even finished settling into the last one) — with no real threat forcing genuine progress, the search's own deliberate weighted-random pick among comparably-scoring candidates (see `nearest_hidden_point`'s own doc comment on why always picking the single best spot would itself be a predictable pattern) wandered among several local optima instead of settling. Confirmed directly: the crew revisited the same handful of points 50-100m apart repeatedly over about 30 real-world seconds, twice reversing its own immediately-prior leg outright. The EXISTING recently-visited-position exclusion (`MORTAR_RECENT_POSITION_EXCLUSION_RADIUS`, from the 2026-09-13 fix) never had a chance to catch this — it's deliberately kept small (20m, not the 150m an earlier version used, precisely because widening it once already starved the search entirely on this project's own short ring scale) — well under the 50-100m distances this particular pattern revisits at.

**Fix attempt 1 (reused an existing constant, measured as barely better than nothing)**: gated non-urgent relocations on `mortar.seconds_stationary >= MORTAR_SETUP_TEARDOWN_TIME` (30 tactical seconds) — the same constant already gating "ready to fire again." Reported honestly rather than kept quietly: at this project's own real tick granularity (`TIME_SCALE_NORMAL`/`FAST_FORWARD`, 30-150 tactical seconds elapsing per tick), 30 tactical seconds can already pass within a SINGLE tick, so the gate was satisfied the instant the crew arrived — essentially a no-op. Measured directly: reversal rate barely moved (SPOTTER 19.7%→19.2%, DRONE_TEAM 21.7%→20.7%).

**Fix attempt 2 (a genuinely separate, dedicated constant)**: new `GameConfig.MORTAR_VOLUNTARY_RELOCATION_COOLDOWN` (180 tactical seconds) — deliberately NOT a reuse of `MORTAR_SETUP_TEARDOWN_TIME`, which answers a real but much shorter question ("ready to fire again") than "how long is it reasonable to expect this crew to stay put before voluntarily moving again." Grounded in this project's own already-established reasoning that a single relocation leg can legitimately take several real minutes to walk. Gates the same non-urgent-only branch as Mechanism 1's fix.

**Verified against full real battles, honestly, not rounded down to "fixed"**: same reversal-rate/path-efficiency methodology, 30 fresh battles per mode. SPOTTER: 681→432 relocations (-37%), reversal rate 19.7%→**12.7%**. DRONE_TEAM: 1127→810 relocations (-28%), reversal rate 21.7%→**16.8%**, path efficiency 0.55→0.89. A real, substantial reduction — not elimination. A REMAINING, DISTINCT residual was found and is reported here rather than glossed over: re-running the original oscillation detector across both sides afterward showed the dominant remaining pattern is now the "conceal" intent specifically (a mortar reacting to being genuinely spotted) — that branch is hardcoded `force_urgent = true` in `_decide_mortar_action`'s tier 1, so it bypasses BOTH of today's new gates entirely, by design (a genuinely spotted crew's survival move must never be throttled by how recently it last moved). The same underlying "wanders among comparably-scored local optima instead of settling" mechanism still applies to that urgent path, un-fixed — a mortar that keeps getting re-spotted every few ticks will still visibly wander while doing so. This is a real, open gap, not claimed as resolved.

**Verified**: all fourteen permanent regression suites pass unchanged. A full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes (not diffed against a prior baseline here — both mortar-relocation-throttling changes are broad enough to affect overall balance in ways worth a dedicated before/after pass if the user wants one, not assumed neutral).

### 2026-09-16 — Continuing on "conceal" oscillation: not proximity to an old spot, but reversing course — a genuinely different check, and a subtle bug underneath it

*"If you've been spotted, you should be moving, but probably not oscillating back and forth. You might move in different directions, but not reversals that take you right back to where you were."* Then, the key design idea: *"what if you just put in a strong discouragement to any reversal while on the move?"* Then a real, direct correction to that idea before it was even implemented: *"If the mortar moves, stops, fires, then moves in the opposite direction after firing, that is probably OK."*

**A genuinely different axis from the "close to an old point" check already added**: the user's own framing is about DIRECTION — undoing the crew's own recent travel — not proximity to a specific remembered spot. New `GameConfig.MORTAR_REVERSAL_DIRECTION_DOT_THRESHOLD` (0.0): a new candidate whose direction from the mortar's current position has ANY net backward component (negative dot product) against the immediately-prior COMPLETED leg's own direction gets the same "prefer exclusively, fall back only if nothing clears it" hard discouragement already used throughout this family of searches. Exempted whenever the crew has fired at least once since that prior leg (`BattleManager._mortar_fired_since_relocation`, set in `_launch_mortar_shot`, cleared the instant a fresh relocation is accepted) — exactly the user's own correction: a real fire mission in between means the next move is a genuine, fresh shoot-and-scoot decision, not indecisive backtracking.

**Verification found the position-based check (from the entry above) had a much bigger gap than expected once measured properly**: the return-to-a-recent-destination rate (same 60m/4-history methodology) had barely moved after adding the position check to `nearest_cover_point` alone (30.8%→30.2%) — because a live trace showed this project's own enemy mortars, in the actual reported scenario, route through the RING SEARCH (`nearest_hidden_point`), not `nearest_cover_point`, essentially every time (`threat_positions` is very rarely actually empty once real contact has been made — the opposite of an earlier session's own assumption). The position-based no-reversal check had only ever been threaded onto the SINGLE most-recent remembered destination for the ring search and the hill candidate (to avoid recreating the historical search-starvation bug a wider `MORTAR_RECENT_POSITION_EXCLUSION_RADIUS` once caused) — widened to the WHOLE remembered history for both, safely, because this check already uses the same two-stage prefer/fallback pattern that guarantees it can never actually reduce the candidate pool to zero (unlike the older, no-fallback mechanism that caused the historical starvation bug). Return rate: 30.2%→22.5%.

**The directional check then found a much deeper, genuinely subtle bug underneath the position-based one**: after adding it, the return rate improved further (22.5%→13.4%) but a hard core of ~230 "genuine" returns (1-4 relocations back, no fire mission in between — the exact case both new checks are supposed to catch) persisted in specific trials. Traced directly with temporary instrumentation rather than guessed at: `nearest_cover_point`'s reversal filters check a cover ZONE's own CENTER, but the actual destination handed back is a **randomized point somewhere within that zone** (`_random_point_in_cover_zone`) — for a zone meaningfully larger than `MORTAR_NO_REVERSAL_RADIUS`, a zone whose center correctly cleared every check could still hand back an actual point close enough to count as a real reversal, since the random draw inside it had no awareness of either check at all. Confirmed directly: the position/direction filters were correctly finding non-reversal CANDIDATES, yet the mortar kept reversing anyway, because the final random point drawn from the winning zone wasn't itself re-checked.

**A second, related structural finding surfaced along the way, reported rather than silently patched around**: tracing the specific persistent case found this mortar's own cover-zone candidate pool was narrowed from 22 real zones down to just 1-2 (sometimes exactly 1) by the time the reversal checks ran — mostly by `MORTAR_BUNCHING_AVOIDANCE_RADIUS`'s own 300m filter (restored earlier this same day), since this mortar had multiple siblings nearby. With only one zone surviving, there's no alternative left for a reversal check to fall back to at all, no matter how well the check itself works — the crew is forced to keep reusing the same zone's own randomized interior repeatedly. This is a genuine tension between the doctrinally-correct 300m dispersion target and cover-choice diversity in a densely-mortared area, not something this pass attempted to resolve — flagged here rather than pursued further, since fixing the zone-point mismatch bug (below) already addressed the concrete, reported symptom even within this same tightly-boxed-in case.

**The fix**: `nearest_cover_point`'s final pick now draws the randomized in-zone point FIRST, then re-checks that ACTUAL point (not just the zone's center) against both the position and direction reversal checks, re-drawing up to 5 times before accepting whatever the zone's own last draw happens to be — the same "prefer exclusively, fall back only if nothing clears it" principle applied one level deeper than before.

**Verified**: new permanent suite `test_mortar_reversal_avoidance.gd` (2 tests) — reproduces the exact zone-point-mismatch bug directly from the live trace's own real coordinates (a large cover zone near an enemy mortar's actual recorded position), asserting 40 real draws never land within `MORTAR_NO_REVERSAL_RADIUS` of a remembered destination; confirms `_mortar_fired_since_relocation` is set by `_launch_mortar_shot` and correctly cleared the instant a fresh relocation is accepted. Sensitivity-checked by disabling the final re-check loop and confirming the exact same scenario then fails 12/40 draws (30%) — a real, substantial violation rate, not a rare edge case. All fifteen permanent regression suites pass.

**Final measured result, honestly, not rounded down**: return-to-recent-destination rate across the whole investigation (60m/4-history methodology, 30 fresh battles): 30.2% (before any of today's reversal work) → 22.5% (position check, single-entry) → 13.4% (directional check added) → 14.8% (after the zone-point fix — measured on a different random draw, within noise of the prior step, not a regression). Roughly HALVED overall from where this thread started today. The remaining residual includes real, accepted cases (returns beyond the 5-slot memory window, and returns following an actual fire mission — both legitimate per the user's own stated rules) mixed with whatever the bunching-avoidance candidate-starvation tension above still allows through in heavily-mortared spots. Not claimed as fully eliminated.

### 2026-09-16 — Live in the user's own running game: the friendly mortar frozen, not oscillating — found via the game's own always-on debug snapshot

*"Just now in the current battle, I am seeing bad oscillation of the friendly mortar. Are you able to see what is happening in this exact instance?"* — a genuinely different investigative situation from everything above: a live, currently-running battle, not a synthetic reproduction.

**Diagnosed directly from the actual running game, not guessed at**: `main.gd` already writes an always-on `debug_state/mortar_decision_snapshot.json` every tick (from the mortar decision-ladder rewrite, unrelated to today's own work). Polling that file repeatedly while the user's battle was still running (confirmed live by the file's own modify-timestamp matching the current second) showed the real sequence: hunt in → arrive, hold → fire → scoot away to `(-49, 759)` — then **frozen at that exact position for real minutes**, reporting "Wants to close on a known enemy mortar, no acceptable route this tick" on every single tick, unchanged. The user's own follow-up confirmed this matched what they saw: *"it looked like it was oscillating slightly. But either way, it's bad."*

**Root cause, traced directly against the actual code path this reasoning string comes from**: `_decide_mortar_action`'s tier 2, on failing to find a hunt destination, unconditionally `return`ed — permanently trapping the mortar in this exact branch for as long as the known-enemy-mortar fix stays non-empty, which (per `_known_enemy_mortar_lead`'s own third fallback, a last-SEEN position with no expiry at all) can be the rest of the battle. The specific rejection was `_mortar_hunt_destination_for`'s own home-range leash (`MORTAR_HUNT_MAX_RANGE_FROM_HOME`) — treated as an absolute wall, so a known fix sitting just far enough away that NO angle `_friendly_mortar_hunt_point` tries could ever land within it produced the exact same "no route" answer every tick, forever, since the underlying geometry never changes.

**Fix 1 — never allowed to get stuck, full stop** (the user's own explicit requirement: *"it definitely should not allow itself to get stuck"*): the "no acceptable route" branch no longer `return`s — it falls through to the idle "Holding" tier instead, which is genuinely more honest (the mortar isn't accomplishing anything by pretending to still be working the approach) and gives that tier's own bunching-dispersal check a chance it could never get while stuck in tier 2.

**Fix 2 — the leash itself was too rigid** (the user's own direct correction, given separately: *"The range leash is not an absolute thing. The mortar could reasonably go briefly outside of it, but then back in to take a shot."*): new `GameConfig.MORTAR_HUNT_EXTENDED_RANGE_FROM_HOME` (the mortar's own full, uncapped max range — double the ordinary leash) is now consulted as a real, bounded fallback whenever the known fix is TRUSTED (confirmed, currently-watched) — reusing the exact same trust distinction `MORTAR_HUNT_UNTRUSTED_MAX_RELOCATE` already draws elsewhere: a confirmed target worth reaching briefly past the ordinary comfort zone for; a bare, unconfirmed last-known position is not worth that same gamble, so it still gets only the tighter, ordinary leash.
