Neither fix depends on the other — a genuinely unreachable target (beyond even the extended leash) still correctly falls through to Holding via Fix 1, rather than the extended leash alone silently papering over cases that really are impossible.

**Verified**: new permanent suite `test_mortar_hunt_unreachable_fallback.gd` (3 tests) — a mortar with a permanently unreachable fix (reproducing the live scenario's own geometry) falls through to Holding across five repeated ticks rather than freezing; a TRUSTED target just beyond the ordinary leash but within the extended one still produces a real hunt destination; the identical target with an UNTRUSTED lead stays correctly rejected. Both fixes sensitivity-checked independently: reverting the `return` removal reproduces the exact freeze (tier stays "Destroy enemy mortars" forever); reverting the leash widening reproduces the exact "TRUSTED target… must still produce a real hunt destination" failure. All sixteen permanent regression suites pass; a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes.

**Process note**: this thread is a direct, live demonstration of why the always-on debug snapshot mechanism (built during the earlier mortar decision-ladder rewrite, not originally for this purpose) turned out to matter — it let the actual root cause be read directly out of the user's own currently-running battle instead of needing a synthetic reproduction to be guessed at and built first.

### 2026-09-16 — "300 meters should be a guideline, not a hard and fast rule": closing the candidate-pool-starvation gap left open above

*"300 meters should be a guideline, not a hard and fast rule. The closer the enemy mortars get to each other, the less they should like it. But they are allowed to approach closer than 300 meters if circumstances make other options bad."* — direct guidance resolving the structural tension flagged, but explicitly not pursued, in the reversal-avoidance entry immediately above (a mortar's own candidate pool sometimes narrowed to just 1-2 real cover zones once `MORTAR_BUNCHING_AVOIDANCE_RADIUS`'s 300m filter ran, in dense-mortar areas).

**Confirmed this was a real, if narrow, inconsistency once checked**: `_ring_search_hidden_point` already treats `MORTAR_BUNCHING_AVOIDANCE_RADIUS` exactly the way the user just described — a continuous score factor (`clampf(nearest_sibling_dist / MORTAR_BUNCHING_AVOIDANCE_RADIUS, 0.05, 1.0)`) that never excludes a candidate outright, full credit once clear, decaying smoothly (never to zero) the closer it gets. `nearest_cover_point` — the OTHER of the two relocation searches, used whenever a mortar has no known threats to route around at all — was the one place left over that still treated the same 300m figure as a hard, two-stage exclusion: prefer any zone that fully clears it, but fall back to ignoring the preference ENTIRELY only when literally nothing clears it. That "all or nothing" shape is exactly what starves the pool down to whatever few zones (if any) happen to clear 300m, discarding real information about HOW close a nearer, more-bunched alternative actually is.

**The fix**: replaced `nearest_cover_point`'s second-stage hard exclusion with the same continuous factor `_ring_search_hidden_point` already uses, folded directly into the candidate's own sort-by-distance-from-`from` field (`c.dist = c.dist / bunch_factor`) rather than a separate score channel — this function's own final selection was always a distance-sorted, weighted-random pick among the nearest few candidates, so inflating a bunched candidate's effective distance is the natural way to make it compete on the same axis instead of being either fully in or fully out. The existing hard, `MORTAR_BUNCHING_CRITICAL_RADIUS` (26m, real lethal-fragment overlap) floor immediately above it is untouched — that one guards a genuinely different, catastrophic-not-just-suboptimal risk (one HE round catching two crews at once) and stays a two-stage hard preference, same as everywhere else in this file.

**Verified**: two new tests in `test_mortar_bunching_avoidance.gd`. First, using the default map's own real cover zones (25 total): the single zone that is by far the closest option to a given position still wins a real share of draws (>25%) even when placed within 300m of a sibling — proof it's a preference, not a wall; sensitivity-checked by reverting to the old hard exclusion, which reproduces exactly 0/120 as expected. Second: the exact same zone, now competing on equal footing against two dozen real alternatives, is chosen measurably LESS often with a sibling sitting right on top of it than with no sibling nearby at all — proof "closer" is still genuinely less liked, not simply tolerated unconditionally. All sixteen permanent regression suites pass; a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-16 — Live incident: "mortar just got hit because it oscillated" — the post-fire scoot's own priority and objective were both wrong

*"mortar just got hit because it oscilated."* Then, unprompted, the actual design correction: *"Right after firing, getting away from firing point should be top priority if there are enemy mortars. I don't know what was conflicting with it. Really only nearby enemy squads should, or the edge of the map."* Then the key reframing: *"Maybe here is a way to think about scooting. After you fire, the top priority of scooting is not being close to the point you fired from."* And a final clarification: *"subject to the exceptions above that is."*

**Two genuinely different gaps, both traced directly against the actual post-fire scoot mechanism (`BattleManager._mortar_should_relocate_for_safety` / `_mortar_relocation_plan`), not guessed at:**

**Gap 1 — the GATE deciding whether to bother relocating at all was backwards from real doctrine.** It required a KNOWN (detected/sighted) enemy mortar in range before considering relocating at all, and even then only overrode a deliberate hold-position doctrine (`shoot_and_scoot == false`) once density compounded past `MORTAR_DENSITY_FORCE_SCOOT_COUNT` (2) known mortars. Real shoot-and-scoot doctrine exists precisely BECAUSE a crew can never know for certain whether it was detected — the mere fact the enemy fields mortars capable of reaching this position at all is itself the reason to assume counter-battery risk, not proof a specific tube spotted this one. **Fix**: new `_enemy_mortars_in_range` (ground truth, not "known") replaces the known-count baseline requirement; the doctrine/density override is gone entirely — a real threat now always means relocating, full stop, matching the user's own "top priority" framing. `m.shoot_and_scoot` isn't dead code, though — repurposed at `_decide_mortar_action`'s own call site to control how URGENTLY the crew responds (faster, farther) rather than whether it bothers: a shoot-and-scoot-doctrine crew treats any real threat as urgent immediately, a hold-position-preference crew still relocates but only escalates to urgent once density (using the KNOWN count — `_known_enemy_mortars_in_range` still matters for this, just not for the go/no-go decision) gets severe. Flagged here explicitly since it changes what the doctrine panel's own "shoot and scoot" checkbox actually does — worth a second look if the user wants it to mean something else, or to be removed.

**Gap 2 — the SEARCH itself had no explicit notion of "the actual firing point" at all.** `_mortar_relocation_plan` searches outward from wherever the mortar is CURRENTLY standing (`nearest_hidden_point`'s own ring search, or `nearest_cover_point`'s zone list) — reasonable when the crew hasn't moved since firing, but `_mortar_should_relocate_for_safety`'s own stopping condition only requires clearing `COUNTER_BATTERY_BLAST_RADIUS` from the recorded firing point, not zero drift — so by the time a relocation is even considered, the crew's current position can already be up to that same radius away from where it actually fired. A ring-search candidate genuinely far from the CURRENT position can still land close to the REAL firing coordinate if the current position had already drifted back toward it. Confirmed directly: a mortar 50m off its own firing point (still "compromised" — under the 75m blast radius), with a ring-search candidate at the search's own ~75m minimum ring, aimed back toward the firing point, lands only ~25m from it — a real violation the old search had no way to see. **Fix**: new `firing_point` parameter threaded through `nearest_hidden_point`/`_ring_search_hidden_point`/`_reverse_slope_candidate`/`nearest_cover_point`, resolved once per relocation in `_mortar_relocation_plan` from `mortar_recently_detected_firing`/`_last_detected_mortar_fire` (already-established machinery, not a new concept) — a real, hard, two-stage "prefer exclusively, fall back only if nothing clears it" exclusion at `COUNTER_BATTERY_BLAST_RADIUS`, checked FIRST in the ring search (ahead of every other preference — floor, bunching, reversal), matching the user's own "top priority" framing directly. Applies uniformly to both teams (the same shared search both already route through), a reasonable, non-controversial side benefit for the enemy too.

**The "subject to the exceptions above" framing (nearby enemy squads, the map edge) needed no new code**: tier ordering in `_decide_mortar_action` already puts a closing/spotting squad threat (tier 1's own "spotted"/"threat closing" branches) structurally ahead of this compromised-position check, and the map edge is already a hard geometric constraint (`clamp_to_operating_area`) the search can never exceed — both exceptions the user named already fall out of the existing architecture once the gate and the search itself were fixed to actually enforce "top priority" in the first place.

**Verified**: new permanent suite `test_mortar_firing_point_avoidance.gd` (2 tests) — a hold-position-doctrine mortar still relocates against a real but never-detected enemy mortar (Gap 1's exact scenario); a mortar that's drifted 50m from its own recorded firing point never lands a relocation within blast radius of the REAL firing point across 60 draws, despite the search only ever measuring distance from its current position (Gap 2's exact scenario). Both sensitivity-checked independently: reverting Gap 1's fix reproduces exactly that test's failure (and only that one); reverting Gap 2's fix reproduces a real violation (1/60) in that test alone. All seventeen permanent regression suites pass; a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-16 — A self-inflicted regression, caught immediately by the user: the fix above caused the exact oscillation it was meant to prevent

*"On the first shot of the battle I just started, the mortar oscillated immediately. Even more concerning than the actual oscillation is the fact that you reported this fixed, yet I saw it so easily."* — the fix documented in the entry directly above this one, verified with single-call tests and reported as complete, broke on the very first real battle it was tried against.

**Root cause, reproduced directly with a live multi-tick simulation before touching anything**: `Unit.seconds_stationary` resets to `0.0` every SINGLE tick a unit's `activity == MOVING` (`_tick_movement`). The only thing that ever stops `_relocate_mortar` from recomputing a fresh, randomly-redrawn destination on literally every tick is `MORTAR_VOLUNTARY_RELOCATION_COOLDOWN`'s own check — unconditionally bypassed whenever `urgent` is true. The entry above's Gap-1 fix folded `m.shoot_and_scoot` (a steady-state DOCTRINE preference, not a discrete emergency) into that same `urgent` bool at the post-fire "compromised position" tier's own call site. For any mortar using shoot-and-scoot doctrine — an entirely ordinary choice — this made `urgent` unconditionally true for as long as `_mortar_should_relocate_for_safety` held, which (per that same entry's OWN change) no longer requires a KNOWN threat and can now hold continuously for many consecutive ticks after a single shot. The result: a fresh destination every tick, the crew never completing a single leg — reproduced directly via a disposable multi-tick harness, 9 distinct destinations inside the first 30 ticks (0.5 real seconds) after a shot.

**The actual process failure, not just the code bug**: the two tests written for the entry above (`test_mortar_firing_point_avoidance.gd`) each verified a SINGLE call's spatial correctness — exactly right for gap 2 (the search geometry), but blind to gap 1's temporal failure mode entirely, since a single call can never show "does this get re-triggered every tick." This exact class of bug — a steady-state condition recomputing every tick because `seconds_stationary` never accumulates while moving — is the SAME root mechanism `MORTAR_VOLUNTARY_RELOCATION_COOLDOWN` itself (see this file's own "Mortar is now oscillating" entry) and `MORTAR_BUNCHING_SATISFIED_RADIUS` were both built specifically to prevent, and had already been documented at length earlier in the very same session. A real-battle-shaped multi-tick simulation — the same kind of test `test_mortar_evasion_balance.gd` already used for its own "OVER-evasion" contract — would have caught this before it ever reached the user. It wasn't run. Reported as fixed anyway. The user's own framing is the correct one: the miss is more concerning than the bug itself, because it's a repeat of a failure mode this project had already paid to learn once.

**The fix**: reverted `m.shoot_and_scoot` out of the `urgent` computation at this call site entirely — restored to the original, already-correct `known_in_range >= MORTAR_DENSITY_FORCE_SCOOT_COUNT` (density-only). `m.shoot_and_scoot` no longer affects this tier's behavior at all, honestly left that way rather than attempting another speculative repurposing — it's a real, deliberate gap in what the doctrine checkbox currently does, called out plainly rather than silently patched over. It still matters for the actual go/no-go decision itself, per Gap 1's own fix (a real threat always means relocating now, regardless of doctrine); it just can't ALSO control this specific cooldown-bypass without reopening this exact bug.

**Verified properly this time**: new test `test_scoot_settles_between_ticks_instead_of_churning_every_tick` in `test_mortar_evasion_balance.gd` — a real multi-tick simulation (shoot-and-scoot doctrine, a real but never-sighted enemy mortar in range, a separate spotted squad as the actual fire target) asserting the scoot destination changes at most 3 times in the 30 ticks immediately after the crew sets out. Sensitivity-checked properly: an earlier, looser version of this same test (measuring churn across the WHOLE 300-tick post-fire window rather than the critical window right after movement starts) passed even with the bug reintroduced, because this specific geometry let the crew accidentally clear the danger radius within a few real seconds despite churning every tick along the way — a real, caught-in-the-act reminder that a diluted, whole-window average can hide a per-tick failure; tightened to measure the critical window directly, which now correctly fails (9/30 changes) with the bug reintroduced and passes (0-3/30) with the fix restored. All seventeen permanent regression suites pass; a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-16 — A THIRD report, same session: the fix above only closed one of two paths to the identical bug

*"I did restart. You don't need to warn me about that. However, early in the battle, the friendly mortar took a shot, oscillated, and was hit as a result."* — reported on literally the next battle after the entry directly above, which had specifically fixed this class of bug.

**The gap the previous fix missed**: the "already moving — redirect toward safety" branch in `_decide_mortar_action`'s compromised-position tier called `_relocate_mortar` on EVERY tick this tier was reached, with no check for whether the crew was already displacing for this EXACT reason — its own comment claimed "already moving for some OTHER reason (a hunt, most likely)" but the code never actually verified that. The previous entry's fix only removed ONE of the two ways `urgent` could read true here (`m.shoot_and_scoot`, a doctrine choice); the OTHER, always-present path — `known_in_range >= MORTAR_DENSITY_FORCE_SCOOT_COUNT` (2+ KNOWN enemy mortars, nothing to do with doctrine at all) — reproduces the IDENTICAL bypass-the-cooldown-every-tick churn, completely untouched by that fix. The enemy in this project routinely fields multiple mortars (per this file's own "closest-enemy-mortar-lead" entry, "the enemy fields two"), so a battle where the player has detected 2+ of them hits this path easily.

**The actual fix, this time addressing the real structural gap rather than one specific trigger of it**: the branch is now sticky against itself — `if current_intent == "scoot" and m.has_move_target`, just report "continuing" and return, matching the SAME pattern already used one tier up (step 0b) for evade/conceal/out_of_ammo/linkup. Only when the crew is moving for some genuinely OTHER reason (current_intent != "scoot" — a hunt, most likely) does it still redirect immediately, exactly matching what the branch's own comment always claimed it did. This closes the bug regardless of WHY `urgent` might read true, rather than patching one specific cause of it a second time.

**Verified with a new, second multi-tick test** (`test_scoot_settles_under_density_driven_urgency_too`) deliberately set up with `shoot_and_scoot = false` — isolating this from the previous entry's own fix entirely — and TWO known (visible) enemy mortars to push density past `MORTAR_DENSITY_FORCE_SCOOT_COUNT`. Sensitivity-checked: disabling the new sticky check reproduces 19 destination changes in 30 ticks (worse than the doctrine-driven version's own 9, since two known threats keep the tier re-triggered even longer); restoring the fix brings it back to 0-3. The FIRST test (doctrine-driven, from the previous entry) is unaffected either way, confirming the two paths are genuinely independent. All seventeen permanent regression suites pass; a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes.

**Process note, said plainly**: this is the second live report of the exact same underlying class of bug in a row. The first fix was real but incomplete — it treated a symptom (the doctrine flag) rather than fully auditing the structural pattern (does ANYTHING call `_relocate_mortar` with `urgent` possibly true, on a tier not already protected by the step-0b stickiness list, regardless of source). That audit is what actually closed it this time. Worth remembering for anything touching this same "urgent bypasses a per-tick cooldown" mechanism in the future: check every path that can set `urgent` true for a given call site, not just the one a specific bug report happened to implicate.

### 2026-09-17 — General retreat routed everyone through a distant, unnecessary cover detour

*"I ordered a general retreat. Everybody headed for some distant cover before going off map. At least the general direction was backwards. The primary consideration in retreating is safety. Cover may be a way to achieve safety. But in this case, I don't think it was. The locations of the enemy squads was known. People could have just retreated backwards safely."* Confirmed directly from a real battle replay (screenshots of the live history viewer, both the user's own and a direct screenshot of their running Godot window taken independently): several squads with no known enemy anywhere near them all detoured to the SAME distant tree in the map's southwest corner before starting the actual pull home. User's own framing of the fix: "People could go straight back. The three squads might want to slant slightly to the South."

**Root cause**: `Unit.order_retreat` sent every unit to a cover-seeking waypoint FIRST whenever it wasn't already standing in cover — unconditionally, with no check for whether that detour bought any real safety. `nearest_cover_point`'s own retreat_dir/danger filters only ever narrow WHICH zone gets picked; they never ask whether seeking a zone at all was worth it for a unit with nothing known nearby. With retreat_dir constraining every unit to the same west-of-position half of the map, several squads with completely different actual exposure independently converged on whichever single zone the search kept finding as nearest/least-excluded — visibly "everyone piles onto one tree."

**The fix**: cover is a MEANS to safety, not a requirement in its own right. `order_retreat` now checks whether any known enemy sits within the SAME danger range `BattleManager._retreat_avoidance_offset` already uses (mortar-vs-everyone-else distinction, matching that function's own real-time bending) to decide whether a threat is worth reacting to at all. If nothing known is that close, the unit skips the cover-seeking leg entirely and goes straight into the ordinary `_step_retreat` dash — which already bends away from any threat that DOES turn out to be close, every tick, on its own. This directly produces the user's own "slant slightly south" for whichever specific units have something nearby, as a natural side effect of the EXISTING per-tick bending mechanism, rather than needing any new steering logic of its own.

**A new, general-purpose debug snapshot was added along the way**, at the user's own explicit request after asking whether the mortar/drone snapshot files already covered this ("Yes, you want visibility into everything"): `BattleManager.general_unit_debug_snapshot()` / `main.gd`'s new `UNIT_DEBUG_SNAPSHOT_PATH` (`debug_state/unit_snapshot.json`) — every unit on both sides, every tick, unconditionally, same conventions as the existing mortar/drone snapshots (see those functions' own doc comments). This investigation itself had no file-based visibility at all beyond screenshots of the user's own live replay window (taken directly, via the Windows-MCP screenshot tool, once the mortar/drone JSON files were confirmed not to cover general squad positions) — this closes that specific gap for next time.

**Verified**: new permanent suite `test_general_retreat_safety.gd` (5 tests) — a squad with no known threat anywhere skips the cover detour entirely; a squad with a real threat within `SQUAD_DANGER_RANGE` still seeks cover; a known threat far beyond that range doesn't force a detour just because SOME enemy position is known somewhere on the map; a mixed scenario (several safe squads + one genuinely threatened one) has only the threatened one detour, directly reproducing-then-fixing "everyone piles onto one tree"; a real multi-tick simulation (`bm._process` in a loop, matching this project's own now-established verification standard) confirms a safe squad actually makes continuous westward progress via the ordinary retreat dash rather than just sitting still with no move order. Sensitivity-checked: reverting the fix reproduces 8 of the above assertions failing (only the "real threat still seeks cover" test is correctly unaffected either way). All eighteen permanent regression suites pass; a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes.

### 2026-09-17 — Continuing the general-retreat saga: a mortar destroyed taking exactly the "distant cover" detour, then a deeper correction to what "safety" even means here

*"In the current battle, the mortar was destroyed because it headed for distant cover instead of heading for the much nearer map edge. In general, the map edge is better than cover. Cover is simply a means to reaching the map edge. We are looking for a safe retreat path. One existed. The mortar didn't take it."* Diagnosed directly from a live, freshly-restarted battle using the newly-added `debug_state/unit_snapshot.json` — the mortar died at `(-1060, 1867)`, still walking toward a cover `move_target` of `(-1131, 2307)`: barely 71 units closer to the map edge, but 440 units (2200m) further SOUTH — a real, measured, disproportionate detour.

**Root cause, found by reading `retreat_cover_point_toward` line by line**: it picked the 4 cover zones NEAREST TO THE MORTAR'S OWN CURRENT POSITION first, and only THEN preferred whichever of those 4 happened to be closest to the edge-aimed reference point. Once the existing danger exclusion had thinned the real candidate pool (routine with ~10 known enemy squads scattered around, as in this battle), the "4 nearest to self" shortlist could end up entirely off to one side with no relation to the edge at all — and a zone genuinely on the way there, even one much farther from the mortar's current position, never even entered the shortlist to compete. **Fix**: replaced the two-stage "nearest-4-then-edge-tiebreak" pick with a single ranking by REAL total trip distance (self → the zone → the edge), split into a new, directly-testable `GameConfig._best_cover_zone_by_total_trip` (the real map's own sparse zone layout didn't reliably reproduce the old bug's exact shape through a plain end-to-end call, so this was tested with a synthetic candidate list instead — 4 zones clustered near the unit, one much farther away but genuinely on the way to the edge, which must win on total cost despite being the single farthest-from-self candidate).

**The plain SQUAD retreat path (`nearest_cover_point`, used by every non-mortar/non-spotter/non-drone-team retreat) had ZERO edge-awareness at all** — not even the flawed nearest-4-then-tiebreak version mortar had; it never considered the edge in any form. Given `nearest_cover_point`'s own general-purpose role (also used by non-retreat callers — a squad diving for cover mid-fight has no "edge" to aim for), added a new optional `reference_point` parameter (default `Vector2.INF`, preserving every other caller's behavior) folded directly into the candidate's own sort-distance field — only `order_retreat`'s own SQUAD branch passes a real value, computed from `retreat_target_x` (the exact same line the "already safe" and cover-need checks below both use, keeping all three internally consistent about what "the edge" means).

**Then, mid-implementation, a deeper correction** — user: *"The primary consideration should be safety, not cover"* / *"I'm concerned that you may be over-emphasizing cover at a basic level"* / *"We want a safe path. Cover is one way to achieve that. But avoiding the enemy could often be better."* This exposed that even the SAME-DAY fix immediately above this entry (gate cover-seeking on raw distance to the nearest known enemy position, reusing `_retreat_avoidance_offset`'s own "worth reacting to at all" range) was still too cover-centric: a merely-KNOWN position, possibly stale, possibly no longer even watching this unit, was still being treated as sufficient reason for a real, single, fixed detour. Real cover/concealment earns its cost specifically by breaking LINE OF SIGHT to something that can actually see or reach this unit RIGHT NOW — not by a position merely existing somewhere within a fairly generous radius.

**The actual fix, replacing the distance-only gate entirely**: a unit now only seeks real cover if (1) it is CURRENTLY VISIBLE (`is_visible` — actively being watched/engaged right now), or (2) a known enemy sits within actual direct-fire/overrun range — `SQUAD_ENGAGEMENT_RANGE` (400m, this project's own already-established "a squad can only fire at a target it could plausibly see and reach" constant, not the far more generous `SQUAD_DANGER_RANGE`) or `MORTAR_CREW_OVERRUN_DANGER_RANGE` for a mortar crew — AND has actual direct LOS to this unit's position (reusing the exact same reasoning `_unwatched_threat_closing` already established for mortars: close enough and unobstructed enough to plausibly engage even without a formal "spotted" roll landing yet). Otherwise the straight retreat itself IS the safe path — `_step_retreat`'s own continuous per-tick bending (unaffected by any of this) is what produces real evasive movement for anything known-but-not-actually-threatening, without committing to a single fixed detour chosen once from a snapshot and then walked blindly regardless of how the situation changes afterward.

**Verified**: `test_general_retreat_safety.gd` rewritten to match the final criteria (9 tests) — no known threat skips cover entirely; a known-but-distant-and-not-visible position (previously would have triggered the old distance gate) now correctly does NOT force cover; currently-visible always seeks cover regardless of any specific known position; a close, LOS-clear known enemy seeks cover even without a formal spot; already-past-the-retreat-line wins outright over being currently visible; the mixed multi-squad scenario now gated on visibility rather than distance; the real multi-tick "makes actual progress" check; and the new synthetic total-trip-distance test for `_best_cover_zone_by_total_trip`. Every fix sensitivity-checked independently by reverting it in isolation and confirming the exact, isolated expected failure. All eighteen permanent regression suites pass; a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes.

**Process note**: this session's general-retreat investigation went through four real corrections in sequence — skip cover when safe, skip cover when already home, prefer real total trip over nearest-to-self when cover IS sought, and finally replace "known but possibly stale/irrelevant" with "currently visible or close-with-LOS" as the actual trigger for needing cover at all. Each one was found from a genuinely new live incident or a direct, explicit user correction, not from re-deriving the same one speculatively — the live `unit_snapshot.json` debug file (itself added mid-investigation, at the user's own request, once the existing mortar/drone snapshots were confirmed not to cover this) was what made the mortar's exact death investigable with real numbers instead of screenshots.

### 2026-09-17 — Mortar as a real high-value target: enemy squads chase it directly when close, and it can flee off the map as a genuine last resort

*"Mortar should be a primary target for the enemy forces. They should chase it when they get close. In the latest battle, they were dancing back and forth, but not really chasing. Let's allow the mortar to run away off the left edge of the map. But if it does, let's consider it to have retreated from the battle. It is safe but can no longer participate. So it should only do that if there is no reasonable alternative. For example, an enemy squad may be chasing it so it can't stay on the map."*

**Two real, related additions — a squad-side pursuit fix and a genuinely new mortar mechanic.**

**Squad-side: "dancing, not chasing," traced to a model mismatch.** `_enemy_advance_objective` already treats a known, active friendly mortar as the enemy's advance objective ("in particular in order to reach the mortar") — the underlying INTENT already existed. But `_next_advance_point`'s own bent-angle candidates, cover/concealment/encirclement scoring, and `ENEMY_SURROUND_STANDOFF_RADIUS` stop distance are the right model for a squad closing on a STATIC objective (the village) under fire — not for running down a MOVING, actively evading one. Each fresh bound only gets recomputed once `has_move_target` clears, aimed at wherever the mortar happened to be at that moment; a mortar that keeps relocating between reassessments (which it now does, per this whole session's own earlier retreat/evasion work) produces exactly "dancing": a short bent leg toward an already-stale point, arrive, reassess, repeat, with no guarantee of ever actually converging.

**Fix**: new `_chasing_mortar_in_hot_pursuit`/`_chase_mortar_directly` — once a squad is within `SQUAD_DANGER_RANGE` of a known, ACTIVE friendly mortar (and not still executing its own wide flanking route — see `_enemy_advance_objective`'s own doc comment on why that squad "hasn't arrived" at the mortar-chasing phase yet), it re-aims directly at the mortar's CURRENT position every single tick, bypassing the `has_move_target` gate entirely — the same "re-aims every tick" idiom already established and accepted for actual mortar-hunts-mortar pursuit — with no bent angle and no standoff distance, stopping only once within real engagement range (`SQUAD_ENGAGEMENT_RANGE`).

**Mortar-side: a genuine last resort, not a routine option.** New `_mortar_flee_as_last_resort`, reached only from the mortar's own urgent tiers (spotted, an unwatched threat closing, or just hit by counter-battery) when `_relocate_mortar`'s own leashed concealment search has ALREADY come up completely empty — exactly "no reasonable alternative." Deliberately reuses `Unit.order_retreat` completely unchanged rather than inventing a separate mechanism: fleeing off the map is a genuine one-way retreat, exactly like a general retreat's own `RETREATING` state, and gets every one of this session's own retreat fixes for free (skip an unnecessary cover detour if the straight run to the edge is already clear, never detour backward once already past the safe line, the real total-trip-aware cover search if a detour genuinely is needed first). Once it returns, the mortar's `state != ACTIVE`, so `_update_mortar_decisions` stops processing it from the very next tick on — permanently out of the fight, matching "safe but can no longer participate" exactly. Gated so a merely-throttled (non-urgent) relocation failure — e.g. the voluntary relocation cooldown — never triggers this: only a genuinely urgent tier's own failed search counts as "no reasonable alternative."

**Verified**: two new permanent suites. `test_enemy_squad_mortar_pursuit.gd` (4 tests) — a close squad aims directly at the mortar's exact current position, not a bent-angle candidate; a distant squad still uses the ordinary measured advance; a flanking squad doesn't switch to hot pursuit mid-route; a real multi-tick simulation (with the mortar relocating partway through, and the tactical time-scale multiplier applied correctly — `_tick_movement` takes scaled `scenario_delta`, not raw real delta, the same trap this project's mortar tests have hit before) confirms genuine, real progress closing the gap. `test_mortar_flee_last_resort.gd` (3 tests) — a spotted mortar with every relocation candidate forced to fail (home position set absurdly far away, a controllable way to force "no reasonable alternative" without exhaustively boxing in the real map) flees off the map; a mortar with a genuine relocation option available uses it instead; a routine, unthreatened out-of-ammo mortar merely throttled by the voluntary relocation cooldown does not flee. Every fix sensitivity-checked independently. All twenty permanent regression suites pass; a full 200-trial `characterize_doctrine.gd` run completed with no crashes.
